Plan and handover  ·  Imperivm: Great Battles of Rome

Imperivm Reforged

Rebuilding the Haemimont HMMSYS engine as a portable, open source executable that runs on an unmodified original install. Parts 0 to 5 are complete or nearly so. It plays. --play opens a retail map, runs its 1,542 scripts, draws the live world under the game's own command bar and info bar, takes orders from the mouse, starts the AI for fifteen players and can declare the match won. Rebuilt entirely from the shipped data: the interface is 24 declarative INI files, the orders come from the class graph's own tables. This page is the resume point.

Start here

Resuming cold

Repository: alesdi/imperivm-reforged. It ships no game assets and never will; it reads an installation you already own. Below, $GAME is a retail install.

Verify the tree, about a minute
# The dependency-free core build is the one that must never break.
# RelWithDebInfo is the default. -DIMPERIVM_SPATIAL_CHECK=ON checks every
# spatial query against the straight scan: on only to change the index.
cmake -S . -B build-core -G Ninja -DIMPERIVM_BUILD_APP=OFF
cmake --build build-core && ./build-core/engine/tests/core_tests
→ 133,767 checks, 0 failures

python3 tools/check_core_boundary.py   # CI runs this too, on every pull request
→ core boundary intact across 199 files

cmake -S . -B build -G Ninja && cmake --build build   # needs SDL3

pip install -e ".[dev]"
IMPERIVM_GAME_DIR=$GAME python3 -m pytest -n auto --dist loadgroup --no-loadscope-reorder
→ 446 passed, 3 skipped (the long network runs, opt-in)  (the corpus half skips without an install; a module is one unit
  unless marked spread, longest first by the last run's times -- tests/conftest.py)
Run the simulation
# A retail map, headless. The last argument picks a map inside a container:
# the conquest numbers its seven maps 3, 4, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10 -- not an index.
./build-core/engine/tools/imrun $GAME \
  $GAME/Adventures/GreatBattles/2_Great_Battles_Numantia.bfhp 50 800
→ 1,680 objects · 1,066 scripts (1,049 idle, 17 sequence) · 710 combatants,
  393 able to attack · 55 areas · 8 distinct traps, most-hit first

# Determinism. Two runs from one seed must agree per turn, per channel.
./build-core/engine/tools/imconform map $GAME $GAME/Scenarios/Balcans.BFHP --no-scripts
→ 6 checks, all passed

# Lockstep. Two *peers*, one command stream: the same orders on the same
# turns. The second line is what stops a pass that drove nothing.
./build-core/engine/tools/imconform lockstep $GAME \
  $GAME/Adventures/GreatBattles/1_Great_Battles_Zama.bfhp 20 800
→ 10 orders, 36 commands queued, 0 refused · two peers agree on all 20 turns

# Netplay. A peer per player, each told only its own orders, through the
# turn negotiator over a loopback that is late, out of order and repeats
# itself. Fails outright if no peer ever had to wait.
./build-core/engine/tools/imconform netplay $GAME \
  $GAME/Adventures/GreatBattles/1_Great_Battles_Zama.bfhp 20
→ 16 stalls, 16 duplicates, 0 refused · lengths 313..789 · 2 peers agree on all 20 turns, and with the unnetworked run

# And between two processes, over a real socket. --drop loses datagrams on
# purpose; the two "hashes" lines must match each other, not any earlier run.
./build-core/engine/tools/imconform udp-host $GAME $GAME/Adventures/GreatBattles/1_Great_Battles_Zama.bfhp --port 47800 --drop 300 &
./build-core/engine/tools/imconform udp-join $GAME $GAME/Adventures/GreatBattles/1_Great_Battles_Zama.bfhp 127.0.0.1:47800 --drop 300
→ datagrams dropped on both sides · hashes identical on both · udp ok, 20 turns, and the same as the unnetworked run

# The computer's war between peers that give no orders. A skirmish owns no
# units at turn 0, so --skirmish builds it as imrun does, seats the peers as a
# networked match's humans, starts each from its own seat, and asks for a blow
# between two players instead of orders. --setup map is imrun's own game.
./build-core/engine/tools/imconform lockstep $GAME $GAME/Scenarios/Crossroads.BFHP 2369 800 --skirmish --setup map
→ two peers, as players 0 and 1, agree on all 2369 turns · p2 takes p0's stronghold at 2,342 · session hash = imrun's
./build-core/engine/tools/imconform netjoin $GAME $GAME/Scenarios/Crossroads.BFHP 1500 1 --skirmish
→ the computer takes p2 at 453, gives it back at 605, takes it at 757, gives it back at 905 · netjoin ok
python3 tools/verify.py --war   # also runs these long network runs (IMPERIVM_LONG_NET)

# The editor's passability rebuild, from nothing, against the shipped layer.
# Every map a player can open comes back identical; the corpus test insists.
./build-core/engine/tools/immap passability $GAME $GAME/Scenarios/Crossroads.BFHP 1
→ 1024 x 1024 cells: stored 400835 set, rebuilt 400835 set, 0 missing, 0 extra, identical

# Save, load into a fresh session, and prove the round trip three ways.
./build/engine/tools/imsave --help

# A player profile's career, aggregated the way the Change player screen
# aggregates it. The last two lines are the whole proof of that format.
./build-core/engine/tools/imcheck profile $GAME Nome
→ records 57 · rank Master · rating 128 · hours 53 · most_units 519
  hash -1650049073 · stored -1650049073

# How much of the shipped host surface actually has a body. Finds the
# install itself; the last column is a correctness figure and must stay 0.
python3 tools/host_coverage.py
→ 33,957 of 33,965 call sites (100%) · unknown 0 · ceiling 99.976%

# Or all of it at once: the build first, as the stale-binary guard needs,
# then the net tests alone, then everything else side by side.
# Run the smallest tier that can see what you changed.
python3 tools/verify.py            # quick: after most changes
→ build, net tests, core tests, boundary, fixtures, coverage, short trap pass · about 10 s wall (58 s if build-core is Debug)
python3 tools/verify.py --full     # the save path, the session, the app, the tools, a milestone
→ quick, plus the app build, the save sweep and the Python suite, the last two from build/'s optimised tools · about 3 minutes wall, 439 passed + 1 deselected (save_sweep runs it)
python3 tools/verify.py --war      # in no tier; see the war pass below

# docs/legal.md rule 1: is any fixture in the tree a copy of the game's
# own bytes? Skips where there is no installation to compare against.
python3 tools/check_fixtures.py
→ clean · no fixture in the tree is a copy of installation data
# It has caught one since: two map labels, names and coordinates, in
# test_xml_patch.cpp. Invented ones now; the test asserts the shape.

# What to write next, computed rather than remembered, and the work list
# over every map. Both trap figures matter; they rank different things.
python3 tools/host_coverage.py --leaders
→ 8 names with no body, over 8 call sites · gbr.exe registers none of the eight

# And the cheapest wins: not which name blocks the most calls, but which
# blocks the most scripts, and which is the *only* thing blocking one.
python3 tools/host_coverage.py --scripts
→ 5 scripts blocked · every one of them can never run on the original either

# And the third ranking: which names, taken *together*, finish the most
# scripts. A family that always appears together scores zero on the other two.
python3 tools/host_coverage.py --block 8
→ the ceiling: every reachable call site has a body; the 5 left can never finish at all
python3 tools/trap_sweep.py        # maps in parallel; --jobs 1 prints the same
→ 25 maps run, 4 refused · 0 distinct (message, source), 0 messages, 0 hits — with the AI running and every stronghold standing, nothing in the installation traps
→ war: 4,000 turns on each runnable skirmish · 0 hits over 5 maps · about 8 minutes wall for the pass  (--war only: a second tally that sees what the AI reaches late; too slow for any tier)

# And the save path, against the inputs the app uses: every map saved,
# loaded into a fresh session, and run on identically. In verify --full.
python3 tools/save_sweep.py        # maps in parallel; --jobs 1 prints the same
→ 25 of 25 runnable maps round-trip · 0 failed; mid-war 1 of 1
Play it
# The real terrain, the live simulation, the game's own command bar, and a mouse.
# Left-drag selects. Right-click orders. Middle-drag pans.
# Space pauses; `.` steps one turn, which is how a divergence gets looked at.
./build/engine/app/imperivm --game $GAME \
  --map Adventures/GreatBattles/2_Great_Battles_Numantia.bfhp --play --player 0
→ 1,680 objects, 1,542 scripts, AI started for 15 players

# Headless: no window, no sound, no focus taken; a scripted run and its shots.
# This is what the tests run. IMPERIVM_HEADLESS=0 shows the window again.
./build/engine/app/imperivm --headless --game $GAME \
  --map Adventures/GreatBattles/3_Great_Battles_Alesia.bfhp --play --frames 240 --screenshot /tmp/alesia.png
→ gpu: metal, headless 1100x850 px at 60 Hz, no window · screenshot: /tmp/alesia.png (1100x850) at turn 40

# A skirmish to its end, headless, and its end screen.
./build/engine/app/imperivm --headless --game $GAME --map Scenarios/Crossroads.BFHP --play \
  --difficulty 0 --turn-length 800 --turn-interval 1 --input "over;wait:5;shot:/tmp/end.png;press:Quit"
→ *** match over: you lose ***  decided: at turn 2369, hash 11dc4c1e235fadfe

# Two machines, one match. Same map on both; the lobby refuses a different
# data.pak or map. Each plays its own seat; pause and saves are off.
./build/engine/app/imperivm --game $GAME --map Adventures/GreatBattles/1_Great_Battles_Zama.bfhp --host 47800
./build/engine/app/imperivm --game $GAME --map Adventures/GreatBattles/1_Great_Battles_Zama.bfhp --join HOST:47800

# Crossroads declares a victory condition, so there 14 victory scripts start
# and the match can be won. 19 of the 22 shipped containers declare none.
See it draw
# A real map, pannable. Arrow keys or drag; --at X,Y --map-index N --screenshot F
./build/engine/app/imperivm --game $GAME \
  --map Adventures/GreatBattles/2_Great_Battles_Numantia.bfhp --at 10600,1600

# Sprites on the GPU in four player colours
./build/engine/app/imperivm --game $GAME --players --animate

# Export every sprite (3,898 images, ~33s) and browse them
imsprite export-all --game $GAME --out /tmp/export && imview serve /tmp/export

Where things live. docs/formats/ specifies every shipped file format, one document each, every one carrying a mandatory “what is still unknown” section. docs/engine/ holds the five design documents: architecture.md (the rule that governs everything), projection.md, tick.md, state-vector.md, rendering.md. docs/tools/ documents the CLI. src/imperivm/ is the Python reference implementation, which is ground truth for every C++ port. engine/ is the engine.

Status

Where the eight parts stand

6Interface layerbars and menus live136 of 136 screens load; the bars show the selection and issue its commands; the front, the skirmish setup, the campaign map, the game menu, saves, notes, diplomacy, the zoom map, the end of a match and its Statistics are the shipped screens, in the installation's language; the editor places, paints the terrain, the height and the decorations, draws areas, turns, opens an object's property sheet and saves, keeps the passability layer the way the original keeps it — proven bit for bit against all 23 playable maps — and its Adventure Palette edits every document the container holds, makes new ones, and adds, imports, deletes and switches maps
7Campaigns, saves, multiplayercampaigns, saves and multiplayer donesaves are files the app writes and resumes; a conquest carries across its missions; multiplayer started from the protocol end — the command stream, the turn negotiator, the link layer and the lobby are proved on a hostile loopback and between processes over UDP, and the app hosts and joins from the command line and from the shipped screens; a player who leaves is handed to the computer and the match goes on; speed is an order; players chat; a player can join a running match on a seat the computer took over

It plays, and Part 7 is under way. A right click resolves its verb from the class graph's own <defaultcmd> lists and the verify= scripts that gate them; the interface is the game's own 24 INI files; the AI is the game's own Main.vs. The application knows nothing about what “attack” means or what a command bar looks like, which is the property the whole project is built on.

The measurement moved, and not in our favour. Coverage was reported as 79% for months. It was 79% of data.pak, which is 65% of the shipped scripts; the campaign containers hold the other 308, and they use 79 entry points that appear in no pack. Against the whole installation the real figure is 69%, and the campaign half of it is 57%. Nothing regressed — the number was always measuring less than it claimed. It is now computed by a tool from the built table, so it cannot drift again.

Load-bearing knowledge

What has been established

Verified across the whole retail corpus. Later work depends on these; each was expensive to learn and is cheap to forget.

The thesis held

Haemimont shipped nearly all of the game as editable data. 845 classes bottom out in 26 native ones. The AI, economy, hero skills and unit behaviour are 577 plain-text scripts, all of which parse and compile. The interface is 139 INI files. This is a new host for content that was never code, and that is why four parts finished quickly.

Formats — all decoded, all validated

FormatStrongest check
pak — 14,602 filesall 13 archives rebuild byte-identical
LZIS47 MB decompresses byte-identical
IMGRLE — 400 MB store197,432 frames tile it exactly
HPFS — maps, saves23 of 23 rebuild byte-identical
vqbm, GRID, APFheader arithmetic exact; renders legibly
XML — 2,867 documents2,866 parse; 2,862/2,863 hash-identical vs ElementTree
VS — 577 scripts577/577 parse and compile; 0 of 17,196 call sites unresolved
ini — 150 filesall parse; two independent readers agree on every counter
AI.INI — the AI profilesthe constant families it declares close exactly against the scripts that use them
loc.xml — 3,887 translationsall parse; no duplicate key, no empty result, no placeholder mismatch
The last target search, and the node it looks in (done)Unit::BestTargetInGAIKA/0 — 2 sites, the sole blocker of both HERO_AI_KILLALL.VS and UNIT_AI_KILLALL.VS, and the last member of the target-selection family. Names with no body 187 → 186; unimplemented sites 322 → 320; blocked scripts 131 → 129.

It was refused for a year on the wrong ground. sim/combat.cpp said it “needs the node table”, and the node table has existed since the partition thread; what it actually needs is the node table and the settlement store, both of which the last three blocks put in reach. That is the third GAIKA member in a row whose stated blocker had already been cleared.

It is the one member of the family that does not sweep a circle. 0x00431090 walks the squads standing in the receiver's node and hands them to the search object one at a time, so the radius every other variant gets from its grid query is simply never applied — a candidate on the far side of the node is as eligible as one underfoot, and a tie keeps the first offered rather than the lowest id. The four squad tests are strength (Squad::Eval), the enemy bit of 0x0044e250 read off the receiver's own row, SF_PEACEFUL refused, and here rather than heading here: 0x0044e1f0 answers 4 for a squad that is here and staying, 2 for one that is here and leaving, 1 for one that is only on its way, and the caller masks with 6.

The refinement between those tests cannot change the answer, and that is measured rather than waved away. 0x0044eb80 re-reads the value through the asking player's AI memory and can do exactly two things to it — promote 2 to 4, or demote 1 to 0. Both pairs sit on the same side of the & 6 mask, so the entry point is blind to it, and nothing here models the AI memory it would need.

What answers today is the fallback, and it is a catapult. With no squad candidate the original walks the world's settlement vector, resolves each settlement's central building, and takes the first that is an heir of "Catapult" — a Building declaring is_central_building = 1, so a placed siege engine owns a settlement and turns up beside the town halls — that is standing in the receiver's node and that counts the receiver an enemy in the transpose: the row read is the catapult owner's, indexed by the receiver's player, not the other way round. That is the whole of the observable behaviour here, because nothing in this engine assigns a GAIKA to a squad or writes Squad::Eval; the squad chain is written out in full anyway, on GetAIControlledUnits's precedent. Both callers guard the call with sq.AIDest == sq.GAIKAIn, and on this engine both are kNoGaika and therefore equal, so the call is reached rather than skipped.

And the fallback is narrower here than in the original, which is the node approximation showing through for the first time in a way that costs something. Every settlement gets a node centred on its central building, so a catapult is a node centre and the receiver shares its node exactly when that catapult is the nearest node centre to it. The original's slot grid makes a node a region, where any catapult inside would match. The reading is identical; the reach is smaller — “an enemy siege engine right here” rather than “somewhere in this region” — and it widens with no change to the code the day the partition is measured.

19 faults injected, 18 caught, and the survivor was a guard rather than a bug. GaikaTable::at cannot answer an id the table does not have, so the second half of the receiver's node check — the original's separate test of the pointer 0x0044e8e0 returns — could not be made to fail and is gone, on the precedent sim/squad.cpp set for the bounds check on the same walk. The first half is not redundant and now has a case of its own: kNoGaika is every squad's default GAIKAIn, so carrying it into the walk instead of refusing it would read every squad in the world as standing here.
The long tail, taken four at a time (done)Twenty-seven names in four passes, chosen for being cheap and evidenced rather than for being large, and between them they close eleven scripts. Names with no body 186 → 159; unimplemented sites 320 → 281; blocked scripts 129 → 116.

Seven are one slot on a class descriptor. Building::minlevel/0, levelperitem/0 and itemtypes/0 are the whole of what RUIN_BEHAVIOR.VS was waiting for — +0x6bc, +0x6c0 and the string at +0x6a0, which is itemtype singular because the value is a comma-separated list the script runs ParseStr down twice. GetNumSentrySlots/0 and GetSentryClassName/0 are num_sentry_slots and sentry_class_name, which the walls declare and the gates override one of. Obj::IsRam/0 and IsPeasantAmbient/0 are one IsHeirOf apiece against a class the function looks up once and caches — descent, not name equality.

GetClassRace(str) is Obj::race with the class named instead of carried, and that is why it exists: BARRACK_TRAIN.VS (64 sites) asks about the class it is about to train, before anything of that class exists to ask. Hero::maxarmy/0 is the cap and not the class number — [hero+0x1f8] is the same field IsHeroArmyFull compares against, so administration moves both. Settlement::IsCity/0 is two hard-coded ten-byte comparisons against "GTownhall" and "RTownhall", so a subclass of one is not a city. Squad::No/0 answers the index half of a packed handle without resolving it. GAIKA::Empty/0 is the node's squad container tested for null, asked from the squad side.

Two are always the same number, and saying which is the point. Settlement::PopulationDied/0 reads [settlement+0xf0], and a sweep of .text for a write to [reg+0xf0] finds four, all of them stack slots — the same shape as Squad::Eval. GetResearchHack/0 is false and stays false: the only writer of its global is a debug path that sets and clears it inside one function.

Seven are accepted and dropped on GlobalSpellStart's precedent — the input is named, nothing is stored. SetGlobalBloodlust/1 (whose global only prices a stonehenge spell), InvalidateRegenConsts/0 (a cache this engine does not keep), PopTransportationUI/0, SetBuildFrame/1, ShowBuildAnimation/1, SetDebug/1 and Unit::RecalcBonuses/0. The last is a claim about this engine and not the original: vtbl+0xc4 rebuilds cached bonuses and nothing here caches them, so there is nothing to rebuild.

ONFINISH_RESEARCH.VS — 146 sites — needed three names and got all three. SetExperienceModifier(player, percent) is the Battle tactics research and it is per player: 0x005d2fb0 writes the player record at +0x84, not a global, so CombatConstants::experience_modifier is now only the default. It is turn state, so it is saved and hashed, unlike the difficulty addends beside it. Settlement::SetFoodProduction/1 writes [settlement+0x48], which is food_rate — the field whose comment has named this entry point since the production block was written.

A mule's load is on the mule. Wagon::amount/0 and restype/0 read [obj+0x1cc] and [obj+0x1d4], and the shipment records this engine keeps for settlement-to-settlement trade are a different thing with no world object — ES_OUTPOSTSELLGOLD.VS settles it by walking a group of objects and summing their cargo. ObjectState grew the two fields; LoadGold/1 and LoadFood/1 are one body with the resource swapped, and their four steps are the settlement held in, the one-kind rule, the max_load clamp and whatever the warehouse can pay. The kind is written before the warehouse is asked, which is visible in exactly one arrangement — a class with no max_load — and that is the case the test builds.

One decision was reversed and the reason is worth keeping. Catapult::SetBuildFrame/1 was deliberately left trapping, with a note saying its call sites were “unreachable as well as invented” because CATAPULT_IDLE.VS was blocked on eight other names. Six of those eight have since been implemented; the trap was not protecting anything, it was one of the two things stopping that script, which is now down to one blocker. Its test asserts the opposite of what it used to.

68 faults across the five passes, 68 caught, eleven of them only after a fixture grew to construct the arrangement that separates two readings: minlevel and levelperitem are both 4 in the shipped data; a class name that resolves only by altid; a squad whose GAIKAIn names a node the table does not have; a duplicate row in a per-player table that sorts ahead of the old one and answers first; a row filed under kNoPlayer that answers for nobody. The last two are invisible to every getter and show only in the saved bytes, which is where they are now asserted. Two guards came out as redundant and two are kept and labelled. The conformance golden moved twice, because the object hash grew three fields.

Obj::SetSight/1 came last and made a fourth. Two refusals that store nothing — under 100 and over 2048, the second message's typo included — a reveal that runs only when the radius widens and only for an object with a position of its own, and a store that happens either way. It turns WorldObject::sight into turn state: it used to be resolved from the class at spawn, written into the save because a reload could not recompute it, and deliberately left out of state_hash. A script can move it now, and two peers who disagree about how far a scout sees disagree about what it finds.
The army the AI actually sends: GAIKA::Recruit (done, three equivalences labelled)RECRUITER.VS runs. It was the last name standing between that file and its loop, and the loop is the AI’s whole army-dispatch decision. Names with no body 25 → 24; unimplemented sites 31 → 29; blocked scripts 11 → 10; the trap sweep holds at 20 hits over 25 maps.

It computes almost nothing and asks three shipped scripts everything. 0x00437b80 runs EvalRecruit.vs once per squad (how good a candidate is this squad for this node, and whose destination does taking it cost?), CalcMaxTake.vs once per node some candidate would be taken from (own - MinNeed as the answer and own - MaxNeed through the out-parameter), and SendSquad.vs once per squad dispatched. All three resolve through the asking player’s profile directory, which is why DATA\AI\DEFENSIVE\EVALRECRUIT.VS exists: the defensive profile overrides the candidate test and nothing else. The unit of account throughout is Squad::Eval, which is also what nAvail — an in/out parameter, int, GAIKA g, int idPlayer, int nMin, int nMax, int *nAvail — is denominated in.

The one thing it decides for itself is a budget, and it decides it twice. A first sweep in squad-index order spends each donor node’s MaxTake and accumulates the excess as overflow; if anything overflowed, the candidates are sorted best-first and the same budgets are spent again in that order, dropping what no longer fits and clearing the dependency of anything that fits inside the donor’s Overneed — a take that leaves the donor above its maximum need is free, and a free take is not a dependency. Then two send passes: everything unencumbered, up to nMax; and only while the total is still short of nMin, the encumbered rest. A node takes what is free before it robs a peer, and it only robs one to reach its minimum.

A fourth filter is skipped, and skipping it is faithful rather than partial. Between the two the original walks the MAIKA routing deque at [0x8c8e54]+0x38 for a way-point between each squad’s GAIKAIn and this node, and abandons the recruit if dropping the squads that have none takes the total below nMin. The block is gated on that deque being non-empty, and the only thing that ever fills it is SetMAIKA — which f_set_maika has always accepted and dropped for want of a routing table. So the gate is false here for the same reason it is false on a retail map that never calls SetMAIKA. It is the first thing to restore alongside that table, and it is now the second entry point waiting on it.

Four narrowings. The player window is closed at the bottom (the original tests only player - 1 ≥ 16, and a player of 0 reads the record before the array — ExploreCircle’s off-by-one, a fault rather than a behaviour). The sort gets a tie-break on the squad index, because std::sort on the score alone leaves equal scores in an order the standard does not fix and iteration order is state here. A squad that disappears while one of the three scripts runs is skipped rather than read through a stale pointer. And gDepend is bounds-checked against this table’s node count rather than the original’s.

Thirteen faults, ten caught, three labelled. The survivors are all equivalences and all of them look load-bearing: the early total - overflow test can never be stricter than the re-run’s own, because the overflow telescopes to exactly max(0, spent - take) per node and the greedy fit is bounded by the same sum; the sort’s tie-break cannot be observed from one platform, being a guard against two builds disagreeing rather than against a wrong answer; and the bounds check on gDepend guards an out-of-range write no test can see without a sanitiser.

It sends nothing on every shipped map today, and for a reason that is not this entry point’s. Nothing in this engine writes Squad::Eval — the standing gap sim/squad.hpp records and MilEval already answers zero from — so the original’s own short circuit ahead of EvalRecruit.vs refuses every squad. It is bound anyway so that the recruiter runs its loop and sleeps rather than trapping on the first node it looks at.
Whether there is a way in: CanExplore (done, two equivalences labelled)Half of the cheapest remaining pair, and the half the node adjacency had just unblocked. Names with no body 26 → 25; unimplemented sites 32 → 31; blocked scripts hold at 11, because RECRUITER.VS needs Recruit/4 as well and that is the next block. The trap sweep holds at 20 hits over 25 maps.

The recruiter asks it of a node it has not explored, just before deciding whether to send an army there at all: can this player get to it to look? 0x0042afc0 answers yes on either of two grounds, and both are an Explored test — 0x0041cb80, the very body GAIKA::Explored calls — on somebody else’s node. Either a neighbour of this node is explored, which is the adjacency list the last block built, or a teleport standing in this node comes out somewhere explored: the walk is the world’s settlement vector, keeping what casts to the class token Obj::AsTeleport uses, and it asks GetGAIKA(point) of the teleport and again of its far end.

The old note called it “the node’s slot list” and it is the neighbour list. That was the third time a name was recorded as waiting on the node cut and turned out to be waiting on something the cut had already provided or would provide in one line.

Two narrowings, both the partition showing through. A teleport is a settlement here, so it is a node centre and the node it stands in is its own — where the original’s slot grid makes a node a region and a teleport shares whatever region it sits in. And it answers false on every shipped map today, for a reason that is not this entry point’s: nothing in this engine sets Explored, because there is no per-node visibility sweep. That is the standing uniform answer m_laika_flag already records, and this is one more reader of it — bound anyway so the recruiter runs and chooses rather than stopping.

Eight faults, eight caught, two equivalences labelled. The node resolution and the pair test are both the original’s and neither can change the answer here: an id the table does not have has an empty neighbour list and is a node no teleport can stand in, and an unpaired teleport’s far end resolves to nothing two lines later. Both are kept for the reading rather than for the answer, and both say so.
A unit becomes another: Mutate, and the object that keeps its identity (done, eight fields recorded rather than invented)The last name that finished a script on its own. Names with no body 27 → 26; unimplemented sites 34 → 32; blocked scripts 12 → 11 — TEUTON_CONVERT.VS is finished, and nothing in the installation is now blocked by a single name: every one of the eleven that remain needs two or more, and five of them call a name gbr.exe never registers and could not have run on the original either.

It is a replacement, and the handle swap is the point. 0x005df420 resolves the name in the class graph and hands it to 0x005deef0, which mints a fresh object of the new class, copies a list of fields into it, destroys the old one and then swaps the handles — so every script reference, every group membership and every name still finds the survivor. Ids here are stable and never reused, so the swap is the one part that does not translate; World::mutate_class re-classes the slot in place and arrives at the same place by the road this object model already has.

What survives is a list, and each entry is a field the copy names. Position, owner, health ([obj+0xc0]), stamina ([obj+0xc4]), holder ([obj+0x154]), and two bits of the +0x2c word — bit 19 in_party and bit 26 messenger. Everything keyed by object id survives because the id does: the unit’s food ([obj+0x16c], which turned out to be SetFood’s field, the sibling one entry along from SetUser), its experience ([obj+0x180], which inherentlevel is derived from), its carried items, its name binding, its groups and its settlement. What does not survive is everything class-derived — sight, art, and every combat figure including the maximum health, so a unit whose health was above the new class’s ceiling keeps the higher number — and everything that belonged to the destroyed object: the rest of ObjectState (user, [obj+0x164], is conspicuously not in the copy), the commands, the running script, and the squad, which the copy zeroes outright.

The deferral is real there and unnecessary here. When the object is the one whose script is running, the original stores the class in a global and returns a status that ends the call; the mutation then happens at the per-object hook on vtbl+0x24, the same hook a deferred Erase uses. It has to defer, because it destroys the object the running script holds a pointer to. A script here holds an ObjectId and looks it up on every use, so the mutation is done where it is asked for — and the shipped call cannot tell: Mutate is the last statement on both branches of an if/else.

Eight copied fields are recorded rather than invented. [obj+0x84], [obj+0x178], [obj+0x18c], [obj+0x1a8], [obj+0x1ac], the string at [obj+0xa0], the point pair at [obj+0x48]/[obj+0x4c], and bit 16 of the +0x2c word are copied there and are not modelled here; they are written down in sim/world.hpp so that the next reader knows what is left rather than finding a silent gap. CombatSystem::reclass exists for the one that is modelled and would have been lost: a combatant rebuilt from scratch starts at level 1, so it is re-classed rather than replaced, and what resets is what the destroyed object was in the middle of — its target, its swing, and every bonus an effect had put on it.

Twenty-one faults, twenty-one caught, one equivalence labelled. Three survived the first sweep and all three were the tests being too small: a refusal was only checked against the class index and not against the squad and the commands it also leaves alone, and no class carried an altid, so resolving the name by id alone could not be told from the original’s id-then-altid. The equivalence is the host’s own guard on an unknown class, which mutate_class would refuse anyway; it is kept because that is where the original’s message goes.
Which nodes are next door, and the AI that counts them (done, four equivalences labelled)One name, and the per-node adjacency sim/gaika.hpp had listed as not modelled. Names with no body 28 → 27; unimplemented sites 35 → 34; blocked scripts 13 → 12 — DEFENSIVE\MAIN.VS is finished; the trap sweep holds at 20 hits over 25 maps.

The adjacency is measured on a grid this engine already keeps. 0x0044a3c0 allocates an n-by-n matrix, clears every node’s count and list, then walks the slot grid: for each labelled slot and each of its eight neighbours — the offset table at 0x007b1678, read out in prose here as the full ring — the two slots’ nodes are made adjacent when they differ and neither is zero, once, both ways; the matrix is then read off row by row, which is ascending by id. sim/lsa.hpp’s 128-unit slot grid is that grid — its own header already said so — so what was missing was never the grid but the slot-to-node stamp, and GaikaTable::at is the answer to exactly that question. Unlike the area adjacency one level down there is no type test: a land node and a sea node that touch are neighbours.

Two properties fall out and both are the original’s. A channel narrower than a slot is invisible — one terrain cell of water is 64 units and a slot is 128, so no slot centre lands in it and the two shores come out as neighbours. And corner to corner counts, because the offsets are the ring rather than the cross.

ControlledNeighbors is then a count. 0x00422950 walks the list and takes the neighbours whose settlement resolves and whose owner is the argument less one — the player number is 1-based, decremented once and compared raw, so 0 and 17 match nobody and answer 0 without a trap. A node is never its own neighbour, so the asking node’s own settlement is never in the count, and a region node has no settlement to have an owner. GAIKA::EvalNeighbors’s note was corrected in the same breath: both things it was waiting on are now here, and what is still unread is its own filter over the asking player’s node view and three settlement predicates.

Thirteen faults, thirteen caught, four equivalences labelled. Seven survived the first sweep. Three became tests: a four-neighboured walk needed two landmasses meeting at one corner, an out-of-range player number needed a settlement owned by nobody to be told from a comparison against “no player”, and confusing the slot grid’s rows with its columns needed a map that is not square — sixteen cells by eight, because on a square grid the swap is a transpose and adjacency survives it. The four that stayed are equivalences and are written down where they live: marking the pair matrix one-directionally cannot change the answer when every pair is seen from both sides, the settlement guard is redundant while for_object refuses a null handle, and sampling the slot at its corner rather than its centre changed not one pair on any picture tried.
Whom to shield: BestProtPos, and the census that is not a strength (done, four approximations labelled)One name, and it is the second half of the search the last block built. Names with no body 29 → 28; unimplemented sites 36 → 35; blocked scripts 14 → 13 — ENCHANTRESS_IDLE.VS (188 sites) is finished; the trap sweep holds at 20 hits over 25 maps.

The candidate walk is BestMDPos’s, instruction for instruction, and it is now one function that both entry points call: the enemy squads filed under the caster’s own squad’s node, offered by their first member’s position, banded by distance. What changes is the question asked of each crowd, and it is a different question than the family’s other sweeps ask.

0x00423280 counts bodies, not strength. Every object in a circle of Range around the candidate is bucketed by 0x0044e250’s relation category — the caster’s own, an ally, an enemy — and a fourth counter tallies how many of the friendly side are not already under a cover of mercy, which is ObjectFlags::half_damage, the bit whose one reader halves an incoming blow. Anything that is not a unit is offered to a cast against the class token Obj::AsSacrifice uses, and the caster’s own Sacrifice is kept if one is standing there.

The shield tally is the original’s own slip, and it is reproduced. The ally arm adds to that counter and the own arm assigns to it, so what survives a sweep over an army of one’s own men is the shielding of the last man looked at rather than a count of the crowd. It matters through the first of three tests: at most two thirds of the friendly side may be unshielded (3 · u ≤ 2 · A), which for an all-own crowd degrades into “the last man is unshielded, so there had better be two of them”. Then neither side may be less than a tenth of the whole — (E + A) · 10 / 100, which is how a rout is told from a battle — and the score is E · A, the size of the engagement, biggest when the sides are matched. Ranking is BestMDPos’s: the score plus MaxRange − distance, first offered wins a tie, and the winner still has to clear the literal 3.

Two answers, and the crowd is preferred. A candidate whose circle held the caster’s sacrifice is recorded against that object; every other candidate against its own crowd. The two are ranked in separate slots and the crowd is asked first, so the sacrifice is a fallback and not a prize — and it must clear the same 3.

Four approximations, labelled in sim/ai.cpp. Everything BestMDPos already lists (this engine’s node, the two squad fields refreshed once a turn, 0x00420c00 left out because it overwrites its own answers); and, new here, which friendly unit is last: the original sweeps cell by cell over the object grid and this walk runs in object order, so the slip above lands on a different man. The sacrifice arm is written out in full and no shipped map can reach it — the ritual machinery that spawns one is not in this engine, which sim/world_host.cpp settled long ago — so it is a synthetic world that proves it.

Thirty faults, thirty caught, and one equivalence labelled. Nine survived the first sweep and every one of them was a hole in the tests rather than in the reading: no allied unit anywhere, so the arm that adds could not be told from the arm that assigns; no unit standing exactly on the radius; no garrison; no tie; no squad with two members. The equivalence is the order of the two guards on the sacrifice slot, which cannot be observed because a non-sacrifice fails the second test whenever it would have failed the first.
What a squad is worth and where it stands, and the spell that reads both (done, three approximations labelled)One name, and the two stored fields that nothing in this engine had ever written. Names with no body 30 → 29; unimplemented sites 38 → 36; blocked scripts 16 → 14 — GDRUID_IDLE.VS (175 sites) and GHOST_IDLE.VS (16) are finished; the trap sweep holds at 20 hits over 25 maps.

BestMDPos was listed as waiting on the node cut, and the node cut had already been taken. That is the fourth entry point in a row whose stated blocker had been cleared before it — but this one was waiting on something real underneath it, and the something was two fields. Squad::Eval and Squad::GAIKAIn are both stored, both saved, both read by nine shipped callers between them, and both were permanently zero here, which is why BestTargetInGAIKA’s squad half, ApproachingSquads, MilEval and EnemyPlayersEval have all been answering conservatively since they landed. Neither is computed in the original; both are maintained, and finding the writers is what this block is.

Strength is the census valuation, kept in step. 0x0041e900 adds one unit’s object_power (0x004439d0, sixteen bits of it — the same number the power score sums) to [squad+0x1c] and 0x0041e890 takes it away, and they are called from exactly three places: Unit::SetHealth (0x005d3a80, which subtracts before the write and adds after), the stat recalc behind vtbl+0xc4 (0x005d9e90, same bracket), and Squad::RemoveMember, which subtracts the leaver and zeroes the field outright when the squad empties — and then destroys the squad, which is this engine’s prune_empty. Every term of the valuation can change only through one of those, so the stored number is the sum of the members’ current valuations at every instant; summing it once a turn lands on the same value. The same pair of calls also feeds two per-player AI totals split by IsPeaceful — IsHeirOf("Peaceful") or IsHeirOf("Animal"), 0x005402f0 — which nothing here reads yet and which is recorded rather than built.

A squad stands where its first member stands. 0x0041f530 runs on every unit move: it asks GetGAIKA(point) for the node under the new position and, when the mover is 0x00443df0’s answer — the member deque’s front, which is the leader whenever there is one and the next member along when a leader has left — 0x0041eac0 unfiles the squad from the old node’s list, files it under the new one and writes the id. The same wrapper stamps SrcGAIKA from it the first time it is anything but zero, which is the whole of what “the node this squad was raised in” means.

And the spell. Unit::BestMDPos/5 (0x00439de0) answers where to drop a mass-damage spell, and both callers read a negative x as no. Like BestTargetInGAIKA it walks a node’s squad list rather than sweeping a circle, so the only candidate points in the world are the first members of the enemy squads filed under the caster’s own squad’s node — a unit in no squad, or in a squad filed nowhere, is refused before anything is measured. Each candidate is scored by building the very same accumulator Eval builds and handing it to the same sweep, so the number is Eval(caster.player, candidate, MDRange)’s enemy bucket; bProtectFriendly subtracts the own and ally buckets, which is the whole of what the flag means. A candidate survives a distance band inclusive at both ends and a floor of MinEval, and what ranks them is the score plus MaxRange - distance, so a nearer crowd beats an equal one further off and a tie keeps the first offered. The winner is answered only if that ranked value clears a literal 3.

Three approximations, labelled. The two fields are refreshed once a turn where the original writes them inside the move and inside the health write, so a script that damages a unit and reads sq.Eval in the same turn sees the turn’s opening value — nothing shipped does, the readers are all polling loops that sleep. The unit flag at bit 23 of [unit+0x194], which freezes a unit’s contribution in the original, is not modelled. And 0x00420c00 is not called at all: the original asks its node for five things between BestMDPos’s two loops and then overwrites four of the five slots before reading any of them, using the fifth as a scratch point buffer.

Thirty faults, thirty caught, one test rewritten. Reading the diplomacy row the wrong way round survived the first sweep, because the test that should have caught it made both players the same one: with the squad’s owner turned friendly, Eval moved its members out of the enemy bucket too and the score went to zero for the right answer as well as the wrong one. The case that separates them is the original’s own shape — a squad owned by one player whose member belongs to another — and it now proves that the relation read is the squad’s and that it is read off the caster’s row alone.
The siege planner: Siege on a list and on a squad (done, four approximations labelled)Two names, one body, and the two helper files that were the head of the block list for eleven blocks. Names with no body 32 → 30; unimplemented sites 44 → 38; blocked scripts 18 → 16 — SIEGE.VS (259 sites) and SIEGE GATE.VS (111) are finished, and GS_SIEGE.VS and GS_CAPTURE.VS each lose one of their four blockers; the trap sweep holds at 20 hits over 25 maps.

ObjList::Siege/3 (0x00438f20) and Squad::Siege/4 (0x00438e60) are marshalling thunks over 0x004383c0, the routine behind CSiegePlan::Execute - Command %s not found; the squad form first writes its fifth argument into the squad’s state with the time and refuses SF_NOAI. The planner, in its order: catapults are wanted only for a target whose settlement is not the sixteenth player’s (0xf, not the independents’ 0xe that AttackSetForTraining tests) — always for a gate or a non-central building, for the central building only while its holder has someone in. The list splits on Unit::CanAttack (vtbl+0xb4, 0x005b1da0, read in full): a unit that cannot hurt the target, is not a hero, is an heir of Military and lacks the freedom special is a crew; everyone else attacks, and the attackers that can hurt it have their class damage summed. The predicate’s last switch is damage_type: slash and pierce reach a unit always and a building only through a projectile_class (the loader’s **Invalid** default is what 0x0053f5b0 tests); siege reaches anything; none, nothing — which is why swordsmen crew the engines and archers do not. Five times the sum against the target’s health calls the engines off; the central building runs a five-round skirmish against the garrison’s health instead. The crews vote a race by majority, Britain and Germany are capped at one engine, and the race picks the class through PlaceCatapult’s eight-way table, now shared.

Then the roll: every catapult of the caller’s player already aimed at the target — [cat+0x228], which is GetCurrentTarget’s own slot — takes the nearest crews to its holder’s cap; new engines go on the nearest free siege site to the first unit’s position while crews and the cap last. A site (0x00448470) is one of eight points at range - 128 around an Outpost’s central building, or one of nine along a gate’s wall axis pushed outward by the same radius — the axis is the entity’s two type-7 markers shifted to pass through the mean of its type-10 markers (0x00529380, in 10-bit fixed point that truncates toward zero, the sweep’s first catch), outward being away from the central building. The crews then leave their hero, queue build_catapult and are regrouped into fresh squads carrying the siege state (0x00447330, already read for Train); crews with no engine rejoin the attackers, at the end. With fewer than ten crewing and either no engines wanted or a melee majority, everyone attacks — ai_attack_gate for a gate — and otherwise everyone moves to 200 then 400 units beyond the rally point along the ray from the target, clamped to the map, then idles; both loops stop at the first hero ordered. PlaceCatapult gained the settlement every placed engine anchors in the original, so a crewman’s GetHolderSett now reaches its engine.

Four approximations, labelled in sim/siege.hpp. The footprint test is passable_3x3 plus no building within the class radius, because the simulation does not load the .pass masks the original lays over the grid; the circle’s diagonals are round(r · 0.70711) in integers; ties in the crew ordering keep list order where the original’s introsort may not; and CanAttack treats the visibility mask, two unit flag bits, one class flag and [settlement+0x40] as passing. Named rather than hidden: this engine’s combat loop strikes for any combatant that can attack and reads no built flag, so an engine at one health shoots before its crew assembles it — already true of everything PlaceCatapult placed.

Two of the twenty-two were caught by the test that pins site 0 of the gate to (5528, 4493), the number the fixed-point shift decides.
A fight to learn from: AttackSetForTraining (done)One name, and it finishes the 120-site TS_ATTACKATWILL.VS. Names with no body 33 → 32; unimplemented sites 45 → 44; blocked scripts 19 → 18; the trap sweep holds at 20 hits over 25 maps.

AttackSetForTraining/3 (0x004397d0, 406 instructions) is the native replacement for a block the script itself carries commented out: the AI’s warrior-at-leisure picking a fight it can learn from, tried before the slow animals and the training camps. It walks the settlement roll from the last to the first and keeps the best independents’ outpost in the area. Per settlement: owned by player fourteen; within radius of the centre, inclusive; its central building an heir of one of four classes, each with a rank and a defender — COutpost 10 and CMaceman; IOutpost 9 and ISlinger, with the exclusive engage; TTent 8 and both TTeutonArcher and TTeutonRider, for a unit whose effective level (vtbl+0x114, the pair sim/match.cpp adds for the same slot) is above 4; TOutpost 7 and TValkyrie, above 8. A rank below the best is out, an equal rank only if nearer the unit. Then two defenders of the outpost’s kind are needed, counted in the settlement’s holder and, short of two, within the building’s range around it (0x004233b0). The winner gets approach set on the unit at the building and engage appended — or engage_unit_type_exclusive aimed at the last defender met, for Iberia — and the call answers true.

One arrangement is this engine’s own: a held unit is located at its holder here, where the original’s cell sweep does not see it at all, so the sweep around the building skips the holder’s members rather than count a garrison twice; the sweep is ascending id where the original’s is cell order, which can change which defender the exclusive engage names and nothing else. Fourteen faults, fourteen caught after two tests grew: the area’s inclusive edge, and an equal rank at an equal distance, which the reverse walk resolves to the later settlement.

The pathfinder bootstrap, with nothing left to build (done)Nine names, one file, and the answer is derived from what this engine already did at load. The nine SPF* names are the driver interface of the original’s smart pathfinder, the area-connectivity structure its AI queries walk. Each is a few instructions into a builder at 0x008c529c: the three _Quant calls do one slice of a pass and answer whether more remains, the five stages run one stage whole, and SPFDone (0x0040f8c0) destroys the live structure at 0x008c528c, installs the built one and frees the builder. The builder starts the script itself — 0x004165d2 pushes data/pathfinderinit.vs and runs it — so the script exists to spread the build over frames, Sleep by Sleep. This engine computes its area partition and connectivity at map load, by the rule sim/lsa.hpp records from 0x00453130, and answers every question the original puts to the live structure — CheckLsaPath, IsWaterLsa, GAIKA::LSA — from that; nothing here reads the structure the script would have built. So each _Quant answers false, nothing remains, and the six stages finish at once: the script runs its shape in one pass of sleeps and ends, as it does in the original once the build is through. The counter census, and an AI stopped (done, five equivalences labelled)Two names, one whole file, and one of the seven trap messages replaced by the next line’s. GetCounterUnits/2 (0x0042eef0) fills the six weights ESH_COUNTERUNITS.VS draws a unit type from. It tries the AI’s ten army rows first — the enemy formations it has seen, at [ai+0x30], which this engine does not keep — and when none answers falls back to a census (0x0041c400 → 0x0041c320 → 0x0041c0e0), which is what is here: for every countered class of COUNTERUNITS.XML, count it standing for every active player the owner counts an enemy (its own row, bit 0 clear); at or above the row’s threshold, take the owner’s own weighted counters off the enemy’s weighted mass, shift the remainder by ten, and add the table’s coefficient times that to each of the owner’s race’s six unit types. The weight and threshold are not in the document. Its parser (0x0041c8e5–0x0041c9e0) looks each <unit class> up in the unit catalog, finds its train command, and takes costgold + costfood as the weight — a tenth of it for an Imperial Rome arena or temple recruit — and 2000 / weight, floored at 1, as the threshold; one name is patched on the way, TTeutonArcher read as TTeutonRider. The array is added to and never zeroed: the zeroing belongs to the army-rows path. CounterTable grew a view of its entries and countered classes for the two sums.

AIStop/1 (0x00422720) is AiSystem::stop through the calling script’s scheduler: the player’s AI destroyed and its slot nulled, every coroutine it owned killed. The original prints for a number outside 1..16 and then reads off the end of the player table with it; here the refusal is quiet. Its one site, Egypt’s seq2.vs, waited on AddBonus/5 and CmdDisable/1, and both have bodies now. The reading of the first that this paragraph carried was wrong in its second half and is in the corrections table.

Training, summoning, a starving army, and a bit read on the wrong word (done, one correction, one equivalence labelled)Seven names, three whole files, and a correction to the row two below. Names with no body 53 → 46; unimplemented sites 69 → 60; blocked scripts 24 → 21; the trap sweep holds at 20; world version 31 and section version 11.

The correction. The row two below read Unit::StartTraining’s vtbl+0x44 with 0x10000000 as bit 28 of the second flag word, the one the damage formula halves on. It is not: vtbl+0x44 and +0x48 are the SyncFlags setters — Unit::SetParty drives bit 19 of [obj+0x2c] through the same pair, the food tick drives bit 29 — so the training pair writes bit 28 of the sync word, a runtime bit absent from the map census, and a training unit takes full damage. ObjectFlags::training is that bit, packed into the sync word the dumps show; half_damage keeps its one writer, the cover, and its doc says what it used to claim. The corrections table carries it too.

Training. Unit::BestTrainingTarget/0 (0x005d5bf0, registered under the internal misspelling GetBestTrainigTarget) sweeps the unit’s own sight with the predicate at 0x005d3cb0: an armed enemy ends the sweep and is what comes back — how UNIT_TRAIN.VS’s if (.IsEnemy(u)) is ever true — and otherwise a unit carrying the training bit is scored 40 · threat + distance, plus 100 for a hero, 40 inside min_range, 80 for a unit with a path; within range a score under 50 is taken where it is met and otherwise gets 100 off; lowest wins, the first met keeping a tie. threat is the decaying counter at [unit+0x1b0] that sim/combat.hpp already declines to model for the target family, so the term is 0 here and is labelled. Unit::TrainAttack/1 (0x005d5d10, suspending) refuses a held position, a partner that does not resolve and a receiver at one point of health, and otherwise enters action state 2 and suspends until it clears: one swing of the attack animation, which AttackWait already measures, then true. The strike is the combat system’s, since GotoAttack set the target. UNIT_TRAIN.VS is finished.

Summoning. GetStaminaDecTime/0 (0x005ab580 over [obj+0xec], 0x005d8080 over [unit+0x17c], one body here) is the class’s stamina_dec_time, floored at 0, which SUMMONING_BEHAVIOR.VS sleeps between stamina points. Obj::MagicActionEnd/0 (0x005c9f40 → 0x005d5aa0) sweeps the whole map’s grid and writes 0xffff into the tag at [unit+0x14c] of every unit the receiver was burning — the release the sacrifice’s own teardown also reaches — and here clears every ObjectState::mist that names the receiver. SUMMONING_BEHAVIOR.VS is finished.

The starving army. HasStarvingArmy/1 and StarvingArmyPos/1 (0x00559c20, 0x00559d50) walk the session’s notification registry to the army starving entry and answer whether a record is at least the asked age old, and which; the records are posted by the food tick (0x005dbedf) for the local player’s units found at zero food. Obj::SneakCommand/1 (0x005b0930, the whole of TEUTON_MUTATE_GD.VS) clones the running command to the front, pushes the named one ahead of it and ends the runner — do this now, then pick up where you were — so [run, rest] becomes [verb, run', rest] with the runner’s argument kept. Obj::MistAction/1 (0x005ca050, MIST_IDLE.VS) burns every unit within the object’s range — [obj+0xd4], the slot a catapult keeps its maximum range in, range="200" on the shipped Mist — that no other live mist has tagged at [unit+0x14c], tags it, calls the unit’s vtbl+0xa8 (the virtual Obj::Damage reaches at 0x005ab66c) with the argument, and adds the argument to both owners’ counters, the argument rather than the health removed. ObjectState::mist is the tag; a dead mist’s handle stops resolving and frees its units, and nothing clears it otherwise. Obj::CoverOfMercyAction/0 (0x005ca6a0 → 0x005ca5a0, COVEROFMERCY_IDLE.VS) lowers half_damage on everyone it listed last animation, collects the units of every player its owner is not at war with — the complement of the war mask at [player+0x10], read one way round as Obj::IsEnemy reads it — within the class radius ([class+0x2dc], 1000 on the shipped cover), raising the bit on each as it goes (0x005ca3ba), and keeps the new list. The list is kept the other way round here, one ObjectState::sheltered_by per unit, and the one arrangement where that differs is two covers over one unit: the original’s second cover clears what the first raised until the first acts again, where the tag keeps the bit up under whichever wrote last — labelled, in the unit’s favour. A training unit that walks out from under a cover loses its own bit with the cover’s, one bit and two writers, exactly as the original.

The bit. Unit::StartTraining/0 and StopTraining/0 (0x005d5de0, 0x005d5e30) are the set and clear virtuals with 0x10000000 — bit 28 of the second flag word at [unit+0x194] — and a byte search of .text finds the bit exactly one reader: 0x005110c6 in the damage formula, which halves the blow landing on a unit carrying it, after every special-ability branch and before the cap. Its other writer is the cover of mercy, which raises it on everyone it shelters. So a training unit spars at half damage, which is what lets UNIT_TRAIN.VS run TrainAttack three times with both partners standing. ObjectFlags::half_damage is the bit, named for its reader, hashed and saved; CombatSystem::hit reads it off the world and resolve_damage applies it as the last modifier ahead of MinDamage, so a halved 3 is floored back to 3 — the placement is the one the original has relative to the only step the two chains share, and it is labelled inferred beyond that. The correction. The count core 0x00425a10 divides warehouse+0x18 less the 0x00425890 reserve by the cost the caller loads from the row’s +0x1e8, and CheckTechBudget adds that same field to GoldSpentOnTech — so +0x18 is gold, +0x1e8 is the gold cost, and the reserve comes off gold, not food, which is what the notes at affordable_count and StopReserving now say. The population and food terms return early, the gold term last; a reserve above the store is a count of zero.

The reserve is a share of army spending since the reservation. Settlement::ReserveFor/2 (0x0042cd60) writes Reserve<owner>, ReserveFor<owner> (the name as the script spelt it) and ReservedAt<owner> (the current GoldSpentOnArmy), and answers false to the research already reserved for and true-without-writing to the percentage already stored. Training, and the regroup it ends with (done, and one reading labelled)Two names, two whole files. SquadList::Train/5 was the sole blocker of the 67-site GS_ENTERSETTLEMENT.VS, the strategy an AI runs over a node whose settlement it means to fill; Sacrifice::AddDruid/2 was the sole blocker of the 35-site DRUID_HELP_SACRIFICE.VS. Names with no body 112 → 110; unimplemented sites 112 → 110; blocked scripts 52 → 50; the trap sweep holds at 20 hits over 25 maps.

Train is a decision, not an accessor. 0x004366c0 sorts every unit of every listed squad — SF_NOAI squads skipped — into three bins: already training (a train, waitarmytrain or unittrain command anywhere in the queue), able to train, and the rest. Able is five tests in order: the player’s maxtrainlevel, read once per call and kept less one, is at least 1; health at least half; level below that lessened figure, so a level-4 unit under maxtrainlevel 5 does not train here where UNIT_TRAIN.VS would still let it; not a BaseMage; and the class binds train at all. Nobody training and enough able starts (1); above the stop threshold with somebody able continues (2) and the trainers are left alone; otherwise training stops (3) and the trainers go inside too. The rest always go inside. Trainees are detached from their hero first; the units sent inside that are neither held nor already entering get SetCommand("enter", objEnter), which on its own turns an undecided answer into 4. A held trainee first walks to centre + vec_of_angle(radius + rand(64, 128), rand(0, 359)), the central building’s class radius — [class+0x2dc], which the loader stores at 0x005a1a8c after pushing that attribute name — and vec_of_angle is GetDirByAngle’s short constant at any length: a Q30 table generated from 3.1415/180, checked against the int8 table on all 360 angles.

The regroup (0x00447330) is now a function of its own. Every unit leaves its squad for a fresh one carrying the old squad’s flags, SrcGAIKA, DestGAIKA and LastFightTime — or, with no squad, the flags its class implies and the node under its feet — with bit 0 rewritten from the unit’s own no-AI flag, the lock bit cleared, and the state written in a final pass with state_time at now. A unit still attached to a hero ends up back in the hero’s squad by the original’s own assignment pass and takes no state, so it never leaves here. One reading is labelled: a hero keeps its own squad and that squad takes the state, because the assignment’s leader branch was not followed to its end. Squadize/3, when it comes, builds on the same function.

AddDruid is a lease no script here can take. 0x00595af0 answers false for an invalid receiver and otherwise attaches the druid to the sacrifice’s list until now + time, unhooking it from any other sacrifice first and refreshing the deadline on a repeat. No sacrifice exists in this engine — nothing spawns one, as GetSacrifice already records — so the valid branch is unreachable from any shipped script and stores nothing; what is bound is the false the helper’s own guard was written for.

The three that remain are equivalences and say so where they live.
The skill a hero develops next (done)One name, and it finishes HERO_SKILL_BEHAVIOUR.VS. Hero::AIGetSkillToDevelop/2 has a single call site and was the sole blocker of the 74-site idle behaviour every AI hero runs. Names with no body 113 → 112; unimplemented sites 113 → 112; blocked scripts 53 → 52; the trap sweep holds at 20 hits over 25 maps.

It is a fifth trampoline, and the draw is the only native part. 0x00426680 sets both out-parameters to −1, then for each of the 25 skills the hero’s class offers and has not taken to 10 it formats HeroSkill %s.vs from the executable’s own name table at 0x00824b00 — the same 25 strings in the same order as hero_skill_name, checked entry by entry — resolves it on the hero’s owner’s AI search path, and falls back to HEROSKILL DEFAULT.VS when the file does not exist. No per-skill file ships anywhere on a retail install, so every skill takes the default. The script is declared void, Hero hero, int skill, int *skill_points, int *weight: it is handed the current points and a weight of 100, and what it leaves in those two slots is read straight back. A failed run, a weight of zero or less, or a goal not above the current points drops the skill; the rest sum their weights. A total of zero returns the −1 pair and touches no random number; otherwise rand(1, total) on the same [low, high] generator entry GetExitPoint uses, the first skill in table order at which subtracting weights reaches zero or below, and its goal clamped to 10 — the same figure set_skill clamps against.

Twenty-eight faults, twenty-four caught, four labelled. The first sweep left six alive. Two were tests too coarse to see the fault: a draw taken over [1, total − 1] agrees with the two-weight case on every seed that does not land on the edge, and a receiver that is not an object at all had never been passed. Twenty-five equal weights turn the pick into the draw itself, less one, and disagree on half of all seeds; the integer receiver is one more line in the invalid-receiver case. The four that remain are labelled where they live — three exact equivalences and a guard on an out-of-range read no test can see without a sanitiser.
The area graph, and a path across it (done, and two threads named)Three more names, and the LSA partition grew what they read. Names with no body 120 → 113; unimplemented sites 120 → 113; blocked scripts 57 → 53; the trap sweep holds at 20 hits over 25 maps.

The slot grid is the original’s, and so is its quirk. Beside the cell labels the executable keeps a 128-unit slot grid, claimed by the first fill that touches a slot — and a fill that is then discarded keeps its claims, so what a too-small pond touched, no landmass around it ever owns. The type census votes over those claims at the slot centre, which is why a lake that is mostly coastline is typed ground; this partition used to vote by whichever area owned the centre and now does what 0x00452a60 does. Adjacency is read off the same grid: eight-neighbouring slots of areas of different type, so the graph alternates land and sea by construction, and two seas or two landmasses are never neighbours.

CheckLsaPath is a breadth-first walk over that graph (0x00441b70): 1 for the same id before anything is looked at, 0 across types, a land area entered freely from a sea, a sea entered only when the player has a ship in it whose running command is idle, boardunit or advance, and the answer is the number of areas on the path. An unreached destination bumps the player’s ship need on the first ship-less sea the walk saw — IncShipNeeds by another road. The advance clause’s enemy census at the owning AI’s destination node lives in the node slot lists this engine does not model, so an advancing ship counts and that census is the named condition that would withhold it. SupplyCount counts the same-owner settlements on a class whose own BestToSupply is the receiver; ApproachingSquads sums squad strength bound for a node from outside it, and reads the destination the way AIDest does, which gained its DestGAIKA fallback in the same breath.

Two threads named rather than started. ControlledNeighbors and BestMDPos both read the original’s per-node neighbour lists and per-slot squad lists, which sim/gaika.hpp refuses to cut; they wait on that decision, not on decoding. The siege planner stays at the head of the block list.

44 faults, four labelled equivalences, nine that became maps. A poisoned pond, a lake that is mostly coastline, a sea whose only contact with the land is corner to corner, and two lakes for the tie-break — each a sixteen-cell picture, each drawn because a fault survived without it. The half-cell terrain rounding turned out to seed islands in the water pass through their eastern shores, which is the original’s rounding and is now written down in the tests that met it.
Gates, ship needs, a building’s bruises, and the two evaluations (done, and one thread named)Twenty-one more names in five passes. Names with no body 141 → 120; unimplemented sites 141 → 120; blocked scripts 59 → 57; the trap sweep holds at 20 hits over 25 maps.

The gate family. A settlement’s gates are the CVXGates among its buildings, and BestGate ranks them by distance plus a fifth of their health, strictly less from 0x7fffffff — so a breached gate is preferred to a sound one at equal distance, which is the besieger’s question both call sites ask. OpenAllGates ends what a gate is doing and orders opengate on every gate not already open; IdleAllGates ends every gate that is not idling. Gate::Inside was left absent here with its condition named, and has since been written — see its own row. The condition as stated was close: it is a query per squad member toward the town centre, and what this engine answers instead is whether the member stands in the same LsaPartition area as it.

Two counters became state. The AI’s ship needs are one int per (player, area) that the original keeps on the area table — which this engine rebuilds from the map — so the rows live on the AI system, sorted, and ride in its save section (section version 10); Ships is answered as a census where the original keeps a maintained count. A building’s damage_taken is [building+0x1f8]: every point a blow or Obj::Damage removes, never overkill, hashed and saved (world version 27), and MostDamagedBuilding ranks by it among buildings whose entity has an enter/exit door — bit 12 of the presence mask at [class+0x63c], which is kEnterExitPointType. ClearDamageTaken dispatches on the handle’s type, because a building handle resolves to its settlement too, and a sweep found the version that cleared the whole town.

The two evaluations read one relation category. 0x0044e250 answers 1 for a player and itself, 2 for a pair not at war and 4 for a pair at war, and PlayerTable::is_enemy is that with the self rule folded in. Eval sweeps the standing units within range of a point — edge included, the garrisoned excluded — and buckets each one’s census valuation by its owner’s category; EnemyPlayersEval sums squad strength per enemy, less the no-AI, peaceful and sentry squads, and writes least and most only when positive. UnitsInHolderEval and SettlementCount came with them.

One thread is named rather than started. Squad::Siege and ObjList::Siege — 370 sites across the two siege scripts, the head of the block list — share a worker at 0x004383c0 of some eight hundred instructions over fifteen helpers: it groups the units by class into a map, sorts them by attack, places the catapults through the PlaceCatapult machinery, and issues move and idle orders with formation points. That is a planner, and it is the next subsystem-sized block; it is not a step.

63 faults across the five passes, four survivors, all labelled. Two are equivalences a sweep cannot see — a zero written over a zero, a set that only differs from an add when a scheduler refills the queue — one is a constant no score reaches, and one is a proxy that decides the same cases a line later. The rest became tests: a health that decides between a fifth and a quarter, a soldier at exactly the reach, an unpaired teleport, a garrisoned unit at the point.
The unboarding half, a teleport, and thirty small names (done)Thirty-three names in six passes. Names with no body 174 → 141; unimplemented sites 174 → 141; blocked scripts 78 → 59; and the trap sweep over the 25 runnable maps went from 1,554 hits to 22, which is the figure that says what the shipped content now reaches.

The ship subsystem is whole. UnboardAllUnits, UnboardUnits and FindPointToStay finish the family the boarding row began. 0x005c8010 lays a 25×25 lattice of passability cells over the square of half-side 192 around the ship, keeps the ones inside the map, off a blocked cell and off deep water — sampled with the half-cell offset terrain_at now exports so the lattice and the shipyard doors read the same cell — and 0x005c81d0 draws from it with the world generator and erases in place, so every draw and the survivors’ order are synchronised state. A swap-with-last would be one line shorter and would desync two peers on the second passenger. FindPointToStay is a hundred throws of two dice, y first, kept in the ship’s own LSA, off blocked cells and clear of standing living units; three clauses of 0x005d3f20 that cannot fire for a ship — a rate limit on a pair of fields the object constructor zeroes, a supply refill from a settlement a ship’s holder has not got, and a re-route through an entrance object a ship never links — are named and left out. One approximation is stated: this engine keeps no per-holder list, so passengers leave in id order where the original leaves them in boarding order.

FindTeleport was 493 of the sweep’s hits once Breakpoint — two instructions, xor eax, eax; ret — stopped being 1,532 of them. It is the cheapest pair under two thirds of the straight walk within one area and unbounded across areas, refused outright under 2000 units within one area, with the entry node in the source’s LSA and the exit in the destination’s. Its one gate for a computer player reads two session flags this engine has not identified and is documented rather than guessed. Implementing it uncovered the next thing: UNIT_ATTACH.VS then ran its whole instruction budget at the hero’s feet, because HeroSystem::start adopts what the map placed and nothing adopted what a script placed afterwards — 622,684 refusals in sixty turns of Britain, every one “no record”. attach now registers a late spawn on demand, for a unit or a hero and for nothing else, on the reasoning feed_from_now already applies to the feeder.

Three files’ worth of small names, each with a finding. FormDescription is a rollover string with two quirks kept — a pierce-only bonus prints the slash value, the range bonus wears the piercing icon with a percent sign — and its loader settled the formation fields: BonusArmor writes both defence fields in file order and BonusAttack is a name the executable never reads, so the shipped sixth formation’s attack bonus is inert. ObjAlly is the complement of ObjEnemy, not an alliance. EnterHolder resolves its holder three ways, as 0x005d7210 does. Disease is bit 1 of the second flag word, now carried, saved and hashed, which widened the flag mask and moved the world version to 26. The store bin is accepted and dropped with its one reader named. GetVec is GetVecByDir on a difference; StrMid caps and floors; Party lists the flagged objects in id order where the original keeps roster order; EvalGroup sums the census valuation, now exported from the match slice, over a named group’s units.

84 faults injected across the six passes. The survivors that stayed are the documented kind — a clamp that turns a fault’s crash into a reported failure, a guard blocked_cell already answers for — and every other survivor became a test: a bonus of exactly one, a soldier at exactly the reach, a lattice at the map corner, a pair whose exit is on the wrong island. Two survivors were the fixture rather than the code, again: a fresh relations table is a free-for-all, and a single island cannot exercise a rule about two.
A subsystem, two refusals overturned, and a census (done)Twenty-nine names in five passes, and for the first time the list shortened by subsystem rather than by tail. Names with no body 203 → 174; unimplemented sites 203 → 174; blocked scripts 92 → 78. Every one of the 174 remaining sites is now a distinct name: the ranking by sites and the ranking by names have converged, which is what a surface looks like when nothing is called twice by the same script.

The boarding subsystem is its own row below, and it is the largest coherent block that was left. What it turned on is worth repeating here: a unit’s ship_to_board field is not the ship’s expected-boarder list, because nothing anywhere clears it — which is precisely why the two ..._ONFINISH scripts can reach a ship back through it after the boarding they are undoing has been cancelled.

Two refusals this page recorded were overturned, and neither for the reason it gave. BestTargetInSquadSight/1 was left trapping because its rule is a 200-point nudge on a score scale this engine does not use. That scale argument still holds — the original’s score is class_base + 40 × threat + surface distance, linear world units, where this engine’s is distance² × factor / priority — so nothing is scaled; what did not hold was the conclusion, because the alternative to an unscaled preference was two scripts that never run. It is a rank ahead of the score now, exact wherever the 300-point swing decides the answer. And it turned out to be its sibling’s twin: Exclusive passes 1 where it passes 0, so one prefers a class where the other requires it.

GetTeamOverallScore/1 was refused twice and both refusals named their own condition. The last one was down to a single unknown — the object-grid pass in the power_score census ran through a functor whose per-object body was not identified at all. It is eleven instructions: skip a spawn template, require the unit bit, add the same per-unit valuation the settlement walk already used, then cast to a wagon and add its load to the census’s gold or food total by restype. ObjectState::cargo and cargo_resource are why that last clause was recoverable at all, and they were added a fortnight of commits earlier for Wagon::amount — which is the argument for writing down small fields when you find them.

The valuation has a clause that is a judgement rather than arithmetic. (attack + armor_slash) × (level + 13) × (maxhealth / 4 + health) / 1000 + 1, unless the object is a RamUnit, in which case it is worth one. A battering ram carries a huge health pool and no attack; under the formula it would be the most valuable thing on the map.

MilitaryPresence is g.Empty()’s question asked from one side — the same per-node squad container, three filters Empty does not apply, and the relation is PlayerTable::is_enemy read the other way up. Its viewer == owner short-circuit is deliberately not written twice, and the first fault sweep is what made the point: the copy survived injection because the shared rule was doing the work.

Seventy-two faults across the five passes, three surviving and all three labelled. The pattern that recurred is worth naming: three of the survivors were a guard duplicated between an entry point and the thing it calls, so a fault in the shared half was invisible behind the local one. Two came out; one stayed and says in the code why it is redundant today and what promise it is making anyway. Two more faults crashed the suite rather than failing it, both in the new tests’ own dereference of what a fault had emptied. And two tests were passing on arithmetic that could not discriminate — a valuation’s trailing + 1 vanishes inside a divide by 100 on any realistic unit, so the fixture now carries a class tuned so its formula body is exactly 99.
The second pass down the tail (done)Twenty-one more names in twelve passes, and the shape of the remainder changed underneath them: the cheap tail is gone and what is left clusters into three subsystems. Names with no body 159 → 138; unimplemented sites 281 → 203; blocked scripts 116 → 92.

Three things became turn state that were not.
