A per-player experience modifier (SetExperienceModifier is the Battle tactics research and writes the player record, not a global), a mule's cargo ([obj+0x1cc]/[obj+0x1d4], on the object and not in the shipment table — ES_OUTPOSTSELLGOLD.VS settles it by summing over a group of objects), and a scout's sight radius, which Obj::SetSight can widen and which had been written into the save but deliberately kept out of the hash. A fourth followed: a priest's Jupiter grudge. The conformance golden moved four times, and once against a stale imconform — which that test's own comment predicts down to the symptom.

Four entry points turned out to be about something other than what they compute. MinNeed and MaxNeed do not compute a need: they run GetArmyNeed.vs through the AI-helper runner and divide the answer by the node's Laika::optimism, floored at 1. Squad::SendTo is the writer ai_dest had been declared without since the squad table was built, and the corpus states its effect in a comment. GetRandomHeroClass excludes a hero you already have outright and one whose portrait you are already wearing by preference — [class+0x704] is the loaded icon bitmap, so two classes naming one file are one face. EnemyInRange sweeps a square, not a circle, and unblocks WALL_PATROL.VS's 158 sites.

Two counters have no reader anywhere, and the sweeps proved it rather than the prose asserting it. Settlement::DelSentry decrements [settlement+0x100], which is written in three places and read in none; SetMAIKA records a way-point with no GetMAIKA in the shipped inventory. Both are dropped, and SetMAIKA's seven refusals came out with it — with nothing stored a guard cannot change an answer, which two faults demonstrated by surviving.

The settlement keeps four sentry numbers and scripts can read two. The roster (GetNumSentries), its ceiling (GetMaxSentries), how many of the roster are inside the wall, and the counter nothing reads. GetSentry takes one out of the wall and PutSentry puts one back, and neither touches the roster: a sentry standing outside still belongs to the settlement.

A correction to this page's own record. sim/match.hpp said gold_used was “a counter nothing maintains”; Settlement::GoldSpent is its script-reachable writer and it is here. GetTeamOverallScore is still refused, and now for one reason rather than two: the power_score census.

~110 faults across the twelve passes, all caught. A dozen only after a fixture grew to build the one arrangement that separates two readings — the corner that tells a square from a circle, the y axis every earlier case decided on x, a class with no max_load where only the cargo kind can move, a duplicate row in a per-player table that sorts ahead of the old one and answers first. Four guards came out as provably redundant and three are kept and labelled. One test crashed rather than failing, which is how a Settlement* held across a second create announces itself; it holds ids now.

Constraints the engine must honour

  • Pixels are RGB555, not the RGB565 the executable's message implies. Confirmed by sprites and terrain independently.
  • Team colour is a palette swap over indices 0–63. An index texture plus a per-player lookup reproduces it exactly and avoids a ~2.8 GB expansion.
  • The projection is exact: horizontal scale 1, vertical squash 46/64. A terrain cell is 64 world units and occupies 64×46 px. Integer arithmetic; no constant exists in the data.
  • Mask anchor: X is the grid centre, Y is the mask's own bounding-box centre. Proved by per-object registration.
  • There is no fixed timestep. Turn lengths of 200, 400, 799 and 800 occur; game speed scales them (799 = floor(800 × 999/1000)). The pump turn is the only tick; tick = pump-turn count + 1.
  • Collision granularity is 16 world units, four times finer than terrain. Obstruction is an irregular stamp with interior holes, and Terrain.pass.grid is authored data that cannot be regenerated — reproducing it from object placement tops out at 70.5% recall.
  • Scripts are coroutines and a suspended script is world state. Hence a bytecode VM with an explicit, serialisable frame stack rather than native coroutines.
  • Object state is read-only from script — 0 member assignments in 6,216. All mutation goes through setters.
  • Handles are monotonic and never reused, so a stale reference is dead rather than someone else. Composites (settlement → holder → warehouse) occupy consecutive ids in all 328 cases.
  • A held object's position is (-1,-1) and its location is its holder's. Never cache a shadow position.
  • Eight factions, four seasons. Rome is Republican and Imperial.
  • Diplomacy is a 16×16 matrix of packed words, and it is not symmetric. Five named relations on four bits — ceasefire and allied share bit 0 — read out of the executable rather than inferred. Hostility is one-directional.
  • Scripts count players from 1. Obj::player returns ID + 1, and every entry point taking a player number decrements it.
  • Four families of script constant are data, not code. SS_*, GS_*, ES_* and TS_* are declared in DATA/AI/AI.INI, and the only names of those families in the executable are the four sentinels. AI profiles are overlays; two of the three that ship are empty, and a retail map uses one of the empty ones.
  • Translation must never reach hashed state. A translated string depends on the installed language pack, so comparing one would desynchronise a lockstep match on locale alone.

Verification strategy

The Python readers are ground truth. Validated corpus-wide, so every C++ port is diffed against them file by file rather than merely compiled. engine/tools/imcheck and imentity exist for this, and it has caught real bugs.

The desync dumps specify the state vector. Nine of them, and they are not replayable command streams. Each holds one divergence event plus a complete labelled world snapshot — 11,670 object blocks over 32 runtime types. That is the original specifying its own state per class. SyncFlags packing reproduces the printed word for all 7,580 objects, zero mismatches.

Recovering tick-by-tick replay needs command streams recorded from the original, plausibly under Wine with input logging. Worth doing before the simulation hardens. Until then: single-point conformance and self-consistency (run twice, require identical hashes).

Read before trusting anything

Corrections and traps

Claims that were wrong — several asserted by earlier revisions of this very page — and mistakes that produce plausible output rather than obvious failure. Every one was actually hit.

ClaimReality
ExecCmd: “the cost is not charged here: the shipped AI charges it itself (set.SpentGoldOnArmy(gold + food / 2) right before the call)”; Settlement::Research appends behind what the lab is running; verify and onfinish are “real and neither is implemented”; CanResearch on a name outside the research catalog is “No such upgrade”; a loaded settlement’s buildings roll is its anchorFive readings, one dump and one trace. SpentGoldOnArmy is an Env counter by its own body and no method script pays, so nothing this engine ran ever took a command’s cost: every computer player’s gold only rose. The ASUS tick-2 dump settles who does: 200 ms in, five commands stand queued on five buildings and each town’s gold is its 10,014 less exactly the queued rows’ costgold (8,914, 7,414, 8,414). The command core charges when the command is queued, and refunds when it is cancelled (inference). Research issues its command through 0x004efc00 with the replace flag set (0x0042d2b5), the pair RepairAll sends; appended behind a lab’s idle, which never returns, it never started, and ESH_NEEDTECH.VS queued the same upgrade once a pass forever. onfinish is how the research ledger is written at all — ONFINISH_RESEARCH.VS marks the upgrade researched — so every upgrade any player started stayed researching for the rest of the match; it runs now, (This, bCanceled). 0x00434870 asks the command table, not the research catalog, and then wants an idle building of the settlement that offers the row (0x0042d010), reading the ledger only for a row with NamePlr/NameSet; hireheroE and BuySlaves are neither, and every tactic script opens with them. And the original’s settlement keeps a deque of all its member buildings ([set+0x6c..0x78]), which BestBarrack walks — with the anchor alone on it, BestBarrack(5) answered an invalid handle for a stronghold with a barracks standing in it, and no computer player ever trained from a map’s own town.
Squad::Leader “keeps the leader as members[0] rather than beside the list, so this cannot disagree with the membership”The body read a second field the comment said did not exist. leader_impl answered Squad::leader, which create(owner, leader) fills for a hero’s squad and join never fills — so every squad that was not a hero’s had no Leader, and the comment above it asserted the opposite. Unobservable while every squad was a hero’s; the turn units were squadded at placement, SQUADMONITOR.VS logged Inconsistent squad?!? 32,780 times in four minutes on Balcans and skipped every squad it walked. 0x00421310 answers the front of the member deque (0x00443df0), 0xffff when there is none, and now so does the body. The test that pinned the wrong reading — “a leaderless AI squad answers an invalid object” — now pins the right one.
Every reachable call site has a body, so every reachable script runs; and the squad half of SetState/1 “changes nothing, including the time” when the state is already heldCoverage counts names, not receivers, and seven names lied by receiver. gbr.exe registers pos, player, health, maxhealth, food, InHolder and HasFreedom on Squad as well as on Obj; this registry keys on (kind, name, arity), so the object bodies answered every squad the way they answer an object that is not there — -1, (-1, -1), 0, 0, false — and food trapped. Some sixty sites across the AI scripts, none of them counted as unimplemented: sq.Player is thirty of them and AIOSENDSQUAD.VS hands it to PrepareAiTransportShip, so every AI crossing was asked for on behalf of player 0. The fix is squad_receiver in sim/squad.hpp and a squad branch in each of the seven, read from the seven bodies (0x004210c0 is the handle’s low nibble plus one with no lookup at all; 0x00422cf0 the front member’s position; the two sums; the two all-of-them predicates, vacuously true of nothing). The SetState claim was the object half’s rule asserted of the squad: 0x004211c0 writes the state and then the clock with no compare between them, so sq.SetState(SS_IDLE) on an idle squad restarts StateTime, which SQUADMONITOR.VS times its idling from. The test that asserted the old rule now asserts this one. The general lesson is the one the coverage tool cannot learn: a name with a body is not a name with a body for every receiver, and the census that found these was a grep for sq. and SL.Cur. against the Squad:: registrations.
The GAIKA census fills enemy_hidden from “a second pass (0x00420c00) over remembered rather than observed enemy force”, and skips squads belonging to player 14 or 15Two claims, one function misidentified and one flag guessed at. 0x00420c00 is not a fog-memory pass at all: it is the node’s settlement query, and the census calls it once after the squad walk to add that settlement’s sentries to whichever of the three totals its owner’s relation selects. The memory adjustment is 0x0044eb80, run per squad whose relation came back AI_ENEMY, which rewrites the presence from the asking player’s remembered node table. This engine has no such memory, so the zero is right for the reason the note now gives — but the settlement’s contribution is a real gap and is now written down as one. The second claim was a stand-in for a flag this file had not yet named: the skip is SF_PEACEFUL, bit 2 of [squad+0x30], which the plan’s own BestTargetInGAIKA row had already named a year earlier. The two readings agree on shipped maps and part company in both directions — a peaceful squad of a real player was counted, a hostile wildlife squad was not.
Unit::AddBonus “reads four of its five integers and resolves its receiver off the wrong half of the value (0x005e0d00–0x005e0d2c pops 24 bytes of a 28-byte frame)”Half right, and the wrong half was the alarming one. It does read only four of the five — the fifth sits at top-4 and nothing loads it, while top-8 is stored into the record’s fourth and fifth slot. But the frame pointer is moved by the full 28 (-8, -4, -4, -4, -8, which sums to 28 and not 24) and the receiver is read from the deepest eight bytes exactly as every other member entry point reads it. There is no misresolved receiver and nothing is left on the stack; the sibling Unit::RemoveBonus at 0x005e07f0 pops the identical frame as -4 five times and reads all five, which is what makes the fifth argument a slip in one body rather than a convention in both. The earlier note was written from the decrement list alone, without adding it up.
Unit::StartTraining raises the bit the damage formula halves on, so a training unit spars at half damageIt raises bit 28 of the sync word, and a training unit takes full damage. The pair calls vtbl+0x44 / +0x48 with 0x10000000, and those slots are the SyncFlags setters — Unit::SetParty drives bit 19 of [obj+0x2c] through the same pair — not writers of the second word at [obj+0x194], whose bit 28 the damage formula reads (0x005110c6) and whose one writer is the cover of mercy (or [unit+0x194], 0x10000000 at 0x005ca3ba). The tell was the reader on the other side: BestTrainingTarget’s predicate tests [cand+0x2c] & 0x10000000, a bit no map object carries. ObjectFlags::training is the bit now, packed into the sync word; half_damage keeps the cover alone. One commit carried the wrong reading.
There are sixteen selections, one per player, not one global oneThere is one, and the player id every shipped call carries is a guard rather than an index. sim/orders.hpp reasoned it out from the corpus — all nine ClearSelection sites pass a player, all ten Select sites pass one too — and the reading is sound and wrong. ClearSelection (0x005a8120) turns the argument into a pointer to that player's record, compares it against the pointer the globals block keeps at +0x12c8, and returns having done nothing when they differ; that pointer is the local player's, and Obj::Select (0x005abfc0) and the two-argument Obj::Deselect (0x005ac060) make the identical comparison. So a lockstep peer running the same script for somebody else's player does nothing at all — which is a stronger reason for keeping the selection out of the sync state than the one the header gave, and the reason it could not be in it. The sixteen slots survive as an embedder's convenience; the script entry points reach the one HostContext::local_player names.
SwapSelectedObj substitutes in place, keeping the position — “that is why Selection::swap exists and is not remove plus add”It is exactly remove plus add. 0x004c84e0 asks whether the first object is in the selection (0x005e3930), removes it through Deselect's helper (0x005e7e00) and then runs Select's helper (0x005e7d80) on the second, which appends. So the wolf a druid summons lands at the end of the selection and the druid's slot is not kept. The test that asserted the old reading is deleted rather than adjusted: it was the claim, not a detail of it.
IsSelectionAssigned is “the player has something selected now” and WasSelectionAssigned is “has ever had something selected”Neither is about the selection. Both are questions about the numbered control groups, which is what makes TUTORIALS\GENERALADVICE6.VS’s nest of the two mean something. Is (0x004c6aa0) bounds its player to 1..16 and walks ten groups at record + 0xac, stride 0x2c, answering true at the first non-empty one — which is the evidence ShortcutTable::kSlots did not have and said so. Was (0x004c6a00) does not look at the world at all: it reads the registry key /Player<n>/SelectionAssigned and answers whether any bit is set, and the two writers of that key in gbr.exe are in the input layer — not in SetShortcutSel. So a script assigning a group moves one answer and not the other, which is precisely the distinction the tutorial is drawing: has the player learned the habit? The key is also the one registry path in the binary with no /<map>/ prefix, so it outlives the map.
GetSelection/0 is a declared entry point waiting for a bodygbr.exe never registers it. The string pool holds _GetSelection and nothing else, and GetSelection is in the declared surface only because DATA\SUBAI\DEBUG_DUMP.VS calls it on its fifth line. That script cannot ever have run past there. It is left declared and unimplemented deliberately: binding it would invent an entry point rather than recover one, and would hide the fact about the script. One call site that will stay in the “declared, no body” column permanently.
No shipped script calls AIStartTwenty do, and the census that said otherwise covered 577 of the installation's 885 scripts. sim/ai.hpp had a whole section on it — "zero call sites across all 577 .vs files, which is why script/host_surface.cpp, an inventory derived from call sites, does not declare it" — and both halves were an artefact of measuring data.pak alone. The three-argument form has 20 sites, all inside map containers, and it was the sole blocker of ten scripts; AIStop/1 has one. The inventory has covered the containers since the campaign layer landed, so the names were already declared and waiting when this was found. It is the second time a claim in this tree turned out to be a statement about data.pak wearing the installation's clothes, and the fix in both cases was the same: the denominator is computed, never remembered.
Hero::LastAttacker answers who attacked this heroIt answers which of the hero's own units was last hit. `0x0052eeb0` returns the handle at [hero+0x2bc], and the only writer in the executable (0x005dc23f) stores word [victim+8] — the victim's handle — onto the victim's hero, reached through [victim+0x170], which is Unit::hero. The routine holding it is the virtual at vtbl + 0xa4, and 0x005d5faa calls it as target->slot(attacker) from the strike path, so this inside it is the target. The original's own author wrote the correction down, in HERO_SNEAK.VS line 13: attacker = .LastAttacker(); // this is in fact the unit from the army last being attacked. That line sits inside a /* */ block the file no longer runs, which is why neither it nor the TimePastLastAttack beside it appears in the call-site census — and why the entry point stays unbound while its sibling, whose three live sites are elsewhere, does not. The same offset pair matters twice over: Unit::TimeWithoutAttack is [unit+0x1a4] and means how long since I last struck; Hero::TimePastLastAttack is [hero+0x2b8] and means how long since anyone of mine was struck. They look like one field and are two.
Sacrifice::IsInvisibility asks whether this effect is the invisibility oneIt compares the class name against "Hide", and no class named Hide ships. 0x00594f10 reads the receiver's class descriptor at [obj+0x3c] — the same std::string Obj::class returns — and hands it to the CRT's case-insensitive compare against the literal at 0x007c7664. The invisibility effect the 28-entry factory at 0x00821180 really spawns is class Invisibility, and INVISIBILITY.SC.XML ships; a scan of data.pak and every container finds no HIDE.SC.XML at all. So the predicate is false for every object the shipped data can produce, and UNIT_STAY_VERIFY.VS, whose last statement is return s.IsInvisibility;, can never verify. A second, independent route reaches the same place: Unit::GetSacrifice's virtual is the always-invalid base in every CVXUnit vtable, so that script's s is invalid two lines earlier anyway. Reproduced as the rule rather than repaired — a class renamed late and left behind in one comparison is exactly what this project copies rather than fixes.
AsTower has nothing to test against, because Tower has no distinct cpp_classThe observation was right and the conclusion was backwards. Tower really is not a class in the graph — and the original does not look for one either. gbr.exe registers Obj::AsTower and Obj::AsBuilding at the same body, 0x005aa5e0, whose whole test is bit 23 of [obj+0x2c]; the two registrations differ only in the declared return-type word, 0x19 against 0x16, which the compiler uses to decide what the result may be sent and the call boundary never inspects. So AsTower was left out of sim/world_host.cpp for three shipped sites while the entry point it needed was already there under another name. OUTPOST_ATTACK_VERIFY.VS shows the same thing from the script side: it casts every building in the settlement and then asks IsHeirOf("FakeTower"). The class question belongs to the script; the cast is a flag test.
Rot tabulates the five cardinal angles because a general rotation could not be exactHalf of that is true and the sentence read as the whole of it. sim/world_host.cpp carries a 361-entry Q30 sine and cosine table beside the five cardinals, generated from the retail constant, and has since Rot was written; the cardinals exist because there the vanishing term is exactly a sign and the table would round, not because there is no table. This page then used its own sentence as a premise — “twelve more angles and an integer atan2 is a fixed-point piece of work” — and sized a thread around building trigonometry that was already there. It was the atan2 half that was real, and it needed a tangent table rather than a cosine one for a reason the sentence could not have anticipated.
Squad::StateTime is the game time the state was last set, and its three corpus sites compare it against GetTime()It is elapsed time, and none of the three does. 0x00421400 loads the clock, resolves the squad and returns now − [squad+0x2c] — the stored field is a stamp, which TVXSquad persists as StateSetTime and which that half of the claim got right, but the entry point subtracts. EVALRECRUIT.VS settles it twice in eight lines: it substitutes 10,000,000 for the value when the match is under three minutes old (a duration meaning ages ago, and absurd as a timestamp on a young match), tests it against 1000 * AIVar(AIV_SquadRecruitWait), and divides it by 1000 to get seconds. The GetTime the wrong reading was drawn from is on the line in between, testing the match clock about something else entirely — if (GetTime < 3 * 60 * 1000), whose body is the substitution. A citation that names the right file and the wrong line is harder to check than no citation, because it looks checked.
ObjectFlags::in_air's retail writer is the Flying constructor, and landing clears it at 0x0051b45dThe constructor is a writer and not the writer, and the second half is simply not there. 0x00420b90 is the routine that sets and clears bit 22 of [obj+0x194], and it has exactly one call site in the whole executable: 0x0051bc6d, inside Flying::PlayAnim, where the value it is passed is !(z_from == GetTerrainHeight(pos) && z == −1). 0x0051b45d touches neither the word nor the bit. What the constructor does at 0x0051af6a is real but is an initial condition: if the class declares the int at descriptor +0xb44 it writes it into z_from and ORs the bit in. That property is initial_z — the string at 0x7c8940, stored by the class reader at 0x005a20fd — and exactly one of the 845 shipped classes declares it: EAGLE.SC.XML, at 200. So an eagle is airborne from the moment it is placed and a crow is not, which is what their two idle scripts each open by assuming. Naming the constructor as the writer would have produced a flight state that could be entered and never left.
A flying PlayAnim clamps its point and faces it; the animation's origin is something this engine does not haveThe point is where the bird ends up, and the sentence had the mechanism and drew the wrong conclusion from it. 0x0053e6c0 stores the point as the animation's own end and 0x0053d730 — StopAnimation — asks the animated-position getter where the animation reached and calls SetPos with it, so the object arrives, one StopAnimation late. Obj::pos reads the committed [obj+0x24] and not the animated one, which is why a retail crow's script-visible position runs one animation behind. CROW_MOVE.VS is what makes this load-bearing rather than cosmetic: it is while (.DistTo(pt) > 100) around a PlayAnim whose destination is .pos plus 75 units, so a bird that does not move never leaves the loop — a script that hangs rather than a bird that looks wrong. The z arithmetic beside it is the third coordinate of the same interpolation, which is the shape the reading should have suggested and did not.
Terrain.pass.grid is the only one of the map's six GRID layers the simulation may haveThree of them now are, and the sentence was already wrong when it was last read. It sits in SessionInputs's doc comment directly above terrain, which had been added underneath it — and height makes three. The reason the three are separate fields is the part worth keeping: they declare different geometry (16 units and 1 bit, 64 and 8, 32 and 8), so none can be reinterpreted as another, which is why ObstructionGrid::from_grid refuses anything but the first rather than reindexing it. A comment that says this is the only one there will ever be is a claim about the future, and this file has now been the place three separate map payloads turned out to be missing from.
PickFlyingPoint follows the first flock-mate that is settledIt follows the first that is not. 0x0051c530 calls Obj::IsInState's body and takes the branch on eax == 0, so the mate it wants is the one still mid-action — which is the reading that makes the feature mean anything, because a settled bird's heading is stale and a flying bird's is where the flock is going. The claim came from reading the name rather than the jne, and the two readings differ in every crow on every map. The same sentence also had the query wrong: it said ObjsInRange(self, self.class.name, self.sight − 100) as though the script wrote it, where the body passes −1 as the radius and 0x004fee3c is what turns that into sight minus 100.
The map's extent reaches the simulationIt never has. MatchRules has carried map_name and map_size since the match domain was written, with a comment saying they come from map.xml and the caller fills them in — and no caller ever did. map_size was zero in every session this engine has ever run, headless or rendered.

A zero extent is not a rounding error. Place's rectangle is 0 … map_size − 1, and high is clamped up to 0 rather than left at −1, so the whole map collapses to the single point (0, 0): all 131 Place sites refused every real position on every real map, _PlaceEx clamped its 89 into the same degenerate rectangle, and GetMapRect, IntoRect, ClampToMap and MapSize answered about a map that was not there.

Nothing failed. A refusal hands back an invalid handle rather than trapping, so the cost surfaced two statements later as somebody else's receiver problem — on 2_Great_loses_Spain map 2 it was nine sequences dying on a SetFeeding whose unit had never been placed, and the tally attributed all nine to the feeder. The seventh instance of this project's signature failure and the widest: a subsystem written, tested, ordered and registered, that nothing hands its data to. And it was found from the other end than the last one — not by asking what a declared entry point would have called, but by asking why a trap two layers downstream was reachable at all.

The fix reads Maps/<n>/map.xml into MapPayloads and fills the two fields through MapGeometry::parse, which already existed and was used only by the renderer. size_x, not size_y: the two are equal in every shipped map, so the test that pins the choice is synthetic, which is the only kind of test that can pin it.
PlayAnim(0, …) is a slot no entity declares, and the miss is ordinaryIt is an off-by-one, and under it every crow and eagle animation in the game silently did nothing. The original keeps its animations in a dense array whose element i is the entity document's idx = i + 1, and Obj::StartAnim decrements before it indexes — so the script's number and the document's are the same everywhere except the two entry points that pass their argument raw: Flying::PlayAnim/3 and Unit::GetAnimDuration/1. Add one to those and PlayAnim(0, …) and PlayAnim(16, …) are slots 1 and 17, which every crow declares, and EAGLE_MOVE.VS's .GetAnimDuration(4) / .GetAnimDuration(0) is the attack animation over the walk instead of a division by a missing one.

The claim sat in world.hpp, in the doc comment for play_anim itself, as a *reason* — the miss was "ordinary and not an error", and the cursor was deliberately left alone so that "a failed script call cannot freeze a unit mid-walk". Every word of that is still true and none of it was the explanation. A plausible reason for a symptom is the most durable way to stop looking at it, and this one had been written down, justified and cited for as long as the animation cursor has existed.
An undeclared class property reaches a script as 0xff1b1e40 / "**Invalid**"Almost never, and which case is the exception is the finding. CVXClass's constructor (0x005a6820) does fill every numeric slot with 0xff1b1e40 — signed, exactly −15,000,000 — and every string with "**Invalid**". But ResolveInheritance (0x0059c310) runs two passes per class, both inside its if (parent != null) branch: inherit the parent's value where the sentinel still stands, then write 0 or "" where it still stands after that. The normalise pass covers +0xa4c … +0xa68 — the ten slotted outpost numbers — and not +0xa8c, which is settlement_food. So an undeclared slot is zero, an undeclared settlement_food is −15,000,000, and a root class keeps every sentinel because neither pass runs on it.

This page asserted the opposite and drew a conclusion from it: that OUTPOST_BEHAVIOR.VS:92's if (sDefenderCls2 != "") passes on the sentinel. It does not — the guard is false on the five outposts that declare one slot, and the script does exactly what its author meant. The wrong reading was self-consistent, which is why it survived: the sentinel is negative, so a spawn loop counting to it would also have done nothing, and the mistake would have shown up only as a class name nothing can place. Two readings that agree on the shipped corpus and disagree on what the corpus means.
Erase destroys the object with no death sequence at allOne routine is shared, and it is the one that runs the class's "ondie" script. 0x005141b0 is on both paths, and inside it 0x0059b060 looks ondie up in the class's script map and launches it. Military — the base of every soldier — binds it to DATA\SUBAI\MILITARY_ONDIE.VS, which is the Warrior Tales research payout and the BestHeroLevel bookkeeping. Everything else this page said stands: no kill credit, no loot, no settlement penalty, no dead marker, and the object is freed rather than left as a corpse. The correction matters because "erase runs nothing" is the kind of claim that makes a missing hook look like a decision.
Settlement capture is unwrittenIt was written, tested, hashed and serialised, and nothing ever called it. EconomySystem::capture has a doc comment quoting the shipped script that would drive it — and its only callers in the whole tree are unit tests. So no settlement in any real session had ever changed hands, and WaitSettlementCapture, implemented across 14 sites, was polling for an owner change nothing could produce. The sixth instance of this project's signature failure, and the first found by asking what a *declared* entry point would have called rather than by asking what a system does.

And capture is not the function the entry point calls. It enforces loyalty == 0 and can_be_captured and then writes loyalty; 0x005c4eb0 tests neither and touches loyalty not at all. Those are the script's preconditions — only 5 of the 109 SetPlayer sites carry a loyalty test, and UNIT_CAPTURE.VS writes the guard and the SetLoyalty(11) on the lines either side of the call. Routing through it would have made the other 104 silently do nothing. A guard in the wrong layer looks exactly like a guard.
Only 821 of 845 classes declare cpp_class; 24 inherit itAll 845 declare their own. ClassGraph::add refuses a <class> element without one and the Python reader raises without one, and both read the same 845 files — so nothing in DATA\CLASSES\*.SC.XML inherits the attribute. The claim sat one row below in this very table, attached to the correct CVXDecor 146 → 148 tally, and was believed for long enough to build a test fixture on. The fixture failed, which is how it was found: a class with no cpp_class is not in the graph at all, so it is indistinguishable from a misspelling — which is also what Place makes of it, and what the original's one diagnostic says.
Place's CVXScriptObj cast is a formalityIt is a live filter that nineteen shipped call sites take, and the obvious spelling of it refuses forty-three that must not be. NativeClass's parent table is the shipped data's cpp_class hierarchy, in which script_obj and unit are siblings under decor; gbr.exe's C++ inheritance is not that tree, so native_class_is_a(native, script_obj) would hand back the sentinel for every unit the corpus places. The corpus settles it, and the control is what makes it a measurement: of the 173 sites naming a class literally, 0 of the 19 that name a bare CVXDecor keep the returned handle, against 46 of 46 elsewhere that do. The 19 are ChimneySmoke, Mist, the blacksmith fires, the barrack horses — animated scenery, which is what CVXDecor is the base of, and what _PlaceEx's own MapObj created (%s) names. Labelled: no shipped site names a building or a gate class, so which side those fall is not established.
The sequence family is RunSequence / StopSequence / IsSequenceRunningTwo of those three do not exist. Neither StopSequence nor IsSequenceRunning occurs as a string anywhere in gbr.exe, and neither has a call site in the 885 shipped scripts. The manager registers five entry points in one block at 0x005bcb90: RunSequence, IsRunning, IsWaiting, IsFinished and the debug dump _SequencesStatus. The error was invisible for a specific reason worth keeping — the published evidence figure, 31 of the 46 non-autorun sequences named against 2 of the 262, reproduces exactly, because the two phantom names contributed nothing to it. The real family gives 32 against 5. A search set with dead entries in it is one nobody can audit from its own output. Three places carried the wrong set: this page, docs/formats/map.md and sim/campaign.hpp.
A global that resolves is a global a script can useNot while two files disagreed about what a receiver is. A bare <group type="1"> name resolves to a Query, and receivers_of in sim/command.cpp took Obj and ObjList only where membership_receivers eight files away took all four handle types — so AddToGroup accepted the receiver SetCommand refused. 554 shipped call sites were unreachable, 435 of them SetCommand/2. The trap tally showed about sixteen hits, because a sweep reaches a site only if every statement before it already works; the tally understated it by roughly 35×, which is the general lesson — a trap count measures what a run got to, not what is broken. gbr.exe settles the fix and it is not a coercion: SetCommand is registered three times with three bodies, on Obj, ObjList and Query, and the Query body applies the verb once per member. Two near-copies of one rule is how it survived a commit; there is one definition now.
<scriptobj flags> bits 26, 27 and 29 are unexplainedBit 27 is the spawn-template bit, and it is 39% of every shipped map. CVXGroup::Add (0x00572410) tests [obj+0x2c] & 0x08000000 and files the object in the group's template deque at +0x54 rather than its live one at +0x24, and some forty collection sites — the area grid sweep at 0x004fc6f4, the ObjList collector at 0x0041f4e1 — skip an object carrying it. 10,610 of 27,070 objects across the 29 maps, 77% of 5_Great_Battles_Britain, all of which this engine had in play from turn zero. docs/formats/map.md had the shape of the answer and stopped one step short: "nearly a subset of belongs-to-a-type-1-group — 10,598 of 10,610 — but 5,268 group members do not carry it, so it is not that either." The 5,268 are the live halves. Looking for a property of objects could not find a property of memberships. The control is the part that makes it a measurement rather than a correlation: of the 214 groups a script spawns by literal name, 212 are all-marked and none is none-marked, against 55.7 expected by chance — where bit 22 splits the group table 794/9 and bit 29 splits it 789/12, so neither has a side to be wrong about. Bimodality alone proves nothing here, because group members are homogeneous by construction.
A group's stored member order is unobservable, so it may be sortedBoth halves of that are wrong. SpawnGroup reads the stored order directly rather than through a query and mints ids by walking it, and the state hash folds the list in the order it is held. CVXGroup::Add is a std::find and a push_back with no comparison of ids in it; Remove erases rather than swapping with the back. The shipped line that separates the two readings is mediterranean map 4's Q_Interceptors1..4.AddToGroup("Q_Slingers"). And the save loader had the sorted rule too, so the first correct save of every real map came back load refused — while the whole C++ suite stayed green, because the save fixture added its two members as 1 then 2. Sorted either way.
Whether a named-object binding can be rewritten is not visible from outside gbr.exeIt can, and spawning is what rewrites it. CVXGroup::Spawn's inner loop (0x005728c1–0x0057291c) walks the named-object table, asks each entry through a virtual whether it names the template, and on a hit calls Obj::SetName (0x005b6120) on the copy. So the name follows the object into play: NO_Scipio resolves to nothing until SpawnGroup("Q_Scipio") has run. The TemplatesList/JustSpawnedList/TEMPLATE_UNIT_WITHOUT_GROUP cluster that map.md said "points at a spawn-from-template mechanism, but nothing shows it" is the bit-27 machinery above.
kTypeRect was appended after the last type codeIt was appended onto kTypeObjList. The eight script handle codes are handed out in four different headers, and world_host.hpp was the only one not carrying the running census, so "after kTypeSettlement=4" read as 5 when objlist.hpp (5), squad.hpp (6) and globals.hpp (7) had already taken the next three. A shared code makes is_rect and is_objlist agree on every value, so IntoRect would have read a pool id as a rect-table index with no diagnostic at all. Invisible because no test in this suite has ever handed a value of one handle type to an entry point expecting another — every domain builds its own registry and tests its own handles.
Obj::posRH is a different position from Obj::posIt is the same one. posRH (0x005ad8a0) calls vtbl + 0xC8, which is the thunk 0x005a75d0 in all fifteen classes that carry the shared SetPos at vtbl + 0x38; it forwards to vtbl + 0x3c, which is 0x0063d900 in all fifteen, and that function's whole body is out = {[obj+0x24], [obj+0x28]} — the two fields Obj::pos (0x005add20) reads directly. Nothing overrides either slot. The only difference is the miss: pos prints The function 'Obj::pos' called for an uninitialized or invalid object and posRH is silent, and both then push the same value. 141 call sites, closed by an equality.
The plan's "what leads now" list names the largest unimplemented entry pointsIt named four of the top nine and dropped five that outranked the ones it kept. Recomputing it put Obj::posRH/0 (141), Place/3 (131), SetNoAIFlag/2 (121), Unit::setplayer/1 (116) and Settlement::units/0 (115) above DiplCeaseFire/3, which the list did name. The failure mode is the one this project keeps rediscovering — a number assembled by hand and then quoted — and it had happened in the sentence that warns against quoting it. The thread table's list is computed now, by the join tools/host_coverage.py already performs.
The world save round-trips every flag it packsThree of the eight were never set by any fixture. in_party, unspawned and no_ai could all be dropped by the packer with the whole suite green, because no saved world in it ever carried one. in_party's case was already recorded in a comment above pack_object_flags and the fixture was never changed — so a defect written down is not a defect closed, and two of the twelve faults injected for SetNoAIFlag survived on exactly that ground. The fixture now sets every bit the packer can write and the comparison checks all eight.
Building::IsBroken is a flag, or low healthNeither: it is the worst of four damage tiers, and the tier is stored. 0x004db3d0 computes health * 100 / maxhealth and buckets it on three CONST.INI thresholds — BuildingStateThreshold0/1/2 = 75 / 50 / 25 — with a ±BuildingStateHysteresis = 3 band that a single-tier move must clear before it takes, while a multi-tier drop applies at once. So state 3 is not a function of current health and cannot be recomputed on demand, which is why implementing IsBroken means new hashed object state rather than a one-line accessor. It also explains the corpus's IsBroken() || IsVeryBroken() in five places: IsVeryBroken (0x00424f90) is the unrelated fixed fraction health < maxhealth / 10, and neither implies the other. An invalid receiver reads as broken; an invalid IsVeryBroken receiver dereferences null.
FormKeepMoving(ms) waits the shorter of ms and the ETAIt waits ms. 0x0052eaf0 has no distance in it: first entry raises bit 0x10000 on the hero, writes the argument to the scheduler's wait global and returns 1; re-entry clears the bit and returns 0. The clamp bought a final slice that lands exactly on arrival, which reads well against HERO_MOVE.VS's while (.HasPath()) .FormKeepMoving(1500); — whose exit is the path, not the wait — and cost a divergence on every call whose ETA is under its argument. Worth recording for the near miss as much as the bug: it was nearly justified by blaming a non-yielding loop in HERO_IDLE.VS on it, and that reasoning is wrong — a goto to the position an object already occupies leaves has_path false, eta answers −1, and the eta >= 0 guard was never true there. What spins in hero_idle.vs is closed, and it is Attack — see the row below. The change here was made anyway, on the disassembly, and the corpus sweep before and after it is byte-identical (154 distinct traps, 38,303 hits, both runs), which is the proof that it was not the cause. Unit::GotoAttack was the second candidate and is also eliminated: its slice is the script's own 1500, not zero, so it suspends.
Unit::Attack is an ordinary host callIt is a coroutine, and it is what spins in HERO_IDLE.VS. gbr.exe has two registrars: 0x00699bb0 registers an ordinary entry point and 0x00699eb0 registers a suspending one. Fifty names go through the second, and the list is exactly what you would predict — Sleep, all sixteen Wait*, Unit::Stop, Unit::Idle, all four Goto*, Hero::FormKeepMoving, Conversation::Run — plus three nobody had suspected: Unit::Attack (0x005ddb50), Building::Attack and Catapult::Attack. The body agrees: 0x005ddb50 opens by reading the re-entry flag at byte [cursor − 1] and branching on it, which is FormKeepMoving's own two-phase shape and which only a suspending entry point has. So the spin is the inner while (.Attack(u)) { if (!.IsValidTarget(u)) break; if (!.IsVisible) break; }: a hero with a live, visible, valid enemy ran it until the instruction budget was gone, every pass. Numantia at 200 turns: 51 budget-exhaustion traps to none. The registrar split is a tool, not just a fact — the whole suspending set can be read off it, and auditing this engine against it found no second offender. One number stays inferred: the original writes its suspension length to the wait cell at [0x00a77eac] and Attack has no ms argument to write, so the attacker's own attack_interval is used.
GUARD.VS is blocked on two names, and GUARD AREA.VS is named by 86 sitesOne name, and 84 sites. The two extra RunAIHelper occurrences are inside block comments — 1_Great_Battles_Zama map 6 seq15.vs:225 and 6_Great_Battles_Danube map 7 seq2.vs:184 — so a textual count and a parse disagree by exactly two, and the page had the textual one. And GUARD.VS's second blocker, UnitsAroundSettlement/2, had already been implemented when the figure was re-quoted; only Catapult::IsBuilt/0 was left, which is a nine-instruction flag read. The lesson is the page's own, one layer down: a figure recomputed from the right tool still goes stale if it is quoted rather than recomputed.
A settlement's name is the std::string at settlement + 0xcc+0xd0. Settlement::name (0x005c2700) branches on [settlement + 0xe4] against 16 to choose the long or short form, and that field is a MSVC std::string's _Myres — so the object begins at +0xd0, four bytes later than three places in this tree recorded. Nothing depended on the number, which is why it survived: the scan that GetSettlement and WaitSettlementCapture perform was implemented against the engine's own field, not against an offset.
UnitsInSettlement takes a settlement handleIt takes a name at 92 of its 93 sites, and only the handle form was implemented. Each of the three settlement-unit entry points is registered twice in gbr.exe — a (str, str) form and a (Settlement, str) one — and this registry keys on (kind, name, arity), so one body has to take both and the argument's runtime type is the discriminator. Every container site passes a literal settlement name; only TOWNHALL_BEHAVIOR_GUARD.VS passes a handle. So the container half of that entry point had been minting an invalid query for as long as it existed, silently, with full coverage credit — an entry point counted as implemented can still be unimplemented for the argument shape the corpus uses, and nothing in host_coverage.py can see that, because it joins on name and arity.
Pack files have gaps between themWrong. Files are contiguous; the slack is an undocumented MS-DOS timestamp table, the only part of a pack not recoverable from names and contents.
ProductionInterval = 202000. The figure came from a CONST.INI dump truncated mid-number and propagated for weeks. A hundredfold error in the economy's tick rate.
FullArmor = 8 shapes damageDead constant. Declared in CONST.INI, appears zero times in the executable, while the five surviving damage constants appear exactly once each.
Units have moraleNo such thing. No string in the executable, no constant, no class property, no dump field. Units fight to the death.
The desync logs allow replayThey do not. One divergence event plus a world snapshot; no command stream.
The dumps come from shipped mapsRandom-map games. 33–50 FakeTower objects per dump, zero in any of ten shipped maps. Do not try to match one.
Fixed timestepTurn length varies and is world state.
CVXDecor 146, CVXBuilding 125148, 134. Only 821 of 845 classes declare cpp_class; 24 inherit it. Resolve before counting.
546 passability masks597. 51 are named PASS with no extension. Match on the DIRG magic, never on filename.
Entity XML describes the sprite gridNot authoritative. 430 of 4,034 declarations disagree with the frame table, 1,066 on drawmode. The frame table wins.
Seven factions; two seasonsEight and four.
The relations word's bits, as inferred hereWrong in two of five. Disassembly settles it: ceasefire and allied are the same bit (0), share_support is bit 2. Five named relations sit on four bits. The competing “three two-bit fields” reading is refuted — it needs bits 1 and 3, which no code path touches.
Hostility is mutualOne-directional. Obj::IsEnemy tests bit 0 of the receiver's owner row and never the transpose. The shipped data contains one-sided truces a symmetric model misplays.
Script player numbers are 0-based1..16. Obj::player returns ID + 1; IsOwn, Outposts, Strongholds and MilUnits all decrement their argument; five error strings say so in words. 531 call sites.
The two failing LZIS tests are codec bugsFixture bugs, both. LZIS has no flag for a stored chunk — the reader compares sizes — so a fixture that compresses to more bytes than it produces is a stored chunk by definition. The decoder was right for two sessions while the test blamed it.
GetConst has 73 keys, all in [GamePlay]76, and one resolves nowhere. RecallDistance is in no section of CONST.INI and is read by a live script — which is why an unknown key must return a default rather than trap.
Partition invariance holdsNot with scripts running. Scheduler::advance does not interpolate, so a script's wake count over a fixed total depends on the partition — and shipped scripts draw from the world RNG when they wake (CROW_IDLE.VS sleeps rand(2000)). Lockstep does not need it: peers agree a turn length. The check now runs over the systems alone.
A constant's value is its line in AI.INIAlphabetical rank across every profile. The engine merges all data/ai/*/AI.INI into one std::map keyed by name before assigning indices. The reader keeps declaration order; that is now a documented divergence, invisible until something compares against a retail dump.
settlement.id names an object numNo. 747 of 748 shipped settlements matched — because num is dense, so any small integer matches. A random-object control scored 266/748 against the hypothesis's 271, and the objects at those ids are crops and wildlife. The real structure is nesting.
The shipped host surface is the 577 scripts in data.pak885, and the other 308 are the campaign. The containers use 66 free and 13 member entry points that appear nowhere in the packs — RunAIHelper (285 sites), IsAIHelperRunning (236), SpawnGroup (181), WaitSettlementCapture, GiveNote, PlayMovie — plus five names used at an arity the packs never show, including EndGame/3 at all 61 container sites. Every one read as an unknown call rather than a named trap, which is the one failure mode declare_shipped_surface exists to prevent. 65/65 of the names are strings in gbr.exe; the control — 60 map-declared object names — scores 0/60.
EndGame takes two arguments; the signature string says soIt is registered twice. The signature registrar (0x00699d20) gives "void, int player, bool lose" at body 0x004c9610; the numeric registrar (0x00699bb0) registers the same name again with type codes [void, 3, int, bool, str] at a different body, 0x004c9660. The second keeps the message, and only when the player it names is the local one — it divides (current_player - players_base) by the 800-byte stride and compares. Both are functions, and names, signatures and type codes are their call arguments, so both are readable mechanically: 410 signature registrations and 1,058 numeric ones, 1,272 distinct keys, and exactly two names registered twice — this one and Squad::SetState.
The Env* root scope needs a path normalisation ruleThere is no rule; a leading slash means absolute. Both accessors funnel through MakePath (0x006ab0e0), which is four instructions: if the first byte is / the prefix is empty, otherwise it is the running script thread's Context string ([thread+0x44], serialised under that name, null unless the object-attached launcher set it to the object's id). So "/Bonus" and "Bonus" are different slots under every possible context, and normalising them together would have merged two stores. All 403 root-scope call sites are in container sequences; zero are in data.pak. The negative control: of 588 scoped key literals, not one carries a leading slash.
A serialised coroutine carries the chunk index it is running, so that a load can rebind itIt carries the chunk's name for that, and the index is written beside it for nothing. The claim was in Execution::chunk_index's own doc comment. An index into one session's library means nothing in another — which is exactly why Scheduler::serialize writes the source name — and deserialize overwrites the field from that name before anything can read it, so nothing ever read what was written. What it cost was that a save's bytes depended on the order its session happened to compile in: four of the shipped conquest's seven maps failed the byte-identical re-save check on this one field, off by one, and it was invisible until the fix above got them past the load that had been refusing them first. One defect hiding another, which is why the sweep that found it was widened rather than repeated.
The command pump looks a script up and never compiles oneIt compiles, and it has since the day it was given a library. The sentence was in prime_library's own doc comment, and CommandSystem::launch three files away says the opposite out loud: the file has to be compiled before it can be spawned, and nothing else compiles it. Both were written on this project and neither reader saw the other. The cost was that a class <method> reached by a command was never in a loaded session's library — deer_move, eagle_move, lion_lead, wolf_lead — so Balcans and the conquest refused their own saves with FormatError::not_found, and test_corpus_imsave.py had been saying so the whole time.
Something in the engine reads sequences.xmlNothing did. The string did not occur anywhere in the C++ tree. A mission's sequences are its whole script: 308 files, all 61 EndGame/3 sites, all 403 root-scope Env* sites, ConquestBonus and SetTerritoryState — unreachable source, in a build whose tests were green. CampaignSystem::configure likewise had no caller. Three separate breaks, none visible to any unit test, because no test loaded a container the way the game does.
The 1-based player fix covered the whole engineOnly the return direction. Obj::player was corrected across 531 call sites and the comment explaining why sits in world_host.cpp — 250 lines below a local player_arg that was still reading the argument raw. So ClassPlayerObjs, ClassPlayerAreaObjs and Count each answered for the slot one below the one the script named, and for player 1 — the human — that is player 0, populated on most maps, so the answer was a plausible non-empty set. The corpus settles it and the control is the interesting half: of the 33 sites in all 885 scripts passing a literal to one of the three, the values are 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5, and 0 does not occur once. The whole suite passed before and after; there was no test.
A circle contains a point when d² ≤ r²One unit wider. The executable takes a truncating integer square root (0x0067c3d0, table-driven, no floating point) and compares it with r, at two independent sites. floor(sqrt(d)) ≤ r is exactly d < (r+1)², so the rim includes a ring the obvious test excludes. The Python reference reader had the obvious test and was wrong.
Ten return types in the host API inventoryWrong, in two distinct ways, found by auditing all 280 typed rows against the registrars rather than spot-checking. Seven are the script narrowing the result — GetNamedObj returns a NamedObj, not an Obj, which is what explains all 182 sites writing GetNamedObj(x).obj.AsUnit(). Three were a generator bug: Inventory.return_types was keyed by bare name with no free/member split, so eight names that are both had their evidence pooled. Its fingerprint was that six of the eight carried identical rows for the two forms. Free Eval is void and GAIKA::Eval is int; the whole argument column was pooled too. Also SetPlayerStatus, 81 call sites: position 3 is a str, and the corpus never once passes the bool the document claimed.
149 .ini files; no value is ever quoted150, and three are. A hand-enumerated pack list missed local/italian.pak. Scan by content. (The load-bearing claim survived: no value contains a ;.)
A blocking wait needs a waiter list, or an event, or a condition the scheduler can evaluateNone of the three. gbr.exe polls, and the poll is the host call itself run again from the top: opcode 0x1E's handler compares [thread+0x38] against the program counter to tell a resume from a first entry, and a body returning 1 suspends with the counter unmoved. There is no waiter list anywhere and nothing registers a callback. A first draft of this work invented a serialisable WaitCondition record, an opaque kind byte and a new Host virtual to evaluate it; all of it was unnecessary, because the condition is the entry point's own code re-evaluated from arguments that never left the frame stack. The engine already had the primitive and had named the use case in a comment.
A wait yields first and tests afterwardsThe other way round, and it is load-bearing. Every body tests its predicate before considering suspension (0x005ed082 runs before 0x005ed0b4) and one that already holds completes in the same slice. 147 of the 208 WaitQueryCountBetween sites pass a timeout of 100 ms against turns of 200 to 800, so a family that always yielded once would answer false to every one of them — if (WaitQueryCountBetween(q, 1, 60, 100)) is the shipped spelling of "is this true right now".
A dangling Query handle makes a wait failIt makes it succeed. 0x005ed05f re-checks the handle on every poll and, on failure, ends the wait returning true after calling 0x00686eb0 — which in the retail build is a bare ret, so the diagnostic it was written for never prints. A broken wait silently satisfies its condition. Not reproduced: this engine refuses by name, and the divergence is recorded in sim/wait.hpp rather than smoothed away.
A circle contains a point by one rule, the truncating oneTwo rules, and which applies depends on the entry point. The truncating isqrt(d2) <= r — d2 < (r+1)², one unit wider than the obvious test — is the sampler's and AreaDistTo's, at 0x004d6e2e and 0x004d8b0e. Every query uses the plain d2 <= r*r. A query does not store a radius at all: it stores {cx, cy, r, r2} and compares against the fourth field — CVXMapAreaQuery<TCircleArea>'s predicate at 0x004f8c60 is cmp edx, [esi+0x60]; jg, and two independent producers write that field with an imul (0x004d883b, 0x005776ef). The earlier claim was read off the sampler and generalised to the engine; the query path had never been disassembled. The code was already right — AreaShape::contains is the sampler's and its only caller is the sampler, World::objects_in_radius is the query's — so this corrects the document, and a test now pins the disagreement so nobody unifies them.
An area query iterates objects in handle orderNot in the original. gbr.exe sweeps a 256×256-unit uniform grid, 128 columns, row-major by cell, then along each cell's singly-linked object list; the visitor push_backs onto a chunked deque with no sorted insert anywhere (0x004fc980, 0x0041f290), and the base Contains does a linear std::find over it, which is what you write when it is not sorted. It also short-circuits: a cell wholly inside the shape skips the per-object test entirely. This engine yields ascending object id, which selects the same set but in a different order — a recorded divergence, and it matters only when a caller can observe order. The per-cell insertion order was not traced and is the part that would have to be, to reproduce it.
gbr.exe has MSVC RTTI, so the class names are type descriptorsIt has none — 7 .?AV mangled names in the whole image, all CRT, and no Complete Object Locators. CVXMapAreaQuery<TRectArea> and friends are plain string literals pointed at by one global char* per class (0x0082111c..0x00821128), with a two-virtual {GetClassId, Cast} pair on each class's virtual-base subobject. Anyone reaching for RTTI structures to find a vtable will find nothing; the way in is the class-id global and the constructor that writes the vfptr.
An area's geometry never changes, so a query may copy itThe original does not copy it — CVXClassPlayerAreaQuery holds the area object at +0x78 and re-reads it every refresh, which is only worth doing because areas can move: _AdvPlaceAreaCirc (0x004d883b) and _AdvPlaceAreaRect (0x004d6d0e) rewrite an existing area's shape in place. sim/area.hpp's "nothing about it changes as the game runs … and never will" is therefore an overstatement about the original, and true of this engine only because no placement entry point is implemented (_PlaceEx/4, 89 sites, is declared and unimplemented). The copy is exact until one is, and the divergence is labelled where the copy is made.
A reversed rectangle is normalisedNot by the original. The rect setter at 0x004d6d0e copies the four authored integers verbatim with no min/max, the predicate cannot be satisfied when left > right, and the grid sweep computes (right>>8) - (left>>8) + 1 cells, which is <= 0 and exits at once — so a reversed rectangle selects nothing, silently, from every entry point. This engine sorts instead, which is a labelled divergence taken because it is unobservable on shipped data: 0 of the 185 rectangles in the shipped maps are reversed and 0 are degenerate.
The containers declare 611 bare identifiers, and they are the map-declared namesTwo different counts wearing one number. The containers use 627 distinct bare identifiers over 3,832 sites; 596 of them are map-declared <group> names. 611 is the count of names the containers use and no pack script does — 627 minus the sixteen both halves share, the packs' own bare-name set being exactly the documented 245. The plan's "611/600/11" reproduces too, and also not as written: of the 611, 11 are c<class id> constants and 600 are not. It was never a resolved/unresolved split. The residue — names that resolve nowhere — is one. tests/test_corpus_globals.py now pins all of it, each step separately.
The one unresolved global is a map name the map forgot to declareselu, twice, in 5_Great_Battles_Britain's seq8.vs, used as an object (selu.IsValid(), selu.name). It is an engine console global. gbr.exe carries it at 0x3dec4c inside a contiguous block of registration name strings — Desync, then selg selsq sels selb selh selu selo sel, then DumpStats: selected group, squad, settlement, building, hero, unit, object. None of the eight carries a signature string, where DumpStats carries "void, Obj unit" — the shape of a zero-argument accessor. The control is the family: 0 of the 8 is in any table this engine reads, and 0 of the 229 pack-only bare identifiers is a shipped <group> name. Since registered: selu/0 is declared and served by sim/orders.cpp, see the open-threads row on the last nineteen hits.
A fast path that skips a check is just fasterclass_constant's direct graph->find answered for candidates 0x0059c100 could never have minted. That registrar keys its map by the sanitised spelling, so Rock Large 01 is registered as cRock_Large_01 and the string cRock Large 01 names nothing — but the unguarded lookup found the class behind it and handed a value back. Unreachable through the VM, because the lexer cannot make an identifier with a space in it, and reachable through the function the header documents as the way to test the sanitiser. A fast path that answers names the slow path would refuse is not a fast path, it is a second rule. Found by writing the sanitiser's first test.
RevealHiddenEnemyUnits/3 needs a per-object per-player visibility mask this engine does not haveIt needs nothing this engine did not already have, and the sentence is what kept it unimplemented. sim/fog.hpp's “what is still unknown” section carried it as a name that “sweeps objects carrying kSyncHidden and writes into the low sixteen bits of [obj+0x2c], which are a per-player visibility mask rather than the owner this engine reads them as”, and concluded “that needs a field on the object, not a cell in this grid”. Both halves are backwards. 0x004c8460 reads those sixteen bits — they are the one-hot owner, exactly as the SyncFlags decomposition two headers away says — and what it writes is bit 21, cleared. The three tests it applies before writing are kSyncHidden, kSyncUnit, and the owner not being on the revealing player's side, which is the Enemy in the name and is PlayerTable::is_enemy with the bits precomputed. It is a diplomacy predicate and a flag clear, in the file where diplomacy lives, and it never looks at the exploration grid at all.

The cost is measurable and was invisible. The bullet was written during the fog work, when the entry point was correctly found to be out of scope for that thread; what it then recorded was not “not this thread” but “blocked on a field”, and the name sat at rank six of the leaders list for as long as the sentence stood, the sole blocker of three shipped scripts. A “what is still unknown” entry is a claim like any other and ages like one — this is the first one in this tree that turned out to be wrong rather than merely stale, and the format's own rule that every document must carry such a section is what made it findable.
Failure modes that look like success
  • A hash-based round-trip check is blind to whatever does not hash. Of the eleven systems, seven fold their state into the world hash, so losing one at load fails loudly. command, ai and areas do not — losing those is silent, and the only check that sees it is comparing the re-saved bytes against the original. The test that proves the danger asserts it directly: a world with command queues and AI owners hashes identically to one without.
  • Stubs hide receiver bugs. A compiler defect corrupted every nested call's receiver, and all 577 scripts still compiled and ran — because stub host functions ignore their receiver. It surfaced only when one looked. Prefer a real implementation, or a stub that asserts on its receiver.
  • Truncated reads poison everything downstream. Two separate errors — the pack-gap claim and ProductionInterval — came from reading a partial dump and never re-checking. Read the whole file.
  • A fault can repair itself inside one turn. The obvious regression test for combat enrolment — let two units fight, remove a third, check the survivors kept their experience and their target — passes with the fault injected. A re-seeded combatant gets next_action_time = 0, which is in the past, so it acts immediately, twice inside one 400 ms turn, re-acquires the target it just forgot and earns back exactly the experience it just lost. Every assertion holds. The test only works when the real state is far larger than one turn can rebuild: the case now runs a deliberately slow fight — 4,000 health against 5 damage, sixty turns — and asserts the gap. When a fault-injection run says a test caught nothing, the test may be measuring a quantity the fault regenerates.
  • Regenerating a golden with a stale tool writes a golden for a build you no longer have. The conformance literal is regenerated with imconform trace 12 400, and imconform links the same core library the test does. Regenerate without building first and the symptom is diagnostic of nothing: the golden test passes, and the test that proves the golden test can fail fails instead — because the literal now disagrees with the run from turn 1 rather than at the flipped bit. Same shape as the stale-binary problem below, one layer in. The failure hint now says to build first.
  • A manifest of what can happen is a claim somebody has to maintain; a list of what did is a fact. A save's coroutines name their code by source name, so a load has to compile those files before it can bind them — and GameSession::prime_library did it by enumerating the ways a script can start: the victory condition, the AI profile's manifest, each class's idle. Every new way to start one had to remember to join that list, and CommandSystem::launch never did. So every class <method> a command reaches — which is any verb a player or a script asks for — was missing from the library at load, and Balcans and the shipped conquest failed every save round trip on four wildlife verbs. The fix is not a fifth entry in the list. It is to stop keeping a list: the load now reads the names out of the save's own script section, which is the one enumeration that cannot be short because the writer wrote it.
  • A corpus test is only as current as the binary it shells out to. test_corpus_imsave.py found its tool by walking ("build-saves", "build") and taking the first hit. build-saves was a scratch directory from an earlier pass, three-quarters of an hour older than the engine — so the whole module spent a session validating a build in which CombatSystem was still empty, and reporting a pass. test_corpus_imrun.py had the same shape and the same problem. It is the signature failure in a new coat: the check ran, the check passed, and the check was not testing this tree. Discovery is now corpus.find_tool, which takes the newest candidate and skips with the offending filename when that is still older than the newest engine/ source. Switching imrun to the current build immediately moved an assertion that had been true for the wrong reason: seventeen of Zama's sequences used to trap, three of them on an unknown global, and those three now run on. And it happened again in the one place that never got find_tool: tools/host_coverage.py defaulted to build/engine/tools/imcheck and nothing else, so after the two SpawnGroup entry points landed it went on reporting 27,870 of 33,965 against a real 28,178 — the number the tool exists to keep honest was a previous build's. It takes the newest build*/ candidate now and refuses one older than the newest engine source, which is find_tool's rule.
  • A rule nothing checks is a rule that drifts, and the argument for breaking it was a good one. docs/legal.md rule 1 forbids game assets in the repository, "not as test fixtures, not as documentation examples". .gitignore enforces it for the extensions assets arrive in, which stops a .bmp and a .pak and does nothing about retail text in a C++ string literal. There was 15,284 characters of it: territories.xml entire (5,729), 1 ELIMINATION.VS entire (2,138), four .ini screens, four SUBAI verifiers, two playerdata documents, four translated strings and one specification example. Every one was introduced deliberately, with a comment explaining why — and the explanation was correct: a hand-authored fixture tidier than the data has accused a correct reader on this project four times, and the files said so at the top.

    What the argument got wrong is that there were two claims in it. "The reader keeps the last field of a comma-separated list" is about the reader, and its fixture should be written from docs/formats/ — tests/synthetic.py has said why for the Python half of the tree since it was written. "The shipped conquest's territory graph closes" is about the installation, and asserting it against a copy of the installation was never testing the installation; it was testing the copy, which can drift from the file it came from without anything noticing. Those moved to tests/test_corpus_campaign.py, test_corpus_match.py and test_corpus_players.py, reached through two new imcheck subcommands, and they are stronger there: imcheck victory compiles and runs the real 1 ELIMINATION.VS out of data.pak and asserts the match ends.

    tools/check_fixtures.py is the check that was missing, and writing it was where the real number came out. A first version compared whole literals and found ten copies; adjacent string literals are concatenated by the compiler, and test_player.cpp held two whole documents as twenty literals of which none was individually long enough, so the tool merges runs. A trimmed copy is still a copy — one .ini fixture had three widgets deleted from the middle — so it also sweeps for shared runs, at a much higher threshold than the whole-literal one, because a fixture written from a specification necessarily shares that specification's scaffolding with every file in it. Sixty characters of XML indentation is the format; two hundred is not.
  • A crash is a weaker catch than a failure. Two of the twenty-eight faults injected into combat's enrolment were caught only as a SIGSEGV, in a test that wrote CHECK(combat.find(a)->position == …) with no REQUIRE in front of it. The run dies before the harness prints a single FAIL line or its check tally, so everything downstream is hidden and the report says nothing about which test noticed. The harness has REQUIRE for exactly this and the comment above it says so.
  • A guard can be masked by the guards after it. is_combat_object rejects internal objects first, then classless ones, then anything that is neither unit nor building. Deleting the first reject changes nothing any world-built test can see, because World::spawn_internal allocates with kNoClass and a null native object and so fails the other two anyway. The test that walked a spawned holder was passing for the wrong reason. It becomes a real fault the day a settlement is given a resolved class — the three per-session singletons every map mints would enrol as combatants. A guard whose own case nothing constructs needs a case constructed for it.
  • Hand-authored fixtures accuse correct readers. Three times now a synthetic test has been wrong while the reader was right. When a fixture disagrees with corpus-wide evidence, suspect the fixture.
  • GRID bits are LSB-first. MSB-first gives torn-but-believable shapes, not obvious garbage.
  • LZIS is DEFLATE-family but not DEFLATE. MSB-first bits, separate code-length widths, end-of-block 285, distance alphabet split by parity. Chunk size varies (profiles use 128 KB).
  • Level-1 HPFS nodes keep a stale direct list in their tail. Derive the count from size, never by scanning for zero.
  • Empty sprite frames have right < left, breaking fixed-stride parsers. 439 frames use 32-bit row tables.
  • The loader must tolerate, not repair. 38 references dangle in retail data and the original runs anyway.
  • Two domains defining one entry point is silent. HostRegistry::define replaces without saying so, and each domain's own tests keep passing either way. Five domains built in parallel left thirteen collisions. Combat's KillCommand destroyed its receiver where the corpus means “end the running command” — 43 call sites. The fix is a manifest that is data, and a test that counts each domain's entries alone and in sequence and names any difference.
  • One void*, three meanings. CallContext::user was cast to World*, HeroHostState* and CombatHostContext* by different domains, each with tests that set it to the type that domain expected. No embedder could satisfy more than one. Worse, the first two types shared their leading member by coincidence, so a sloppy reproduction of the bug passed.
  • A query can select the wrong set and still look right. EnemyObjs(p, cls) filtered owner == p — the caller's own objects. It composed, hashed and satisfied every test that checked its shape. The same trap has a second mouth: kNoPlayer means any owner in objects_of_class_for_player, so an unresolved player number handed straight through does not answer “no such player”, it answers with the whole map. A failed lookup must select nothing; falling through to a wildcard is how a widening hides.
  • Duplicate constants in nested namespaces. ObjectId, kNoObject, PlayerId and kNoPlayer were declared in both imperivm::core and imperivm::core::sim, identically. Harmless until one file used both namespaces.
  • A subsystem can be complete and unreachable. Four times now, and it is this project's signature failure. The economy's settlement store was seeded through the wrong entry point, so every settlement on every retail map started with ten interval timers at zero — and its own partition test passed throughout, because that test builds settlements through the entry point real maps never use. The command table was never merged: 242 units, 242 unresolved orders, no error. Nothing read sequences.xml, so every mission script in the game was unreachable source. And CombatSystem is registered, hashed, serialised, exhaustively tested — and empty in every real session, because the only callers of CombatSystem::add in the whole tree are unit tests. advance returns at its second line. The game runs, draws, takes orders, starts the AI and decides victory, and nothing can fight. The common shape: a system's own tests construct it and hand it what it needs, so no test can notice that nothing else does. Registering a system is not the same as feeding it. Exercise the path the data takes.
  • A generator's own bugs arrive as facts. Output from a tool reads as evidence, and a document generated from a corpus carries the authority of the corpus even where the tool spoiled it. Two separate cases: the extractor keyed return types by bare name and pooled free functions with members of the same name, and the whole pipeline read one archive while presenting itself as the corpus. Both were caught the same way — by asking what the tool's output would look like if it were wrong, and finding that fingerprint (six identical pairs; a category of entry point with no sites at all). Fix the generator, not the output.
  • A generated table is only as wide as what generated it. host_surface.cpp was generated from a document that was generated from one archive, and the provenance was written down at every step — so the gap was invisible precisely because the pipeline was honest about its inputs. Nobody had asked whether that archive was the whole corpus. It was 65% of it. The fix is a corpus test that pins the split, and a tool (tools/host_coverage.py) that recomputes coverage from the built table rather than from a number somebody remembered.
  • An early break out of a container walk leaves the rest unread. read_payloads stopped as soon as it found the chosen map.obj.xml. Maps/ sorts before player0.xml, so choosing a map left all sixteen player documents unread and the session refused with a bare malformed — for all seven conquest maps. Latent for as long as nothing passed a map index, which is exactly how long nothing had loaded a conquest.
  • A guess that was never made still cost something: the refusal was right, and it was also over-general. sim/wait.cpp refused ten shipped call sites because "how gbr.exe coerces a NamedObj to a Query has not been read". Refusing was correct. But there is no coercion to read: NamedObj is a registered subtype of Query (0x005b6d96 sets the parent edge), so the compiler upcasts at zero cost and the call boundary never inspects a type word at all. The same header asserted that a query wait's 100 ms floor always wins because the poll hint is always zero — true of the two explicit-membership query kinds, and false of the other twelve, which return their own cache-expiry stamp. Both claims were read off one case and written as if they covered all of them, which is the same shape as the circle rule that was two rules, and as the surface table that was 65% of the corpus. A note that says which cases it was read from is worth more than one that sounds general.
  • Fifteen of sixteen injected faults were caught, and the sixteenth was the one worth having. The survivor replaced WaitHealthBetween's equality special case with a zero test — behaviourally identical everywhere except the one path where the retail build divides by zero and this engine refuses instead. Nothing tested the refusal, because a refusal reads like an implementation detail rather than a behaviour. It is a behaviour: it is the only thing standing between a query of objects with no declared maximum and a fault. The test exists now, and the injection is the only reason it does.
  • A call-site count ranks duplication, not importance. IntoRect/1 and Rot/1 are the two biggest unimplemented names in the containers, 691 sites between them — and 689 of those are in two adventures, as 130 distinct lines copied from sequence to sequence. The work behind them is two functions on a position value. The same metric had this document leading its own “what is left” paragraph with three area entry points that were about to be implemented, so the ordering was stale in both directions at once.
