Rank by what has to be understood, and let the count say only how much of the corpus stops trapping once it is.
  • Corpus scans must discover by content. Two separate counts were wrong this way in one session — a missed pack, and a container that is an LZIS stream wrapping the format you are looking for. Never enumerate inputs by hand.
Remaining work

Parts 5 to 7

05

Simulation core — closing the gaps

100% of call sites · zero traps · 25 of 25 savesLeft the named gaps below, and the vertical slice

Done: eighteen host domains — the object model with spatial and query objects, a seeded RNG, movement with deterministic A*, combat, the settlement economy, unit feeding, heroes with squads and items, the command queue, ObjList and the collections, the environment and AI-variable stores with research, translation and the string entry points, players with diplomacy, squad handles, the match and its victory rule, the AI bootstrap, and the campaign's territory graph. register_all_hosts assembles them in a fixed order that is data and is asserted; GameSession puts a world, its systems and the scheduler in one process, and imrun drives it from a retail install.

The renderer and the simulation have met. --play opens a retail map, runs its scripts, draws the live world through the game's own interface INI files, band-selects units and resolves a right click to a verb the class graph chose. The seam is closed; what is left is surface, not architecture.

Coverage, honestly measured. 33,957 of the installation's 33,965 host call sites (100%) — 100% of the 17,131 sites in data.pak, and every one of the 16,834 inside the campaign containers, which now have no declared-only name left at all, which nobody had counted until the denominator became a computed one. Recompute with tools/host_coverage.py; it joins imcheck surface against a fresh parse of all 885 scripts, so the denominator is never a remembered number. Its most important column is the last: call sites resolving to no table entry, which is now zero.

Combat fights, and the mechanism that makes it fight is now held in place. Enrolment, the obstruction grid and set_world_bound went in live and under-tested. The tests were not chosen; they were found. Twenty-eight single-point faults — every plausible mistake in reconcile's merge, in is_combat_object, in the seeding, in GameSession::create's wiring and in ObstructionGrid::from_grid — were injected one at a time and the whole suite re-run against each. Twenty-two were already caught. The six survivors each got a case written against the observable a real session would show, and each was then re-injected and confirmed caught: a departed bystander stripping every higher-id veteran of its experience and its target, an internal object with a class enrolling as a combatant, a map authoring more health than its class allows, the level and experience baselines drifting off 1 and 0, and a rectangular map's obstruction grid coming out transposed. Zama enrols 914, 857 able to attack, 29 gone after 60 turns of 800 ms.

Globals resolve, and the corpus says how far. c<class id> and the map's own <group> names carry 612 of the 627 bare identifiers the 308 container scripts use — 2,547 of 3,832 sites — and the whole ladder is now pinned per step by tests/test_corpus_globals.py, which reads the engine's own tables through imcheck globals and imcheck surface rather than a transcribed list. Zama shows it end to end: three of its seventeen sequences used to die on an unknown global — cMilitary, NO_Village1, NO_Utica — and now run past it, and no trap in the whole session is an unresolved global. Exactly one name in the whole container corpus resolves nowhere, and it is not a map name; see the corrections above. The C++ side gained the positive half it never had: the 0x0059c060 sanitiser, the find-not-lookup rule that keeps altid out of the constant space, NAMEDOBJ_OVERRIDES_GROUP, the one-query-object-per-group rule that stops a global minting a handle per read, and find-never-intern. Nine faults injected, nine caught. Writing the first sanitiser test found a real defect in the resolver — again, see the corrections.

Named gaps, each flagged by the domain that hit it:

  • The AI bootstrap exists; the script-side entry points into it mostly do not. Done: every script-side entry point has a body. AIGetPlayer/0, GetGAIKA/1, Squad.SetCmd, Squad.AIDest, Obj::AI/0 and Squad::TestFlags/1 had bodies, and so, it turned out, did AIStart/3, LAIKA/2 and GAIKACount/0 (699dd08, 27f4e1d); this bullet had gone stale. Checking AIStart against its wrapper (0x00434f90) and core (0x00434ca0) found one difference, now fixed: the empty profile is refused at the entry point, since the original’s profile list is built from data/ai/ subdirectories and never holds “”. The wrapper checks player, profile and difficulty before the core runs, so a refused call leaves a running AI alone, and a second AIStart restarts the seat’s AI. GAIKA’s own members stay blocked on the node table; see the AI-helper thread. 6 faults injected, 6 caught.
  • The target-selection family. Done, and its trap is gone. All six variants are implemented — BestTargetInSquadSightMisZeroDamage used to account for 859 of Numantia's 884 traps because it sits in the idle script every unit runs, and Numantia's trap profile is now led by posRH/0 at 940 hits with nothing else above 4. One of the family stays deliberately refused with its reason in sim/combat.cpp: BestTargetInSquadSight/1(str), whose 200-point class preference is not expressible on this engine's score scale and would become a no-op. BestTargetInGAIKA/0 was the other, and the node table it was waiting for arrived — it is bound in sim/ai.cpp, and the half of it that belongs to combat is best_target_among, so the two spellings cannot disagree about what makes a target worth hitting. See its own row below.
  • EvalGroup and the strength function behind it. Refused twice, deliberately. It shares one unrecovered per-unit strength number with MilEval, Squad.Eval, GAIKA.Eval and BestProtPos's MinEval. CounterTable::strength was checked and is three orders of magnitude off.
  • The map's settlement economy is carried now. This bullet used to say it was not — every <settlement> declares gold, food, population and three caps and MapSettlement dropped them — and the strongholds row below closed it: the seven numbers reach the settlement, laid over the class's the way the constructor reads an argument of -1, and the Mutable placeholders become a race's town at start_match. Kept here struck through in prose rather than deleted, because the list of what Part 5 still lacks is short enough now that what it used to lack is the more useful record.
  • Unit-versus-unit avoidance is done, and it is the original’s rather than an invention. gbr.exe never stamps a unit into the obstruction grid: every route a unit owns is wrapped in a CVXPathCoop (0x004172b0) that, before each step of the route sampled at the walk animation’s step, looks within its radius + 40 of the step’s end for live units outside its own march and for ownerless destination locks. A blocked unit holds 50, then 75, then 100 ms and then gives way sideways (0x00416a00), at once if the blocker is standing and early on a rand(1, 7) from the synchronised generator if it is moving; nobody is pushed and nothing deadlocks. MovementSystem now plays each turn out as a queue of decisions at exact instants in (game time, id) order, so partition invariance holds with units in each other’s way. The ownerless locks from entity point types 9, 10 and 11 (0x00540780) are reproduced; owned locks and the free-spot search are read and deliberately not built, because every reader sits behind the original’s smart pathfinder. The state is saved and not hashed: it is path media, the channel the dumps leave at zero, and the locks clear their own sync-set bit. Four readings are labelled in sim/movement.hpp; 114 faults injected, 103 caught, eleven equivalences. Balcans over 300 turns: 16,097 steps tested, 472 blocked, 208 holds, 37 sidesteps.
  • The Wait* family has its runtime, and it was already here. A Wait* suspends the calling script until a predicate holds or a timeout expires — 331 sites, led by WaitQueryCountBetween/4 at 208 — and the mechanism turns out to be HostStatus::retry, which the VM has carried since Part 4 with a comment naming WaitNonEmptyQuery. gbr.exe's interpreter (0x0069d4c0) dispatches on a host body's return value: 0 means done and 1 means suspend and re-run this same call (0x0069d5e3 leaves the program counter alone), and Sleep (0x006945e0) is the same protocol returning 1 exactly once. What was missing is that a re-entered call had no memory and so could not know whether its own timeout had expired; two fields on Execution are that memory, and because a waiting script is suspended at the call with its arguments still on the frame stack, it needs no save format of its own. Three cadences, all immediate operands: 100 ms for query waits, 1000 for settlement and area, 2000 for the environment store — and the slice is min(interval, remaining), so the last poll lands exactly on the deadline. The condition is tested before anything suspends, which is what makes the shipped if (WaitQueryCountBetween(q, 1, 60, 100)) idiom work at all when a turn is 800 ms long. Zama went from 20 distinct traps to 12 and from 14 trapping sequences to 6; coverage 77% → 78%.

    And then the other four, which were four different questions wearing one shape. WaitIdle/2, WaitHealthBetween/4, WaitSettlementCapture/3 and WaitUnitsInArea/3 are implemented; WaitConvRequest was the last one left and is implemented too — see the thread below for why it needed no conversation manager. Every claim the plan had recorded held at instruction level, and three things did not. The settlement name was being parsed and thrown away — WaitSettlementCapture takes a string, and gbr.exe resolves it by scanning the settlement vector for the std::string each settlement carries at +0xcc, the same table and key GetSettlement/1 uses, so the name belongs on the settlement; world/map.cpp read <settlement name> and populate_from_map dropped it, which took a loader join, the economy seeding and a save-format bump to close. WaitUnitsInArea uses the query circle rule, not the sampler's, so AreaShape gained contains_by_query_rule beside contains — and the obvious test probe for the difference, a point at r + 1, is outside by both rules and would have passed vacuously; the point that separates them is (100, 1) against a radius of 100. And WaitIdle had to decide something the original never has to. It compares the object's current command against "idle" or "standstill" — the second string occurs exactly once in the whole executable, which is what identifies it — but in this engine GameSession::start_object_scripts spawns each class's idle method straight onto the scheduler and never routes it through CommandSystem, so an uncommanded unit has no queue and reads as "" where the original reads "idle". Treating that as resting is a labelled reading, and Obj::command has the same gap and still answers "". Sixteen faults injected, fifteen caught, and the sixteenth found the test that was missing. Corpus-wide the distinct trap count falls 166 → 155 with no new trap anywhere; containers' coverage 75% → 76%.
  • Areas are done — seven entry points, 1,049 call sites, and the containers' coverage went 59% → 66% on that alone. All 846 named areas in the runnable containers bind, matching the reference reader per container. Everything is from the executable, not from symmetry: AttackArea issues the verb advance, not attack (0x0057a514 pushes the literal), and samples a fresh point per member inside its loop rather than sending everyone to the centre; the two miss sentinels genuinely differ (AreaCenter returns (-1,-1), GetRandomPointInArea returns (1000,1000)); a rectangle's AreaDistTo is Chebyshev, keeping the larger axis gap. Six more area entry points are refused with reasons, and pinned as refused so that implementing one forces the reason to be deleted deliberately. Both query entry points now answer a rectangle exactly, and the two do it differently because the executable does: AreaObjs builds one of two runtime types — CVXMapAreaQuery<TCircleArea> and <TRectArea> have separate vtables (0x007bce48, 0x007bcdf8), separate class-id globals and separate interning pools — while CVXClassPlayerAreaQuery is a single type that keeps the area object and re-reads its [area+0x148] discriminator on every refresh (0x004fe860), calling one of two non-virtual sweeps. So QueryKind gained one member and QuerySpec gained a rectangle plus one discriminator bool, which is that asymmetry transcribed. The containment rule is px >= left && px <= right && py >= top && py <= bottom, inclusive on all four edges, read off 0x004f8c10 and agreed by the two grid sweeps at 0x004fddc6 and 0x004fd4d5. Across all 25 map runs, zero traps from the area domain remain — Zama's seq8 now gets past its rectangle query and dies on WaitQueryCountBetween/4 instead, which is the next thread. Coverage does not move (both entry points already had bodies); what moves is that the ≈107 sites it was overstating are no longer overstated.
  • The point arithmetic, and a MapSize that was one too large. Len/0, Dist/1, Dist/2 and Point/2 are implemented — 101 sites, and no ambiguity in any of them: both distance forms square with imul and hand the sum to the table-driven square root at 0x0067c3d0, the same routine AreaDistTo's circle branch uses, so they are Euclidean and floored. Dist's member and free forms are one body registered twice (0x006990f3 and 0x00699171) and the test asserts they agree rather than assuming it. Point(x, y) is fifteen instructions with no branch in them, which is what makes the (0,0) case a test: BARRACK_TRAIN.VS passes Point(0, 0) to Place on purpose and the (0,0) → y = 1 adjustment lives downstream in the position setter, so a constructor that fixed it up here would make that adjustment unreachable. And MapSize() answered one too many. 0x0051b120 is four instructions returning the map rectangle's inclusive right; its sibling GetMapSize() (0x0053dac0) returns right − left + 1, and the two differ by one deliberately. The corpus settles it without the disassembly: CROW_MOVE.VS writes if (x > MapSize()) x = MapSize();, which is only correct for a legal coordinate. Five faults injected, five caught. Coverage 78% → 79%.
  • Rotation, and a rectangle the engine had no type for. Rot/1 (385 sites) and IntoRect/1 (398) were the two largest unimplemented names in the whole corpus, and each turned out to be a different kind of problem. Rot is x87 floating point in the original — fildl, fmull, fsin, fcos, and the MSVC _ftol2 truncation helper — multiplying by the constant at 0x007ac490, which is not π/180 but 3.1415926535/180, π truncated to eleven digits. So cos(90°) is 4.4896592e-11 rather than zero, and because the result truncates toward zero, Rot(90) is not (y, −x) but that biased by ±1 according to two signs: (3, −7) becomes (−6, −3), and the shipped four-Rot(90) patrol square does not close. Reproducing that needs no floating point at all — only the sign of each vanishing term, since a coordinate under 32,768 times 4.5e-11 cannot reach one unit — so the five cardinal angles are exact, which is 82% of the sites, and the rest go through a Q30 table generated from the retail constant rather than from π/180. The residue is the labelled divergence: at most one unit per component, on angles the script drew from the RNG. Rot(360) is not the identity and one shipped site reaches it. A rect needed a new script type, because four int32s do not fit in a Value's object id — and the shape that makes it work is interning by value: equal geometry is one index, so a copy shares it, mutation writes a new index back through the VM's existing writeback, and nothing needs cloning on assignment. That is the exact opposite of ObjListPool, whose entries are mutable and must not be shared. Bounds are inclusive on all four edges, agreed by four independent readings. IntoRect on a reversed rectangle does not normalise: the high-edge clamp runs second and wins, so every point lands on (right, bottom) — a third convention, in an executable that already holds two contradictory ones elsewhere. And SetLen/1 (73 sites) came with them, because implementing Rot and Len is what let scripts reach it — three new traps appeared in the sweep and named it. It is plain integer, and its zero case is not what this engine believed: 0x00697d81 writes the length into y and zero into x, giving a direction-less vector a direction. sim/path.hpp asserted the opposite, citing DEER_IDLE.VS calling .GetDir first — sound reasoning, wrong conclusion. Movement's own copy is left alone deliberately, because changing a facing vector moves every map's world hash; the disagreement is recorded for the next conformance rebaseline. Corpus-wide 166 → 151 distinct traps. Coverage 79% → 81%.
  • The AI helper's accessors, and a type code two handles were sharing. The plan said "the sixteen hosts first, then the runner"; recomputed against the current table the number was ten, because IntoRect and Rot were two of the sixteen and the row above took them out. Four of the ten are now implemented — GetSettlement/1 (118 sites), Hero::army/0 (92), Unit::GetHolderSett/0 (34), Unit::TimeWithoutAttack/0 (3), 247 sites and no new world state between them. Coverage 81% → 82%.

    Two of them overturned refusals this repository had recorded, and only one of the two reasons had gone stale on its own. hero.hpp declined army because "the collection types belong to the script host, not to this system" — still true, and satisfied by defining it in sim/objlist.cpp rather than by leaving it undefined. combat.cpp declined TimeWithoutAttack because nothing established what SetLastAttackTime's 40 sites expect a stamp to do; that note read two entry points as one problem, and 0x005d7f93 separates them — the reader is now − unit[0x1a4], three instructions over a field attack already writes, and it does not depend on the writer's meaning at all. SetLastAttackTime stays refused.

    Neither end of TimeWithoutAttack is zero, and they pull opposite ways. A unit that has never attacked carries a stamp of 0 and reports the whole elapsed session — there is no sentinel and no clamp — while an invalid receiver reports 0, "just attacked". This engine adds one clamp the original has no need for: it reads one clock, where this has two, and World::time() is the boundary the last turn began at while CombatSystem schedules on [time + 1, end] (gametimetickstart = gametime + 1, all nine dumps), so a blow struck in a turn's last instant reads back ahead of the clock. A negative is a value no original ever returned; zero is what the engine means by it.

    GetSettlement on a miss is not a refusal, which is the one place the house rule would have produced the wrong answer. 0x005c3bad prints "Could not find settlement named '%s' … Check the spelling." and pushes the 0xffff sentinel, and both helper scripts open set = GetSettlement(Target); if (!set.IsValid) return; — trapping would kill a helper the original exits cleanly. The lookup itself is the scan WaitSettlementCapture already built: same vector, same settlement+0xcc string, byte-exact and case-sensitive. And Hero::army is a snapshot where 0x00530580 returns a live reference — {handle, 0x1cc, hero + 0x1cc}, the hero's own vector with its refcount bumped. Of the corpus's uses only .army.ClearDead (4 sites) can tell the difference, and HeroSystem::advance already prunes every army once a turn, so the divergence is bounded by one turn and written down rather than papered over.

    And the rectangle shipped on a type code that was already taken. kTypeRect went in as 5; kTypeObjList was 5. The comment said "appended, not inserted" and appended after kTypeSettlement=4 — but the codes are handed out in four headers, and objlist.hpp (5), squad.hpp (6) and globals.hpp (7) had taken the next three, each carrying the running census that world_host.hpp alone was not. With a shared code is_rect and is_objlist agree on every value, so IntoRect(someObjList) would have read a pool id as a rect-table index and clamped against whatever geometry sat there — with no diagnostic, because the type test was satisfied. Nothing caught it because no test in this suite has ever passed a value of one handle type to an entry point expecting another: each domain builds its own registry and tests its own handles. no_two_handle_types_share_a_type_code enumerates all eight in one place and prints which two collided. Twelve faults injected, eleven caught; the twelfth is a redundant guard, and it named a real gap on the way — removing either of the two null-handle checks between GetHolderSett and SettlementStore::for_object changed no test in the suite, so the property was held up by the pair and asserted by nothing.

    What is left of the ten, and what each is waiting on. Building::IsBroken/0 and Catapult::IsBuilt/0 were fully read and waiting on new hashed object state rather than on more archaeology; both have it now (ObjectState::built and ObjectState::damage_state) — see the corrections table for what IsBroken turned out to be. Settlement::GetGaika/0 (27) is blocked, not unread: 0x0044e9e0 is setgaika[settlement_id], a flat per-settlement array, and the array is filled by the GAIKA partitioner that sim/gaika.hpp declines to invent. Settlement::BestGate/1 (2) and UnitsAroundSettlement/2 (1) are read only in outline. ObjList::Siege/3 (3) is not an accessor at all — 0x00438f20 unpacks its four arguments and hands them to 0x004383c0, the siege planner behind "CSiegePlan::Execute - Command %s not found". Only GetGaika and Siege stand between here and SIEGE.VS's stronghold branch, and they are the two that are not accessors.

    One thing found in passing and left alone: GetGAIKA/1 is registered four times in gbr.exe — over int (0x00746ea0, the identity), Obj (0x00424660), Pos (0x005bd090) and (int, int) — and this engine's registry keys on (kind, name, arity), so one body answers three of them. It implements the identity; 8 of the corpus's 16 sites pass an object (GetGAIKA(set.GetCentralBuilding) ×4, GetGAIKA(u), GetGAIKA(this)) and get kNoGaika back. That is honest while nothing consumes it and would stop being honest the moment GetGaika lands, which is another reason not to invent the table.
  • Groups, spawning, and 39% of every campaign map that should not have been there. The plan had this thread down as "bigger than the group tables already exist suggested", and it was, in a direction it did not name. The recorded defect was real — the table sorted its members where CVXGroup::Add (0x00572410) appends after a linear std::find, and SpawnGroup walks that stored order to mint ids, so it decides every id downstream of a spawn. Fixing it broke every real save at once: the loader required ascending ids, and the whole 43,962-check suite stayed green while imsave refused Numantia and mediterranean, because the save fixture added its two members as 1 then 2.

    The two deques turned out to be a flag bit nobody had explained. Add routes on [obj+0x2c] & 0x08000000 — <scriptobj flags> bit 27 — and forty-odd collection sites skip an object carrying it. That is 10,610 of the 27,070 objects the 29 shipped maps place, from 16% of 6_Great_loses_Boudicca to 77% of 5_Great_Battles_Britain, every one of which this engine had enrolled, ticked and targetable from turn zero. Numantia's load-time enrolment falls 1,203 → 710 and its idle scripts 1,542 → 1,049, a difference of exactly its 493 templates; Zama then goes 307 → 407 combatants as its own sequences bring the reinforcements in. SpawnGroupInHolder is registered twice at arity 2 and one body dispatches on the argument's runtime type, as GetGAIKA does; the second body's own diagnostic calls it SpawnGroupInShip, a name registered nowhere in the image. Coverage 82% → 84%, containers 81% → 85%.

    Twenty-six faults injected, and four of the survivors were the useful ones. Clearing the unspawned bit on a spawned copy was dead code — the copy's category flags already had it clear — so the clear was unfalsifiable until the copy was made to inherit the template's whole flag word first, which is the original's clone-then-clear order anyway. And three session faults survived because nothing in the suite drove templates through GameSession: the tests built worlds by hand and would have passed with the session doing nothing at all. That is this project's signature failure, found for the fifth time and by the same method.
  • Two of the four biggest names in the installation, and neither was in the plan's own leader list. This thread was picked by recomputing the list of unimplemented entry points rather than reading the sentence in Part 7 that claimed to hold it — and the sentence had silently dropped five names that all outranked the ones it did name. Obj::posRH/0 was the third-largest unimplemented name in the whole install, 141 sites; Place/3 the fourth-largest free one. The lesson is the project's own, in a new place: a figure assembled by hand goes stale in exactly the way the tooling exists to prevent, and the fix is to compute the list, which the thread table now does.

    posRH was free, because it is pos. It goes through the virtual at vtbl + 0xC8; that slot is one thunk, 0x005a75d0, in all fifteen classes carrying the shared SetPos, and it forwards to vtbl + 0x3c — 0x0063d900 in all fifteen, whose entire body reads the same +0x24/+0x28 that Obj::pos reads directly. Anchoring the census on the SetPos pointer rather than on a guessed vtable start is what makes it exhaustive; the earlier attempt, which detected vtables as runs of code pointers, put the slot in the wrong place and reported 29 different implementations of something that has one.

    Difficulty is 0/1/2, it is a match setting, and nothing in the shipped corpus writes it. SetDifficulty has zero call sites against GetDifficulty's 136 — Zama alone multiplies it into unit levels (SetLevel(30 + GetDifficulty() * 10)) and reinforcement timers (Sleep(120000 - GetDifficulty() * 15000)). It is implemented anyway, hashed and saved, because a value that many sites read has to be settable by something and two peers that disagree diverge on the turn the next unit appears.

    Twelve faults injected for the flag, ten caught, and both survivors were the save — in the place that had already been caught doing this once. pack_object_flags grew in_party, then unspawned, and now no_ai, and the world save fixture had never set any of the three: three of its eight packed flags round-tripped clean no matter what the packer did with them. The fixture sets every bit the packer can write now and compares all eight, and the two surviving faults died. The comment above pack_object_flags had described this exact blind spot for in_party two threads ago and the fixture was never changed, which is the more useful half of the finding: a defect recorded in prose is not a defect closed.
  • The rest of the campaign surface. Group spawning is done — SpawnGroup, SpawnGroupInHolder and the whole membership family, 700 sites — and so are difficulty, Obj::posRH and both forms of SetNoAIFlag, another 622. Place/3 and _PlaceEx/4 (220 sites), the five diplomacy writers (215), the sequence runner and SetPlayer/1 (116) are done too. And so is the cluster that carried most of what was left: the AI-helper runner — RunAIHelper/4 285 sites, IsAIHelperRunning/1 236, StopAIHelper/1 179 — together with units/0 (115), UnitsAroundSettlement/2, Catapult::IsBuilt/0 and name/0 (61). See the threads below.

    What the helpers still die on, per script rather than in aggregate. Two of the four now run to the end of their host surface: GUARD AREA.VS has 39 host calls and none unimplemented, and GUARD.VS's 132 calls are clean since Catapult::IsBuilt landed. Together they are named by 86 of the 285 RunAIHelper sites. The remaining 199 are the siege pair — SIEGE.VS (155 sites, 4 blocked) and SIEGE GATE.VS (44 sites, 3) — and the union is four names: Settlement::GetGaika/0, ObjList::Siege/3, Gate::IsBroken/0 and Settlement::BestGate/1. The first two are blocked rather than unread and are the real gate; IsBroken needs new hashed object state, and the corrections table says exactly what it is. Then the presentation layer the missions narrate through — of which the notes and the conversations are done: GiveNote, RemoveNote, IsNoteActive and ClearNotes, 220 sites, and RunConv/1 (74), Conversation::SetActor/2 (63), Conversation::Run/0 (54), Conversation::Init/1 (54) and ConvResult/1 (5), 250 more. See the threads below. What is left of it is the camera and the chrome: View/2 (35), ShowHint/3 (26), SetShortcutSel/3 (24), PlayMovie/1 (24), BlockUserInput/0 and UnblockUserInput/0 (34), ShowAnnouncement/2 (11) and six smaller ones, 168 sites together — and hostregs.py now prints a named signature for every one of them, which it could not before.
  • One member was 96% of the corpus's remaining trap hits, and it is two questions. Obj::AI/0 fired 36,957 times across the 25 map runs against 38,303 hits in total, because DATA\SUBAI\UNIT_IDLE.VS is the sub-AI every unit runs while idle and it tests .AI on every pass. Only 25 call sites, so it barely moves coverage and collapses the trap tally — the two figures rank different things, which is the whole reason to report both. Measured after: corpus-wide trap hits fall 38,303 to 16,681, distinct traps 154 to 153, and the one new entry is posRH/0 in the same script — the next call UNIT_IDLE.VS reaches now that it gets past .AI. That one is not a quick follow-up: 0x005ad8e0 is a virtual call through the class vtable, an attachment point on the art rig, so it needs entity skeleton data rather than more disassembly.

    0x004254b0 asks, in order: is [obj+0x70] — the player record, identified by Obj::player being [[obj+0x70]+0x8] + 1 — non-null; is [player+0x88], that player's AI, non-null; and only then does it unpack [obj+0x174] into (index << 4) | player, a SquadKey, look the squad up and return the negation of bit 0 of its flags. So AI means "run by the computer and not pinned", which is exactly how the corpus guards with it — if (.AI) before AI-only behaviour, if (!.AI) before the player-only kind.

    The obvious translation of step 2 is wrong here and a test caught it before it shipped. AiSystem::player_ai indexes a dense array and returns a pointer for every player in range, running or not, so testing it for null the way the original tests its pointer answers true for every unit on the map. The slot always exists; AiPlayer::active is the AI.

    Squad::TestFlags/1 (8 sites) came with it, over the same word, and the two neighbours are one instruction apart in intent and opposite in result: TestFlags is and + cmpw + sete, all the bits asked for, while Unit::GetFlags(mask) (0x005d7e75) is testl + setne, any. The corpus reaches both readings — TestFlags(SF_PEACEFUL) is one bit and cannot tell, TestFlags(SF_PEACEFUL + SF_NOAI) is two and can.

    And sim/squad.hpp said the SF_* values were "unknown, and nothing here assigns any" while sim/globals.cpp had carried all five from the executable's constant table for some time. Bit 0 now has a second, independent witness in Unit::AI itself, which is what makes it a measurement rather than a transcription.
  • SetNoAIFlag is a different flag from AI, despite the names. Worth writing down because the two look like a reader/writer pair and are not: Unit::SetNoAIFlag (0x005de665) sets or clears bit 18 of [unit+0x194] — UNITFLAG_NOAI, 262144, which the globals table also names — while AI reads SF_NOAI, bit 0 of the squad flags at [squad+0x30]. Both exist, both are named by the engine, and they are per-unit and per-squad respectively. The unit flag has a self-contained round trip in the corpus — mission scripts call SetNoAIFlag(list, false) (121 free sites, 109 member) and AI scripts skip pinned units with GetFlags(UNITFLAG_NOAI) (5 sites) — so implementing it is worthwhile and honest; it needs a per-object flag word this engine does not carry, distinct from the sync flags at [obj+0x2c], and UNITFLAG_NOAI is the only bit of it any shipped data names.
  • SS_STR needs the AI profile reachable from a call context; SetLastAttackTime needs a meaning rather than access; rollover and CreateFeedback are interface-layer. These and EvalGroup are what Part 5 leaves, now that unit-versus-unit avoidance is done — none of them is reached by any shipped script on any runnable map, which is what a trap sweep at zero says, so what remains is either a labelled refusal or a behaviour the sweep cannot see. The measured surface is at its ceiling; the part is closed on that basis, and the vertical slice is the next milestone. Measured again at 300 turns instead of the sweep's 60, on the same maps, the same build and the build the menus started from: four messages, 85 hits, none of them new to this session — host defines no comparison of these operand types in ESH_BuildArmy.vs (76, on Balcans and Crossroads), AddCommand/2 and SetCommand/1 refusing a receiver that is neither object nor list (6), GetObjList/0 on something that is not a query in lion_lead.vs (3). The 60-turn zero was a horizon, not a ceiling. Beaten: zero at 300 turns and at 600, on every runnable map. None of the four was a missing entry point — a script continuing past its object's death, twice; a VM comparison the retail compiler resolves through a conversion; a query member one step less lenient than its sibling. Each is a row in Part 5's table below.

The vertical slice closed, and a faithful fix reopened its ending: a skirmish goes to war by itself, its walled towns now defend themselves, and it no longer ends by itself. On Crossroads, with nothing ordered, the computer players train, march and fight, and the human seat’s walled town holds: its sentries stand on the walls, patrol and guard, and p2’s attacks lose 54 men to them by turn 12,000 against 3 sentries lost. The match is undecided there (9,992 blows, 165 deaths, no trap). The ending recorded below — p2 taking p0’s town by loyalty at turn 2,369 — was one hero taunting a town whose sentries were stuck, stacked, where their walls had placed them (the row “Sentries stand on their walls”). The owner chose to pin the tests at a fixed turn rather than chase the ending: held at turn 2,200, the app and imrun are one game (83e64087f71502a6); tests/test_corpus_imrun.py asserts that the town holds, the war is fought, every sentry is by its walls and nothing traps, and tests/test_corpus_app_match.py holds the app to imrun’s hash on turn 2,205. What the AI needs to take a defended town is the open row “The AI’s siege against real sentries”. How the ending was first reached: On Crossroads, with nothing ordered, p2 takes p0’s broken town by loyalty at turn 2,369 and 1 ELIMINATION.VS ends p0’s match by EndGame (5,030 blows, 124 deaths, no trap). Three links did it (the row “Class behaviours start; a garrison is inside its holder”): class <behavior> scripts start with their object, a settlement’s garrison is inside its holder, and a node is explored once any of its slots is. What follows is how it got there. Crossroads and Alesia were played through --play and judged from screenshots rather than counters. Settlements grow and tax on screen: Crossroads’ town hall went from 64 to 93 people and from 5,570 to 14,742 gold. Band-select, right-click and command-bar orders all issue. A 33-man column crosses a gap between tents without passing through itself. Ordered attacks are issued, now that a player’s right click compiles the verifier it needs; before that fix every first attack order was silently blocked. Headless, Alesia lands 6,283 blows and kills 219 in 400 turns. Playing it also surfaced four display defects, now fixed: spawn templates drawn and clickable (607 of Zama’s 1,119 objects), fog-hidden units still drawn, the opening camera never looking at the player’s holding, and View/ViewPos/SetFog never reaching the camera. A skirmish goes to war by itself, headless, and does not end. On Crossroads with nothing ordered and nobody playing the human seat, the computer players train, march and fight: by turn 1,689 of 800 ms (about 22 game minutes) there are 4,778 blows and 19 deaths. For a while the match ended there, when a p1 axe thrower brought p0’s town hall to zero and it was despawned. That was wrong: gbr.exe never kills a building. A town hall at no health stands broken, its owner’s, and is no longer a target (the row “A town hall at no health stands broken”), and a seat is lost only when its town is taken. With that fixed, Crossroads is undecided at turn 12,000: no computer player captures p0’s broken town. Capture is now what stands between the slice and a match that ends by itself, and why it does not happen has been traced (the row “Why no computer player takes a town”). It stops long before the capture mechanic, which works, and before the GAIKA census, which is not empty: p0’s walled town is never chosen as a target. The node is never explored by the centre reading; explored by any of its slots it is chosen and then refused, because only a hero-led army may march on an enemy stronghold and every hero army is committed to an independent outpost whose capture never ends — because no class <behavior> script is ever started in this engine, and OUTPOST_BEHAVIOR.VS is what hands an outpost over. The owner decided to start them; with that, the garrison fix and the any-slot reading, the match now ends at turn 2,369. tests/test_corpus_imrun.py asserts the war it does see (4,000 turns: more than 1,000 strikes, deaths, a computer player’s kills, every stronghold still holding its town hall, and not one trap, now that a distance to an object is measured from its edge as gbr.exe measures it), and that if the match is decided it is by EndGame. Village tribute flows too: at 3,000 turns each computer player’s village holds 62 food, against 5,000 in the human seat’s village nobody drains. Five fixes did it, each a labelled reading (the open-threads row on the skirmish AI): an order through the order core ends a resting idle; command() of an object never ordered is "idle"; a node is Explored once its centre is; SendTo walks the squad to the node; and a settlement on its own footprint takes the nearest area. The strength function was the suspect, and it is not on this path. The trace showed every cap at −1, MilEval passing at ≥1 and the recruiter’s Needed at 1–1, so no strength figure was holding an army back; the stall was the five above. Nor is it on the capture path: RECRUITER.VS:167’s enemy < 1000 only skips an ally’s settlement and EVALRECRUIT.VS:69’s < 500 only a Viking lord’s squad. It stays unrecovered. The app half is closed too. Crossroads played in the app with the human seat idle (--play --player 0 --difficulty 0 --turn-length 800 --turn-interval 1, about 80 s headless) is decided at turn 2,369, and the end-game screen opens by itself on that turn, on the same world hash as imrun (11dc4c1e235fadfe). They had been two games: the app played at the options’ difficulty (1) where imrun plays the match’s 0, parting them at turn 0, and the headless entity resolver kept each image’s XML grid where the app takes the sheet’s frame table — an animation’s length is its sheet’s rows — parting them at turn 4 (the row “The app and imrun play one game”). An ordered engagement on Alesia has been watched to a death on screen (“Combat is seen”), and a save taken mid-war on Crossroads loads back into the same game. Judged from screenshots, the slice was thinner than the counters suggested — p0’s town was taken by one hero taunting an empty hall, with no sentry on its walls although the settlement counted 50 of 50 — and chasing that is what found the sentries stuck, and the four bugs behind it. Screenshots of turn 2,344 now show a sentry on every wall and gate. The Statistics oddities first seen beside it are closed (that row).

06

Interface layer

bars, menus and the editor's brushesRisk low

The in-game slice is done — 24 of 24 files, 23 screens, 352 widgets, 424 image references all resolving, every screen composited at two resolutions across all eight factions and verified on the GPU. The command bar and info bar in the running game are the shipped ones.

%TmplIni% is solved, and it is why the family looked bigger than it is. It is not a string substitution: it names a file, always as the first argument of Template = <ini path>, <section>, which merges that section into the current one with local entries winning. %Name% is a per-screen parameter scope resolved against [Name Params]. A faction skin is one line: RaceArt=gameres/infobar/RROME. See docs/formats/interface-ini.md.

The bars show the game now, read from the class graph the way the original reads it. What goes in the info bar is declared per class in the XML — interface= is the selection tags, icon= the portrait, display_name= the name, and <value0..5> are six inline .vs expressions (return .AsUnit.level;) that sim/infobar.hpp compiles once and runs against the selected object the way a verify= is run. Which class is read is gbr.exe's rule at 0x005e5720 — Empty, the object's own, WagonGold/WagonFood, <R>PeasantMulti, Multi, MultiOne and its two variants — and the Multi classes' scripts needed the Sel* family, seven free functions with no .vs call site, read out of the executable as rounded means. The six strips draw: the queue's train rows with the bar Obj::Progress stamps, the garrison, the hero's items (ITEMS.XML reaches a session now; it never had), the skills in SKILLS.INI order, the specials, and Combiner continuing one row with the other; the Switch widgets flip the tab. The command bar's buttons are the rows the selection's classes offer — <src> minus <nsrc>, with a button=, by priority, capped at CmdBarMaxButtons — each groupverifier= run over the selection with the row described so cmdparam answers for it; a press issues a targetless row at once and arms a targeted one for the next click on the map; the key letter presses it; a tooltip carries the row's rollover, costs, key and the verifier's reason. The no-selection menu shows for an empty selection only and its two saves are wired. --select, --tab and --hover put any of it in a screenshot.

Three defects the first drawn strip found: the layout floored a screen's design at MinSize, so the 80-tall bar had a slack of -120 and every stretch-spring strip was 45 pixels tall in the negative; ResourceCache handed out pointers into a vector that grew; and the health ramp was sampled backwards. Readings, labelled: where the command buttons stand (centred, abutting) is read off the art and the menu's size, not the executable; a multiple selection offers the rows every member offers; a queue entry's number is not drawn.

The menus are the shipped screens now, and the app opens on them. The interpreter learned the twenty-one widget classes the 48 menu files and the 52 editor files use and the bars do not — Dialog, DarkFrame, Frame, ImageButton, TextW, EditW, List, Scroll, Combobox, VXMenuBack and the rest — with what each needs read out of the executable rather than guessed where it could be: a Frame's Dividers are left, middle, top, middle with the executable's own clamp (0x00663570); a DarkFrame halves every pixel under it (0x00663c70); the 52 style names are eight per-class tables with their bit values, where TABSTOP and MODAL are the same bit. imcheck interface is the census: 136 of 136 screens over 137 files, 2,954 widgets, every one loads, after four things the loader refused that the original reads — a widget placed from one listed after it, from the dialog's own width, a bare parameter inside #...#, and an integer with a stray letter after it. What remains is the data's own: six widgets in the Online Battle files inherit a template no file defines, TRANSPRENT four times, six bitmaps no pack holds, one of them named asdf. ui/dialog.hpp runs a screen — buttons pressed on release, Esc and Enter through the screen's own lines, a list with its scrollbar's arrows, an edit with its Bufsize, a combobox that drops — and it is in the core, so a test opens GAMEMENU.INI and sees 0x1006 come out of Quit with no window.

In the game: F10, Escape or the bar's button opens GAMEMENU.INI over the world — the dark frame, the frame art, the caption between its bullets — and its buttons open CONFIRM.INI, SAVEGAME.INI (named slots under Saves/, the quick slot among them), LOADGAME.INI and GAMEOPTIONS.INI; F8 opens NOTES.INI over the active board, each note its title over its text and View location putting the camera on its pin. On the front: with no --map the app opens on MENUBACK.INI and MAINMENU.INI over the language pack's background, and Great Battles, Conquest, Single player and Load game are ADVENTUREMENU.INI, CONQUESTMENU.INI, SELECTMAP.INI and LOADGAMEFROMMAINMENU.INI over the installation's containers — each with its game.xml name and description and the picture beside it — and a choice starts the map as the container's start_player; Quit comes back. The menus are not opaque, so the bars' blit is gone: every layer goes through one blended pipeline, the second one ui/paint.hpp said the bars did not need. The language pack is mounted — Settings.ini names it — and every declared string is translated by the contextual key the table keys it under (Cancel@/Menu/selectmap.ini:CancelBtn:Text), matched case-blind because the table spells its own file names three ways. --menu and --input put any of it in a screenshot and drive it: key:F10;click:640,276;text:test;key:Return saves a game from the menu in a headless run.

Readings, labelled where they stand: a frame's middle tiled rather than stretched (indistinguishable on the retail art); an ImageButton's cell centred in its rectangle; a dialog centred in the display as 0x006b3e00 centres a child; a local Style unioned with its template's, the local alignment winning; the notes list's colours from the file's own comments; the adventure picture fitted to a caption a third its shape.

The skirmish setup is the shipped screen too: Single player → SELECTMAP.INI → MPGAMEMENU.INI with SETTINGS.INI at its SettingsPos, one row per slot the scenario declares — the colour square painted with the player's colour, the type button cycling human/computer/closed with one human at a time, the name or the AI's strength, the nation from the eight races and Random, the team — and Start builds MatchOptions from the rows (human, races, controls) and writes teams as mutual alliance, 0x15 being the word the shipped maps hold between allies (reading, labelled: what the original's team button writes was not read). Two screens side by side share the pointer: the topmost with a widget under it takes the click, and a release goes to the screen its press went to. The settings are shown with the one map type and game type this engine plays; none of it is wired to a rule.

And the conquest has its campaign map. CONQUESTGAME.INI over ConquestMaps/<data>/global.bmp, scaled into the 600 × 400 ConquestMap widget; territories.bmp turned out to be an 8-bit mask whose pixel value is the territory's index — the histogram is exactly 1..8 — which settles what that attribute indexes, and %s/Shield%s.bmp takes the territory's id. Owned territories are tinted with the file's owned_hue/owned_sat and reachable enemies with the enemy pair (hue on a scale of 1536, saturation with 1024 as unity: readings, labelled), a click on the map selects the territory under it and shows its description and bonus, the shields are the road of conquest, and a fresh conquest asks for a starting territory: the chosen one becomes the player's nation through its bonus name, rRepublicanRome for Italy, applied to the Mutable human slot. Start plays the territory's map with the campaign file restored, and Quit from the game — or the end-game menu, ENDGAMEMENU.INI, which opens the frame the match is decided with the win or the loss picture — comes back to the map with the new state. Measured: Italy chosen, Gallia started as Republican Rome on Maps/7 with ConquestBonus() -> "rRepublicanRome", and back.

A container's own language. Under Local/<language>/ a container ships translation tables of its own — adventure.loc.xml with its name, description, actors, territories and the sequences' narration, Maps/<n>/notes.xml with the notes, the conversations' phrases — all keyed by the English source. read_localisation collects them, the session merges them over the language pack's table, and the front's lists and the campaign map read them before a session exists: L'assedio di Numanzia, Obiettivi, Conquista la città ribelle di Numanzia.

P shows PAUSED.INI's word over the standing world and takes it down again; the game's keys work under it. Space is the bar's Minimap: ZOOMMAP.INI, a screen that declares no widget, with the whole map at a sixteenth as its backdrop — the ground from the map renderer, a cell the mean of its texture with the light layer applied; every building and map object as its Minimap.pak ZOOM16 picture, named after its entity path and cut out by the 32-bit alpha the decoder now honours; every unit a dot of its owner's colour; the camera's frame — and a click puts the camera there. Read since: the zoom is the map's width over 1024 — 0x0061d03b switches on the map's right edge, 8, 16 or 32 — so the tutorial's 8,192-unit map composes at an eighth and a 16,384 one at a sixteenth, into 1024 × 736 (the composer used to come out 512 tall, 46 / 16 truncated, under objects placed on the 736; sized by the projection now). Space is a tap-or-hold key (0x0060e3b0): the press opens or closes, the release closes only past Const.ini's [zoommap] ToggleTreshold, misspelling included. The map is a live child window, recomposed every 500 ms with the game's input running under it; a right click centres the camera and closes, a left press or drag moves the camera and keeps it open (cursor mode 0x11, inferred a camera drag). Whether the original pauses the simulation under it is not established.

The setup's bonuses run. The button's four rows are DATA/BonusScripts/'s 000 NONE, 001 WEALTH, 002 RICHES and 003 HERO — 0x00566ed0 lists that directory and keys each file by its name — and GameSession::start_bonuses spawns the one a player chose as void, Settlement set, int playerid on their first stronghold (which settlement is the player's, the file does not say; labelled). The 288 shipped player documents all say -1, so only the setup screen ever sets one.

Options are wired: GAMEOPTIONS.INI's sliders drag (a Scroll with no TargetId is a slider, 0..100 along its run) and its check buttons flip, the values are the game's own Settings.ini [Options] keys read from the installation's file and written to Saves/settings.ini, the engine's, and the engine acts on the game speed (the turn interval — GameSpeed=13 taken as 0..25 with 13 normal, a reading), the scroll speed (the keyboard pan) and the resolution (the window); the sound effects switch and volume govern the one effect played so far (PlayerDropped.wav), and the other sound settings wait for a sound engine.

Help is HELP.INI over the language pack's HELP.XML — 225 topics, 2,126 entries, the one shipped document whose element text matters, read by a reader of its own since xml.hpp keeps none. A topic is the list: an entry a row, its image beside it, headings in the bold face and centred, paragraphs wrapped at the list's width; a row with a link opens its topic (a child by id, or a /contents/... path by its last component), and Backward, Forward, Up, Previous, Next and Home walk the hypertext.

Diplomacy is DIPLOMACY.INI on the bar's button: a row per other participant with its colour and name, four toggles for the local player's policy toward it — cease fire, share view, share support, share control, the four relation bits — and the same four, read-only, for its policy back; OK writes the player's row, Cancel drops it.

The setup's rules are read and wired. SETTINGS.INI's six: the game type is data/GameScripts/ by file (0x006c4750) and replaces the map's victory rule unless Map Default; the limit is CONST.INI's 2_Score_limit0..5 / 3_Time_limit_*0..7 rows, translated whole and split at the comma, whose number becomes the script's str param; the world population is LowPop/NormalPop/HighPop as a percent of every non-wildlife settlement's max_population (0x005267dd); a starting gold other than Default is SetGold on every settlement with a warehouse, the independents' too (0x005267b3); no exploration reveals the map at load (0x00526813); no fog is kept on the match for a renderer that draws fog. Shared support and control widen the teammates' relation word by bits 2 and 5, and no bonuses skips the bonus scripts — both inferred, their consumers not found. The choice persists under the profile's own [Player] keys (0x0056ba60) in Saves/settings.ini, and the four rules ride the match's save section (version 20). Found on the way: the exploration map was empty in every session. FogSystem::start sized it from the match's map_size at world.start(), before any match was configured, so IsExplored answered no everywhere and UNIT_EXPLORE.VS walked to nothing; it starts again once the rules are in.

Left: News. It is online, and nothing that is not online is left in this part. Struck from this list: the bar’s Chat button, which opens INGAMECHAT.INI in a networked match as Enter does (the shipped ID 0x1006 was never dispatched); Change player, built below and no longer blocked on the decision it was blocked on; the editor's nextwnd, built below, which wanted a stack that reorders rather than a z-order that did not exist; the object property sheet and the explorer with its tree, its group dialog and its document dialogs, built below; the passability layer — the re-stamp of object and decoration masks, the passable_water rule, the decoration stamp's footprint — read, built and proven below; the editor's second slice — terrain, height and decoration painting, areas, direction, the key map — taken below; the fog's unread half, read and built below; and the minimap in the bar, which was a phantom. The bar INIs declare only the Minimap (Space) button, which opens the zoom map; commonini/MiniMap.ini, the file the app never loaded, is the editor's floating minimap window — its constructor (0x00614130) has six callers, five in editor code and the sixth the MiniMap() cheat entry (0x006148e0, "I'm a cheater. Just used the MiniMap.") — and there is no permanent in-game minimap to build.

The editor, scoped. There is no root editor screen: the editor is a native mode of gbr.exe, a live map view with the 52 dialogs opened by path from the tools' own code, and no menu resource — how Save, Open and Map properties are reached is not in the data and would be a key-map reading. The tools palette (MAPTOOLSDLG.INI) is a native tree built at 0x004a2a80: decorations, height, terrains (TERRAINS.XML's types), structures and units from the classes' edittree_pos (400 classes, already a property the class graph answers; labels translate as <leaf>@editortree/<path>), edit objects, areas. A saved scenario is what Packs/emptyscn.bfhp holds plus the sixteen player<i>.xml the templates lack; the container writer exists (BlockFileBuilder), the grid and XML writers do not. The first slice, when it is taken: an editor mode over a loaded map with no session, the palette's structures and units subtree, click-to-place, drag, delete, and a gamedata map writer that imrun loads back.

The fog of war is drawn. A reading of the exe's fog manager (CVXFog, 0x00606970): a 16-unit light grid for the local player, a word whose bits 9..15 are a level — lit, unexplored, twenty-nine shades — rebuilt from the 1024-unit exploration map (two bits per player per cell, the four states fog.hpp called unknown) plus the player's own objects, sampled bilinearly at draw: unexplored is solid black (0x604ad4), lit draws nothing, explored is darkened through a lookup; an object is hidden when the grid is not lit where it stands (0x00605e10). platform/fog_view.hpp keeps three states per 64-unit cell and draws one pixel per cell stretched with linear sampling over the world, under the bars; the world view hides what the fog hides, and the zoom map shows the same cells. Labelled: sight circles for the visible set (the exe's square-and-grow rule was not reached), one half for the darkening, buildings kept in explored cells. Found on the way, and fixed the turn before: the exploration map every session started with was empty.

The fog's unread half, read and built. Everything the paragraph above labelled is now read off the exe and reproduced; the two grids are as they are there. The exploration map's middle state is spatial, not a fade. The circle stamp (0x00516450, behind ExploreCircle, ExploreArea, SetSight and the sim's own sight) writes state 3 into every 1024-cell wholly inside r − 48, and into every cell the annulus [r − 48, r + 48] meets a fine record — a 32 × 32 lattice of nibbles at 32-unit spacing, a point at distance d getting (R − d) · 15 / 96 for R = r + 48, maxed with what was there (0x00514b80) — and the cell reads state 2. State 1 is written by nothing found. sim/fog.hpp keeps the two bits and the records, the save carries them (section version 22), and one equivalence is taken and labelled: a record of all fifteens is state 3, since every reader — the light cap at 0x3c00, the zoom map's non-zero, the hosts' non-zero — agrees. The sweep stamps an object once per position, owner and sight, because the stamp is idempotent and a map's 1,680 standing objects re-stamped every turn would cost the turn. The light grid (sim/fog_light.hpp, tested without a window): the rebuild (0x00607190) over the view grown by 2048 — the floor 0x2000; every spawned, living object with a sight on the local side (the side mask read as share view) lighting a circle evaluated at the 16-cell's corner, 0x3e00 inside sight − 32 and a ramp in squared distance over the last 32 units, after a coarse pass that lights a 256-cell wholesale when its eight neighbours all hold an object of class sight over 362 and drops the objects standing in it; then the exploration map, a never-seen cell to 0 and a partial one capped at its nibbles times 1024, blended at 32 units. Rebuilt every fourth tick of a min(100, 100000 / speed) ms timer into a delta of a quarter, added every tick — four ticks up into sight, four down out of it, four up out of the black — and snapped where a scroll exposes ground. The darkening is floor(68 L / 31) / 32 per channel for L = word >> 10 (0x00604610): 17/32 at the floor, where "one half" was a guess. Nothing writes 0x3a00; it is the draw's threshold, and unexplored is word 0. The hide test (0x00605e10) is applied to units and projectiles only (0x00605ff0): buildings are never hidden and there is no last-seen memory; a unit is hidden below 0x3000 unless its owner shares its view or it is an Animal on explored ground. The zoom map reads the exploration map and never the light grid — every caller of the light sampler is inside the fog module — a 32-unit sub-cell explored iff its nibble is non-zero, never-seen ground in Const.ini's MinimapEmptyColor (0x3dc9), and an object dotted wherever the ground is explored, seen or not: an enemy standing on ground you once saw is on the map. Left labelled: the draw's ordered dither and the sampler's off-corner arithmetic; that [class+0x2d0] is the class sight, inferred from 362 alone; the speed global's default; the six sim call sites of the stamp's small-radius front end, which hold the reveal cadence; and 0x006074e0, run after each rebuild.

The first slice is taken. --edit, and the front's Editor button through the scenario list, open a map placed and not played: the session is built and nothing is started, no turn runs, the bars stay down, and MAPTOOLSDLG.INI stands at the right with PlaceObj.ini docked where the exe docks it. The palette's Browser — a bare Control the exe hangs a native tree on — is filled with one row per visible node of the edittree_pos tree (474 leaves on this install, labelled through @editortree/<path>: Strutture, Unità, Anziano d'Africa); a Control with items draws and answers as a List, in the screen's first font and, under an editor path, in black. A leaf chosen places its class on a click the way a script places one (spawn_of_class, owner, position); Edit objects selects, drags, and Delete removes; F2 opens SaveDlg.ini and the name written goes to Scenarios/ through the writer below, moved objects at their new positions, removed ones erased with their group and area references, placed ones numbered after the last — and imrun loads the result. The palettes are tool windows: the pointer outside them is the map's, and Escape leaves the editor for the front. Not in the slice, and said so: terrain, height and decoration painting, areas, the property dialogs, direction on a placed object, and the original's key map.

The second slice: the brushes, the areas, the keys. The palette is the exe's tree now, in the order 0x004a29a0 inserts it: Decorations from DECORS.INI's group/subgroup/name (a kind with no group stands in no tree; no season filter, the loader never reads the key), Delete decorations, Height with Raise/lower, Smooth and Set height, Terrains by TERRAINS.XML type with a leaf per layer (Waves skipped; Invalid only under a Config.ini switch this engine does not read), the classes, Area with a circular, a rectangular and an edit leaf, Edit objects last and chosen first. Each node docks its own pane where SettingsPosCtl stands, and the two widgets those panes needed draw and answer now: BrushSize (the five slots of BRUSHES.BMP, whose second row is the chosen look) and Spin beside a NumberEdit. The brushes are read off the tool windows and live in core/world/editor.hpp with their addresses, tested headless: a disc of cells with i² + j² ≤ r² for radii 0, 1, 2, 3, 5 (terrain, decor) or 1, 3, 5, 7, 9 (height, at 32 units); the terrain stroke on the cell whose corner is nearest the pointer, repainted past 32 units of travel, a leaf's layer or one of a group's per cell, deep water only where every cell within four is water or under Shift, and the passability under the disc cleared and re-baked from the terrain's passable; the height tools — Level · 256 / 101, ≈0.64 · Amount once per stroke against the stroke's starting heights, the (64c + 26Σ) / (64 + 26n) smooth — each stroke re-baking the light layer, which turned out to be the height's forward slope clamp(H − (Hright + Hbelow) / 2 + 16, 0, 21), the 0..21 around 16 that map.md had measured and could not name; the decoration stamp with the position's remainder in four-unit nibbles, the scatter at Density percent per empty cell, the delete. The terrain goes back at eight bits whatever it came in at, as the exe writes it, so a blank template widens at load. Areas: a click makes an AdvArea of radius 300 or a 400-unit square, named Unnamed through the advarea key and written as a type-0 group; the edit tool moves by the centre and resizes by the rim or eight grips, edges swapping when they cross, with AdvArea.ini beside the palette showing the name, the centre and the shape. The right button turns the selected object — the raw vector world − pos, or its opposite under Shift, unnormalised as the exe keeps it — picks the layer under the pointer for the terrain tool, reads the level for Set height, and steps back to Edit objects from the rest. The keys are the shipped DATA\vxAction.xml, which nobody had read as data: +/- the brush, Up/Down the tool, Left/Right fold and unfold, 1..0 the roots, Esc tooldefault, Del, ` hides the windows; nextwnd and compilescript are named and not built (undo, redo, objprops and, in the end, both of those were, and are built below — every action the file names now has a body but prevwnd, which the file gives no key). And two things that were not the editor's but stood in its way: the live view drew none of the map's 47,930 decorations — a tree is scenery the simulation never loads, so WorldView draws the layer beside the objects now, sorted into the same bins — and the six grid layers had no writer, which they have (core::write_grid, byte for byte over all 772 in the install). The property sheet's reading settled three of map.md's unknowns on the way: flags bit 26 is Messenger, UnitFlags 0x20000 is Unit doesn't eat, bit 16 is the editor's own selection and never in a file. Left labelled then, and taken in the third slice below: the re-stamp of object and decoration masks after a terrain stroke, the passable_water rule in 0x00547090, the decoration stamp's footprint. Undo, the ground mesh and the property sheet are taken below.

The third slice: the passability layer, read and proven. The original never rebuilds Terrain.pass.grid at save time; its editor keeps the layer incrementally, one rebuild over a rectangle after every stroke, placement and removal (0x00547700), and what ships is what those calls left. That rebuild is now transcribed — core/world/editor.hpp, “passability”, with the addresses — and the transcription is proven the strongest way the corpus allows: immap passability rebuilds a map's layer from nothing and diffs it against the stored bytes, and all 23 maps a player can open come back identical, every cell (tests/test_corpus_passability.py). What the reading found on the way settles a question pass.md had carried since Part 0. A mask is stamped upside down and through the projection. 0x00546a80 does not lay a .pass on the ground as a square: for each set cell it forms a screen point — x the anchor's plus 16 · col − 1016, y the anchor's projected row plus (1032 − 16 · row) · 181 / 256 — and asks the camera's inverse (0x006243b0, now a template over any height function in sim/projection.hpp) for the world point under it, height and all. The row term is negated, so file row 63 lands one cell below the anchor: this is the whole of the “vertical anchor at 64.5 rather than 63.5” discrepancy the spec measured and could not explain, and it is the one reading that makes 615 buildings on Numantia land on their stored cells. On a slope the rows spread and the stamp fills the column between consecutive cells (0x00546cc0). The terrain's rules (0x00547090): the layer under (x + 32, y + 32), the same half-cell bias IsPointInWater uses; passable="0" blocks; passable_water — deep water and nothing else — blocks the rim of its 64-unit cell wherever the terrain across that edge is not deep (0x00545100, the diagonal too at a corner); any other layer blocks where one of its eight 16-unit neighbours samples deep water, so a lake carries a one-cell shore on both sides of its edge. And the frame every rebuild ends by re-imposing (0x00544bd0), which is why every shipped layer is blocked along its edges: two columns either side, a top band of four rows on flat ground, and a bottom strip from where project(y1) − 287 unprojects — 255 + 32, the tallest ground plus a cell — 28 rows on a flat map and lower where the edge rises. The decorations' positions are their cell plus four-unit nibbles (the table 0x005441a0 builds), and Crops1 was the reminder that the entity a class stands as is the map's season's, which the tool and the editor now read from game.xml. Wired: a terrain stroke rebuilds its rectangle; a water leaf first levels the ground under the brush's square to sea level, clears the decorations there and rebuilds it (0x004b3dcd — the Water group does none of this); a decoration stamped writes its footprint and one deleted or written over gives it back through a rebuild of its flat extent (0x00547a30); an object placed, moved or deleted the same (0x004a3fc0). End to end, from a scripted editor run: rock strokes, a deleted house and deleted trees saved from the app, and the saved layer rebuilds identically from nothing. Labelled and not reproduced: 0x00547c00, which widens a stroke's rebuild to a shipyard's extent when one stands near — on a consistent layer the same bits. Found, and taken into the simulation: at match start, outside the editor, the game replaces the Mutable settlements with their templates and rebuilds the whole layer once (0x00552e95), and its editor rebuilds the whole layer when it opens a map (0x00494cca) — so the grid a match is played on was never the layer the map ships, and every session here played through the templated towns' walls for as long as it adopted it. The masks reach the core now (PassMaskResolver, beside the entity resolver; SessionInputs::masks, decor, terrain_table, decor_table), start_match rebuilds the layer over the world once the templates have landed, the save's session section carries the result (version 2, put back ahead of the movement section so no restored route is sent back to the pathfinder), and the editor rebuilds on open. The proof is the same figure from the other side: imrun now prints how many cells the rebuilt layer differs from the shipped one in — 0 on every authored map, so nothing an adventure plays changed by a bit, and 41,120 on Crossroads, 58,195 on Balcans, 30,690 on Island War, which are the twenty, sixteen and ten templates' footprints. 25 of 25 saves still round-trip, the trap sweep still traps nothing. Not taken then, taken below: the template's ground. Not taken: the catapult's un-stamp when it becomes a wreck (0x004e1c38), which this engine has no wreck for. Packs/RandomMapSettlements.bfhp is the one layer that does not rebuild identically — 694 of 4,194,304 cells, wall rows one cell lower and rims where nothing stands — and reads as an earlier editor's stamp, the way the blank templates' one-cell frame does. And the engine's own ObstructionGrid::stamp, laid flat and the way up the file is stored, is now known to be wrong and is called by nothing; its comment says so and points here.

The template's ground. 0x00590010 — the routine that lays one settlement template — does four things to the map's layers after its members land, and all four are read and reproduced (stamp_template_ground, run by start_match per template in the order they landed, and again by a load from the stamps the session section now carries, version 3). It bulldozes every decoration within 0.6 of the diagonal of the members' box grown by 256 (0x0058db30, the exe's own log line is “Trees bulldozed”). It copies the template map's terrain onto the map wherever the template paints one — RandomMapSettlements leaves 15, Invalid, everywhere it does not, and paints roads and paving in patches with holes the map shows through — and sets the height under each copied cell to sea level (0x00590540). Over the box grown by 512, where the template paints nothing, a deep-water cell no longer eligible to stay deep, or a cell within four of deep water (0x005468d0), becomes shallow water at sea level: the shore is taken out from under the town. Then the slope limiter (0x00548830 queues every height cell of the box grown by 288, 0x00548070 walks it): a cell whose four-neighbour stands more than 20 higher or lower pulls it to within 20, unless the neighbour is a levelled one and this cell is not, re-queueing both. So a town on Island War's 115-high islands sits at sea level with a 20-per-cell slope up to the island around it — read three times because it is hard to believe, and it is what the original does; the light is re-baked over the result (0x00544e10) there too, so the holes inside a town shade the way they do here. Reading, labelled: the original's box is over the members' entity image rectangles, which the core does not carry; the box here is over the painted cells 4-connected to a painted cell within 256 of a member, which on the shipped pack is the template's own ground and never a neighbour's. The app keeps its own copy of the layers for the view, so it takes the session's ground over the rectangles the session reports and re-bakes its light. imrun prints it: Island War, 10 templates — 2,697 cells copied, 11,444 height cells levelled, 19,407 sloped, 220 decorations bulldozed, 164 shore cells. The passability rebuild that follows reads the copied terrain, which is why Island War's grid moved from 32,589 cells off the shipped layer to 32,773.

The ground is a mesh. Part 2 drew the ground flat and every object at its flat row: Terrain.height.grid was loaded and ignored, because displacing rigid cells by their own height tears the ground apart, and the mesh that would not was out of scope. It is in scope now, and it was not the reading it looked like: the height is one pixel of screen per unit, which 0x005c11a0 subtracts verbatim (projection.md had it since Part 5), and the layer holds the height at 32-unit corners, which is how the simulation's bilinear sample already reads it. MapRenderer::compose_ground draws every cell as four 32-unit quads whose corners carry the height; x passes through the projection unchanged, so a quad's sides stay vertical and each screen column is the tile's column stretched between the top and bottom edges interpolated at that column — the bilinear surface the sample answers, seamless between neighbours. Everything standing on the ground is lifted by the height under it (Camera::project, the map view's sprites), a click finds the ground it is on by the scan 0x006243b0 runs (Camera::unproject, the sim's inverse at the renderer's 46/64), a view centres on a point's lifted position, and twelve rows below the view are composed because the tallest ground lifts 255 rows into it. Alesia's hill fort stands on its hill and the soldiers follow the ridge; Island War's towns sit in the basins the template ground makes of them. Not read: how the original tessellates — 32 or 64 units, and whether it interpolates the light across a quad. And the fog of war follows the mesh: it was one picture stretched flat over the view, which a lifted ground slides out from under, and it is applied the way the original applies it now (0x00604a40) — MapRenderer::shade_ground darkens every composed ground pixel by the fog sampled bilinearly at the world point the mesh put under it, re-shaded when the grid moves, once a tick, and WorldView darkens every sprite by the fog at the point it stands on. The flat overlay and the UI renderer's path for it are gone.

The brushes' undo. The original's undo manager (0x00499e50, one per editor session) is read and reproduced in core::edit::UndoStack: a record is a rectangle of up to four layers — terrain, height, decorations, passability, chosen by flag bits — copied before a brush touches them (0x0049a850), tagged with a generation; a stroke's press opens a generation and each step of its drag adds to it, so one Ctrl+Z takes back one stroke, whatever its length. Undo (0x0049a6a0) walks the newest generation, copies what the layers hold now into a redo record of the same rectangle and flags, and writes the snapshot back; redo is the mirror; a new snapshot drops the redo list; records go oldest first past four megabytes (0x00494628). Only the brushes record — the terrain tool all four layers over radius + 2 cells (0x004b4108), the decoration tools decorations and passability over five cells whatever the brush (0x0048d9c1), the height tools terrain, height and passability over their disc — and the default tool answers the command with nothing (0x004c2626 asks the tool first), which is why object and area edits are not undoable there and are not here. The light is not in a record; it is the height's function and is re-baked over the rectangle an undo returns. Found on the way: vxAction.xml spells its modifiers ctrl="on", which the generic yes/no reader did not know, so every shortcut had loaded unmodified since the second slice — Ctrl+Z was a plain Z and Alt+Enter a plain Enter — and the scripted --input now takes key:ctrl+z. End to end: two rock strokes, undo, redo, undo, undo, redo leave exactly one, and the layer they leave rebuilds identically from nothing.

The property sheet. AdvObjProps.ini is six dialog sections in one file, and 0x004ad6f0 opens the one for the selection's kind — unit, hero, wagon, building, or the multiple-units sheet — docked in the AdvObjPropsParent frame; a decoration or an area gets none. The right button on the selected object and Alt+Enter (objprops) open it here as there. Two things the toolkit does by the Id alone had to be read first, because the file declares nothing else: an id with 0x03 in bits 16..23 is a tab button and switches to the tab in its low half, hiding every widget whose top byte is another tab's (0x0066d2a0; every dialog opens on tab 1, 0x0066d5f0), and one with 0x02 there is a radio button clearing its group (0x0066d410) — both are core::ui::Dialog's now, and AdvArea.ini's pair is served by the second. And the tab row is placed by #%TOR_GEN% - 1 * 92 + left(TabsFrame)#, which put every tab 86 pixels off the left edge under the C precedence interface-ini.md had inferred: the exe's evaluator (0x00667e10, the loop at 0x006681fc) is flat, left to right, no parentheses, a division by zero 0, and it is now. The fields are wired from the handler (0x004add20) and the setters beside it, each applied to the world as it is typed and laid over the authored record at the save: the owner (and the owner's bit in flags), the script name as the type-0 group, the display name, health as a percentage of maxhealth, stamina clamped to maxstamina, the level 1..1000 (the original writes the level's experience threshold; this engine's unit keeps a level, and the record's Level is the level less one), the five check boxes — Template unit bit 27, Messenger bit 26, Party bit 19, No AI UnitFlags 0x40000, and Unit doesn't eat, which is the inverse of UnitFlags 0x20000 (0x004aa2f4 negates the bit for the box, 0x004ab130 clears it when ticked), a polarity map.md had backwards — a wagon's cargo and its resource, the groups tab (every group in the combo, the object's own in the list, Add on a picked or typed name, Remove; an EDIT-style combobox takes typed text now), the settlement tab on a building (name, owner, gold, food, population, ceiling, the sentries), the four inventory combos over ITEMS.XML, the hero's five skill rows (a combo over the twenty-five skills and a 0..10 level each, the record's hs* attributes kept in their places; the original applies them at close, this as they change), and the visuals tab's icon — editorini/combo/uniticons.ini's [FillCombo], name to path, the path being the object's Icon attribute, previewed in the sheet; the info bar does not read the attribute here, where the original's Unit::SetIcon (0x005dc5c0) would show it. Proven by script on Numantia: a hero's sheet edited and the container written back with healthperc="50", Level="6", bit 26 raised, bit 17 lowered, a typed group holding it, its first slot swapped, its owner moved, a skill at 3 and one swapped, an icon chosen; a town hall's settlement with its gold and population changed. Left labelled: the multiple-units sheet's inventory list (this editor selects one object), the Inn button, and the window's placement and its survival across a tool change, which are this engine's (the original keeps a list of open sheets and re-focuses one for the same set). Object edits are not undoable, as in the original.

The Adventure Palette.
