AdvExplorerDlg.ini is the editor's other window — the exe builds one per container flavour (0x0047b000, 0x0047b420, 0x0047b81b) on the same file with a different root — and its Browser is a tree the managers fill as they load, each node registered with the dialog it opens (0x00463250: the node path, the .ini, the label): the root (AdvAdventure.ini or AdvScenario.ini), Players with a leaf per slot (AdvPlayer.ini), Players diplomacy, Conversations/<name>, Notes/<name>, Sequences/<name>, Item types, Settlements (AdvResources.ini), and Map (AdvCurMap.ini) with Areas, Named units, Groups (AdvGrpProps.ini), the item holders and the outposts under it. The window opens beside the palette with that tree over what the session holds, and a node docks its dialog where SettingsPosCtl stands. Wired: a group's dialog — rename (the world's table has no rename, so the new name is interned and the members move, and the document renames the element), Delete, the members as class at (x, y), Select, Select All, Properties (the member's sheet), Remove, Add selection — an area (chosen in the Edit-areas tool, whose dialog it already has) and a named unit (selected and looked at). And the document dialogs are filled and written — not by a writer per document, which would have to carry every attribute this engine does not read (AllowedRaces, bonus, the 128-digit relations), but by patching the container's own text in place (core::xml_patch: an attribute's value where it stands, a new one on its own line, an element cut out, one laid in; MapEdits::documents carries the texts by path): the root's AdvAdventure/AdvScenario (name, author, description, starting map and player, race; the scenario's victory condition and threshold, single player only, the map's two fog flags, the minimap image), a player's AdvPlayer (name, race, control, the colour square, the starting point set to the view's centre and gone to), AdvCurMap (name, display name, description, the fog flags, persist state, the start point), a note's AdvNotes (title, text, map, icon from note_icons.ini, show on minimap, its location set, viewed and cleared, and the note deleted), and a sequence's element with its source in the SrcEdit (Courier, typed into, Enter a line break; the caret is not drawn and the text does not scroll, so the editor serves short scripts), written back to the .vs the element names, and Compile running this engine's own parser and compiler over the session's host surface and printing the first failure's line and message, or OK. Proven by script: the adventure renamed, a player renamed with its start set, a note retitled and placed and another deleted, all read back out of the written container with every other byte as it was. The diplomacy sheet too (AdvDiplomacy.ini, read at 0x0046636b): sixteen rows of a colour, a name button that makes that player the one whose words the rows show, and the toggles over the word's bits — cease fire bit 0 (unticking it clears the four and unpresses the other three), share view bit 4, share support bit 2, share control bit 5, each ticked with bit 0; a sixth shows whether the two directions agree, and the shared-victory toggle reaches nothing in the handler — written back as player<p>.xml's 128-digit relations. Its buttons are authored at RectWH = x, y, 0, 0, which the toolkit sizes from the bitmap's cell; Dialog::fit_to_images does the same, labelled. The Settlements node's AdvResources.ini lists the settlements under its four filter radios and edits the chosen one's gold, food and population, with Equalize writing the number to every one listed; an outpost's AdvOutpost.ini (a leaf per outpost under Map) edits its name, owner, gold and food and views it — both through the world's economy and the anchor's settlement element at the save (the Level edit is shown and not wired: the settlement level is not modelled here). An item holder (a leaf per CVXItemHolder under Map) opens AdvItemHolder.ini: its class, position, script name and four slots over the item catalogue, viewed and deleted. The container's own item types (itemsCustom.xml, which the session now loads into the catalogue after ITEMS.XML — the campaigns' druid ash was unmintable for as long as it did not) are leaves under Item types, and AdvItems.ini edits one: id, name, description, image and sound from the combo files, Important, the wear bonus (armor standing for slash and pierce together, as every shipped element keeps them), the use count and the use script with Compile, and Delete; use_script is stored with the document's own escaping (\n, \l, \g, \a, \'), read off the two campaign documents that hold one. And the conversations: AdvConverse.ini's three tabs over one .conv.xml (found by its name among the map's and the root's documents, never by suffix) — the phrases listed as actor: text and made, deleted and moved (the patcher indexes siblings, phrase[n], and swaps two), the chosen phrase's label, actor (declared or typed), choice text, text, follow-up mode and list, the three scripts with Compile, and comments, and the conversation's name, startup mode and list, comments and restore view; Delete conversation empties the document. And the overlay-text labels: the Overlay text tab of AdvCurMap.ini and AdvScenario.ini over labels.xml (the handler at 0x004a1640) — the labels listed, the chosen one's text typed and its position shown as <x>,<y>, New laying <New label> at the centre of the view, Delete, and Place opening MapPlace.ini: the minimap on its INACTIVE button, the labels drawn over it as the exe's one drawer draws them (0x0049f7a0, centred on the point, kept inside, tahoma16b), the one being placed in the screen's yellow, and a click putting it there. Read on the way: the labels are the editor's alone — the loader's two callers and the drawer's two are all editor code, and the game's screens never open the document (map.md, "labels.xml"). The toolkit learned that a widget carrying a composed picture takes the click and claims the point for its window, and --input learned press:<widget>, press:<list>@<row> and press:<list>@=<text>, so a scripted proof names what it presses rather than where. Proven by script on the Tutorial and Crossroads: a label deleted, one made, retitled and placed by a click on the minimap, read back out of the written container with every other byte as it was; Crossroads' empty <root> takes its first. And a player's AI combo, as 0x004743b0 fills it: None, then %s1(%s2) rows of each data/ai/ subdirectory (less default and names beginning with _, the first letter kept and the rest lowered) with easy, medium, hard; a row carries its profile and a difficulty of 0, 1 or 2, which the apply (0x00475884) writes to AI and difficulty. Proven by script on Crossroads: Defensive(medium) chosen for the first player and player0.xml read back with AI="DEFENSIVE" and difficulty="1". And the New leaves: the exe registers one browseable per manager through the same routine every other node goes through (0x00472385 makes the notes', with AdvNotes.ini and the translated label), so New note, New sequence, New conversation and New item type stand last under their branches, and choosing one makes the thing — a note with the shipped attributes, empty, in the map's Notes.xml; a sequence element naming the first free seq<k>.vs, made with one blank line so it compiles to nothing rather than starting as no source; a cnv<k>.conv.xml with its root alone; an item with its <bonus/> child, the patcher laying an element with a child in — named after the leaf and numbered past a taken name, the tree rebuilt and the new node opened. A note's id, a sequence's name and an item's id are renamed from their edits now (the handler's node follows the rename, so the next edit patches the renamed element), and a sequence has its Delete. The tree lists notes and sequences from the documents, as it did conversations, so what this run made is listed. This engine's, labelled: which document a new thing goes to — the exe keeps a manager per document and a New leaf under each, where this tree lists the map's and the root's together, so the map's it is. Found on the way: the patcher counted an indexed sibling twice when it did not close in its own tag (<sequence ...></sequence>), so sequence[1] was sequence[2]; fixed and tested. Proven by script on Crossroads: one of each made, the sequence renamed, the container written and run headless — the new sequence starts and finishes, no traps. And the Maps tab of AdvAdventure.ini and AdvCurMap.ini, as 0x0046fac0 fills it: a row per map, its map.xml name, the map being edited marked with EditorRes/curmap.bmp, and StartMapCombo offering the same maps and writing start_map. New (0x0046f380) takes the first number from 1 no map has and copies the blank template's Maps/1 in as Maps/<n> — the exe copies Packs/newmap/Maps/1, this engine reads it out of Packs/newmap.BFHP; Import adventure maps and Import scenario map open OpenDlg.ini over Adventures/ or Scenarios/ (0x004aedd0, bfhp) and copy the chosen container's maps in under fresh numbers; Delete leaves the chosen map out of the container written (MapEdits::removed_directories, exercised by immap rewrite --drop-map and a corpus test on the conquest); Edit makes the chosen map the one being edited. That last is a Save first: the exe works on a mounted copy of the adventure (currentadv.bfhp in the installation's root) and switching is just opening another of its maps (0x0046f050), where this engine writes nothing before Save — so it writes a working copy of its own in the temporary directory, every edit of the run in it, and opens the editor again on that copy's map; Save then writes from the copy under the original's name, which the shipped-map guard still knows. Readings, labelled: that the current map cannot be deleted or re-edited (refused here); that a deleted map's Local/<language>/Maps/<n> translations stay and an imported map's are not copied. Proven by script: a blank map made in the Tutorial, switched to, and the container written with both — Maps/1 byte for byte the source's, Maps/2 the template's with its terrain widened and its passability rebuilt, both running headless with no traps; the conquest's Maps/4 deleted and Crossroads imported as Maps/1, the container written holding seven maps, the imported one running. Two things the windows needed on the way: every editor window is dragged by its caption strip now (Move, %ID_MOVE%), its docked dialog following, and a dialog closes wherever it stands in the stack rather than only from the top. This engine's, labelled: the window's place (top left, narrowed to what stands left of the palette; the original sizes it by its grips). nextwnd is built below. The Collapse button is built now, on every editor window: the palette's handler (0x004a25b0) keeps the height and sets it to 0xac, hiding what stands below the strip, and the next press puts it back; here the window is laid out again at that height, its docked pane parked out of sight, the same 172 pixels for every window, labelled. Found on the way: in the editor a release went to the top window rather than to the one its press went to, so a button on a window under a docked pane — the explorer's Close or Collapse — never fired. Proven by script on Numantia: Bonus1 renamed and a member removed, the container written back with Bonus1X holding six.

The map writer is in. core::write_map_objects puts map.obj.xml back byte for byte on all 28 shipped documents — the reader now keeps every attribute of every element verbatim and in order, because the original serialises each class's own attributes between num and player (a hero's hs* skills, a unit's Level, a wagon's amount, none of which this engine reads yet), and a writer that emitted only what it understood would have stripped them on the first save. The typed fields are written back into that list, a fresh object gets the shipped tail and nothing invented, MapObjectList gained the mutable tables, next_num and erase. gamedata::write_map_container copies a container with one document regenerated; immap rewrite is its front end and imrun reaches the same hash over the original and the copy. Recorded in map.md, "Writing", with what the original does for a fresh element left as unknown.

The Switch blinks, and the trigger is read: 0x006c0f30 turns it on when the selection's head is a hero the local player controls with skill points to spend — 0x0052da80 is AvailableSkillPoints, the level's allowance less the points spent — and the timer message at 0x006c1019 draws 1 - row every BlinkTime milliseconds while it is on. The skills switch flashes until the points are spent.

territory/@interface is the territory's race. 0x00506560 keeps the attribute only within 0 ≤ v < 8, the eight races, and all seven shipped territories carry the index their bonus sequence's r<Race> name spells — rIberia is 3, rGaul 0. It names the interface skin, INFOBAR_<RACE>.INI, which is where the attribute's name comes from, and it is now what the human's race is when a conquest starts from the territory; the bonus name is the fallback for a file that has none. Closed in adventure.md.

Inline markup renders. There are two tags, <color r g b> and <imagetransp path>, and gbr.exe composes with no others — its own tag-shaped strings are these and XML. The command tooltip is now built the way 0x004f3590 and 0x004ea790 build it: the title through the table's template (%s1 the rollover, %s2 the key in upper case, the hotkey icon between), the description, then one icon and number per non-zero cost, white while the selection's settlement can pay and red when it cannot, the stamina icon becoming Loyality.bmp at ten; then the verifier's reason, which the shipped scripts end with \n<color 255 0 0>. core/ui/markup.hpp parses a paragraph into lines of coloured runs and images; a colour holds across a break, an image stands in the line like a glyph and the line grows to hold it, imagetransp keys at the top-left pixel. The bonus HelpText in the setup shows its three icons the same way. Not read: where the original puts an image vertically, and whether a colour survives a break — every shipped composer resets to white before its own.

The Statistics screen. STATISTICS.INI, the end-of-match report behind ENDGAMEMENU.INI's Statistics button, which this engine had disabled. Read off the exe (built at 0x006fc800, one fill per tab): a row per player in the game's table, the colour and the name; four tabs of three columns, each writing the title, the headings and per row the value and its share of the column's maximum, the maximum's row in yellow; Resources is gold and food spent, Gold production is From taxes, Other and Captured, Units is killed, lost and the most at once, Scores is the military rating, gold_used / 500 and that plus the power census times the rating over 100 (0x0056a260, 0x0056a2a0, 0x0056a970 — the screen's own arithmetic, dividing by 500 where GetTeamOverallScore divides by 1000); Game time as hh:mm:ss. The numbers are the player's statistics record, whose fields both serialisers name and this engine had five of: the other eight are on PlayerScoreCounters now, saved (section version 23, state vector 18) and not hashed, since nothing a script reaches reads them. Each is written where the exe writes it: gold and food spent with the command core's charge and back with its refund (0x004df1a7, 0x004df49f), Settlement::GoldSpent and GoldConverted (0x005c29d3) on the owner's counts, a settlement's warehouse gold moving between the two owners' captured at SetPlayer (0x005c4f03), one killed beside every counted kill (0x00511905), and the unit counts — a Military unit produced for every owner it takes (Unit::SetPlayer, 0x005dc3f0, gated on the is-heir-of at 0x00540130), the most at once raised as the standing count exceeds it, one lost for every one that leaves the world (the destructor, 0x005db4c0) — through a seam the world did not have: WorldObserver, which the match installs in start and the world tells at set_owner, at the spawns that give an owner and at despawn, with a census of the soldiers already standing when the match starts, kept through configure and setup_match, which used to wipe the rows. Readings, labelled: From taxes is credited on every production tick, the writer found (0x005c424f) storing a new settlement's starting gold rather than adding income; a spawn template counts nothing until it is in play; which players are rows is read as participates. Proven: the counters follow a synthetic match in test_match.cpp (produced, most at once, lost, the same owner again booking nothing, the allied kill not counted, all eight through a save); imrun prints them beside the soldiers standing, and on Numantia the two agree at turn 0 and turn 30 for both participants; the save sweep holds at 25 of 25; and the screen, opened over a Balcans save at turn 150, shows the eight players' taxes with their shares.

The main menu's Tips, and the Credits. The Tips frame the main menu had hidden shows CURRENTLANG\TIPS.XML's tips as the exe shows them (0x006d8050): the next in order until every one has been shown, then one drawn at random and never the same twice running, the place kept in settings.ini under [Tips] as the original keeps it; Next steps, More Info opens the help at the tip's link. And the Credits button the menu had disabled: CREDITSMENU.INI names its own source and speed in its [CreditsDescription] section, and CurrentLang/credits.txt rolls up through the Credits block at PixPerSec, the toolkit having learned a text_scroll on a multiline text; Close, or the last line passing, returns to the main menu (the end is this engine's reading, labelled). The tips document has a reader of its own beside the help's, tested.

A conquest won. When a mission's win leaves no territory in state 0, the exe shows CONQUESTVICTORY.INI with Congratulations!!! You have been victorious. and OK goes to the main menu (0x0074ae84: game type 2, the mission won, 0x00501110 finding every territory taken); this engine does the same where it used to reopen the campaign map. And --declare-won, imrun's knob, on the app: the human's match ends won on the first frame it runs, so what follows a win — the end-game menu, the campaign carry — can be driven without winning. Driven that way from the front over a conquest with one territory left: the carry is written and the campaign map reopens on it, because the victory sequence that would have taken the territory did not run; the box itself waits for a mission won for real.

The Great Battles, all twelve. The main menu's button opens PreAdventureMenu.ini in the exe (0x006e944a), two lists under two headings — The Great Victories of Rome over Adventures/GreatBattles/ and Rome's Enemies Fight for Freedom over Adventures/GreatChallenges/ — with the chosen adventure's picture, name and description beside and a difficulty combo; this engine had opened AdventureMenu.ini over the first folder alone, so the six Great Challenges could be reached from no screen. Built in the Great Battles mode, the screen's other widgets (ListAll, Conquests, Custom, StartConquest) hidden as the exe's mode calls hide them. And the difficulty reached no match before: GetDifficulty's 136 readers saw 0 whatever the options said. The options' setting, or what the adventure and campaign screens' combos last chose, is set on the match at start now, labelled.

Change player, and the number that proved the reading. The main menu's last disabled button. PROFILE.INI lists the profiles in one pane and the selected one's career in the other — twelve lines, and not one of them is stored: they are aggregated on every open from a [game<n>] journal the executable appends to after every match, 57 records in the shipped profile. docs/formats/profile.md is the specification, core/game/profile.hpp the reader, and both rest on one comparison. The hash comes back. [Player] hash is a rolling fold over the journal, and recomputing it from the 57 records — 1,824 folds — returns the file's own -1650049073 bit for bit. That single number settles the record's field order, which fields fold and in what order, the seed (0x48564849) and the nine-slot nation histogram at once; nothing else in the specification is separately evidenced and nothing else needs to be. It is also what the page said had stopped this slice, and it stopped it for the wrong reason: the hash matters to a journal this engine writes, and this engine reads profiles and does not write them. The decision the page asked for was that one, and it was a decision rather than a discovery. Three things the fold turned out to be. A string field folds a constant, the same for every value — mix(0x0EA75617) is exactly the 0x75B00047 the binary xors in, which is what says the step is an ordinary fold of a literal and not a hash of the bytes, so two journals differing only in a unit's name hash alike. Eight of the record's thirty fields are never folded, poser_score and both damage counters among them. And the nation histogram has nine counters behind a guard that admits ten values, so a record claiming nation 9 counts towards no nation; nothing shipped does, and it is reproduced rather than repaired. A defect in the shipped data, and a rank nobody can reach. CONST.INI's [Ranks] carries the comment the ranks MUST be ordered in ascending order on their points and then RankPoints10 = 2 where ascending order wants something above 200. The walk stops at the first row above the rating, so every rating from 200 to 299 answers Legend and Hero, row 9, names no rank in the retail game. Kept; a test walks a thousand ratings and insists it stays unreachable. What this engine does differently, and why. docs/legal.md rule 1 keeps the installation read-only, so the profiles under it are read, shown and playable and cannot be renamed or deleted — the two buttons are disabled for such a row rather than failing when pressed — and New, Rename and Delete work on Saves/Profiles/, beside the settings file the engine already keeps there. A renamed copy that shadows the installed one was the other reading and is not taken: the directory is the name — the original's list and its caption read the same string, which its scan takes from the directory entry, and [Player] name is a separate field the setup screen uses, which the shipped profile proves by spelling them Nome and Angel — so a renamed copy has a different key and the list would show the same player twice. Proven. The reference reader (src/imperivm/formats/profile.py) and the engine are two implementations of one specification and tests/test_corpus_profile.py compares them field by field against the installation on every run, with the file's own hash= as the third opinion; imcheck profile is the seam. The unit tests keep the arithmetic on records written from the specification — rule 1 forbids pasting a retail journal into this tree, and the first draft of them did, which the CRLF in the shipped file is the only reason check_fixtures.py did not say so. Driven from the front on a retail install: the screen opens on the installed profile with its twelve lines in Italian, a profile is made, renamed, selected and deleted, the choice survives a restart, and Profiles/Nome/player.ini is byte-identical afterwards. Found on the way: the heading table has fourteen slots and the screen draws twelve — slots 4 and 8 hold OUT FOR LUNCH and OUT FOR DINNER, are translated along with the rest and are never shown — and ChangePlayer is 0x1011, not the 0x1003 a first reading took it for, which is Multiplayer. The wrong one is silent, because the button it names is disabled.

Lockstep: two peers, one command stream. Part 7's first slice, and the one that needed no socket. The determinism harness proves the simulation is a function of its seed and its schedule — and it feeds it no input at all, which is the half that matters, because a desync in a real match is almost never the world drifting on its own. It is two peers resolving one right click differently, and the whole order-resolution path — the per-actor candidate walk, the share-control filter, the verifier — runs only when somebody clicks. sim/netcmds.hpp is the stream (an actor list, a target that is always a point and sometimes an object, two booleans and an issuer, which is what GROUP_UNITSOUT.VS's own signature says an order is), the canonical order every peer applies it in, and the fold; conformance::check_lockstep is the same comparison the self-check makes, with both runs driven. The channel had to come from the driver. The command queue deliberately hashes nothing, so two peers handed different orders have identical world hashes until those orders take effect — possibly many turns later, in a system with no idea why. That is exactly why the shipped build hashed the command stream as its own channel: netcmds is non-zero in seven of the nine dumps and zero in precisely the two whose cmdsprocessed is zero. So Driver::drive returns the stream's hash through that turn and the harness files it under Channel::netcmds, where compare_traces already reports it. The fold is not the original's and cannot be — no dump prints a command payload, which is the same fact that makes the dumps unreplayable — and an undriven run files zero, so no golden moved. The ordering rule is this engine's, and labelled. By issuer, then by the order's own sequence number, then by arrival — a total order every peer computes without talking. state-vector.md records the original logging CmdNotUI, idx: n before each command and says the index "correlates with player slot in the samples but is not confirmed"; that is consistent with sorting by player and is not proof of it. What correctness needs is only that the rule be total and computed, and a rule that depended on arrival order would be neither. Two false passes, both of which looked exactly like success. The check reported two peers agreeing on every turn while driving an empty match, twice, for different reasons. Crossroads is a skirmish map and owns no units at turn 0 — every object a player owns there is a building or scenery, none with a ground block — so every order resolved to nothing. Then, on a map with armies standing on it, every order still resolved to nothing: imconform's scenario had never loaded DATA/COMMANDS/*.XML, so the candidate list said move and the table had no row of that name, 982 times, without a word. Both are why the tool prints what it drove and why tests/test_corpus_lockstep.py asserts on commands queued rather than on ok. Zama: 10 orders, 36 commands, nothing refused or unresolved, twenty turns agreed.

nextwnd, and the check that could not see the thing it exists to see. Ctrl+Tab in the editor, the last thing in this part that is not online. The original walks two lists (0x00493a00): it finds the active window in the first and takes the node after it, falling through to the head of the second at the end and back to the head of the first at the end of that, and a window in neither starts the walk at the head of the first. The two lists are filled by the constructors of two different window classes and the executable carries no RTTI, so which of this editor's windows would sit in which list was not read; the order here is the tools palette, then the others as they were opened, labelled as this engine's. prevwnd is the other half of the pair: DATA\vxAction.xml names it among its actions and gives it no <shortcut>, so nothing on the original can reach it either — read, recorded, left unbound. What it actually wanted was a stack that reorders. The dialog stack always had a z-order — later dialogs draw over earlier ones and the hit test searches from the top down — but nothing had ever moved a dialog within it, so there was no raise_dialog. There is one now, a rotate that keeps the rest in order and carries each dialog's composited layer with it, and the docked pane follows the window it is docked in. Two things it broke on the way: the tools palette was found as dialog 0 in four places, which held only while nothing reordered, and a stack index held across a reorder means another dialog afterwards — the index of the press a release is looking for is one, so it is resolved to its dialog and found again. And the raise had a second half nobody had told it about. menu_handlers_ is a vector of one handler per open dialog, indexed by its position in the stack — open pushes, close erases at the same index — and nothing had ever reordered the stack, so nothing had ever had to rotate it. The first nextwnd did not, and every handler ended one place out: after a Ctrl+Tab a click on the tools palette ran the explorer's handler. The screenshots that proved the z-order could not see it, because looking at which window is in front is not the same as clicking one; a printf at the dispatch said it in one line (dialog=MapToolsDlg handler=AdvExplorerDlg), and says handler=MapToolsDlg now. There is one way to reorder the stack from the app — raise_menu, which rotates both — and the invariant is written where the vector is declared rather than where it is used. compilescript, the last name in the key map. Ctrl+Enter, and the one action in DATA\vxAction.xml that this page had never named. Two of the editor's dialogs carry a Compile button — a sequence's Source tab and an item type's use script — and both already answered it with this engine's own compiler over the session's host surface, so the key only had to find the button and say it was pressed, through the same path a click takes. It shows Ok or the first failure's line and message: line 2: unexpected character, over Ambush's own source with three characters typed into it. Reading, labelled: the original's editor looks its shortcut up and then asks the active window what has the focus, skipping the action for three widget classes (0x00493c62, 0x00493c7f, 0x00493c9d), which is the rule this engine already had — so Ctrl+Enter does not fire while the script edit has the focus, in either engine, and Escape leaves the edit first. Whether the multi-line SrcEdit is one of those three classes was not read. The check that let two fixtures in. check_fixtures.py is what enforces docs/legal.md rule 1, and it read git ls-files — tracked files. A fixture in a file that has been written but not yet added is invisible to it, so it can only report a violation after the commit that made it, never refuse one. That is not hypothetical: both of this session's retail fixtures, a match journal and the rank table, passed it on the way in and failed it on the run after. It reads the working tree now (--cached --others --exclude-standard), and a fixture in an untracked file is refused, which is the case it was written for.

07

Campaigns, saves and multiplayer

campaigns and saves done; multiplayer doneLeft the rated drop path, and polish

The campaign layer is decoded. Three container flavours share one layout and are told apart only by game.xml's game_type — scenario 0, adventure 1, conquest 2 — and the editor's three blank templates carry exactly those three values, which is as clean a confirmation as the format offers. Every shipped adventure holds exactly one map, so an adventure is one mission in a container and the campaign it belongs to is not declared inside it at all: gbr.exe enumerates adventures/GreatBattles/ and adventures/GreatChallenges/ and orders by the 1_…6_ filename prefix. The one conquest holds seven maps plus a document no other container has — territories.xml, a seven-node graph, each node naming a map, its neighbours and a bonus. CampaignSystem reads it, hashes its mutable half, and is in the session's system order; its three entry points are in the registration manifest. See docs/formats/adventure.md.

Saves round-trip on every shipped map. 25 of 29 map runs across all 23 map-bearing containers — full session with scripts, match and AI, 100 turns, save, load into a fresh session, 20 more turns on both — pass three independent checks: the hash verification at load, a per-turn comparison of every channel on a deliberately ragged 400/400/800/200/799/400/800/200 schedule, and a byte-identical re-save. Each check was proved to catch what it claims by deleting each system's section in turn and requiring a catch, and no deletion goes unnoticed on any run. The four that do not are Packs/'s blank editor templates — newmap.BFHP, emptyadv, emptyconquest and emptyscn — which declare six of their sixteen player documents and refuse to build at all, so nothing is round-tripping and there is nothing to catch. Every container a player can actually open passes, on every one of its maps. The sweep is wider than the sentence it replaces (19 containers, 25 runs), and widening it is what found the second of the two save defects in the corrections table above — four of the conquest's seven maps, which no test in the suite had ever opened.

And combat's section now carries a map. It never had real-map evidence before, for the reason the audit below records: the system was empty in every session, so its section at turn 100 was byte-identical to its section at turn 0 and --verify-faults reported nothing to lose rather than a pass. The control is exact — the same tool built at the parent commit prints combat nothing to lose on Zama and a 174,483-byte save; this build prints combat caught by load refused and 279,592 bytes, and caught by load refused on all 24 runs. command moved with it on Zama, from nothing to lose to caught by re-save differs, because units that fight are units that take orders.

Saves are ours to define. Every other format here specifies something the original engine wrote; this one does not exist to be decoded, because the retail install ships no saved game — Adventures/, Scenarios/ and Conquests/ hold maps, and currentadv.bfhp is adventure progress, not a session. What gbr.exe gives instead is a checklist. The container writer is byte-identical and the script VM already round-trips a suspended coroutine, so the mechanism is in place; the work is making every system serialise, and the trap there is specific: seven of the ten systems fold their state into the world hash, so losing one at load fails loudly — command and ai do not, so losing those is silent. See docs/formats/save.md.

Every shipped container now loads and runs its own sequences. imrun takes a map number — the conquest numbers its seven maps 3, 4, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, which is not an index — resolves .vs sources against the container ahead of the pack, starts the sequences sequences.xml declares, and reports the campaign state. A conquest map prints 7 territories and /Bonus = "", which is ConquestBonus/0, the root-scope EnvWriteString/2 and CampaignSystem::configure all resolving at once, from the mission's own seq0.vs. Whether a sequence autoruns is decided by evidence rather than assumption: 32 of the 46 that declare autorunallowed="no" are named by a RunSequence/IsRunning/IsWaiting/IsFinished literal somewhere in their own container, against 5 of the 262 that allow it — and RunSequence starts them now, so those 31 sequences are running content rather than unreachable source. The two names this sentence used to search for do not exist; see the corrections table.

What is honestly not proved: EndGame/3 and SetTerritoryState/2 are registered and their 68 sites compile, but not one is reached in any headless run — no longer because a sequence dies first (nothing traps now) but because winning Spain takes longer than a sweep runs. What is proved on real data is EndGame/2: Crossroads starts fourteen copies of the shipped elimination script and eleven players are decided lost by it. All 22 campaign containers declare victory_condition="0" and so get no victory script at all — ending a mission is the sequences' job.

The save is a file now, and the app writes and resumes it. The core had a reader for the HMMSYS block container and no writer; BlockFileBuilder is a port of the Python reference writer, step for step, and tests/test_corpus_imsave.py holds the two together over a real save — taken apart with the Python reader and rebuilt with the Python writer, the file is the same bytes. A save file is that container holding the ISAV envelope beside save.ini, a manifest naming the container, the map number played, the seed and the turn: the original's shape (AdvSave/ is a mounted filesystem), and the shape that lets a conquest keep its state beside the session. imsave --out writes one, imrun --load resumes one headless, the app resumes one with --load and writes Saves/quicksave.bfhp on F5 and reads it on F9. Two things found on the way: imsave had never chained a container's own scripts nor started its sequences, so no mission had ever run inside a round trip and the save sweep measured a skirmish on every campaign container — it runs them now, and 25 of 25 still hold; and the app read its simulation payloads with no map index, so --map-index 7 on the conquest drew the seventh map over a simulation of the third.

And a conquest crosses its mission boundary. CampaignSystem::carry is what a finished mission leaves for the next: the territory states as its victory sequence wrote them, the territory appended to ConqueredOrder, and the next reward set to the last conquered territory's — the inference this page has recorded since the layer was decoded, acted on in that one line and labelled there. It is a file, Saves/<conquest>.campaign.ini, the engine's spelling of the territories.xml the original rewrites under ConquestTempFolder/, and GameSession::restore_campaign installs it before the sequences start, because the container-root StartBonuses reads ConquestBonus() on its first pass. Measured on the shipped conquest: Spain won on map 10 → active_bonus=rIberia → a fresh session on map 4 restores it and its seq0.vs reads ConquestBonus() -> "rIberia" and runs that sequence. imrun --campaign FILE --human 0 --declare-won is the headless harness, and the last flag is a knob that says so in its output: it ends the human's match the way the victory sequence would, because nothing wins Spain in a minute. The app reads the file on any conquest map, writes it when the human wins, and prints which --map-index values the carry opens — every unowned territory next to an owned one — because the campaign map that would show them is Part 6's. The adventures carry nothing, and that is the shipped content's decision rather than a gap: docs/formats/adventure.md records the party carry-over the executable has and no shipped adventure uses.

A defect in the shipped game, reproduced rather than repaired. The conquest's seq0.vs writes the chosen bonus to "/Bonus", but seq1, seq3 and seq6 guard their loops on EnvReadString("Bonus") — no slash, a different slot. Three of the seven conquest bonuses never run in the retail game. Faithfulness means keeping that, and the reason it is discoverable at all is that the store refused to normalise the two spellings together.

Left: nothing of the host surface. It is at its ceiling — 33,957 of the installation's 33,965 call sites resolve to a body, the containers' 16,834 every one, and the eight that do not call a name gbr.exe never registers and could not run on the original either (Choose, ListFolder, GetSelection and five more development leftovers, listed in the corrections table). The sentence that stood here counted six campaign names over seven sites and a data.pak tail of 181 names over 315; both have gone to zero since. Recount it with python3 tools/host_coverage.py --leaders rather than quoting this sentence: every figure that has stood here has been overtaken, usually by the work that was about to start when it was written. AreaCenter/1, ClassPlayerAreaObjs/3 and AttackArea/2 led the list once; then IntoRect/1 and Rot/1; then SpawnGroup/1.

And this sentence was itself out of date in a way worth recording, because the tool that would have caught it exists. It read "then GetDifficulty/0 (136), DiplCeaseFire/3 (116) and the narration layer's GiveNote/1 (115) and RunConv/1 (74)" — a list assembled by hand that silently dropped five names that all outranked DiplCeaseFire: Obj::posRH/0 (141), Place/3 (131), SetNoAIFlag/2 (121), Unit::setplayer/1 (116) and Settlement::units/0 (115). All five are now done.

And it happened once more, in the sentence that had just been rewritten to stop it. The replacement list was still assembled by hand: it ran to fourteen names and ended at DiplShareView/3 (55), which is rank 21, silently dropping seven that outranked it — cur/0 (72), rollover/2 (70), tsadvheroskills/2 (68), setactor/2 (63), name/0 (61), gaikain/0 (61), cansee/1 (57). Every count it quoted was right; the ranking was truncated selectively, which is the same failure one revision later — and not a free/member split, because the list did carry setplayer/1, units/0 and state/0. The list is a tool now rather than a method — host_coverage.py --leaders — and this page quotes its output instead of curating it. What leads now, of 138 names over 203 sites:

sendto/2 13 · minneed/4 12 · delsentry/0 11 · besttargetinsquadsight/1 6 · FindTeleport/3 5 · setsight/1 4 · getshiptoboard/0 4 · getsentry/0 4 · amount/0 4 · Party/0 4 · MilEval/1 4 · GetTeamOverallScore/1 4 · siege/4 3 · siege/3 3 · rrepair/0 3. The head of this list is now smaller than the tenth entry of the list it replaces, which is the shape a finished surface makes: no name blocks more than thirteen sites, and what decides the next piece of work is --block rather than this ranking. And the two rankings have come apart, then stopped mattering. The tally used to be dominated by two names the site census barely mentions — PickFlyingPoint/1 at 207 hits and IsEnemyInSquadSight/0 at 132 — and both are now gone. The whole installation traps zero times — down from 62 over 19 messages, from 404 over 22 before that — and every one of the 25 runnable maps runs its sixty turns, with the AI on and every stronghold standing, and traps nothing at all, up from eight, then five. The last two were the invalid squad handle; see the thread below. Rank by sites to decide what a mission can express; rank by hits to decide what stops an object standing still — and the second ranking has run out of things to say. (Members print unqualified because imcheck surface carries no class name for them; the Unit:: and Settlement:: prefixes this page used were human annotation the join cannot recover.) And tools/host_coverage.py itself was reading a stale binary until that pass: it defaulted to build/engine/tools/imcheck and nothing else, so the number it exists to keep honest was a previous build's. It takes the newest build*/ candidate now and refuses one older than the newest engine source.

And the figures above went stale a third time, in the one way the tool could not catch: it was refusing to run. The guard that rejects a stale binary carried its own copy of “what counts as an input to imcheck”, and that copy counted every tool's .cpp. Editing imsave.cpp therefore made imcheck stale, and no rebuild could clear it — ninja correctly declines to relink a binary whose inputs have not changed — so the script printed one line of complaint and exited 2 for as long as the edit stood, and the number it exists to publish went unmeasured. tests/corpus.py had the same defect and had already been fixed; the second copy did not get the fix, which is what a second copy is for. There is one copy now: find_imcheck asks corpus.find_tool. The same pass found the other end of it — corpus.py's list of build directories to search was seven names typed by hand, the tree now holds twenty-eight, three of them hold built tools, and one of those three was not on the list. It was harmless only because the omitted copy happened to be the oldest; had it been the newest, find_tool would have returned a stale binary while believing it had found the newest, which is the exact failure it exists to prevent. It is a glob now. Both halves are the hand-curated-list failure this paragraph already records twice, wearing a third coat. Lockstep has started, from the end that is not networking. sim/netcmds.hpp is the command stream a match exchanges and the order every peer applies it in, and conformance::check_lockstep is the check that two peers driven by one stream agree — on shipped maps, with real orders. What is left of multiplayer after it is transport, a lobby and a turn negotiator. Mission-to-mission carry is not next and should not be: ChangeMap moves between Maps/<n> inside one container, all twelve of its call sites in the install sit inside /* */, and the MainMap hub every one of them names exists in no container in the installation — so it has no caller, no target and no retail behaviour to check an implementation against. That is the rectangle family's rule with a third reason attached, and it belongs beside those refusals rather than on a list of work.

Seen at the keyboard

Playtest reports

Bugs noticed while playing the app, written down as reported and not yet investigated. Each is an observation, not a diagnosis: reproduce it, find the cause, and when it is fixed either strike it here or promote it to an open thread if it turned out to be a thread.

#SeverityWhat was seenWhere, and how to get thereStatus

Clicking either portrait does nothing; it should select that unit. A plain click selects the cell, Ctrl adds one unit, Shift-clicks apply when Shift is released, and it works only when the fort’s owner grants the local player cease fire or shared control. First look. The save loads in imrun with its hashes verified: turn 2399, 23,239 objects, 3,662 scripts, 15 AI players, the player as Britain. A headless render of it (--load … --headless --frames 30 --screenshot) opens on the player’s holding at 1870, 2471, not where the report was taken, and shows one soldier by the barracks and no others, so it may reproduce. The app’s start-up line placed: 1009 of 1024 objects counts only the map container’s own objects and is not a cap on what the live view draws.		Scenarios/Balcans.BFHP, 16 players, late in the match. Every sheet first needed after that got no art, so most units — resolved later than buildings and props — were neither drawn nor picked. Rows are now shared by content (about 920 on a fresh Balcans match, 1,093 on the save) and the table holds 4,096. test_corpus_app_draw.py holds it, and each screenshot prints palette rows used, shared and refused. The band select in a headless repro missed the units too, which fits the cause.
4HighThe buttons in the Diplomazia window do nothing. The window opens and draws in full: one row per other player (Player 2 to Player 8), four policy toggles under La tua politica and four under La sua politica (Cessate il fuoco, Visuale condivisa, Viveri condivisi, Condividi controllo), the Vittoria alleata box, and Accetta / Annulla. Clicking the buttons has no effect. When fixing, check each group separately: your own policy toggles, the Vittoria alleata box, and Accetta / Annulla. The La sua politica column shows the other side’s policy, so it may be meant to be read-only. Behind it are the five Dipl* bits already read out of gbr.exe, with ceasefire and allied the same bit.Open diplomacy from the in-game bar and click any toggle or button. Seen on 2026-09-30 at 35387d9.Fixed at 08432cb/cfb6f7b. OK and Annulla had the wrong ids (0x1000/0x1001, not the legend’s inert 0x1002/0x1003), and the toggles were drawn upside down (row 0 means on). OK now posts a diplomacy order, as the original does (0x006cb680, executed at 0x004e5f80): the whole relations word for one row, applied by every peer on the next turn. Annulla discards; Vittoria alleata sets playerdata/@allied; La sua politica is display-only and follows the other side’s changes; the rows are the setup’s real players, so Crossroads lists three, not seven. Tested in a single-player match and across two networked windows. Not modelled: the fog merge on shared vision, and the dialog shrinking around missing rows.
5MediumThe ground has jigsaw edges where two terrains meet, and stair-stepped light and dark patches where the slope changes. The original shows soft, continuous borders in both places. This is visible in the screenshots for #1, #3 and #4. The fix is to read how gbr.exe composes the ground and reproduce it. The original has no GPU shader: it ran on DirectDraw in 16-bit colour, so the logic to read is its software ground compositor, not a shader.

Two readings in the renderer are already labelled as guesses, and each matches one symptom. Terrain borders: MapRenderer uses the transition masks in TERRAIN\TRANSITIONS\, and kTransitionStyle fixes the whole map on style 'A', one of the hard organic edges. Styles C and D are the soft gaussian ramps, and how the original chooses a style per cell “is not established” (map_renderer.cpp, and docs/engine/projection.md). The map’s own transition layer is zero in every shipped cell (map.md, Transitions), so the choice must be made at draw time. Slopes: the baked light layer is applied per 32-unit cell as a step, a gain of 128 + level * 8. The comment says “whether the engine interpolates is not established”, and the Part 6 ground-mesh note leaves the same question open (“whether it interpolates the light across a quad”). Interpolating the light bilinearly would remove the stair steps, but only if the original does the same. How the original scales the 22 light steps into pixels is also still open (map.md).		Anywhere two terrain types meet or the ground changes slope. Every playtest screenshot so far shows it. Seen on 2026-09-30 at 35387d9.Fixed at 6059ec4, read off gbr.exe’s software compositor (0x0061f8a0, 0x006217f0, 0x0061e210). The terrain byte is a vertex, and a tile blends the four at its corners, where the old renderer sat half a cell off; every layer’s transition is 2, so borders are the soft C/D masks alternating by row; and the light is bilinear per pixel across 32-unit quads with a gain of (L+4)/20. Still not drawn: the shore animation.
6LowAn open gate is still drawn closed, and its opening animation never plays. In the screenshot a Roman soldier stands in a gatehouse arch whose portcullis is drawn down.

So the likely fix is to run the gate entity’s open and close animation from the flag, and let the view draw that frame. Worth checking at the same time whether an open gate’s passability changes, so that units can actually walk through it.		Any gatehouse that has been ordered open (opengate) or opened by a script. 12MediumA walking legionary stops for about a second every four seconds or so. The unit walks across open country with nothing in its way, halts, then walks on, in a steady rhythm. At the app’s 100 ms turn that is about 10 turns stopped in every 40.

The order was the player’s: a right-click move to a fixed point on the map. The unit keeps heading there correctly; only the rhythm is wrong. That rules out a moving target’s repath (repath_threshold() in movement.cpp only runs when the order follows an object), and it makes a script reissuing its own move unlikely. What is left: any change to the passability grid (grid_generation_, for example a building finished anywhere on the map, or #6’s gates if they ever touch passability) re-checks every route in movement.cpp and may re-lay it, which on a busy map could happen every few seconds. Or something else takes the unit’s command for a moment. For example, the unit’s idle behaviour (unit_idle.vs) or a class behaviour could wake on a timer and issue its own command over the player’s, after which the move resumes. 13HighAttacking units converge on one point and overlap. Seen with valkyries, the mounted women: a squad sent to attack closes on a single spot and ends up as one stacked clump of horses, bodies drawn through each other, instead of spreading out around the target. Other units probably do the same.

Lead: in movement.cpp’s step check, a unit is blocked by any body within its radius except members of its own march: “0x005d2f80: one march does not block itself”, keyed on state.party. So a squad whose members are all routed to the same point never collide with each other and stack there. That rule is read from gbr.exe, so the original must spread an attacking squad some other way. Check whether an attack order keeps the formation’s per-member offset (offset_at, the marching drift) or gives each member its own slot around the target, and whether a member that has arrived stops counting as part of the march and becomes a hard block again. As gbr.exe does, a route for a band now ends on the band’s goal ring (0x417830) and is cut back to a free spot (0x4141bd); it reserves its end with an owned destination lock (SetMedia, 0x418770); and a unit counts as arrived only on a free spot (IsArrived, 0x417c10), a test with no march exemption. 14HighOrdering a unit at the Caserma (barracks) replaces the training in progress instead of queuing behind it. Clicking a second unit’s button while one is training cancels that one and starts the new one. Each click should add to the queue.

Select a Caserma and click two unit buttons in a row, without holding a modifier. Seen on 2026-09-30.Fixed at bc53e10. A traincommand="yes" row never replaces: gbr.exe’s bar clears the replace flag for it whatever Shift says (0x005e39f0, [def+0x1b4]), and the per-object issue every order reaches masks it again (0x004efbbb), so each press queues behind the unit in training. Follow-ups done at e6cbc07. Keys: the bar reads Shift as append and Ctrl as the modifier (0x005e39f0), and a right click the same (0x005e6050); the app had passed Shift as both, so now a Shift move queues and a Ctrl one replaces. Ctrl on a train button queues TrainMultipleCount (5) as one order with a count (0x005e3680, executed by 0x004e5e60). Cancelling: a click on a queued unit in the building’s queue strip posts a cancel through the command stream (0x006bf7c0); Ctrl cancels it and everything behind it (0x006bfebc). Every peer removes it and refunds its gold, food and population (0x004e63c0 → 0x005b07d0 → 0x004df400), whether it was training or waiting.

Queueing below cost fixed at c59a179. This engine charged what there was and refunded the full cost, so a training the town could not afford, queued and cancelled, made gold: two archers queued at Numantia’s Iberian barracks with no gold and cancelled left it 120. In gbr.exe every insert asks the accept test first (0x005b1760), which for a row with a cost calls the building’s payment (0x004df070): gold below costgold, food below costfood, or population below costpop plus MinPopulation refuses the command whole, before anything is taken or queued. The bar posts the press without asking (0x005e39f0), so the refusal is where the order executes, on every peer. The charge is at queue time and a cancel gives back exactly what it took; a Ctrl press’s five repeats, each a whole execution, queue as many as the town can pay for; a replace pays before it refunds the queue it clears; and a refused order leaves a resting idle running. Checked by core_tests and on the same barracks with imconform buttons --press: there, both presses queue nothing, the cancels find nothing, and the gold stays 0; and with 2,500 gold, Ctrl presses stop at twenty archers, when the population reaches MinPopulation. Not modelled: the owner’s “gold lack” message, and that only a building pays in the original (0x00467a30 for anything else), though no shipped row with a cost is offered to anything else. Still open: the rows’ onaddremovescript is never run (the accept test runs it on the add too, and may refuse); and a right click on a queue cell (help) is not modelled.

16LowA gate should stand closed by default and open by itself when a friendly unit needs to pass.

19HighSiege machines (Macchine d’assedio) build themselves before their crew has entered. A machine placed with the build command rises to completion while the units ordered to build it are still walking to it.

The original’s logic is shipped data. BUILD_CATAPULT.VS takes the closest CatapultMaxUnits of the selection, calls PlaceCatapult (the machine at 1 health, unbuilt), and gives each builder build_catapult. UNIT_BUILD_CATAPULT.VS walks each builder to the machine’s enter point and then calls building.settlement.AddUnit(this). CATAPULT_IDLE.VS runs the construction every 500 ms: unitsinside = .settlement.UnitsCount(), and only when that is ≥ CatapultBuildUnits does it heal maxhealth × CatapultBuildSpeed × unitsinside / (CatapultMaxUnits × 100) per second, calling SetBuilt at full health. With nobody inside it decays by CatapultDecaySpeed after CatapultBuildOnEmptyStartDamageTime.

Lead: our PlaceCatapult (fn_place_catapult, world_host.cpp) matches the original, so the fault is probably in what .settlement.UnitsCount() answers for a new machine. Check that .settlement is the machine’s own single-building settlement and not the town or village it stands in: a machine placed inside a town would then read the town’s garrison. Check too that UnitsCount counts only units that have entered, not builders merely SetEntering(true) and still on their way.		Select at least CatapultBuildUnits units, give the siege-machine build command on open ground, and watch the machine before the builders reach it. Try once inside a town and once in the open. Seen on 2026-09-30 at e6cbc07.Fixed at d546572/7a44fd0. Health never rose before the crew: CATAPULT_IDLE.VS builds only once .settlement.UnitsCount() reaches CatapultBuildUnits, the machine’s .settlement is its own (0x004dcdd0, in a town as in the open), and a builder counts only after AddUnit. What rose was the picture. SetBuildFrame was dropped, so the build animation played through to a finished machine; it now holds the machine on the stage its health has reached (0x004e2ed0, presentation only). And an unbuilt machine fought at 1 health; it no longer does, since the original fires only through autofire, after SetBuilt. Checked headless with imconform buttons --skirmish --select 3 --watch on the Mediterranean conquest. Still open: builders enter a dead machine from far away; an unrouted GotoEnter counts as arrival; construction steps are quantised to the turn (5/8 speed at 800 ms); and map-placed siege machines now stay silent until a script calls SetBuilt, which was not checked across the shipped maps.
20MediumRoads are not shown on the minimap. Read as roads, the terrain type; to be confirmed with the reporter, who wrote “routes”. The original’s minimap shows the road network between towns.

Lead: roads are terrain type group 6, layers 7, 14, 19, 20, 27, 29, 31, 32 and 36–39 (map.md, Terrain type). MapRenderer::compose_zoom (map_renderer.cpp), which builds the minimap and the Space zoom map, colours each cell with the mean of one texture: the base terrain, replaced only by an overlay that covers all four corners. The code says “a partial one is not drawn”. A road is a narrow strip, so most road cells carry it as a partial overlay and come out as the grass or dirt under them. Also check that DATA\TERRAINS.XML gives every layer a minimap tile (map.md), and whether the original colours the minimap from that tile rather than from the ground texture’s mean. Read the original’s minimap builder in gbr.exe for how it handles partial overlays: a weighted blend, or road over anything.		Any map with roads, for example Crossroads: compare the minimap with the roads on the ground. Seen on 2026-09-30 at e6cbc07.Fixed at b9a94c8, read off gbr.exe’s zoom-map builder (0x00616f10 → 0x006186a0, 0x006180c0, 0x00617a50, 0x00617bd0, 0x00617820). The zoom map is composed like the ground, from Minimap.pak’s own minimap tiles and its C/D masks: the base is the lowest corner layer and every higher one blends in ascending order through its mask, at twice the scale, lit and halved. So a road one vertex wide is drawn; before, only a road covering all four corners of a cell was. On Crossroads the brown tracks between towns are Ground and always showed; its road layers 7 and 19 are laid at load by the settlement templates and now show. Not reproduced, labelled: the warp’s height lift, and the builder’s row spacing and an off-by-one in its row loop.
Pick up here

Open threads

Everything below is scoped and evidenced, ordered by value rather than by size, and 96 of the 100 are done, and the ninety-seventh has started. This paragraph used to end “nothing here is blocked on a decision; each is blocked only on someone doing it”, and that sentence ran out; then it said four were blocked on something that is not effort, and two of those four have since been lifted, both of them by deciding rather than by discovering. The rectangle family’s tail has no caller anywhere in the installation, so writing it would be writing for nobody. The GAIKA node needs a decoded CVXGlobalAI block and there is nowhere on a retail install to get one — see its row. Multiplayer is done, late join included. Boarding is done: it was a refusal on purpose, the reason for the refusal was that nothing boarded, and building the boarding removed it. The squad former was the fourth and is now done: it was blocked on a decision rather than on effort — building it meant this engine starts forming armies, which is a change to what the simulation contains — the decision was taken, and with it the last reachable script the surface blocked runs. Starting class behaviours was the latest such decision: it changes what every map contains, it was taken, and with it a skirmish ends by itself. So the next move is a decision about which of the remaining constraints to spend effort lifting, and that decision is the owner’s.

ThreadWhere it stands, and what it needs
Gates open for friends, close for enemies, and bar the way when closed (done; the formation search labelled)Gate::LookAround walks units only (0x00529c09), so a gate’s own walls, towers, garrison and sentries never keep it open. A gate’s passage, which the map’s pass layer leaves open, is barred by the gate’s line of cells (0x00529380 → 0x00529e40, each cell with the one below, 0x005298f0), and that line is never in the shared grid: gbr.exe lays it for one route search and puts the cells back afterwards. A unit’s route (0x00419110) is searched first with no gates laid, and the gates it crosses are listed in route order (0x00418fb0); if one counts the mover an enemy, the route is searched again with every enemy gate’s line laid, except one fully open (0x00529070) or destroyed within 1,500 of the mover. The second route is taken, except that a route of 1,300 or less across exactly one enemy gate is kept when going round does not arrive. A friend’s search never lays a gate. Near the next gate on its route (0x00418260), a friend walks through when no enemies are near the gate, closed or not; anyone else walks through when the gate is above 20, destroyed or opening, and otherwise stands and asks again. The portcullis motion is hashed and saved, and a route’s gate crossings are saved with it (state vector 25). Labelled: the formation search (0x005f41b0) has its own rule — a gate within 1,600 of either end is laid for an enemy always and for others while shut — and this engine has no shared formation route, so it is not reproduced; nor are the “c” action-name test, the group reach floor of 275, the two-sides check (0x00417dc0) or the parallel-leg branch. No slower: Crossroads and Balcans time the same. Held by test_gate.cpp (26 of 26 faults caught) and two Crossroads corpus tests: a friend walks in through a gate that opens for it, and an enemy sent in from outside is not routed through a shut gate. Open: a stealthed enemy is not shut out; gate sounds wait on the sound event queue; Gate::Inside could be built on route crossings (0x005295d0).
Siege machines are built by their crew, and a map-placed one starts unbuilt (done)A siege machine’s health never rose before its crew was inside: CATAPULT_IDLE.VS builds only once the machine’s own settlement counts CatapultBuildUnits. What looked like self-building was the picture (SetBuildFrame was dropped, so the build animation played to a finished machine; it now holds the stage the health has reached, 0x004e2ed0, presentation only) and an unbuilt machine that fought at 1 health (the original fires only through autofire after SetBuilt; combat now skips an unbuilt one). A map-placed machine starts unbuilt too, and no shipped map places one. All 29 shipped map.obj.xml documents, the random-map templates and RandomMap.pak hold no object of the eight CVXCatapult classes. For an editor-made map, the constructor zeroes [cat+0x208] (0x004e1ede), the map reader never touches it (0x004dee10 → 0x005af280), Built is a key only of the save serialiser (0x004e1c90), and only SetBuilt (0x004e2e64) and the death handler (0x004e3fd0) write 1; CATAPULT_IDLE.VS counts down from maxhealth − 1 whatever the health, so a full-health placed machine is built by its crew like a new one. Pinned by a_map_placed_siege_engine_is_unbuilt_and_holds_its_fire. Open: builders enter a dead machine from afar; an unrouted GotoEnter counts as arrival; construction steps are quantised to the turn; and the sync-word projection lacks bit 25 (set by the constructor, cleared by SetBuilt), which marks a machine under construction.
Sound (open, stages 1 and 5 done: acknowledgements, clicks, select sounds, re-panning and music)gbr.exe plays everything through one sound manager (0x006b0910). An entity is named by its file’s stem; its weights are evened out to 100 with a silent filler (0x006b1400); the C runtime’s rand() picks the variant, with no repeat among three or more (0x006b0da0). Channels are pools per sound type from config.ini [SoundChannels] (0x006b0450): music and the two ambiences steal their last channel, every other type drops the new sound, and priority is stored but never read. Volume and pan are in hundredths of a decibel, with distance measured from the view box and everything on screen centred (0x006b0650). The options map to types (0x006e7210): Speech governs unit voices, SoundFX the rest. The unit voices are 388 ADPCM WAVs in the language pack, not in Sounds.pak; music is 8 Ogg Vorbis files. Built: engine/sound (WAV and ADPCM decoder, sound entities, mixer; no SDL, no core), the SDL3 device (the dummy driver when headless), order acknowledgements (one random member of the selection speaks, on the single UnitOrder channel), interface clicks, the options, and IMPERIVM_SOUND_LOG for tests. Nothing is hashed or saved, and the draws use the app’s own generators. See docs/engine/sound.md. Since built: music, through the vendored stb_vorbis v1.22 — the project’s first third-party source, public domain or MIT, its own warning-free C library that only engine/sound links (recorded in docs/legal.md). The menus play PregameUIMusic once per visit, not in a loop (a flag lowered by the first successful play, 0x00748620–0x0074885e); a match draws a music/ track not starting with _, never the last one, on a 2-second game-time look once the clock passes 100 ms (0x00550f90, 0x00550e80); everything stops at a match’s start and end (0x006aff00). A building’s select sound plays as it enters the selection, when its owner grants the local player control (0x005e7d80, 0x005e2f80); units have none. Live sounds follow the camera and the options (0x006b0810). PlayMusic/MusicPlaying are not bound: no shipped script calls them, and MusicPlaying would hand presentation state to the simulation. Next: a presentation-only, unhashed event queue on the world for combat, death, walking, idle and gates (needs the class sound table’s 28 action slots read); notifications, chat and items; ambience by terrain and season; conversation speech.
The AI’s siege against real sentries (open)Since the sentries man their walls, no computer player takes a town on Crossroads in 12,000 turns. p2’s squads reach SS_Siege over and over (GS_CAPTURE prints “Capture state: SS_Siege”), and GS_SIEGE.VS counts EvalSentries into what holds the town; yet at turn 6,000 all four of p0’s gates are at 5,000/5,000: squad.Siege(gate, 2, SS_Catapult, SS_Siege) never lands a blow on a gate. Nothing becomes very broken, so no squad gets SS_Enter, and attackers die under the walls. Next: trace Squad::Siege and the catapult branch, and whether a sieging squad’s units ever target the gate. Noted and not acted on: EnemyInRange’s grid sweep (0x004dbea0) tests a per-cell player mask; which objects that grid holds was not read, and ours counts every enemy-owned object. Also open: a sentry that walks back into its wall is erased and seems to count as a unit lost, and a placed one as produced; the original’s bookkeeping for that was not checked. And after a --load the app and imrun part (at turn 1,504, on the tree before this work too).
Sentries stand on their walls (done, one reading labelled)The sentries were objects all along: their walls placed them at walkway ends, and a Goto along the blocked walkway never arrived, so they stacked out of sight and never reached guard, the only sentry order that shoots. gbr.exe routes a class with ignore_passability in a straight line: every path request passes the flag (0x00419986, 0x005de31e, 0x005de593), and 0x0040b580 answers it with start → goal before any search; the spot-free arrival test is skipped too (0x00419f0f). Labelled: the goal list at 0x008c517c is read as the request’s goal. Three more breaks fell out. A VS point is two 32-bit integers (0x00696e80, 0x00696f00, 0x00696f40); sixteen-bit halves wrapped SENTRY_PATROL.VS’s route1*4 east or south of 8,191 and walked patrollers across the map. State vector 22; the owner chose no migration, so a save from an older build is refused, naming both versions. HasFreedom (0x005d7d30) is the specials word’s freedom bit or a RamUnit heir (0x00540460); ours was never true, and SQUADMONITOR.VS marched towns’ sentries off in heroes’ armies. A unit a script kills loses its scripts at the next script slice; a sentry its wall put down spun on a refused Attack (0x005ddbd2). A death trace showed that all 50 of p0’s losses in the old ending were its own sentries, killed by their walls after the town changed hands, and that 30 of its 31 kills were enemy sentries wandering past. The owner’s playtest saves #7, #8 and #11 read correctly after 300 turns. 29 faults caught of 31 injected (a redundant guard, removed; an equivalence, labelled).
The app and imrun play one game (done)Found by bisecting per-turn hashes of the app’s --play against imrun on Crossroads. Turn 0: the app applied the options’ difficulty (Settings.ini says 1) where imrun leaves the match’s 0; the app takes --difficulty N now, overriding the options without writing them. Turn 4: an animation’s length is its sheet’s rows, and gamedata::Installation::entities() kept each image’s XML-declared grid, which 430 shipped images contradict, where the app’s resolver takes the grid from the sheet’s frame table; the headless resolver does the same now (adopt_frame_tables). Every headless hash moved with it — imrun, imconform, the sweeps — and blows and deaths did not; Crossroads is still decided at turn 2,369, now on 11dc4c1e235fadfe. Two app faults fell out: a frame ran its whole turn budget past the decision, ending the match up to seven turns late; and the end-game screen refused to open over any dialog, so a match decided under the zoom map, which does not stop the clock, showed nothing and ran on. The turn loop now stops on the deciding turn, and the end-game screen closes the zoom map and menus first. --input gains turn:N and over, and the app prints decided: at turn N, hash H. tests/test_corpus_app_match.py opens the zoom map, plays Crossroads to its end in the app beside imrun, and requires the same turn and hash and an end screen shot on that turn (--full, about 85 s). The three Statistics oddities are closed. Rows are the setup’s real players: 0x006fc8b0 takes the first eight description records whose real (+0x14, named by the serialiser at 0x00567686) is set, and only the players’ screen’s Start (0x006dc039) and the automatch (0x00702d86) set it — over the first eight records, skipping any the map declares Computer (0x006dbdf3). So Crossroads’ seats 4–13 are never rows, and their Military 10 was the rating of nothing (0x0056a260 on zero counters). p0’s Captured −8,368 (−100%) is the original’s own book-keeping: SetPlayer takes a taken town’s warehouse gold from the loser and gives it to the taker (0x005c4f08, 0x005c4f29), and the share is value × 100 over a maximum found from nought (0x006fb25f, 0x006fab50). Statistics replaces the end menu rather than stacking on it: 0x006ce1d5 destroys it before opening, Close does not restore it, and F10 brings it back as CVXUIEndGameMenu (0x005e8656). A cheap --declare-won test holds the rows and the replacement, and the long Crossroads run holds the Captured book. Labelled: matches that bypass the players’ screen get its rule; the 0x0051cce0 gate on the slot word +0x290 is not modelled. Left: the app’s players’ screen still lists the map’s Computer seats, and Diplomacy still lists by participates, not real. Open: no sentries are drawn on p0’s walls; and a 3000×2000 frame draws the bars across only part of its width, outside the supported resolutions.
A spatial query answers from a grid, and verification is tiered and parallel (done)Once class behaviours started, every guard and patrol asks where the enemy is, and a profile of Balcans put about 40% of the run in World::objects_in_radius — a scan of every object per query. A uniform 512-unit grid now files every standing object by position and keeps held objects in a list resolved at query time; a query gathers candidates from the cells its box touches, sorts them back into spawn order and applies the scan’s own test, so the answer is the scan’s, id for id. It is derived state — never hashed, never saved, rebuilt on load, refiled in every mutator that moves an object or changes its holder — and a box wider than 256 cells falls back to the scan. A debug build (IMPERIVM_SPATIAL_CHECK) checks every answer against the scan, and a randomized test does the same over moving, spawning, despawning and garrisoned objects. Byte-identical output on Crossroads to its end (turn 2,369, hash 5421f773e9ba4ebf at the time; the frame-table fix below moved every headless hash since), Alesia at 400 turns and Balcans at 600. The gain is modest, and a first figure overstated it. Both sides in Release, one run at a time: Balcans at 600 turns 42 s → 38 s, Crossroads to its end 83 s → 77 s, about 10%. The 5–7× first quoted here compared a Debug build of the old code against a Release build of the new. The 40% the profile showed was late-game Balcans at 4,000 turns, with far more units alive than these runs have; it was not re-measured cleanly there, because the two trap fixes changed that run too. Three maintenance faults injected — no refile on a move, on leaving a holder, no rebuild on load — three caught.

Verification had become a large share of this project’s time. verify.py has a quick default tier, --full and an opt-in --war, runs its checks side by side after the build, and a planted failure makes it exit 1. Both sweeps run their maps in parallel with output identical to a serial run; pytest-xdist is in the dev extras.

The debug check was the cost nobody saw. IMPERIVM_SPATIAL_CHECK answers every query a second time by the scan and walks the whole index, and a Debug build-core compiles it into every corpus tool. The earlier timings were taken on a build-core not yet reconfigured for it; on a fresh one Crossroads to its capture took 683 s instead of 84 s, the Balcans fault run passed imsave’s 900-second timeout, and --full ran over 25 minutes and failed. verify.py now names the build each check runs rather than taking the newest binary. The quick tier runs build-core’s, so on a Debug build the check still covers every map in the trap sweep, and core_tests keeps its randomised oracle in any build type. --full also builds build/, the app the app tests run, which nothing had rebuilt, and runs the save sweep and the suite from its optimised tools. --corpus-build build-core puts the long runs back under the check.

The suite runs with --dist loadgroup. A module stays one unit, so its fixtures are built once, unless it is marked spread: six modules of independent subprocess runs (imsave, imrun, app-net, udp, lockstep, map writer). The four app-net tests that host through the shipped screens share the default port and are one group. Units start longest first, by the last run’s times in pytest’s cache: 201 s under loadfile became 149 s, and the floor is the Balcans fault test (about 130 s). The mid-war save test runs save_sweep.py’s exact command with its assertions, so --full deselects it (439 passed + 1). The fixtures check keeps each file’s verdict in .cache/, keyed on the file’s bytes and valid only while the installation, the checker and the readers are unchanged. It searches new files across every core, catches planted whole, trimmed and literal copies as before, and takes 0.1 s warm and 8 s cold instead of 40–57 s. Now: quick 63 s → 58 s on a Debug build-core, 9 s on RelWithDebInfo (core tests and the trap sweep 3–4 s each instead of 50). --full went from over 25 minutes and failing to 191 s (166 s on RelWithDebInfo). Open: the fault test is the suite’s floor, and a per-system --verify-faults would let it spread. With the index, the war pass takes about 8 minutes wall for its five skirmishes and reports 0 hits, the two Balcans traps having been fixed (the row below).
Class behaviours start; a garrison is inside its holder (done, three readings labelled)Behaviours. gbr.exe’s object start virtual (vtbl+0x2c, 0x005aec40), called by every factory path, sizes the object’s script slots to behaviours + 1 and spawns behaviour i into slot i+1 (0x006a07e0) with the object as argument. It is skipped during a load ([0xa87390]) and in the editor ([0xa8734c]). The scripts are the object’s: saved with it, gone with it, never restarted on capture. The class list is the class’s own behaviours, then its parent’s, duplicates kept, at most eight (0x005a046b, 0x005a5fd0). Behaviours start before any idle (inferred). VILLAGE_BEHAVIOR_SUPPLY.VS and TOWNHALL_SENTRIES_CONTROL.VS replace their native copies: they reach the same sentry maxima one to three turns later, and ship about the same food (45 against 54 mules on Crossroads, 109 against 90 on Balcans, at 1,000 turns). State vector 21.

Garrison. AddUnit/ForceAddUnit put the unit into the holder at (−1,−1) (0x005d3e10). Goto, FormSetupAndMoveTo and FormAcceptMove take it out (0x005d3f20): one unit per exit interval, food topped up from the store, and an RNG-picked exit door. The dead and the erased leave the roster. Nothing targets the held sentinel. Labelled: an army leaves unthrottled, and the wait is the original’s interval − last + now as read. Not reproduced: the target test’s exception for a broken outpost or shipyard with a garrison.

Exploration. A node is explored once its centre or any slot credited to it is.

Result: Crossroads ends at turn 2,369 by capture. Reverting either the garrison or the slot reading leaves it undecided at 12,000 turns. Alesia at 400 turns lands 6,283 blows and 219 deaths. Faults: 18/18, 23/23 and 5/6 (the survivor is an equivalence).

Closed: the two traps the war pass found once behaviours ran. Balcans at 4,000 turns now traps nothing (25,219 blows, 433 deaths), and the war pass reports 0 hits over its five skirmishes. UNIT_TRAIN.VS ran its budget out 236 times because TrainAttack answered at once, which in the original it never does: past its three refusals (held, partner invalid, health ≤ 1) 0x005d5d10 sets action state 2 and returns 3, so the call always yields.
