Here it answered true without suspending when the unit had no attack animation to time — GSentry1, the Gaul sentries TOWNHALL_AUTOTRAIN.VS sends to train, whose entity declares only idle and fight — and with GotoAttack already in reach answering at once in both engines, the loop never yielded. It now suspends to the next pass. Labelled: how long action state 2 lasts with no animation was not read; one pass is the shortest wait a 3 can mean. GetCommanded trapped once, on a unit that died during a Sleep(1) (TOWNHALL_AUTOTRAIN.VS:182-184: AsUnit of the dead unit is the invalid handle). 0x005d81e0 and 0x005d8180 print into the bare-ret sink and answer false or do nothing; so do these now. Not acted on: the original keeps that flag in bit 19 of [unit+0x194], where this engine uses the running command’s user flag. 10 faults injected, 10 caught. The run itself was slow: a profile put about 40% in World::objects_in_radius; the spatial index (the row above) was the fix.
The skirmish AI raises armies and attacks (done, five readings labelled)Every computer player on Crossroads raised one unit in 15,000 turns and never attacked. Two investigations traced it to five breaks in one chain — train, choose a target, march — and none of them was the strength function the vertical-slice paragraph used to suspect. Each fix is a labelled reading. With all five the AI trains, marches and fights, and that is what this row delivered. It did not by itself end a match: the turn-1,689 ending it first produced came from razing a town hall, which the original never does (the row “A town hall at no health stands broken”), and with that corrected the match was undecided at turn 12,000. A match ends by itself only since class behaviours start and a garrison sits inside its holder, and a node is explored once any slot credited to it is (the row “Class behaviours start”): p2 takes p0’s broken town by loyalty at turn 2,369. Measured before those rows, each of the five reverted alone left the match undecided or decided far later: at turn 7,761 without the first (7,593 blows, 34 deaths) and not by turn 12,000 without any other (2 and 3: not a blow; 4: 8,422 blows and 66 deaths; 5: 2,156 and 19). test_a_skirmish_on_crossroads_ends_by_itself_with_a_capture caps the run at 4,000 turns.

1. An order ends a resting idle (CommandSystem::append_order). BARRACK_IDLE.VS and OBJECT_IDLE.VS are while (1) Sleep(...), so ES_STRONGHOLD.VS:268/299/319’s barrack.ExecCmd(cmd, pt, obj, false) queued behind an idle that never returns — the failure Settlement::Research met and fixed with its real replace flag. ExecCmd, a queued player order and UpgradeBestBarrack, all through 0x004efc00, now end a resting head first. The insert past 0x004ef3d0 (which serialises the order into the lockstep stream) was not traced. It is deliberately not AddCommand’s behaviour, which the investigators’ prototype made it: eleven shipped sites (ESH_FOODTRADE.VS, ES_OUTPOSTSELLGOLD.VS, CATAPULT_DISBAND.VS, the mule scripts) write AddCommand(false, verb, arg); KillCommand(); on an object running idle, and that only works if the append leaves the idle for the kill. The narrower reading reproduces the prototype’s Crossroads run to the same final digest.

2. command() of an object never ordered is "idle". The original keeps the running command’s name at obj+0x10c, which WaitIdle compares with "idle" (0x005ece5a); here the first idle runs outside any queue, so the name was "" and barrack.command == "idle" never held. CmdCount still counts the queue.

3. A node is Explored once its centre is (AiSystem::advance). Nothing set the bit, so RECRUITER.VS:104-116 skipped every node but home. The centre rather than any cell is the reading; LastSeen is stamped on every pass that finds the node explored, since exploration is the only per-player sight kept (the original stamps the moment sight is lost); Revealed and enemies need current sight and stay unset.

4. Squad::SendTo walks the squad there — dest_gaika and advance to the node’s centre for every member, SQUADMONITOR.VS:77-79’s verb and point. A stand-in for the unrecovered consumer of the AI order list 0x004494c0 posts to; it sets no squad state (not SS_Approach).

5. A settlement on its own footprint takes the nearest area (LsaPartition::at_or_near: square rings to 512 units, each walked top row first and left to right, first labelled cell wins). Villages and outposts stand on blocked ground, so their nodes had no LSA, BestToSupply was always invalid and no tribute flowed; now the computer players’ villages are drained to 62 food by turn 3,000. The original takes a node’s LSA from its slot-grid partitioner ([node+0x10]). unit_is_inside (Gate::Inside) had the same weakness on the town’s side and takes the same fix; FindRuins and WaterLsa ask at of a building’s anchor the same way and are recorded, not changed.

The strength function is off this path: the traces showed caps at −1, MilEval passing at ≥1 and Needed 1–1. It does not decide the capture target either: RECRUITER.VS:167’s enemy < 1000 guards an ally’s settlement and EVALRECRUIT.VS:69’s < 500 a Viking lord’s squad (the row “Why no computer player takes a town”). imrun prints the turn a match was decided and stops there under IMRUN_UNTIL_OVER.

Faults: 48 injected, 41 caught on the first pass and 45 after the tests grew, plus the five reversions above, all five caught by the corpus test. Fix 1: 12, 11 first pass, 12 once the onfinish test covered a displaced head. Fix 2: 11, 10; the survivor was an empty-view skip that could change nothing, now gone. Fix 3: 9 of 9. Fix 4: 4 of 4. Fix 5: 12, 7 first pass, 10 after the tests grew; the two left are a whole-row walk that only revisits rings already found empty (an equivalence, said so in the code) and the unit-side kNoLsa guard in unit_is_inside, which a 1,024-unit test map cannot separate. Two pinned test shapes moved with fix 1: the barrack bench now fills queues with training orders rather than idles, and ExecCmd on a resting barracks counts three orders, not the idle plus three. Trap sweep 0, save sweep 25 of 25, core_tests and the conformance checks all pass. The regression is closed. A save taken mid-war on Crossroads loaded into a different game because a load rebuilt the AI’s node table over the restored world, and a razed town hall resolves to nowhere. By turn 1,600 two had fallen, their nodes moved to (-1,-1), and a squad beside a ruin was filed under another node (GAIKAIn 24 against 23). SquadMonitor and EvalRecruit branched on that, and a priest marching on the fallen town lost its route three turns after the load. The original keeps a node’s centre (GAIKA::Center is persisted); the session section now carries the nodes and a load restores them (session layout 4, state vector 20). imsave prints how many settlement nodes outlive their central building; a corpus test and save_sweep.py’s new mid-war case (about 20 s) run Crossroads at 1,600 turns and require one. Crossroads round-trips at 1,300, 1,600, 2,000, 3,000 and 6,000 turns. The same fault was latent on Great Loses German map 2 and is gone with it. Faults: 11 injected, 10 caught; the survivor is the node-count guard, which changes only an allocation. Alesia saved mid-battle round-trips. The war is now covered by conformance checks between peers (the Multiplayer row).
A town hall at no health stands broken (done, one reading labelled)Since: Crossroads ends by capture, not by razing, at turn 2,369, with every town hall standing. The mid-war save at 1,600 turns now shows 2 broken and 2 taken.

gbr.exe never kills a building. The damage routine (0x005a7720, via the building override 0x004db950) clamps the blow to the health left, stores it, and calls the death virtual only if vtbl+0x50 says dead; in every building-family vtable (CVXBuilding 0x007ba488, CVXTownHall 0x007d0500, CVXBarrack 0x007b97f8, the gate and three more) +0x50 is return 0 and +0xb0 a bare ret. So a town hall brought to zero stays standing and its owner’s, alive to IsAlive (0x005aae00), and enters damage tier 3, IsBroken, whose entry (0x004e0d70) runs the class’s broken method. The target test (0x005b1da0, 0x005b1f69) turns it away, so nobody goes on hitting it. A seat is lost only when its town is taken: 1 ELIMINATION.VS counts BaseTownhall and starts its countdown with a comment about having “managed to recapture”. No class or script names a razed or ruined town hall.

Here a building at zero used to enter the dying state and be despawned, which is how Crossroads ended at turn 1,689. Now it stops at zero and is refused as a target by targetable, order_attack, the engage check and IsValidTarget, and Crossroads is undecided at turn 12,000: no computer player captures p0’s broken town. imsave prints broken and taken beside fallen (3 and 1 at turn 1,600), and the mid-war save test and sweep ask for any of the three.

fc6c438 did not make shots land. With it reverted, p2’s units land 92 strikes on the same town hall from turn 7,970 and capture it at 8,033. The runs part through chaos around turns 600–625: reverting only the facing change moves the end to 7,303, reverting only the corpse refusal leaves no shot at the town by turn 9,000, and the death duration and swing make no difference.

Labelled, not reproduced: the target test’s exception for a building of an Outpost or BaseShipyard settlement (0x00440060, 0x004400b0), and the garrison condition inferred from the +0x40 field it reads; combat has no settlement in hand there. Settled since: UNIT_CAPTURE.VS’s loop for an independent camp spun out its instruction budget (916 traps in 4,000 turns on Crossroads, tolerated by name in the corpus test) when a unit stood on the camp’s own anchor. The original’s Goto answers such a call without yielding too; what it never does is put the unit there, because it measures an object destination edge to edge (the row “Why no computer player takes a town”). The tolerance is gone. 13 faults injected, 13 caught.
Why no computer player takes a town (done: Crossroads ends at turn 2,369)Closed. The four gates below were lifted in order: behaviours start (gate 3), a garrison is inside its holder (gate 4, the break the experiment found: UnitsCount never fell because AddUnit only wrote a roster nothing ever shrank), and a node is explored once any slot credited to it is (gate 1, adopted and labelled). Gate 2 lifted by itself once outposts could fall: p2’s recruiter sends armies to node 1 five times, and p2 takes p0’s town by loyalty. See the row “Class behaviours start; a garrison is inside its holder”. What follows is the trace as it stood.

The capture mechanic works and the census is populated; p0’s town is simply never chosen. Traced on Crossroads to 12,000 turns with IMRUN_AILOG and IMRUN_TRACE on GetGAIKAStrat, EvalRecruit, UNIT_CAPTURE and OUTPOST_BEHAVIOR. Villages are taken the ordinary way — a unit walks up, taunts, and DecreaseLoyalty runs the loyalty to zero — and GS_CAPTURE.VS reaches SS_Capture over and over. The chain for p0’s town stops at four gates, in this order:

1. Nobody explores it. GAIKAMONITOR.VS asks GetStrat only of a node where the player has a military presence, so the strategy for node 1 (p0’s town) is never computed: no army ever stands there. RECRUITER.VS:104-116 skips it as [EXPL] on every pass: not Explored, and for two of the three computer players not CanExplore either. By the centre reading no player has explored p0’s centre, a walled corner, at turn 4,000, though p2 has explored two of its neighbours. Measured, not adopted: explored once any slot credited to the node is explored (the link pass already credits all of them) makes node 1 explored for p2, its priority 39 → 56, and the recruiter then tries it with Needed 6000–15001.

2. Only a hero may march on a stronghold, and every hero is spoken for. EVALRECRUIT.VS:58-62 refuses any squad not led by a hero or a freedman for an enemy settlement that is not independent (−8, 522 of the 1,799 asks about node 1 in 8,000 turns); a hero squad in a stronghold node needs 15 to 41 men by the clock (−12). The hero squads that pass are all dependent: their current destination’s priority is at least node 1’s (50 against 39), CalcMaxTake.vs’s budget there cannot spare them, and GAIKA::Recruit sends nothing — [tried], every time.

3. Those destinations are independent outposts, and an outpost is never taken. UNIT_CAPTURE.VS’s loop for an independent camp walks round it until a defender shows in squad sight and ends only when the outpost changes hands; GS_CAPTURE.VS sits on it in SS_Capture with Enemy: 0 for thousands of turns, holding the army. What changes an outpost’s hands is OUTPOST_BEHAVIOR.VS (enemies in range, every defender dead: SetPlayer), and it never runs, because no class <behavior> script is ever started here. ClassGraph::resolved_behaviors has no caller outside imcheck. In gbr.exe the object virtual at vtbl+0x2c (0x005aec40, overridden and chained by units, buildings and more) walks the class’s behaviour list (count at [class+0x1ec], at most eight, accessor 0x0059aec0) and starts each through 0x006a07e0 with its slot number and an argument of 100 (a delay, inferred), unless a load is in progress. The slot is inferred from that routine’s place in the building table (CVXBuilding 0x007ba488). 39 scripts across the class tree; on Crossroads 88 instances besides the two below: wall_patrol, gate_patrol, townhall_behavior_guard, townhall_autotrain, outpost_behavior and the rest. Two are reproduced natively already (VILLAGE_BEHAVIOR_SUPPLY.VS as tick_supply, TOWNHALL_SENTRIES_CONTROL.VS as the sentry timer), so starting the scripts as well would run them twice.

4. Starting them is not enough on its own. An experiment that started every behaviour but those two at match start put 4 to 22 defenders in every independent outpost, and in 12,000 turns no outpost changed hands and every garrison was still full at the end. That is the next break, not traced.

The proposal, which is a decision rather than a fix: start class behaviours the way 0x005aec40 does — at an object’s start and after a load, one coroutine per slot, owned by the object — and retire the native copies of the two that are reproduced, or skip those two by name as a labelled reading. It changes what every map contains (patrols, town guards, auto-training, outpost garrisons), the way the squad former did, so it is the owner’s call. Then trace the garrison exit, then adopt the any-slot exploration reading.

Done here: a distance to an object is measured from its edge. The spin UNIT_CAPTURE.VS reached (916 traps in 4,000 turns) was a unit on a camp’s anchor mirroring itself onto itself. gbr.exe’s Goto (0x005d61b0) answers a call whose arrival is already made without suspending, as this engine’s does, after waiting out an animation still playing (not reproduced); a held unit is taken out of its holder first (0x005d3f20), so a held unit cannot spin there. The difference was how the unit got there: the route record keeps a point goal and an object goal apart ([rec+0x28] 0 and 1), and for an object the arrival test 0x00417c10 subtracts both class radii ([class+0x2dc]) before comparing the range, so Goto(b, 0, …) stops where the edges meet. Obj::DistTo is edge-based too — less the receiver’s radius to a point (0x005aa350), less both to an object (0x005aa3e0 → 0x005a77b0), negative when inside — and had to change with it: Goto alone made SHAMAN_IDLE.VS and EPRIEST_IDLE.VS spin 14,556 and 12,387 times, because if (.DistTo(.hero) > 250) .Goto(.hero, 250, …) never yields when the two measure differently. Labelled: the arrival test’s inner bound (a mover overlapping its object target is routed out there, counts as arrived here, since this movement never acts on a minimum) and the formation radius. Crossroads now traps nothing in 4,000 or 12,000 turns and its war is thinner (6,165 blows and 3 deaths at 4,000 turns, from 6,655 and 9); the mid-war save shows 3 broken and 0 taken at 1,600 turns, from 3 and 1. Alesia at 400 turns and imconform map, lockstep and netplay on it are unchanged. And a finding for the scheduler, not acted on: the original never kills a coroutine for running long. Its slice is 15,000 instructions (0x0069f7ea), and a slice that runs out outside an atomic section just ends the pass (0x0069d51e), so a script that does not yield is resumed next pass; only an atomic section that runs far past its slice is warned about and broken. This engine traps at 220 and kills, which is what makes a runaway visible and is why it stays that way until the owner decides otherwise. tools/trap_sweep.py gained a war pass — every map with no mission sequences, 4,000 turns under IMRUN_UNTIL_OVER, a second tally — that reports the 916 hits with the fix reverted.

Faults: 11 injected into the distance change, 8 caught first pass, 10 after a class with a negative radius joined the tests; the survivor is an equivalence and says so. Reverting the change fails the Crossroads corpus test and shows in the sweep’s war pass.
The dead leave their hero and their squad, and their hooks run inside the death (done, three readings labelled)gbr.exe’s unit death virtual (0x005db270) runs from the damage that killed the unit, in this order: the Wisdom roll, SetHealth(0), the ondie hook, then DetachHero (0x005db190, which clears the army entry and the back-link together) and the AI unregister that takes the unit out of its squad, and only then the dying state. A hero’s override (0x0052fbb0) detaches its army first. All 15 dying units in the dumps have no hero and no squad.

A hook is not a coroutine. All three launchers (ondie, onkill, onenter) reach 0x006a0360, which runs a fresh context through the interpreter with a 0x0fffffff budget and deletes it before returning (0x0069dca0); 0x0069d4e1 saves the current-context globals so it nests inside a running script’s host call. That is why CMERCENARY_ONDIE.VS, the only shipped hook that reads .hero, can still pay MercenaryPactGold. Scheduler::call is that launcher: a real id from the same counter spawn uses, the teardown fired for it, never saved. The chunk store became a std::deque, because a chunk compiled during a nested call moved the one a running script was reading.

What moved. Leaderless AI squads used to keep their dead for the rest of the match; now they shrink. On Alesia, 165 of 170 deaths in 300 turns carried a membership that is now removed at death, and the run diverges from turn 5 (kills and strikes unchanged). Crossroads is unchanged by it: none of its first 19 dead had a membership. The Wisdom roll is implemented: [hero+0x20b] is Wisdom, [0x821298] is ExpFromArmyDivider, and a success is one point.

Labelled: a hook that yields is abandoned where it yields (the launcher deletes the context whatever the interpreter returned; no shipped hook sleeps); the Wisdom point is paid at once, where the original credits an accumulator that converts at one point per divider; a dead hero’s squad key is cleared along with the squad. The dying state is marked before the hook, where the original sets it last, so that a hook that damages its own unit cannot kill it twice. Open: the unregister’s census counts and GAIKA-node removal (this engine computes both on demand), the list removal at 0x00518c40, and erasing a held unit the moment it dies (it detaches when the corpse goes). Found, not fixed: combat’s end-of-turn write of its own health figures undoes Egoism’s drains on any combatant. 24 faults injected, 24 caught.
Combat is seen, and the dead lie down (done, two readings labelled)A fight used to show nothing: HP fell and units died while every engaged unit stood frozen in its arrival pose, and a dying one held that pose for an invented second and popped out of the world. CombatSystem kept its own Combatant::anim and never called World::play_anim, the one thing the renderer reads. Now a blow turns the attacker to its target and plays one held cycle of slot 5; a unit whose fight is over settles through slot 18; a death plays slot 9, and the dying state lasts one cycle of it, then the object goes.

The dumps said so. Their Anim is the entity slot minus one wherever it can be checked: 0 on movers, 4 on all 92 engaged units, 8 on all 15 dying, 16 on 27 carrying villagers. The vector beside it, which this page had marked unknown, is the facing: exactly target minus own position on 88 of the 92 engaged units whose target resolves, and within 90° on the other four, whose targets had moved. The long tails of the death strips (8.8 s for a Gallic horseman, 16.4 s for Briton infantry, 26 s for an elephant) are the corpse rotting to bones, and the battle dump’s 14 dying units sit 1 to 14 s past their last attack, which fits a 16-second death and not a one-second one.

Nothing new is hashed. The animation state and the facing were already in slots and in the save, and the original’s table carries both; combat now writes them. An animation started mid-turn is brought forward to turn.end — one unit past the clock, which a partition test caught at one millisecond — so what a fight shows does not depend on how time was cut into turns.

The longer window exposed corpses that fought on. The attack order checked only that a unit was alive, which stays true while dying, so a corpse’s own engage script could order it back into the fight at zero health, and it was never removed. The 15 dying dump objects have an empty method, an empty queue and no path bit; corpses are now refused as attacker and target, stopped, and their owned scripts reaped at death. Battles resolve accordingly: on Alesia most Gauls are dead by turn 205, where three deaths had happened by turn 241.

Labelled: settling through toidle comes from the entities’ own state graph, not the dumps; and the object goes exactly when the death strip ends because nothing in the data names another time. Open: the blow lands at swing start rather than at action_time into it (horseman 568 of 1,065 ms), and toattack is not played. The in-world health bar is plain lines: width from healthbar_type (0 none, 1 class-derived, 2 about 34 px, 3 about 62 px), shown by a global mode the backtick key toggles and Ctrl+backtick cycles through all, own and others; its colours, offset, default and type-1 width are unread, so it is not built. Melee stacking is movement’s: IsArrived’s free-spot test and the owned destination locks are not reproduced (see sim/avoidance.hpp). A dying unit used to stay in its hero’s army and squad until removed; it now leaves both at death, after its ondie has run synchronously — see the row above. 22 faults injected, 22 caught.
GetExitPoint (done)The sibling of GetEnterPoint, 7 sites, two overloads behind one arity-2 registration — (Obj, point) and (point, bool), told apart by whether the first argument is a point.The door is drawn, not chosen, and the weight is the inverse fourth power of the distance. 0x0056fa40 sums 1 / (d2 * d2 + 1e-7) where d2 is already the squared distance, draws r in [0, 10000] — inclusive at both ends, settled by rand(n) at 0x004c63a0 passing n - 1 to the same generator entry — and takes the first element whose running sum strictly exceeds r / 10000 of the total, falling through to the last. The epsilon is what makes a door standing on the reference point a ten-million-fold favourite instead of a division by zero. Rule 1 forbids the double, so the weights are a 20-bit fixed-point ratio to the nearest door’s, floored at one — the floor is what keeps the zero draw answering the first element the way never-zero doubles do.The (-1,-1) two shipped scripts test for is their own argument coming back. Every fallback on the path returns the caller’s point unchanged, so ARENA_HIREHERO.VS’s and BARRACK_TRAIN.VS’s sentinel check can only fire when .settlement.GetCentralBuilding.pos was already (-1,-1): it reads as a failed-call test and is a missing-building test. 12 faults injected, 12 caught.
WaitConvRequest (done)The last of the Wait* family, and it turned out to be a predicate over state this engine already holds after all — the family is twelve entry points now, eleven names, over 338 sites. Two overloads, (Obj, Obj, int) at 0x005ed8d0 and (Query, Query, int, Obj&, Obj&) at 0x005ed480, 7 sites, six scripts unblocked.The conversation manager exists and is not needed. gbr.exe keeps a singleton at 0x996adc holding a list of {state, party a, party b, met a, met b}: the entry point appends a record on its first call (gated on the interpreter’s own re-entry byte, which is what CallContext::first_call already is), polls it, and withdraws it on success and on timeout. What completes a record is the input layer — 0x005ea300, the function that also reads UserInputCenterSelectionWaitTime, offers the player’s current selection every frame — so the record exists only to carry a question from the script to a test the script cannot make itself. Drop the selection gate, which is presentation state and must never be hashed, and nothing is left to store: this engine evaluates the meeting directly, on sim/entrance.hpp’s building-door argument.The reach test is Obj::sight, edge to edge, and asymmetric. 0x005a7870 refuses outright when the destination’s sight is zero — before it measures anything, so a sightless object is unreachable rather than reachable at distance zero — and otherwise compares it with isqrt(dx²+dy²) less both class radii (0x005a77b0), which is not the centre-to-centre measure Obj::DistTo answers with. All seven shipped sites put the unit that walks first, which is also the order the five-argument overload writes its two out-parameters in — and it writes the invalid object into both on timeout, which is what stops a wait that gave up from handing the next conversation the previous one’s actor. 14 faults injected, 14 caught.
The AI-helper runner (done)RunAIHelper/4 (285 sites), IsAIHelperRunning/1 (236), StopAIHelper/1 (179) are implemented — ~700 sites, the largest single cluster in the installation, and every one of them inside a map container. Coverage 87% → 89%; the containers alone 89% → 93%. Three things it turned out to be, and a seam that had to exist first.

The key is the caller's, not the file's. gbr.exe keeps a std::map<std::string, int> on the CVXAIHelper singleton at [0x00996888] + 0x24 — its own serializer names it "idmap" at 0x004d42ad — and the mapped value is a script id, not a pointer and not a struct. RunAIHelper's first argument is that key; the second is the helper file, and nothing looks the file up again. 275 of the 285 sites pass a distinct literal key for exactly that reason and five compute one.

There is no already-running branch. The only lookup before the spawn is on the path, into a compiledhelpers vector; the instance name is never consulted, and the tail at 0x004d321c is an unconditional operator[] store (0x004d3268). So a second RunAIHelper under a live key starts a second coroutine and forgets the first, which keeps running unreachable by name. Reproduced rather than tidied.

IsAIHelperRunning consults the table and only the table — 0x004d1cd0 is a find and a comparison against end(). It never asks whether the coroutine is alive. The difference is visible for exactly one turn, between a helper's last statement and the reap that erases its entry, and that one turn is what the test suite discriminates: asking Scheduler::alive instead agrees on every other case.

The seam. RunAIHelper's file name comes from a running script, so there is no manifest to prime from — and the core cannot compile. HostContext gains a ScriptLibrary*, which GameSession::Impl implements with the chunk_for it already had; null degrades to find_chunk_exact rather than dereferencing. find_chunk is never used, because its basename fallback matches DATA\SUBAI\TOWNHALL_BEHAVIOR_GUARD.VS for "guard" and data.pak holds ten other files whose basename ends in GUARD.VS.

The table lives on AiSystem, unhashed — not an omission: aihash is zero in all nine dumps and the standing decision is to respect that, and this table is on the AI singleton in the original. It is serialised, because AiSystem::hash is a no-op and nothing else would notice it going missing. Registered at arity 4 only: gbr.exe registers it eight times, arities 2–9, but only arity 4 has a call site and only arity 4 can pass the spawned script's parameter-count check (0x004d300e), since all four shipped helpers open // void, str GroupName, str Target. The other seven follow the rectangle family's rule — read, recorded, left unbound.

Ten faults injected, eight caught, and the two survivors named the two cases above: the same-turn predicate, and a body that drops the helper's parameters entirely, which every other case passes because no helper in those cases reads them. Two Zama sequences left test_corpus_imrun's pinned trap set on this alone.
The settlement query family (done)UnitsAroundSettlement/2 is the ring half of a query this engine already had, and it closed one of the two names blocking GUARD.VS. Three names, one runtime type, one field. gbr.exe registers UnitsInSettlement, UnitsAroundSettlement and UnitsGuardingSettlement in one run at 0x00577934–0x005779c2 — each twice, once (str, str) and once (Settlement, str) — and all six bodies converge on 0x004fe080 with a mode literal as the only difference. That mode lives at +0x54 on the query and is part of the interning key (0x004fe0fb compares it before handing an existing query back), so it is a field on QuerySpec beside area_is_rect rather than two more QueryKinds: the executable draws no type distinction, and a kind's ordinal is what the save writes and the hash folds.

Refresh (0x004fb640) turns the mode back into two independent booleans — ring = (mode != 0), garrison = (mode != 1) — and Settlement::CollectUnits (0x005c58f0) runs them as two passes over one list. The ring is one circle per building of the settlement, each at that building's own sight from [building + 0xd0], inclusive (0x005c5304 is cmp/jg), skipping the dead (vtbl + 0x50) and spawn templates (bit 27). Then sort and unique — and only when the ring ran: the garrison branch's early exit at 0x005c5a0a jumps clean past both, which is why mode 0 keeps holder order and mode 2 does not.

Two divergences are written down rather than papered over. The garrison half still reads the settlement back-link instead of the holder's own vector, because sim/session.cpp calls add_building for nothing and holder.units is empty on every retail map; and the ring skips held objects where objects_in_radius resolves them, because resolving would make mode 1 a superset of mode 0 and collapse the distinction the mode encodes. UnitsGuardingSettlement is read and deliberately unbound — zero call sites, the rectangle family's rule — but SettlementScope::both exists and is tested directly, because the mode field has to hold every value the executable can put in it. See the corrections table for the argument form that was missing.
The catapult flag, and a ninth bit (done)Catapult::IsBuilt/0 is nine instructions (0x004e2dc0): resolve the receiver, read the int at +0x208, setne. Catapult::SetBuilt/0 (0x004e2e20) writes the literal 1, and the CVXCatapult constructor zeroes the field at 0x004e1ede — so a catapult is born unbuilt, and this is one flag whose starting value is a fact rather than a convention. The two disagree about a bad receiver and both readings are transcribed: IsBuilt answers false, SetBuilt returns having written nothing, and neither prints, because 0x00686eb0 — the sink both call — is a bare ret in the retail build. Three of SetBuilt's four side effects are left out and named, including vtbl[0x48](0x2000000) on a SyncFlags bit state-vector.md lists as unexplained; SetBuildFrame/1 is declared, refused and pinned by a test.

With this, DATA\AI HELPERS\GUARD.VS has no unimplemented name left in it — 132 host calls, all with bodies.

The flag word grew. ObjectFlags had eight bits and no_ai had taken 0x80, so pack_object_flags is a u16 now. And the world hash folds that word directly for the first time: pack_sync_flags is a projection onto the original's +0x2c word, which by construction cannot carry no_ai (a second word at +0x194) or built (+0x208), so hashing only the projection left two worlds a save round-trip can tell apart and the hash could not. A gap closed rather than a field added.
name/0, and what spun in hero_idle.vs (done)61 sites and two entry points sharing one spelling, which this registry keys on (kind, name, arity) and so serves from one body. Settlement::name (0x005c2700) is 47 of them and reads a field; Obj::name (0x005aea60) is the other 13 and is not a field at all — it loads the named-object manager at [0x009bdacc], the one GetNamedObj searches forwards through 0x004c93c0, and calls 0x0054b3a0, which walks its map comparing each node's mapped pointer against the object and returns the first key that matches. So o2.name == "NO_mercenary" and "A_" + bild.name are asking which <group type="0"> an object is. First key in ascending name order: when one object carries two names the alphabetically smaller wins, and this table is a vector in document order, so the smallest is chosen explicitly rather than inherited from the storage. Numantia's seq2 dies on name/0 at line 38, and that line is a RunAIHelper.

And the hero_idle.vs spin is closed. It is Attack, and the evidence is a second registrar nobody had noticed — see the corrections table. Auditing this engine's implemented set against the fifty names that go through it found no second offender: Idle, Stop, the Goto family and FormKeepMoving all suspend already. Numantia at 200 turns goes from 51 budget-exhaustion traps to none.
Settlement capture (done)SetPlayer/1 — 116 sites, and the mechanic that decides campaign missions. See the corrections table for what it turned out to be sitting on top of. Registered four times in gbr.exe (Obj, ObjList, Query, Settlement), so one body dispatches on the handle type — and deliberately not through settlement_at, which maps any object with a settlement back-link to its settlement, units included.

The dominant shipped path is a building. Obj::SetPlayer goes through vtbl + 0xA0, which for the building family is 0x004dbb00: read the settlement back-link and redirect. Only 8 of the 116 sites pass a settlement handle. What moves is the owner, the loan and every building in the settlement's own list; what does not is loyalty, gold, food, population, sentries, the timers and the garrison — AddUnit files units in the holder, which the buildings sweep never reaches, so a captured town comes with the previous owner's soldiers standing inside it. Nothing traps, and the range check is not uniform: Obj and ObjList test the player number, Query and Settlement do not, so the same object reached two ways answers differently.

Fourteen faults injected, thirteen caught. The survivor cleared orders unconditionally, and the case it named is the original's own pair of conditions — an object with no owner keeps its orders, and a spawn template is left alone — neither of which is reachable through the capture path, which is why nothing had constructed them.
The partition, and a refusal deliberately lifted (done)GAIKA::LSA/0 (42 sites), Settlement::GetGaika/0 (27), GAIKA::Center/0 (23), IsWaterLsa/1 (23), GAIKACount/0 (6), the GAIKA branch of settlement/0, and the four arena and temple walks — 125 sites, the largest single step this project has taken. Coverage 33,285 → 33,619; names with no body 240 → 231; sites with no body 680 → 555.

This is a decision before it is a change, and the decision was the owner’s. sim/gaika.hpp refused to model the AI’s node table at all, on the grounds that a table written from the persist field names would be “a guess wearing the clothes of a measurement”. That refusal was correct about the evidence and it stood in front of 314 of the 680 remaining call sites — 46% of everything left, unreachable by any amount of care elsewhere. The trade has now been taken deliberately: build the partition as a stated approximation, ship it, and keep the evidence for going back. That header now carries the decision, what it costs, and what would settle it.

The two halves are not the same kind of claim, and the code says which is which. The node partition is the approximation: one node per settlement, then one per connected region nothing was built in. The original cuts its nodes from a slot grid at slotresx × slotresy that nothing recovered reproduces, so node ids differ and no retail save could be read against this table. The LSA partition is measured, and turned out to be recoverable exactly.

And the LSA half is where the recon earned its keep. Three things had to be settled and each changed the code: SlotLSA.bmp and DetailLSA.bmp are not read and do not ship — their only cross-references are arguments to a function that is ret 8 in the retail build, so the partition is computed, not authored; the fill runs over the passability bitmap at 16-unit cells, four-connected, with terrain deciding only which of two passes may seed a cell; and the size threshold is measured against coverage (cells − slots touched), not the cell count.

The best moment was a contradiction. The fill has no terrain test at all — four neighbour blocks, each a bounds check and one passability bit — so read literally the water pass should flood the entire map from its first seed and IsWaterLsa could never distinguish anything. It does not, because the shipped maps wall the coast: every 16-unit cell with a four-neighbour of the opposite deep-water-ness is blocked — 11,162 of 11,162 on Island War, 21,998 of 21,998 on Balcans — while deep water itself is only 5% blocked, indistinguishable from land’s 10% of trees and cliffs. The coastline is in the asset, not the algorithm. That is now a test, and the header carries the numbers.

Two collisions handled the way this registry requires. settlement/0 is registered on Obj and on GAIKA, and GetGAIKA/1 three times over — from an int (the identity), a point and an Obj. One body each, dispatching on what it is handed, which is what a registry keyed on (kind, name, arity) demands. The GetGAIKA one is why the AI domain’s entry count went down by one: the new body absorbed a registration that was already there, and the test that counts them is what said so.

GAIKACount was withheld for a reason that has now gone. sim/ai.hpp refused it because PRIORITIZE.VS is a while(1) whose only Sleep is inside a for (i = 1; i < GAIKACount; …) — a count of 0 or 1 is “a lie that also hangs”. There is something to count now.

19 faults, 18 caught. The survivor is the pass order, and it is annotated rather than tested: the only thing it can change is which of two areas gets the lower id, and the header argues at length that the numbering is this engine’s own to choose. A test that caught it would be asserting the one property the design says is free.
Who hit us, and when (done)Squad::LastFightTime/0 (4 sites) and Squad::GetLastAttacker/0 (4) — 8 sites, one new field, and a hook in combat. Coverage 33,277 → 33,285; names with no body 242 → 240; kStateVectorVersion 10 → 11.

Half of it was already here and unwritten. Squad::last_fight_time has been a field on Squad, saved and round-tripped, since the squad table landed — with nothing writing it and no entry point reading it. This is the writer, the reader, and the field beside it that had neither.

A squad remembers the first blow of a tick, not the last. gbr.exe 0x0041e6c0 compares the squad’s stored time against the current tick and returns before writing anything when they are equal — one comparison, and it is the difference between “who is shooting at us” and “whoever shot last”. It also means the time and the attacker can never describe two different blows, which is what lets SQUADMONITOR.VS read them as a pair: fought within three seconds, attacker still valid, attacker a building or a sentry — and then walk away along sq.pos - sq.GetLastAttacker.pos.

The stamp is on the swing, and it shares a hook with the hero’s clock: 0x0041e6c0 is called from CVXUnit::OnAttacked one line before the hero stamp this engine already had, so record_squad_attacked sits beside record_army_attacked in CombatSystem::strike. The handle is stored raw and not re-checked on the way out, which is why the shipped site tests IsValid itself — a squad can name an attacker that has since died.

Saved, not hashed, which is the rule SquadTable::hash already states for every AI field on a squad: the original’s desync dumps carry squad membership and no squad state, so hashing it would pull a subsystem into the determinism contract that the shipped build left out of it. The squad section also had no round-trip test at all until now.

Two things deliberately left out. The original follows the stamp with a GAIKA notification (0x0044fc60) telling the node table that a squad was attacked moving between two nodes — sim/gaika.hpp does not model that table. And its third guard, a 0x800000 bit in the second flag word, is not reproduced: that bit is one the state-vector doc still lists as unexplained, and a guard on a bit nothing sets would be a no-op wearing the clothes of a transcription.

14 faults, 14 caught.
The nearest base, and what is in the chest (done)NearestStronghold/2 (6 sites) and /3 (1), and Obj::OpenItemHolder/1 (2) — 9 sites, no new state, and one correction to the block before it. Coverage 33,268 → 33,277; names with no body 245 → 242; blocked scripts 194 → 192.

NearestStronghold has two arguments that both look harmless and both are not. The player is 1-based and the wrapper decrements it unconditionally, so NearestStronghold(pt, 0) asks about player −1, which the search reads as no filter at all — the executable’s own one-argument form is that call with −1 written in. And the two-argument form is not “any stronghold”: the wrapper writes in AI_OWN, so the short spelling means mine. The relation code (0x0044e250) answers exactly one of 1, 2, 4 and reads the caller’s own diplomacy row, never the transpose — so a one-sided truce makes the two sides disagree about who is an ally. The tie-break is the third trap: distance is the integer square root of the squared distance, so 100 and 101 squared units are the same distance and the earlier settlement keeps it. Comparing squared distances would pick the other one and nothing else would notice, which is why there is a case for exactly that.

OpenItemHolder is a transfer, not a window. 0x0053c350 walks the holder’s slots downwards into the receiver until the receiver is full — so a holder that does not fit keeps its lowest slots — and destroys itself afterwards if it ended up empty and its class declares delete_empty="1" (DEFITEMHOLDER does; DEADTREE and TREETRUNK declare 0 and stay). The bool is at least one item moved. The one genuinely user-interface thing on the path — the "ItemGotFromHolder" caption at 0x005a93a0, projected against the camera rectangle and gated on the local player’s fog — is left out, and it is the only thing that is.

The correction: IsRanged’s property was stated too confidently. The behaviour is read and stands — the class descriptor’s string at +0x960, non-empty and not the "**Invalid**" sentinel. Which XML attribute fills that slot is not read: the class reader writes projectile_shadow into +0x97c and projectile_fire into +0x998, and nothing in the image writes +0x960 with that displacement at all — the nearest evidence is the literal _projectile_class, evidently composed with a prefix at run time. What is settled is that 0x005b1d20 resolves +0x960 as a class name and reads a range-like number off the class it names. projectile_class is the reading taken and building_projectile_class is the alternative; the two differ for a tower, and the comment now says so.

20 faults, 18 caught, and the two survivors are one annotated redundancy. Removing OpenItemHolder’s capacity check, or reading the holder’s capacity instead of the taker’s, moves exactly the same items — because ItemStore::give refuses a full destination itself. The check is the original’s stated stop condition and is kept, on the precedent spawn_group_into_list set for a redundancy nothing can observe. Two more only became visible after the tests grew: a tie in the distance, and a delete_empty holder that is only partly emptied.
Three siblings of things already here (done)Settlement::StopReserving/0 (7 sites), Obj::IsRanged/0 (2) and ObjList/Query::AddCommandOffset/3 (2) — 11 sites, and not one of them needed new state. Coverage 33,257 → 33,268; names with no body 248 → 245; blocked scripts 196 → 194.

Each is one step from something this engine already had. AddCommandOffset is SetCommandOffset’s queueing form — the same group spread, add_command instead of set_command, and gbr.exe registers the pair four times over (ObjList and Query, 0x00562280/0x00562520 and 0x00578280/0x00578460) differing in the two calls to 0x005b07d0 that only the Set form makes, which is the clear. It inherits the inference the Set form is honest about: nothing proves the spread is measured from the centroid.

IsRanged is not a range comparison. 0x0053f5b0 reads one std::string off the class descriptor at +0x960 and answers true when it is non-empty and not "**Invalid**" — the original’s uninitialised default, an 11-byte literal it compares against by name. The property is projectile_class, which CombatProfile::projectile already resolves from the other side: a unit is ranged when it has something to throw. It inherits, too, so a class that declares nothing under one that does is ranged.

StopReserving completes an arithmetic this file had already written down. sim/economy.cpp’s affordable_count has carried the food-reserve formula — Reserve > 0 ? ((GoldSpentOnArmy - ReservedAt) * Reserve) / 100 : 0 — with a note that its three terms are Env ints written by two unimplemented entry points. One of them is now here, and its key is the same shape the earlier SpentGoldOnArmy block established: /<root>/Settlement<id>/Reserve<owner>, zero-based owner and all. The other half, ReserveFor/2, is one site and is left for its own change — so the percentage is still never non-zero and this writes a zero over a zero, which is why the test sets it by hand.

Settlement::BestToSupply/0 was in this block and came out again. 0x0042c800 is the nearest same-owner settlement whose central building IsHeirOf("BaseTownhall") — and which shares this settlement’s LSA, read through the GAIKA table at 0x008c8e54. `ES_VILLAGE.VS` even comments “the return value is always stronghold”, so two thirds of it is settled; the third is the connected-region partition sim/gaika.hpp declines to invent, and dropping the filter would pick the wrong stronghold on any map with more than one landmass.

14 faults, 14 caught, one only after the test grew a longer queue: with a single command already ordered, “insert at index 1” and “append” are the same slot, so a body that ignored the front flag in either direction passed. It takes four queued orders to tell the two apart.
What a unit keeps to itself (done)Unit::Taunt/1 (6 sites), GetUnitsInSameHolder/0 (4), SameHolderAs/1 (2) and IncKills/1 (1) — 13 sites that between them are the sole blocker of nine scripts. Coverage 33,244 → 33,257; names with no body 252 → 248; blocked scripts 205 → 196; kStateVectorVersion 9 → 10.

Taunt and Idle are the same function. 0x005d6030 and 0x005d60f0 differ in one byte — the activity constant, 4 against 5 — and both branch on the receiver’s position: (-1, -1), the constant pair at 0x008212bc with no writer anywhere in .text, which is what a unit inside a holder reads. That branch suspends for the argument; the other one drops the combat target and ends the time slice with no timed wake. So the 2,000 every shipped site passes is only ever consumed by the branch where the unit cannot taunt at all, and a taunt on the map costs one scheduler step. The activity write itself is deliberately absent, on the grounds Idle already gives for not selecting an animation: +0x130 is a transient enum this engine does not model and is not in the original’s serialiser list. Dropping the target is modelled, and it is the same prologue Stop, both Gotos, Attack and AttackEveryone share.

And that is a correction this change does not make. Idle here suspends for its argument unconditionally, which is right for the held branch and wrong for the other; gbr.exe also registers Unit::Idle once, at arity 1, and the arity-0 form this engine serves from the same body is Ship::Idle (0x005c6ea0), a different function that writes the ForceIdle flag. 63 call sites is its own change with its own evidence, and it is recorded in docs/formats/vs-host-api.md rather than folded in here.

GetUnitsInSameHolder has no filter of any kind. 0x005df620 assigns the holder’s member vector into the new list with a single call — no loop, no predicate, no owner test and no self-exclusion — so the asker is in its own answer and so is the enemy standing beside it. A unit in no holder gets an empty list rather than a refusal, because the original indexes the handle table with 0xffff and relies on the slot being null. Its sibling SameHolderAs compares the raw field, which means two units both out in the open are in the same holder: 0xffff == 0xffff. Both shipped sites guard with InHolder first, so the retail data never sees it, which is exactly why it is pinned rather than tidied.

IncKills is one add, and the interesting part is what reads it. Nothing does. An exhaustive scan of .text for the unit field at +0x188 finds four touch sites: the reset to zero at spawn, the add inside this entry point, the serialiser registration that names it kills, and one reader — the unit description at 0x006c53b0, which formats “%d kills / %d experience to next level”. So veterancy is experience-driven and this number sits beside it rather than behind it, and the engine never increments it either: it rises only because a mission script says so. Kept, saved and hashed all the same, on ObjectState::traversed_by’s precedent. It refuses a dead receiver, so a unit that dies in the same tick as the kill it scored does not get the credit.

18 faults, 18 caught. The by-product of the field scan is worth as much as the entry points: the whole CVXUnit tail is now named from the serialiser walk at 0x005dd710 — holder at +0x154, experience at +0x180, level, kills, target, attacks, danger, parry_mode and the rest — which is what settled three of these four without a guess.
The counters that had names and no writer (done)GetTeamMilitaryScore/1 — 2 sites, and it took a save bump and a new obligation on combat to get them. It is the sole blocker of 3 TIME LIMIT (MILITARY RATING).VS (72 sites). Coverage 33,242 → 33,244; blocked scripts 206 → 205; kStateVectorVersion 8 → 9 and the shared section version 4 → 5.

The arithmetic was already in the tree and nothing fed it. sim/match.hpp has carried PlayerScoreCounters and military_rating since the match domain landed, recovered by name from the two serialisers at 0x0056b0e0 and 0x0056cb00 and tested against the recovered expression — under a heading that said “the scores, recovered but not wired”, with the entry points left unbound because a rating every player scores 10 on forever is not a rating. So this block is the wiring: CombatSystem credits four of the five counters on every blow it lands, and they are saved and hashed.

One number, two rows, and it is the health actually removed. All four writer pairs in gbr.exe add the identical value to the attacker’s damage_inflicted and the victim’s damage_taken, and that value is the victim’s health delta (0x0051151d) rather than the attack roll — so overkill is not counted. A kill is worth the victim’s class maximum health, read off the prototype at [victim + 0x3c] + 0x294: a finishing blow on a sliver is worth the same as a kill from full.

Allied blows book nothing, and that is transcription rather than tidying. When both sides are units and the attacker’s owner grants the victim’s owner bit 0, 0x00511550 takes a capped non-lethal branch at 0x0051164f and jumps past every counter. This engine has no such branch, so the exclusion is stated where the counters are written. A player is allied to itself, so friendly fire and self-damage fall out of the same test. The booking is in hit and deliberately not in apply_damage, for the reason record_army_attacked is already kept out of it: Obj::Damage(n) is a script dealing damage out of nowhere and reaches none of the original’s four writers.

“Team” is the alliance matrix, both directions, and it scans eight slots. 0x00522e20 inserts the caller first and unconditionally, then looks for mutual allies — and the loop bound is esi < 0x1900 over a 0x320 stride, with a dead cmp esi, 0x3200 inside the body that a sixteen-slot version left behind. That is transcribed, and it has an odd consequence the test pins: a mutual ally in slot 8 is not on the caller’s team, but the caller is on the ally’s. The membership filter on the others is CVXPlayer + 0x64 — the same unmodelled field PlayerTable::are_allied already declines to require, which is a nice piece of corroboration — and participates stands in for it.

GetTeamOverallScore/1 (4 sites) stays unbound, and the refusal is the interesting half. It multiplies the military rating by a power score (0x0056a8c0) whose formula is only half recovered: the field-army and garrison power terms are settled — per unit ((attack + defence) * (level + 13) * (maxhealth / 4 + health)) / 1000 + 1 — and so is the settlement weighting, 20 for a town hall and 3 for anything else. The other two terms are headcount buckets read off a per-object component at +0x1cc, split on a mode flag at +0x1d4 whose meaning was not recovered. Half a formula is not a score, and 2 SCORE LIMIT.VS ends matches on this number.

20 faults, 20 caught, four only after the tests grew — and each of the four names a class of blind spot rather than a typo. A one-directional team test passes against a predicate that reads the other direction, so it has to grant each side alone. A single “the hash moved” check does not notice three of four fields dropped, so each counter moves the hash on its own. A kill test cannot see a body that books a kill on every blow, so there is a survivable blow. And the section version is pinned by patching a saved blob to the literal previous number: if the constant had not moved, the patch would be a no-op and the expected refusal would not come.
Three names, three whole scripts (done)SpawnNamed/1 (3 sites), Settlement::AddToMaxPopulation/1 (4) and Obj::IsPeaceful/0 (1) — 8 sites, and the reason to write them is not the sites. Each is the only unimplemented name in a script, so eight call sites buy 466: the tutorial’s seq0.vs (292 sites, the largest single script in the installation), ESH_RESEARCHTRAINING.VS (93) and 6_Great_loses_Boudicca’s seq10 (81). Coverage 33,234 → 33,242; names with no body 256 → 253; blocked scripts 211 → 206.

SpawnNamed is SpawnGroup for a name that holds exactly one object, and the walk it needs was already written. <group type="0"> names one object — 1,378 of 1,378 across the shipped maps — and 10,610 of the 27,070 placed objects are unspawned templates, so a name can be bound to something not yet in play. gbr.exe hands the work to CVXNamedObjQuery::Spawn (0x00571d40), which is CVXGroup::Spawn’s body for one element: the same template-bit test, the same clone through the object’s own vtbl + 0x30, and the same two table walks filing the copy wherever the template was a member. So docs/formats/map.md’s remaining open question — whether SpawnNamed rebinds the name too — is answered by not being its question: the rebind is in the shared spawn. It makes the entry point single-shot, and that is observable: after the first call the name points at the copy, a copy is not a template, and the second call spawns nothing.

IsPeaceful is two IsHeirOf calls — Peaceful or Animal — and the interesting half is which name the second one is. ANIMAL.SC.XML hangs Animal off BaseAnimal, and LIONM, LIONF, ATTACKANIMAL and SUMMONINGUNIT hang off BaseAnimal beside it, so a predicate written against the obvious-looking root would call a lion peaceful. It is also class ids and not cpp_class, for the same reason BestBarrack was: every class in the family is CVXUnit.

The population ceiling was missing from both ends. AddToMaxPopulation is one add and was unimplemented, so ONFINISH_RESEARCH.VS’s “Housing” was a researchable that did nothing. Its counterpart was worse: SetPopulation (0x005c2200) stores min(value, max_population) and this engine had no cap, so “Free Beer” (SetPopulation(population + 20)) was unbounded growth. The comment justifying that cited the late dumps’ 101 100 and 165 150 — which are real, and are AddToPopulation, which is uncapped in the original. Two entry points, one piece of evidence, attributed to the wrong one.

And the cap could not be turned on until the ceiling existed. sim/settlement.hpp has always said max_units comes “from the class graph: 10,000 on a town hall, 0 on a village”, and session.cpp never read it — nor max_population. So every settlement on every retail map ran with both at zero: Holder::full() was true before the first unit walked in, and growth was switched off everywhere. Two lines, and the test that holds them goes through GameSession::create because every settlement test above it builds its SettlementInit by hand and so could never have seen it.

17 faults, 17 caught — four only after the tests grew. Three of those were the loader lines, which nothing in the suite exercised; the fourth was “the spawned copy is not fed”, which needed a FeederSystem in the fixture. Putting one there as a field broke two unrelated round-trip tests that deserialize into a bare World, because a save carries one section per registered system — so it is a fixture of its own, and the comment says why.
The region census, and an off-by-one nothing could see (done)GAIKA::Eval/6 (19 sites), Eval/3 (8), Count/6 (5), Eval/2 (2), EvalNeighbors/6 (1) — 35 sites, four arities of one shape, and no new state at all. Coverage 33,199 → 33,234; names with no body 261 → 256; sites with no body 766 → 731.

Every one of them answers zero on a real map, and that is the point of binding them. Nothing in this engine assigns a GAIKA to a squad, so the census matches nothing — exactly as GetSquads fills no list today and for the same reason. A declared name with no body traps, and a trap stops GS_KILLENEMIES.VS, GS_SIEGE.VS, GS_CAPTURE.VS and RECRUITER.VS at the line rather than letting them read a zero and take the cautious branch. The shape and the guards are the original's, so the day squads carry nodes these become right untouched. Which is why the test drives the three GAIKA fields itself: a body that returned a constant zero would pass every corpus run and every trap sweep, so the census is measured against squads that really do have nodes, sides and strengths.

The correction is worth more than the sites. GetSquads's player argument was read as 0-based and it is 1-based: 0x00439500 pops it and decs it at 0x004395a5, and the whole corpus passes AIPlayer, which AIGetPlayer returns 1-based. So gaika.GetSquads(SL, AI_STAYING, AIPlayer, AI_OWN) was asking for the next player's squads. Nothing caught it and nothing could have — no squad carries a node, so every list comes back empty under either reading, and the test that covered it had encoded the wrong convention. The conversion is one function now, shared with the census, and both ends of the range are asserted: the upper bound is only visible at 257, where a byte-wide PlayerId would wrap to player 0 and hand back that player's army.

Two things the census refuses to count, both the original's. A squad whose strength is zero — which is why Eval and Count can disagree about whether a node is occupied at all — and squads of players 14 and 15, the neutral wildlife and neutral-passive pseudo-players, which are nobody's army. enemy_hidden is 0 and stays 0: the original fills it from remembered rather than observed enemy force, and this engine has no such memory; all twelve shipped readers either add it to enemy or ignore it.

GAIKA::Count/3 is left unbound on purpose and it is the interesting refusal. Its first argument's meaning is not settled and the corpus cannot settle it: the live sites write Count(AI_ENEMY, AIPlayer, "Peasant") and a commented-out line writes Count(AIPlayer, AI_OWN, "GDruid"), and the values cannot tell the readings apart because AI_ENEMY and AI_STAYING are both 4. The disassembly fixes the positions — it pops in registered order — but what it does with the mask was not read to the end, and two sites is not worth guessing.

16 faults, 16 caught, two of them only after the tests grew: a player bound with no upper half is invisible until 257, and a presence rule that treats “no node” as node zero is invisible until something asks about node zero.
The teleport pair, and the attribute that says which end is which (done)Teleport::destination/0 (20 sites) and Teleport::Traverse/1 (6) — 26 sites, two fields on the object header, kWorldVersion 22 → 23 and kStateVectorVersion 7 → 8. Coverage 33,173 → 33,199; names with no body 263 → 261; sites with no body 792 → 766.

The bodies are trivial and the question was where the value comes from. destination is a 16-bit id read off the instance; the recon that mapped this family recorded “the pairing is per-instance world state and must round-trip — where the id is authored is not established”. It is authored, and a census over all 28 shipped map.obj.xml documents says how: a teleport's <scriptobj> carries destination_set, which names the far teleport's settlement by that settlement's id attribute. Three things hold in all 54 teleports and together they make it resolvable — every teleport is inside a settlement, no settlement holds two, and the relation is symmetric in 54 of 54. The executable stores one direction only, so a one-way pair would have been legal and simply does not occur.

The id is not the index, and that is the trap: the two differ on 97 settlements across the install, while MapObject::settlement is an index. A reader that subscripted with the attribute would pair the wrong teleports on a real map and the right ones on any fixture where the two happened to line up — so the test's document uses settlement ids 7, 3 and 9 at positions 0, 1 and 2, and puts a settlement with no teleport in the middle.

Traverse moves nothing. It ORs the player's bit into a mask on the instance; the six shipped sites do the movement themselves with SetPos and then call it on both ends of the pair. So it is a record, not an action, and what reads it is still open — FindTeleport is the likely consumer and has not been read to the end. The original's bounds check has no lower half: player - 1 is tested against 16 with a signed compare only, so Traverse(0) indexes one stride below the player table. That is a fault rather than a behaviour and is guarded here instead of mirrored.

The golden trace moved, deliberately. Both fields are hashed — a script reads the pair and then moves a unit through it, so two peers that disagreed would move it somewhere else — and hashing them changes every turn hash in the reference scenario even though it contains no teleports. Twelve hashes regenerated with that as the reason.

16 faults, 16 caught, including the two that matter most for a version bump: dropping either field from the hash is caught by the golden check, and leaving kWorldVersion behind is caught by the round trip refusing a file written by the previous shape.
Two counters nobody reads, and a walk whose name is wider than the function (done)Settlement::SpentGoldOnArmy/1 (15 sites), Settlement::BestBarrack/1 (9), Settlement::SpentGoldOnTech/1 (2), Settlement::TSGetAllBarracks/0 (2) — 28 sites, all of it real behaviour and no new state at all. Coverage 33,145 → 33,173; names with no body 267 → 263; sites with no body 820 → 792; blocked scripts 213 → 211.

The two counters are Env and nothing else. Both bodies are eleven instructions: format a key, read it, add the argument, write it back, under /<root>/Settlement<id>/GoldSpentOnArmy<owner> with the owner as its zero-based index — the field Obj::player reads and then adds one to, without the add. No clamp, no cap, and no effect on the settlement's gold: the spending is ExecCmd and Research, and these record intent so other AI scripts can see what a settlement's income is already committed to. 17 writes across the installation and no reads. What reads them is Settlement::GoldSpentOnArmy/GoldSpentOnTech, registered beside the writers and called by nothing, so those two stay unbound on ShowNotes's rule while their existence is what makes this stored rather than discarded — Unit::user's test. Cheap because Env is already scoped by settlement, already saved and already hashed.

BestBarrack is an argmin whose cut-off is its own seed. The running best starts at the argument, so BestBarrack(100) is “any barrack” and BestBarrack(5) is “one that is not already backed up” under one rule rather than two. The queue length it minimises is [b+0x98], which is exactly what Obj::CmdCount() returns, so the two cannot disagree; the comparison is unsigned and ties go to the earlier building.

Two things the recon had backwards, and the disassembly settles both. 0x004403a0 is not a liveness test: it is eight IsHeirOf calls against eight class ids, with the two commonest results cached in globals. And it matters that it is names rather than cpp_class — the shipped content puts CVXBarrack on every temple, the druid house and two monuments, so a C++ test would let BestBarrack train soldiers out of a temple. That is why BestArena and BestTemple exist separately. The other correction is the damage guard: [b+0x204] != 3 is not a guess about “under construction”, it is Building::IsBroken, a field this engine has had modelled, hashed and saved since the object header was written — the same predicate read from the other side.

TSGetAllBarracks keeps the ruins and BestBarrack does not, which is the one place the two walks disagree: the list test is the building bit plus the eight names, with no downcast and no damage tier. Both shipped callers only sum CmdCount across it, which is what makes the difference invisible to them and worth a test here.

17 faults, 15 caught, and the two that survived are both worth more than a test. One was dead code of my own: a match_all guard copied from the walk above it, under a comment claiming an unresolved class name widens to everything. It does not — ClassFilter::parse leaves it at count == 0 with match_all false, which already matches nothing, and six of the eight names are absent from most graphs so that is the common path rather than the edge. The guard is gone and the property it was pretending to hold is asserted directly. The other is unfalsifiable and now says so: Barrack against GBarracks cannot be told apart by any test, because ClassGraph::lookup resolves an id and an altid alike and one class carries both. It is the executable's spelling, which is the only ground there is.
The largest name in the census, and the guard that would have silenced it (done)Hero::TSAdvHeroSkills/2 — 68 sites, the biggest single unimplemented name left, and none of its behaviour is native. Coverage 33,077 → 33,145; names with no body 268 → 267; sites with no body 888 → 820. Blocked scripts stay at 213: all 68 sites are eleven race tactic scripts that have other blockers too.

It is a trampoline, and this file already had the machine. 0x00426c00 resolves a .vs file on a player’s AI search path and runs it synchronously against the arguments already on the stack — the same shape as Settlement::GetEconomyScript, which run_script_now has served since the AI domain landed. The disassembly names the file itself: three literals, the helper’s name, a “called for an uninitialized or invalid object” guard message, and “Error running script!”. So the behaviour is a shipped script this project already reads and compiles, and the C++ is thirteen lines. The player is the hero’s owner, not the caller’s — GetTacticScript beside it reads the running script’s player and this one does not — which takes two profiles carrying two copies of one file to make visible at all, and has a test that does that.

What had to be fixed first was not in the AI at all. gbr.exe registers Size/0 four times — Squad, SquadList, IntArray, StrArray — and member lookup here is case-insensitive, so one body answers for all four. It answered for one: everything that was not a SquadList returned 0 rather than refusing. That is the collision sim/squad.hpp had already written the rule for (a branch inside the existing body, never a second define), and the branch was the half nobody wrote.

Two silent bugs fall out of it, and one of them was about to become sixty-eight. The shipped skills helper opens with a length guard on its array; with size answering 0 it would have returned on its first line, and binding TSAdvHeroSkills would have cleared 68 call sites while no hero ever gained a skill — strictly worse than the trap it replaced. The other two are already in the shipping build: 17 of the 18 Size sites read a Squad, eleven of them in SQUADMONITOR.VS (sq.food < sq.Size * 3 / 2, needed = sq.Size * dist / 700), so the AI was told every army it owns is empty; and BUILD_CATAPULT.VS’s race tally loop is bounded by an array’s size, so it ran zero times and every AI catapult in the game came out race 0.

12 faults, 11 caught, and the twelfth is annotated where it lives. The arity check is belt and braces on get_script_common’s precedent: the registry keys on arity and the VM pushes exactly the declared count, so nothing that reaches the body can have any other. Two survivors on the first pass were the same mistake in the test rather than in the code — a refusal’s value is a default-constructed 0 and reads as the right answer through as_integer() alone, which is precisely the two cases that are about answering rather than refusing.
Seventeen call sites that were filed under the wrong subsystem (done)Squad::Eval/0 (9 sites), Squad::Count/1 (8), Squad::SrcGAIKA/0 (4), Squad::DestGAIKA/0 (2), Squad::OrderDest/0 (2) — 25 sites. The census had merged two of them into the GAIKA family and they are not there. The case-insensitive member lookup put eval/0 and count/1 beside GAIKA::Eval's four arities and GAIKA::Count, where they looked like part of the map-graph block that is still blocked on the node table. They are not overloads of those: Squad::Eval (0x00421120) is a zero-argument property returning the squad's strength — the input to GAIKA::Eval — and Squad::Count (0x00427980) counts members by class. Neither needs the map graph, and moving them cost 17 sites out of the largest blocked family in the census.

Three of the four GAIKA fields on a squad were already declared and saved, next to the AIDest an earlier block bound; the entry points are one instruction apart in the executable and one template here. OrderDest and AIDest are the two ends of one decision — what the squad was last ordered to against what its AI is working toward — and GS_SIEGE.VS compares them to decide whether to re-order, with the comment beside the line saying exactly that. The test gives all four different values, because reading the neighbouring uint16 is the mistake actually available.

Squad::Eval answers 0 and the header says why. It is a stored field, not a computed one, and nothing in this engine writes it — the standing ai_dest beside it already has. Three of the nine readers branch on it, so a permanent zero makes the AI choose conservatively every time; it is bound anyway because the alternative is a trap that stops SQUADMONITOR.VS at its 479 call sites rather than letting it run and choose. kSectionVersion 3 → 4, kStateVectorVersion 6 → 7.

A setter for a field that was already there, and two that were not (done)Settlement::AllowCapture/1 (7 sites), Building::GetUITarget/0 (3), GetSettlements/2 (3), Druid::SetSummoningDeath/1 (3), Druid::IsSummoningDeath/0 (2), Building::SetUITarget/1 (1) — 19 sites, and blocked scripts 225 → 213. AllowCapture needed no state at all, and the check is what shows it. 0x005c1ce0 stores its argument at [settlement + 0x3c]; Settlement::CanBeCaptured (0x00424ff0) loads [edi + 0x3c]. One word, a setter and a getter — so the can_be_captured this engine already seeds from the anchor's class property is the field the script writes, and the class value is its initial value rather than a separate thing. The test asserts what that buys: is_valid_capture_target answers differently on the next line.

GetSettlements is misnamed twice over. The string is a class filter, not a settlement name, and the list holds the matching settlements' central buildings — every caller downcasts with .AsBuilding() and asks the building for its settlement. And player 0 is a wildcard, read off the body rather than guessed: it decrements the argument and passes a 0xffff placeholder, so zero becomes "no player filter" instead of "player minus one". STONEHENGE_STARVATION.VS sweeps the whole map with it.

The two new fields are both hashed, and the golden trace moved because of it. ObjectState::ui_target is the player's manual target for a tower — the one field here the interface writes and the simulation reads, and CATAPULT_TOWER_GUARD.VS re-reads it inside its volley loop so a player can retarget mid-shot. That a click starts it does not make it per-screen: the click becomes a replicated command, where a selection is per-screen for exactly the opposite reason.
