20 faults, 19 caught first pass. The survivor was the version bump itself — nothing in the suite refused a version-21 world, so the only thing pinning it was a comment. The round-trip test now rewrites the section's version word and requires the load to fail.
One counter, two routes, and a doc comment naming the wrong function (done)ItemUsed/1 (12 sites), GetUseCount/0 (10), Item::use_count/0 (3), Item::SetUseCount/1 (3), Obj::max_items/0 (3) — 31 sites, and blocked scripts 231 → 225. Names with no body 284 → 279.

Two of them take no argument naming an item. GetUseCount() and ItemUsed(n) read the running frame's This, cast it to the item interface and follow the item's own handle; here that is the running script's owner, which is the same thing, because every shipped item script is started on the item it belongs to. Not an item is 0 and a silent return, never a refusal — HEALING WATER.VS runs straight on into if (nHealth == 0).

ItemStore::use's doc comment said it was ItemUsed(n). It is not, and the two rules genuinely differ. 0x00534a30 is count -= n; if (count <= 0) destroy — the count written before the test, no clamp, and no consumable rule at all. use destroys only an item whose definition declares usecount > 0, and 153 of the dumps' 170 items carry usecount="0", so the difference is the common case rather than an edge: ItemUsed(0) on one of those destroys it, which is exactly how HEALING WATER.VS's zero-healing exit says consume me. So ItemStore has both now, and use's comment says plainly that its one-charge rule is fn_use_item's assumption — Obj::UseItem (0x00426480) takes a string and does not touch [item+0x28] anywhere in its body. The assumption is left standing and named rather than quietly changed on another function's evidence.

The member pair misses in two different ways and that is the one thing worth a test: an unresolvable handle is -1, a handle that resolves to something that is not an item is 0. Item::SetUseCount is a plain store without the clamp ItemStore::set_use_count applies, so CMERCENARY_ONDIE.VS's purse can go negative and read back.

16 faults, 12 caught first pass. Two survivors were fixture gaps and were closed. One was answered by deleting the code: an "is the ambient This actually an item" test that no fault could reach, because the store's own guards already answer 0 and false for a handle that names no item — a branch nothing can reach reads as a claim the engine is not making, which is the m_global_spell precedent. The fourth is annotated instead: the original writes the negative count before destroying the object, and nothing can ever read it back, so "not clamped" is transcribed rather than tested and the header says why.
Ten small ones, and the two that are correct as nothing (done)UserNotification/4 (14 sites), GetRaceStr/1 (5), Obj::IsMilitary/0 (5), Obj::IsSentry/0 (5), InvalidateDamageFormulaParams/0 (5), GetRaceStrPref/1 (4), Obj::raceStrPref/0 (3), CLAMP/3 (3), cls/0 (3), Obj::raceStr/0 and Obj::raceStrPrefLow/0 (1 each) — 49 sites, no new world state anywhere, and blocked scripts 239 → 231. Names with no body 295 → 284.

A trap is not a no-op, and that is why the two presentation entry points are bound. UserNotification's body compares its fourth argument against the local player's number and does nothing at all when they differ — so it is a per-screen effect a lockstep peer skips by construction, and a headless core is the permanent "they differ" case. Doing nothing is the faithful answer rather than a stub; refusing would stop SQUADMONITOR.VS, which calls it six times. cls() clears the HUD's message lines and has no registered getter, so it stores nothing for the same reason. Both are tested on the state hash, on both sides of the calls, because neither has a return value to assert.

InvalidateDamageFormulaParams is a no-op with a reason. Its whole body writes zero to one global: a the cached damage-formula constants are stale flag, called by ONFINISH_RESEARCH.VS after each of five researches that change a formula input. There is no cache here to invalidate — DamageInputs is flat and pure by construction, "it takes no world, no object, and no hidden state" — and the comment says so, which is what will make this wrong deliberately rather than silently if combat ever starts caching.

The race strings are one eight-entry table read three ways, and the disassembly confirms both tables this project already carried, including the two that look like typing errors and are not: M for Imperial Rome and T for Germany. They are engine identifiers, not shipped text — EnvReadInt(AIPlayer, GetRaceStr(nRace) + "UnitsEnabled"), cmd = "hirehero" + GetRaceStrPref(nRace) — so rule 1 is not engaged. GetRaceStrPrefLow/1 is registered by gbr.exe and deliberately not bound: no shipped script calls it, only the member form, which is the ShowNotes rule.

CLAMP is not the idiom, and the difference is reachable. 0x00696070 returns at once when v < lo, never comparing hi; written as max(lo, min(v, hi)) it disagrees whenever the bounds cross. CLAMP(9, 5, 2) is 2 in the executable and 5 in the idiom.

22 faults, 21 caught first pass. The survivor made the bound comparisons inclusive, which agrees with the strict form everywhere except a value sitting exactly on a lo that is above hi — CLAMP(5, 5, 2), 2 against 5. One line, and 22 of 22.
The point table was never behind the projection (done)Building::GetPoint/2 — 32 sites, the largest name in the entrance/exit family. Coverage 32,921 → 32,953; blocked scripts 242 → 239; names with no body 296 → 295.

This family was recorded as blocked on an inverse that does not exist here. docs/formats/ent-xml.md said the stored x/y are screen offsets and that reading one back needs the inverse of a height-dependent projection, "which is why none of the four is implemented yet". 0x004dd150 does not project. It returns x verbatim and y scaled by 1448/1024 as a relative offset, and the height term cancels because an offset is a difference — the point and the object that owns it stand on the same ground. Every shipped caller adds the position back (aa1 = .pos + .GetPoint(3, inds[0])), and SENTRY_GUARD.VS uses the answer as a direction, which is consistent only with a relative vector.

Six more point types are named, from what asks for them rather than from a registered entry point, and marked in ent-xml.md as the weaker evidence it is: 1 enter points, 2 the two ends of a gate's line, 3 patrol stations, 8 a facing marker, 10 ram impact points, 17 gate hinges. Six of the sixteen remain unknown.

The sentinel has to be exact. The dominant corpus shape is a walk that terminates on it — RAM_ATTACK.VS counts n upward through GetPoint(17, k) until pt.x comes back -32768 — so a miss, a type the class declares none of, an out-of-range index and an object with no entity all answer (-32768, -32768) and nothing else.

One known and deliberate difference from the original, written into the code: it walks all 64 table slots rather than stopping at the count, so a class with fewer has zero-filled slots reading as type 0 at the origin. This table holds only the authored points. No shipped site asks for type 0, so the two agree everywhere the content goes, and minting 64-count phantom points to match would be reproducing a buffer rather than a behaviour.

14 faults, 13 caught, and the survivor is one no test can catch: 362/256 is the same function as 1448/1024 under integer division, so substituting it changes no answer. The wider pair is written because it is the pair 0x004dd150 uses; the comment says so rather than leaving the fault looking like a gap.
One bit, two documents, and a claim that the map could not author it (done)Unit::GetFlags/1 — 5 sites, and the sole blocker of TOWNHALL_AUTOTRAIN.VS, which is 282 call sites and the largest one-blocker script in the corpus. Blocked scripts 243 → 242; names with no body 297 → 296.

Not a getter. 0x005d7e30 returns bool: it resolves the receiver, tests [obj+0x194] & mask and setnes the answer, and the registration's return type agrees. Two gates and both are silent — an unresolvable handle prints into the discard sink and answers false, and there is no liveness test, which SQUADMONITOR.VS relies on when it asks this of a squad leader that may have died since the list was built.

The interesting half is that two of this project's own documents disagreed and neither knew it. sim/system.hpp named bit 18 of the second flag word no_ai and said the map file does not carry it, so it "starts false on every object". docs/formats/map.md listed UnitFlags=0x00040000, on 14,586 of 16,171 units, among five bits with "no visible correlate". Same bit. The correlation settles the reading and is not subtle: it is clear on every wild animal in the retail install — 226 deer, 207 crows, 101 wolves, 80 fish, 46 eagles, 25 boars, without one exception — and set on the soldiers. A campaign places its troops under the mission script, which hands them to the AI with SetNoAIFlag(list, false) when it wants them fighting on their own; wildlife is the AI's from the first tick and no script ever claims it. Loading the flag false would have enrolled every soldier a map places into the town hall's training list on turn one.

A second unexplained bit falls out with it. 0x00400000, bit 22, occurs on 46 units and every one is a flying unit — which is the bit this engine had already named in_air from 0x0051bd20 and recorded as something nothing sets. So 46 birds start their map airborne. Both crow scripts are safe under that: their two branches differ in a step length and an animation index, not in what they ask of the engine. map.md's five unknown UnitFlags bits are three.

The word is composed, not stored, and the limit is written down. Seven bits of [obj+0x194] are modelled here; the three the map authors and nothing explains — 17, 21 and 25 — are not in the composition, so a mask naming one is answered false rather than correctly. Every shipped call site passes UNITFLAG_NOAI and no other mask, in all five places across three scripts.

20 faults injected, 17 caught first pass, and all three survivors were one shape: a fixture in which two rules agree. Returning the masked word instead of the bool passed because every assertion read != 0; swapping the noselect and minimap bits passed because the test set neither; and taking bit 25 for in_air passed because the airborne bird in the fixture carried 22 and 25 together, as all 46 of the real ones do. A sixth object carrying bit 25 alone — which 207 of the install's flying units do, standing on the ground — and exact-value assertions catch all three. 20 of 20 after.
The eight call sites that were never going to work (done)No new entry points; a correction to the target itself. Joining gbr.exe's 1,498 registrations against this project's census leaves every remaining name matched — same receiver, same arity, not one disagreement in 289 names — except eight call sites whose names reach no registrar at all. They trapped by name on the shipping build the player owns, and they will trap here. So 100% is not the target; 33,957 of 33,965 is, and the ceiling is 99.976%.

All five are development leftovers. Choose/2, Choose/3, ListFolder/1 and ListFolderEx/2 are a level-editor file picker in DBGPLAYSOUND.VS and DBGRUNSCRIPT.VS; the strings "Choose" and "ListFolder" are still in the executable's pool, orphaned, and "ListFolderEx" is not. GetSelection/0 and Obj::script/0 are both in DEBUG_DUMP.VS, on lines it cannot reach. UpgradeEfc/0 and CancelEfcUpgrade/0 are the entire body of two one-line settlement scripts, and EFC does not appear in the string pool in any form — not as a name, not as a diagnostic.

The work list was recommending two of them. --scripts ranks by fewest blockers, and the two settlement scripts are one-blocker rows whose blocker is the sole blocker of exactly one file each — so greedy set cover scored them as one-name, one-finished-file wins, which is precisely the recommendation a work list must not make. --block now leaves the five out and says so, --leaders marks the eight rows, and the summary prints the ceiling under the percentage it is a ceiling on.
One selection, and two questions that only look like they are about it (done)ClearSelection/1 (18 sites), Obj::Select/1 (11), SwapSelectedObj/2 (7), _GetSelection/0 (4), Obj::Deselect/0 (2), IsSelectionAssigned/1, WasSelectionAssigned/1 and Obj::_LastSelectionTime/0 (1 each) — 45 sites and 11 more scripts. Blocked scripts 254 → 243; names with no body 305 → 297. The whole model was already built and documented in sim/orders.hpp; what this block did was bind it, and find that three of the things the header asserted were wrong. All three are in the corrections table.

The player id is a guard, not an index — and that is why a selection could never have been sync state. Three of the entry points compare the argument's player record against the one the globals block keeps at +0x12c8 and return having done nothing when they differ. The no-argument Deselect, the only form the corpus calls, makes no comparison at all: it cannot, there being nobody to compare against.

Obj::Select has four gates and three are silent. An unresolvable handle prints "called for an uninitialized or invalid object" and a dead one prints "called for a dead object" — both into the discard sink, so both are an ordinary return here — then the local-player comparison, then SetNoselectFlag's bit at [obj+0x2c], which drops the object without a word. A trap on any of them would stop CREATE_GOLD_MULE_BIG.VS dead on the line after it builds the caravan.

_LastSelectionTime is a stamp on the object, and the selection-changed handler writes it for the whole selection. 0x005e7af8 runs over every member of the new set on every change, not only over the newcomer, which is why it is a call of its own here (note_selection_changed) rather than something add does — the embedder that edits a selection directly, a rubber band or a shift-click, makes the same one call afterwards. It lives on SelectionTable and not on ObjectState because ObjectState is hashed and this legally differs between peers. That moves the selection section: its own version 1 → 2, and kStateVectorVersion 4 → 5. The golden trace does not move, because none of it is hashed.

And one entry point is deliberately left with no body. GetSelection/0 is a name gbr.exe does not register; its single call site could never have run. That is one site which will stay in the “declared, no body” column for good, and saying so is the point.

41 faults injected, 32 caught first pass. The survivors were the usual shape — a fixture in which two rules agree. A ClearSelection that indexed by its argument passed because the slot it would have cleared was empty; a reversed _GetSelection passed because the test counted the list and never read it; an unsorted stamp table round-tripped because there was only one stamp. One survivor is kept and annotated instead: stamping the selection ClearSelection has just emptied is a no-op either way, so the fault that adds the call back cannot be caught, and a surviving fault should say why rather than look like a gap.
What a settlement can pay for, and where it researches (done)Research/1 (41 sites in 27 scripts, the sole blocker of nine), CanAfford/1 and /2 (24), FindResearchLab/1 (17) — 82 sites and 11 more scripts. Blocked scripts 265 → 254; trap hits 16 → 13, distinct messages 10 → 9.

CanAfford does not answer a bool. The three registrations share one core, 0x00425a10, which answers how many of the thing the settlement could buy — the smallest of three quotients — and each caller compares that against 1 or against its own count. Two details are reproduced because they are visible: the population term is population - 1, so a settlement may not spend its last head, and the order matters — an early zero on population or gold returns before the food division ever runs.

The food reserve is zero, and that is a consequence rather than a choice. 0x00425890 computes it as Reserve > 0 ? ((GoldSpentOnArmy − ReservedAt) × Reserve) / 100 : 0, and all three are environment-store values under /<player>/Settlement<n>/… written by ReserveFor and StopReserving — two entry points this engine does not implement. With nothing reserving, the term is zero by the original's own arithmetic. The flag that selects it is carried anyway, so a reservation lands in one place.

The class-to-command edge runs from the command's side, and that is what was missing. A .sc.xml class lists its <method sig> bindings and never its commands; it is DATA\COMMANDS\*.XML that names the classes, in <src obj="GArena1"/> rows. CommandDef carries them now, matched against the class tree — and 0x0042d010 is exactly that walk: for each building of the settlement, take its class and look for this command's row in the class's list.

One finder, two callers, one flag. FindResearchLab passes 0 and Settlement::Research passes 1, and the flag is an idle test on the building — so the script-visible finder names a lab that is already busy and the one about to queue work does not. Both call sites are in the same executable function and the difference is one argument.

Every refusal is a return. An unknown upgrade prints "No such upgrade %s" into the sink that is a bare ret in retail; an unaffordable one and a settlement with no lab print nothing at all. ESH_BUILDARMY.VS is written for it — it asks CanResearch and CanAfford first and calls Research only when both say yes, so the internal checks are belt and braces from the corpus's side and load bearing from the executable's.

17 faults injected, 15 caught first pass. One survivor was a missing arity: the unknown-name case was tested at arity 1 and the same name at arity 2 is a different body. The other is the count's final clamp, which no caller can see — a negative quotient needs a negative store, and the population and gold terms return zero before they can produce one — and is annotated as belt and braces rather than tested.
The stonehenge's dispatch side, and a template that claimed nothing (done)GlobalSpellStart/2 (9 sites, the sole blocker of seven scripts) and GlobalSpellStop/0 (1). Half the stonehenge was already here. This block is the dispatch side, and the RE that went into it found the reading rather than the code: the five *_DISPATCH.VS scripts are one line each, and STONEHENGE_IDLE.VS is the whole mechanism in eleven lines — with n mages in range it adds a point of stamina a second and comes back sooner the more there are, Sleep(12500 / n - 250).

The guard is looser than its own diagnostic. GlobalSpellStart casts the receiver to CVXTownHall and stops — there is no IsHeirOf("Stonehenge"), unlike the two counters, which make both tests. The message it prints on failure says "called for an object that is not a stonehenge", and RESEARCH.VS shows why that is intent rather than check: it calls th.GlobalSpellStart(gsTribute, .player) on a town hall, and ONFINISH_RESEARCH.VS stops it there.

Validated and dropped, because nothing reads what it writes. The pair maintains a record on the global-spell manager, and the only registered reader — Building::GetGlobalSpell — has zero call sites in all 885 scripts and cannot distinguish "no spell" from gsWindOfWisdom anyway, because 0x0052c260 answers 0 for both. The two research scripts track their own pairing in the environment store (EnvWriteObj(.player, "TributeBuilding", th)) rather than asking the engine. So storing the record would be state with no consumer, which is the test ObjectState::user passes and this fails. The original's three guards are written out in the header and deliberately not reproduced: with nothing stored, none of them can change an answer, and a guard that cannot is validation theatre.

And the fault sweep deleted a template. The explored map (done)ExploreArea/2 (24 sites), ExploreAll/0 (9), IsExplored/2 (5), ExploreCircle/3 (3), GetUnexploredPoint/1 (1) — 42 sites and 21 more scripts. Blocked scripts 293 → 272; trap hits 22 → 17, distinct messages 12 → 11. A new domain, a new system and a new save section, and nothing new in the hash.

This was a validated no-op with a paragraph saying why. sim/area.hpp carried: "reveals fog of war for a player … there is no fog state in this simulation to reveal. Implementing it would mean inventing that state, so it stays declared." The state was not invented. Every number in it is read off gbr.exe: IsExplored (0x004c6820) hands the point and player - 1 to 0x00515950, whose whole body is data[(y >> 10) * stride + (x >> 11)], a half-word select on (x >> 10) & 1, and (half >> (2 * slot)) & 3. So a cell is 1024 world units square, two cells share a dword, and a cell holds two bits for each of eight players.

Sixteen players, eight fog slots — and the short circuit says what that means. 0x00515958 answers true for a slot at or past the eighth: a player above the eighth has no fog and sees the whole board. The missing-manager path is the opposite and comes first: with no grid at all, nothing is explored for anybody, including that player.

The player argument's base took the longest and is now settled. ExploreCircle and ExploreArea read a mask at [globals + 0xfc0 + player * 0x320] and bounds-check 0 ≤ player < 16, which reads 0-based — against IsExplored, which decrements. (This sentence used to name RevealHiddenEnemyUnits as a third member of that group and it is neither: 0x004c8460 reads the word after that mask, +0xfc4, which is a side mask rather than an exploration one, and it has no bounds check at all — it multiplies and indexes. Two words of one record, two different questions.) 0x0051d5b0 settles it: it computes the local player's record as globals + 0x12cc + player * 0x320, so the array begins at 0x12cc and 0xfc0 + p * 0x320 is record[p - 1] + 0x14. The writers index the same 1-based way the reader does, and it is the bounds check that is off by one at both ends — it admits 0, which reads one record before the array, and rejects 16, which is a real player. Reproduced as the indexing rather than as the arithmetic: a read out of bounds is a fault, not a behaviour.

Four states, and only one distinction anything can see. The circle stamp (0x00516640) is a state machine over the two bits — it acts on 0 and on 2 and leaves 1 and 3 alone — so the field is never seen / seen before / becoming visible / visible, and the middle two are the fog renderer's fade. No host entry point can tell them apart: IsExplored is != 0 and there is no other reader. One bit per player per cell is stored, and the header says why rather than carrying three states it cannot observe.

Not hashed, and saved. exploration is one of the four channels the shipped build leaves at zero in all nine dumps, so FogSystem::hash is deliberately empty — and the map is saved anyway, because it is a function of where every unit has ever stood and no save carries that history. The system runs last, after movement, so it stamps the ground a unit reached rather than the ground it left.

And the block named four entity point types on the way past. docs/formats/ent-xml.md has carried "the meaning of each point type is unknown … the single largest gap in this format … requires the .VS host API" since the format was written. Building::GetPoint(type, n) walks a 64-entry table of {x, y, type} on the class's visual record, and four registered entry points are that function with a constant: 12 enter/exit, 13 water source, 14 goods source, 15 fix site. None is implemented — the stored offsets are screen coordinates and 0x005c11a0 converts through (pos.y * 181) >> 8 - terrain_height(pos), an isometric scale with a height term, so reading one back as a world point needs the inverse of a height-dependent projection. The four names are recorded where the gap was.

25 faults injected, 21 caught first pass. Three survivors were fixtures again — a held unit whose holder stood where the corner would have been revealed, a scout whose sight already covered its own cell, and a zero-sight object nothing owned. The fourth is an unreachable guard and is annotated: kNoPlayer is 0xFF, which is a slot far past the eighth, and every writer already drops one.
The fog switch, the minimap, and a tool that finds families (done)SetFog/1 (20 sites), HideZoomMap/0 (8), _ZoomMapLastShownTime/0 (1), ShowZoomMap/0 (0, bound) — 29 sites, 9 more scripts, and the biggest of them is 574 call sites (5_Great_Loses_German map 2 sequence 1), with 345, 197 and 126 behind it. Nearly 1,400 call sites of mission content stood behind four pieces of chrome.

A new tool picked this block, and it is the third ranking this project has needed. --leaders ranks by call sites; --scripts ranks by scripts a name blocks alone; neither can see a family. Eight accessors that always appear together finish nothing until the eighth lands, so every one of them scores zero on both rankings and the cluster never surfaces. host_coverage.py --block N is a greedy set cover over the same join: take the name that finishes the most files, then ask again. It surfaced the fog-of-war family — six names, twenty-six scripts — which the two older rankings show only as one member at seven.

SetFog is display and is not the explored map. 0x006a51e0 allocates a 16-byte command object, writes the argument at +0xc and posts it; nothing on that path is the per-player exploration state IsExplored reads. Lifting the fog for a cutscene explores nothing and putting it back un-explores nothing. It joins ViewState beside the camera, which is where the last chrome block put the same kind of value and for the same reason.

The minimap has a getter, and it is what makes the pair worth storing. _ZoomMapLastShownTime() answers -1 for never, and its one shipped site is DATA\TUTORIALS\GENERALADVICE3.VS, which sleeps 140 seconds and then asks if (_ZoomMapLastShownTime() == -1) before offering the "Minimap" hint — has this player never opened it? That is Unit::user's test again: a registered getter against a registered setter. ShowZoomMap has zero call sites and is bound anyway for exactly that reason, and only opening stamps — if hiding did, all eight shipped HideZoomMap() calls would tell the tutorial the player had opened the minimap and the hint would never appear. ToggleZoomMap/0, ToggleFog/0, BlockMiniMap/1, MiniMap/0, SetMiniMapRect/1 and BuildMiniMap/1 are all registered in gbr.exe with no sites and no getter that needs them, and stay unbound.

10 faults injected, 9 caught first pass. The survivor was an unbound HostContext::view answering 0 rather than -1 — a case no test reached, and one that matters: zero is a time the tutorial reads as shown. One re-injected, caught.
Three field reads, and the one that was not (done)GetUnitsOnBoard/0 (22 sites), class/0 (22), SetEntering/1 (21) — 65 sites and 9 more scripts, six of them mission sequences of 69 to 84 call sites each. kWorldVersion 20 → 21 for one flag bit; the golden trace does not move, because nothing in the reference scenario sets it.

The one that was not is Center/0. It sits at 23 sites with no body — the exact shape of a cheap win, and the coverage ranking cannot see which receiver those sites use. A note left beside m_rect_center by an earlier pass is what stopped it: the rect body was written and deliberately left unbound because every shipped .Center is on a GAIKA (g.Center, sq.AIDest.Center), and binding it would answer all 23 with "receiver is not a rect" — a refusal that claims the entry point exists, where they trap today as host_not_implemented, which is true. That is the whole reason a decision like this is written next to the code rather than in a commit message, and the note now says so.

GetUnitsOnBoard copies where Settlement::Units aliases. 0x005c89f0 allocates a fresh list and copy-constructs from the holder's deque through 0x00438330 — the same plain forward range copy Squad::Units makes, not the refcount-and-push the settlement roster makes. So a script may ClearDead what it gets back and the ship's manifest is unharmed, which is what 5_Great_Battles_Britain map 3 does. The holder is the object at ship + 1, which is the invariant spawn_ship establishes and the dumps show from the other side; membership is then ObjectState::holder, the same back-link every other holder question here uses.

class is the class's id, not its cpp_class and not its altid — EVALRECRUIT.VS's if (sq.Leader.class == "BVikingLord") is what settles it, and a miss is the empty string rather than a refusal. SetEntering is one bit of the second flag word, write-only from script and bound anyway on SetNoselectFlag's precedent; UNIT_MOVE_ENTER.VS sets it before its GotoEnter and clears it after.

12 faults injected, 7 caught first pass. All five survivors were fixtures that could not tell one rule from another: no save round-trip for the new bit (three of them), a non-holder neighbour that was holding nothing, and a ship whose crew was the only held object in the world. A second ship, a barracks with somebody in it and three lines in the save test caught all six on the re-run.
The four that control a script (done)Progress/0 and Progress/1 (23 sites in 23 scripts), AIStart/3 (20), KillScript/0 (13) — 56 sites, 31 more scripts that run, and not one of them needed new state. Blocked scripts 342 → 311; trap hits 30 → 23, distinct messages 14 → 13.

KillScript() is two instructions. 0x0061e730 is mov eax, 2; ret. The host-call return value is a status the interpreter switches on — 0 carry on, 1 suspend — and 2 is stop this coroutine. No argument and no receiver: the script it kills is always the one calling it, and Scheduler::kill is already documented safe on a script that is running right now. Its eight scripts use it as an early return out of a nested loop, seven of them in 4_Great_Battles_Egypt map 4 alone.

Progress is a suspend, and the scripts are written around it being one. Registered suspending twice over; both bodies read the interpreter's first-call byte, stamp the object's progress bar and return status 1 on the way in, and clear the bar and return 0 on the resume. So BUILDING_REPAIR.VS's while (.health < .maxhealth) { .Progress(); .Heal(...); } is a loop that ticks once per bar rather than a busy spin. The two forms differ in one guard, and it is the whole reason there are two: the no-argument form takes the running command's execdelay and, when that is zero, does not suspend at all (0x005ae952); the argument form suspends unconditionally, zero included, which is what BARRACK_TRAIN.VS's .Progress((.cmddelay * perc) / 100) needs. The bar's two endpoints are display and are not stored — nothing in the registered surface reads either back, and a field with no reader is not state here. One quirk read and deliberately not reproduced: the original routes the duration through a global at [0x00a77eac], so a Progress() whose command lookup fails suspends for whatever the previous call left there. That is a stale global, not a rule.

AIStart's absence was measured over the wrong corpus. sim/ai.hpp carried a section headed "No shipped script calls AIStart" — "zero call sites across all 577 .vs files, which is why script/host_surface.cpp, an inventory derived from call sites, does not declare it" — and both halves were true of data.pak and false of the installation. The three-argument form has 20 sites, every one inside a map container, and it was the sole blocker of ten scripts. 4_Great_Battles_Egypt map 4 hands player 4 to the AI when its ambush fires; eleven of the twenty pass GetDifficulty() rather than a literal. Nothing new was needed to answer it: AiSystem::start has been written, hashed and tested since the AI domain landed and was reachable only from C++. The whole of this thread is argument handling — player validated 1..16 and passed 0-based, profile uppercased, difficulty 0..2 with the core's own increment-then-validate — and the discipline that every refusal is a return, not a trap, so a mission naming a profile the installation does not have keeps running with one fewer AI.

12 faults injected, 11 caught first pass. The survivor is not a missing test: the argument form's negative clamp changes nothing any caller can see, because the original has no clamp and a wake-up in the past is ready at once either way. Annotated as belt and braces rather than tested, which is the standing rule for a guard the interface wants and the behaviour cannot show. One re-injected fault (a zero duration refused) was caught by the test written for it.
What a verifier reads about state (done)SetParryMode/1 (31 sites), IsVeryBroken/0 (10), IsBroken/0 (9), GetSacrifice/0 (5), IsInvisibility/0 (3), TimePastLastAttack/0 (3), GetParryMode/0 (2) — 63 sites and 31 more scripts that no longer die. Blocked scripts 373 → 342; data.pak crosses 93%. Three new pieces of hashed, saved state, so kWorldVersion 19 → 20 and kStateVectorVersion 3 → 4, and the golden trace moves — twelve hashes, in this diff, because two fields joined the world hash.

IsBroken and IsVeryBroken are two different mechanisms, and they can disagree in both directions. IsBroken (0x004dd200) is [building+0x204] == 3, a stored tier; IsVeryBroken (0x00424f90) is health < maxhealth / 10, computed on the spot with a compiler's divide-by-ten. So a wall dropped to 20% is broken and not very broken, and one repaired from 5% to 12% is still broken while the ratio has already cleared — which is why five shipped verifiers write b.IsBroken() || b.IsVeryBroken() and a sixth writes &&.

The tier is state because it has hysteresis. 0x004db3d0 computes pct = health * 100 / maxhealth, picks a raw tier against BuildingStateThreshold0/1/2, and then refuses a one-step change unless the reading has cleared the boundary by BuildingStateHysteresis as well — pct <= threshold[old] - h going down, pct >= threshold[new] + h coming back. A jump of more than one step is taken at once. CONST.INI ships 75 / 50 / 25 / 3 under [GamePlay], and gbr.exe refuses to start without them ("Error reading required value : BuildingStateThreshold0"). Two buildings at the same health can legitimately be in different tiers, which is what makes it a field and not a function.

Parry mode is gated on a class capability, on both sides. Both bodies test [unit+0x198] & 1, which is bit 0 of the 64-bit specials mask — and entry 0 of the 36-name table is Parry, the same table Unit::HasSpecial and sim/globals.cpp already shared. A unit whose class does not offer it cannot enter parry mode and reads back 0 whatever the field holds. UNIT_ENTER_PARRY_MODE_VERIFY.VS asks both questions in that order, so the gate is visible from the corpus as well as from the executable. What is not reproduced is the effect object: entering also spawns a class-Parry sacrifice from the 28-entry factory at 0x00821180 and stores its handle at [unit+0x1c8]. That factory is worth recording for its own sake — 28 named effect classes, and it picks the 2 variant (DefensiveCry2, Assault2, Frenzy2) when the receiver's class radius exceeds 20, which is how a big unit gets a big effect.

TimePastLastAttack is about being attacked, and it is not TimeWithoutAttack. The two look like one field and are two: [unit+0x1a4], per-unit, written by SetLastAttackTime, is how long since I struck; [hero+0x2b8] is how long since anyone in my army was struck. The stamp is written by the virtual at vtbl + 0xa4, which 0x005d5faa calls as target->slot(attacker) — so the receiver is the victim, and 0x005dc23f writes onto [victim+0x170], which is Unit::hero. See the corrections table for the shipped comment that confirms it. A hero sneaks when nobody is shooting at it, which is exactly what HERO_SNEAK_VERIFY.VS's > 2500 asks.

Two entry points answer completely and answer nothing, and that is the reading. Unit::GetSacrifice's virtual is the always-invalid base (mov word [out], 0xffff) in every CVXUnit vtable in the executable; exactly one class overrides it, and that class is CVXDruid, reading the sacrifice it is serving at [druid+0x1cc]. This engine has no sacrifices to attach, so the answer is the base's — which is also what a druid standing next to a unit performing no ritual gets in the retail game. And IsInvisibility tests a class name the installation does not contain. Both are in the corrections table.

34 faults injected, 30 caught first pass. Three survivors were the same fixture problem as the last two blocks — a strict comparison with nothing sitting on the boundary, and a hysteresis band too narrow for the shipped thresholds to expose the single-step rule (measured now with a band wide enough to overlap the next threshold, which is legitimate because the rule is parameterised). The fourth was worse and worth naming: "a strike stamps the attacker's hero" survived because the fight was mutual — the victim struck back, so the wrong stamp landed on the right hero. The victim is unarmed now, and the attacker has a hero of its own whose clock is asserted never to move. Five re-injected, five caught.
What a verifier asks about the world (done)NearestObj/1 (19 sites), Settlement::ObjectsAround/1 (16), Settlement::Buildings/0 (9), Obj::AsTower/0 (3), Catapult::GetPointOnTarget/1 (3), Unit::EnemiesInSight/0 (2) — 52 sites, six entry points, and 22 scripts that no longer die. Blocked scripts 395 → 373.

Fifty-two call sites is the wrong way to measure this block. Eight of the twenty-two are 1_Great_Losses_Rome's mission sequences — 256, 253, 253, 253, 253, 251, 107 and 97 sites — and every one of them was held up by NearestObj alone, at two sites each. Over 1,700 call sites of one mission were dead behind nineteen. That is the ranking host_coverage.py --scripts exists to surface and the site ranking cannot.

AsTower is AsBuilding. Not "close enough": gbr.exe registers both names at body 0x005aa5e0, and the body's whole test is bit 23 of [obj+0x2c], the building bit. The two registrations differ only in the declared return type word — 0x19 Tower against 0x16 Building — which the compiler uses to decide which members the result may be sent and the call boundary never inspects. This tree recorded the opposite for a long time ("Tower has no distinct cpp_class, so there is nothing to test against"), which was the right observation about the class graph and the wrong conclusion about the entry point: the original does not test for a tower either. OUTPOST_ATTACK_VERIFY.VS reads the same way from the script side — it casts every building in the settlement and then asks IsHeirOf("FakeTower").

ObjectsAround is a fourth name for a collector this engine already had. 0x005c5b90 allocates a fresh list, resolves the class name, and calls Settlement::CollectUnits (0x005c58f0) with both of its flags set — which is SettlementScope::both, the mode sim/query.hpp recorded as being in the enumeration only "because the mode is one field and leaving a hole in it would be inventing a fourth encoding for a value the executable already spells 2". It has a caller now, under a different name and reached without a query object at all. The dedup that note describes is what makes a soldier standing inside two towers' sight healed once rather than twice.

Distance here is to the edge, and the two NearestObj overloads differ by one radius. The point form scores with 0x005a7900 — isqrt(dx² + dy²) − candidate.radius — and the object form with 0x005a77b0, the same thing less the asker's radius as well. The asker's radius is subtracted from every candidate alike, so it cannot reorder them; the one thing it can do is drag a candidate from just outside the running best's seed to just inside. That seed is 10,000,000 (0x00579576) and the comparison is jge skip, so ties keep the first member the query yields and a query whose every member is further than ten million away answers the invalid object rather than its nearest.

EnemiesInSight wants damage greater than one, not greater than zero. The target sweep's own armed clause is > 0 and this one is > 1 (0x005d530e) over the same field, Obj::attack. Both shipped sites are asking am I in a fight — HERO_RETREAT_VERIFY.VS is .EnemiesInSight() && pt.Dist(.posRH) > .sight/2 — and the bound is what keeps a wandering villager from pinning a hero in place. That same expression is also why the radius is WorldObject::sight and not the combat profile's shadow of it: one script reads both in one line, so they have to be one number.

Buildings snapshots where Units aliases, and the divergence is stated. 0x005c3d40 takes the same shortcut its twin does — refcount the vector at settlement + 0x60 and push it. All nine shipped sites only read, unlike the one Units site that can tell; AliasResolver hands back a vector<ObjectId> and a settlement's roll is vector<SettlementBuilding>, so aliasing would mean a parallel vector beside it — one fact in two places, which is the drift the alias exists to prevent.

The footprint arm of GetPointOnTarget is not reproduced, and the reason is that its candidate list would be a fiction. A unit target answers its own aim point; a building target walks up to 0x40 footprint offsets and returns the first whose edge distance falls inside [min_range, max_range]. This engine stamps a .pass footprint into the grid at load and keeps no per-object inverse, so the offsets would have to be invented. What is reproduced is the arm's decision over the one candidate that is not invented. The original's no-match fallback returns candidate zero — a raw, un-translated footprint offset, so a point near the map origin — which is almost certainly a slip and is not reproduced.

27 faults injected, 22 caught first pass. Four survivors were the same shape as the last block's: a test that could not tell one rule from another because the fixture made them agree — a tie-break with no tie, a held asker with nothing held, a hidden enemy with nothing hidden, and a profile sight equal to the class sight. The fifth is not a missing test but an unreachable guard, and is annotated as one: both producers feeding NearestObj's loop already drop what does not resolve, so no injected fault can reach its null check. Four re-injected, four caught.
The camera, the chrome and the control groups (done)View/2 (35 sites), ShowHint/3 (26), SetShortcutSel/3 (24), PlayMovie/1 (24), BlockUserInput/0 and UnblockUserInput/0 (34), ShowAnnouncement/2 and HideAnnouncement/1 (17), ViewPos/0 (7), ShowTutorial/3 (7), the three PlaySound forms (9), LockView/0 and UnlockView/0 (4) — 187 sites and 46 more scripts that no longer die. The whole install crosses 96%; the containers reach 99%; trap hits 47 → 30 and distinct messages 17 → 14.

Three of the nineteen are read back, and they are the whole of what this stores. ViewPos() reads what View() wrote, IsViewLocked() what LockView() wrote, GetShortcutSel() what SetShortcutSel() wrote — a registered getter against a registered setter, which is the test Unit::user passes and Unit::SetLastAttackTime fails. The shipped idiom is what makes the camera state rather than an event: p_View = ViewPos(); View(AreaCenter("A_View1"), true); …; View(p_View, false); appears in three of Zama's sequences — save the camera, take it somewhere for a cutscene, put it back.

It lives on HostContext, not the world, beside local_player and selections and for exactly their reason: the camera is the one thing two peers of a lockstep match are supposed to disagree about, and hashed state may not hold a value that legally differs between peers.

A control group is a copy, not a handle. SetShortcutSel(1, 1, NO_Scipio.GetObjList()) is handed an ObjList that belongs to the sequence that built it and dies with it; ctrl-1 outlives the sequence. Keeping the handle would leave the group pointing at a released pool entry the moment the mission's opening script ended.

What the write-only ones answer is a decision, not a recovery. PlayMovie is registered bool and ShowHint and ShowTutorial int, and all 57 shipped sites discard the result — so nothing in the corpus separates one answer from another. false and 0 are what a request that did not happen would give, and the header says so out loud rather than letting a later reader assume the values were read off the executable.

18 faults injected, 15 caught. All three survivors were the same shape — a test that could not tell "wrong" from "absent": a View that ignored its lock argument left the flag false at the end, which is what the restoring call wants; a group stored under the wrong player was indistinguishable while only one player had one; an out-of-range slot folded onto slot zero and nothing looked at slot zero. Four re-injected, four caught.
What a script asks the interface to show (done)CreateFeedback/2 (72 sites), rollover/2 (70), rollover/3 (15), rollover_desc/3 (1) — 158 sites, and 45 scripts that no longer die. Trap hits 60 → 47.

This block was chosen by a tool that did not exist that morning. Ranked by call sites these names are 4th and 5th; ranked by scripts they alone block they are the top two in the installation, and host_coverage.py --scripts is what says so. DATA\ITEMSCRIPTS\HEALING HERBS.VS is three lines long and its first statement is CreateFeedback("Heal", owner.AsUnit()): until this landed the script died there and healed nobody. That is the shape of most of the fifty — item and spell scripts that ask for a sparkle and then do the thing. The sparkle is not simulation state; dying before the healing is.

rollover is about the command, not the object. All five forms open by reading two std::string globals and only fall back to the object when the first is empty — and DATA\COMMANDS\*.XML carries exactly two display strings per row, rollover= on 384 of 404 and description= on 253. The form that takes no arguments at all is what settles it. So CommandDef grew the two fields and HostContext grew described_command, beside local_player and for its reason: which button the mouse is over legally differs between peers, and hashed state may not.

Two entry points share a signature and disagree about what it means. rollover/3 and rollover_desc/3 are both (Obj, str, bool); the middle argument is a class name in the first and a message in the second, and all 16 shipped sites agree — every rollover/3 site passes a variable called class or strUnit parsed out of cmdparam, and the one rollover_desc site passes "Already collected".

What is not reproduced, and why that is allowed. The retail formatter is a long string builder whose own literals are <imagetransp gameres/infobar/common/atack ico.bmp> and its companions, the headings Stats and Specials, and the word None. docs/legal.md rule 4 says a specification describes the bytes on disk, and this is not a format; what this composes is the pieces the row carries in the same markup vocabulary. Nothing in the simulation can tell: all 86 sites assign the result to reasonText, the out parameter of a group verifier, and this engine reads a verifier's return value and never its reason.

CreateFeedback stores nothing, on SetPlayerStatus's precedent, and the header names the seam it leaves: the day a renderer wants the effects it wants a per-turn queue drained by the presentation layer — an output, like SessionReport::traps — and not a field on the world, because two peers that draw different sparkles must still hash alike. 19 faults injected, 17 caught; both survivors were a missing assertion about order — the cost pieces' and the value CreateFeedback does not return.
The conversation layer (done)RunConv/1 (74 sites), Conversation::SetActor/2 (63), Conversation::Run/0 (54), Conversation::Init/1 (54), ConvResult/1 (5) — 250 sites, every one inside a map container, and the largest coherent block that was left. Containers 96% → 98%, the whole install 94% → 95%, the trap tally 62 hits → 60.

Two halves again, and the same split the notes have. A conversation is declared in Maps/<n>/Conversations/cnv<k>.conv.xml and run by a script, so the catalogue is configuration and the results — what each conversation last left behind for ConvResult — are world state, because a script branches on one and 3_Great_Battles_Alesia map 5 branches all the way to ChangeMap. New format spec: docs/formats/conv-xml.md, 110 documents, 260 phrases, all parsing.

The file name is not the conversation's name, and a name can contain a space. cnv1.conv.xml declares C_Conv2; the tutorial's are 1 Welcome, A Feed, B Stronghold. The first version of imcheck conv printed a space-separated report of exactly this data, which is a report nobody can parse.

The mode vocabulary is seven names and the data uses three. gbr.exe maps the string at 0x005067d0 — choice=0, random=1, first=2, end=3, cycle=4, cycle then first=5, cycle then random=6, an empty string is first, and an !error! sits beside them in the string table. The containers use first, end and choice. The other four are in the enumeration because a reader that defaulted an unknown mode to first would play the same line for ever and say nothing.

What settles the default candidate rule is a count, not a reading. followup="first" with no list means the next phrase in document order, and the conversation ends when there is none — because 98 of the 110 documents end on a first with nothing after it, and under any rescanning reading those 98 loop on their own opening line for ever.

\l, \g and \a are <, > and &. condition, action and return carry VS source inside an XML attribute, and those three characters cannot appear there. Five escapes in the whole corpus — 207 \n, 204 \', 5 \g, 4 \a, 2 \l — and not one XML character reference, so it is the authoring tool's convention rather than a second layer of XML. i \l ol.count is a real condition. The display strings carry \n too and are deliberately not decoded, which is the call NoteDefinition::text already makes.

Two departures, stated rather than hidden. A conversation finishes inside the call, where the original suspends until the window closes — the flag Run polls is set by the dialog, and neither a voice clip's length nor a click exists here; inventing a duration would invent the number that decides when every other script resumes. And choice takes the first eligible option, because there is nobody to ask and a draw would spend the world's generator on the player's question.

Thirty-nine faults injected, and the first pass left seven survivors that were all one missing test — the restore_view default (76 of 110 documents leave it out, and nothing in the simulation reads the flag), an out-of-order results table, a trapping condition, the walk-forward rule, a choice with no list, a trapping action, and end in the middle of a document. All ten re-injected faults are caught now. Four registered entry points are deliberately unbound — ActorPresent, ConvSetResult, SetDefActor, _ListConvs — each with zero call sites in all 885 scripts, on ShowNotes's precedent.
The mission's notes (done)GiveNote/1 (115 sites), RemoveNote/1 (65), IsNoteActive/1 (24) and ClearNotes/0 (16) — 220 sites, every one inside a map container, and the largest coherent block that was left. Containers 94% → 95%.

Two halves, and only one of them is state. A note is declared in a container's Notes.xml and given by a script, so the catalogue is configuration — rebuilt from the container on both sides of a save — and the board of active ids is world state, because IsNoteActive is a predicate a running script branches on. The board lives on CampaignSystem beside the sequence table, is folded into its hash and rides in its save section.

A note nothing declares cannot be given. The add helper (0x005584a0) looks the id up through 0x00557df0 and returns before it touches the active map when the lookup misses. It fires on shipped data exactly once — and why it is only once is the better half of the finding. Zama map 6's seq5.vs writes GiveNote("Historical Inconsistency"), Alesia map 5's seq4.vs writes the same note as "Historical Inconcistency", neither container declares it, and the Alesia one is commented out. Nobody was ever going to notice the misspelling.

That also cost a figure: a grep finds 206 note calls and a parse finds 204, and the two it adds are the commented ones — one of which is half the evidence. Second time in one session that a textual count and a parse disagreed by exactly the calls somebody commented out; RunAIHelper was the first, at 86 against 84.

RemoveNote has no catalogue gate where GiveNote does, and that asymmetry is unobservable through the shipped surface: only a board written directly can hold an undeclared id. Constructed and asserted anyway, because the state is representable and a RemoveNote that grew the gate would strand the note forever while passing every other case. ShowNotes/0 is the block's fifth entry point and is left unbound: it opens an interface panel and has no call site.

Notes.xml is a new document, specified in docs/formats/adventure.md with a reference reader and two corpus tests. One pins what makes the two-catalogue precedence unobservable: no id is declared twice, and all 122 declarations are in a map's document — every one of the 25 container-root files is an empty <notes></notes>. Seven faults injected, six caught. Across the installation 122 notes declared over 14 maps, 7 given in 60 turns; Zama's seq10 left the pinned trap set, because it was trapping on the ClearNotes in its own lose condition rather than on anything upstream.
The settlement collections (done)units/0 is implemented — 115 sites, the largest unimplemented name in the installation when this thread was written. Coverage 89% → 90%, data.pak 85% → 86%.

The open decision is made: alias. Settlement::Units (0x005c3c70) allocates nothing on its hit path — it reads the holder handle at settlement + 0x5e, points at the list embedded at holder + 0x28, increments its refcount at 0x005c3cd1 and pushes that — and Settlement::UnitsCount (0x005c1d80) reads holder + 0x40, which is that same list's size member. They cannot drift because they are one object. Exactly one shipped script can tell the readings apart, and snapshotting would make it do what its author meant, which is the one thing the standing decisions forbid: reproduce the original rather than improve on it. It is also O(1) where a snapshot is O(n), at a site — TOWNHALL_AUTOTRAIN.VS lines 68 and 70 — that reads the roster twice per iteration over a 10,000-cap list. The .army precedent points the other way and does not govern: that one is recorded here as a divergence, not as a rule.

Aliasing needed a real change to ObjListPool, which until now owned every list it handed out. An entry can now name an object whose collection it is, and items()/mutable_items() resolve that on every access through a function-pointer hook — never cached, because a settlement store is a vector and create can move every row it holds. EconomySystem::start installs it, for the reason install_objlist_teardown is a hook.

Squad::Units (0x0043ae40) copies: 0x2c bytes allocated at 0x0043ae71, copy-constructed from squad + 0x38 through a plain forward range copy, so join order front to back and [0] is the squad's own. GS_CAPTURE.VS proves it from the script side — it takes ol = squad.Units, calls Siege, and subtracts the new count from ol.count, identically zero and dead code unless the first is frozen. The two miss paths differ and both are transcribed: an invalid settlement gets a fresh empty list, never a refusal, and an invalid squad handle has no defined behaviour at all in the original — 0x00443e30 bounds-checks neither the 12-bit index nor the pointer it returns — so it is refused by name.

Eight faults injected, five caught. One was a no-op that read as a fault, because aliasing kNoObject is owning your own storage. One passed because the miss test only read the list it got back: an alias that resolves to nothing is empty too and reads identically, and the only thing that separates them is writing to it. And the resolver's holder-identity check was held up by nothing — for_object maps all four of a settlement's handles to the same row, so without it an alias named on the settlement itself would hand out the garrison, and nothing in the entry point can construct that case.

erase/0 is implemented — 47 sites, and it is not the death path. The two share exactly one routine, 0x005141b0. Death runs Damage → SetHealth → IsDead → vtbl[0xB0], scores a penalty against the holding settlement, sets the dead marker at +0x130 and leaves the object allocated as a corpse; erase enters at vtbl[0x94], touches none of it, and ends at the scalar deleting destructor. No attacker is passed on either path, so there is no kill credit to give, and nothing in the call graph drops loot. GHOST_SAC_IDLE.VS uses both verbs in adjacent lines.

The half that would be missed is that every coroutine the object owns stops. 0x00687c80 cancels the object's pending scheduled messages, and a cancelled message is a Sleep that never wakes — which is what ends HEROGRAVE_BEHAVIOR.VS, whose last two statements are .Erase(); and Sleep(100000);. An implementation that despawned the object and left its scripts running would have it polling a dead handle forever and would pass every test that only asked whether the object was gone.

Deferral is per-step, not per-pass. Erase on the running script's own object sets a latch and destroys nothing; the runner (0x0069f7a0) calls the object's vtbl[0x24] the moment the bytecode returns. So script::Scheduler gains a StepHook beside its pass hook. A pass hook is one turn late and lets every other coroutine in the same turn observe an object the original had already destroyed — a divergence that only appears once two scripts touch the same object in one turn, and one that two of the sixteen injected faults construct directly. Query::Erase skips buildings (bit 23 of [obj+0x2c], what Obj::AsBuilding tests) as a continue and not an abort, reads before it refreshes where every other Query member refreshes first — unobservable here, because this engine caches no query result — and leaves the query object itself alive. The population claim this page carried is refuted: nothing on the erase path touches settlement + 0x50; it is PEASANT_ENTER.VS that writes AddToPopulation(1) on the line above its .Erase().

The outpost block is implemented — all six of GetDefenderCls, GetDefendersMax, GetDefendersOut, GetStartLevel, GetEndLevel and GetOutpostFood, 65 sites, and with them OUTPOST_BEHAVIOR.VS has no unimplemented name left. Eleven class properties in two slots; the constructor copies them into two std::strings at +0x208/+0x224 and nine dwords at +0x240 … +0x260. Read from the class graph rather than copied onto the object, because nothing in the executable writes them after construction. GetOutpostFood is not an outpost property at all: descriptor +0xa8c is settlement_food. The gate is 0x00440060, which is the body of the registered Settlement::IsOutpost and therefore the ancestry test kind_of already runs at load; a failed gate is zero for all six, and the slot is two literal comparisons rather than an index. See the corrections table for the sentinel, which is where this thread's published claim was wrong.

Fourteen faults injected for the outpost block and sixteen for erase; all thirty caught. Coverage 90% → 91%, containers 95% → 96%, corpus sweep 87 → 82 distinct.

Two things are left out and named rather than skipped. The "ondie" class hook and holder eviction — both below, each with the reason it is a thread of its own. The hook has since been written; holder eviction is still open.
Object placement (done)Place/3 (131 sites) and _PlaceEx/4 (89) are implemented — coverage 85% → 86%. Both look a class up by name, create the object, and branch on whether it casts to CVXScriptObj; see the corrections table for why the obvious spelling of that test is wrong and what the corpus says instead. Three differences between the two are transcribed rather than tidied: Place range-checks the player and _PlaceEx does not, so the same arguments give an object from one and a sentinel from the other — and player 0 decrements to −1, so _PlaceEx hands back an object owned by nobody. Place refuses a point outside the map rectangle; _PlaceEx clamps into it inset by 48 on every side, low edge first. And Place tests the bounds before looking the class up where _PlaceEx looks it up first. That (-1, -1) skips the bounds test is what settles the rectangle's minimum without parsing anything new: were the low edges negative the escape hatch would be dead code.

World gained an entity resolver beside its class graph, because this is the first spawn that happens after load — populate_from_map and deserialize are each handed one, and a script that spawns mid-match has only a CallContext in scope. Not owned, not hashed, not serialised.

Seventeen faults injected, fifteen caught, and both survivors named a case worth having. The player check tested only the high end, where the injected fault chose the low one; and nothing in the suite had ever placed a ship, so the composite holder every ship carries at handle + 1 was allocated by code no test ran. Two more were caught only as a crash — the weaker catch the harness has REQUIRE for — and those dereferences are guarded now.
The sequence runner (done)RunSequence/1 (34 sites) plus IsRunning, IsWaiting and IsFinished. 46 shipped sequences declare autorunallowed="no" and so never started at all; 31 of those are named by one of the 34 calls and were unreachable source in a build whose tests were green. See the corrections table for the two names in this family that turned out not to exist.

Status is not a field. The original keeps it as a string in the environment registry under /SequenceStatus/<name> and writes "Waiting" back whenever its getter finds the key absent — so the whole feature needs no save section of its own, because EnvStore already hashes and serialises. Start's middle branch is the surprise: already Running does nothing, and Waiting or Finished both spawn. A finished sequence restarting is the load-bearing idiom at a live site — Numantia's seq3 restarts one 28 times per pass — and an implementation that made the second call a no-op deadlocks it on iteration two. The three predicates never consult the sequence table at all: they build the key from the raw argument and compare, so a key a script wrote by hand answers just the same.

A divergence, deliberately not papered over. Britain's WinCond guards on !IsWaiting("CalgacusArmy"), and the key it really depends on is written at line 32 of its Diplomacy sequence — which dies at line 4 on ExploreAll/0. So Britain reaches its victory branch on the first pass and stops only because RunConv has no body. The cause is ExploreAll, not the runner: ExploreArea/2 stays declared on purpose because implementing it means inventing fog state, and ExploreAll/0 is the same entry point without an argument. The "Waiting" initialisation is what makes that legible rather than accidental and must not be skipped — without it an uninitialised sequence reads as not waiting, Britain behaves correctly by accident, and the next mission to use the predicate breaks silently.

Eleven faults injected, and getting from seven caught to eleven is most of the work. The predicates test only asked about an unknown name, which a table-consulting implementation also answers false. The reap had no test that a live sequence survives it. And the basename-fallback guard took three attempts: the first case put the exact path in the library, where the fallback is unreachable; the second made the path missing and still did not discriminate, because the wrongly-started script writes its key and returns inside one turn — leaving the same "Finished" and the same cleared id a correct refusal leaves. The only trace is which script ran.
Diplomacy's writers (done)DiplCeaseFire/3 (116), DiplShareView/3 (55), DiplShareSupport/3 (22), DiplShareControl/3 (18) and ClearDiplomacy/0 (4) — 215 sites, all inside containers. One row, not both, which is load-bearing because the matrix is not symmetric and the shipped data holds one-sided truces. Four readings agree, and two are worth keeping. The executable ships a symmetric API alongside — the five _Players* entry points take two players and no flag and call SetRelation twice — so two APIs sit side by side by design, and none of the five has a call site, which is why none is in the surface table. And 211 of the 215 shipped calls are written as explicit mirrored pairs, which is not how anybody writes a call they believe is symmetric. One of the four that is not paired is deliberate: 3_Great_Losses_Egypt's seq1.vs fires a lone DiplShareView(4, 1, true) right after a conversation — an ally showing the player what it can see, one way. That beat is a test now, and a both-rows implementation fails it on shipped data rather than on a hand-built scenario.

Because ceasefire and allied are the same bit, a mirrored pair is what makes DiplAreAllied answer true — which is how every campaign alliance in the game is declared. ClearDiplomacy zeroes the off-diagonal and leaves the diagonal alone, and is unobservable on shipped data: all sixteen player documents in each of the four containers that call it are already diagonal-only, so it rests on the instructions and that is said where it is implemented. DiplShareView's fog merge is skipped, on ExploreArea's precedent.

Thirteen faults injected, twelve caught, and the survivor was a fault that set a bit already set rather than a missing test — the shape the plan already records, where a fault repairs itself and reads exactly like a fault that was caught. Chasing it found a real gap all the same: a partial write survives the whole suite because PlayerTable::set refuses it on its own and the host's check is belt-and-braces. A property held up by two checks and asserted by neither is one check away from being held up by none.
Difficulty, posRH and the AI hands-off flag (done)GetDifficulty/0 (136), SetDifficulty/1 (0), Obj::posRH/0 (141), SetNoAIFlag/2 (121) and Unit::SetNoAIFlag/1 (109) — 622 sites, coverage 84% → 85%. Three findings worth keeping:

posRH is pos. It reaches the position through vtbl + 0xC8, which is the thunk 0x005a75d0 in all fifteen classes that carry the shared SetPos at vtbl + 0x38, forwarding to vtbl + 0x3c — 0x0063d900 in all fifteen too, and its whole body is out = {[obj+0x24], [obj+0x28]}, the two fields Obj::pos reads directly. The census is anchored on the SetPos pointer rather than on a guessed vtable start, which is what makes it exhaustive rather than a sample. The two differ only in the miss: pos prints a diagnostic first.

Difficulty is 0/1/2 and SetDifficulty is the only thing that says so — SetDifficulty: The difficulties allowed are 0, 1 and 2 (easy/medium/hard). It is match-level, not map-level: 0x006e80a0 copies it out of a 60-byte settings block at 0x0082ef58, and it is not game.xml's per-player playerdata/@difficulty, which AiDifficulty already models. SetDifficulty has zero call sites in the whole installation and is implemented anyway, because the value 136 sites read has to be settable by something. Out of range is not a refusal: the original prints and leaves the field alone.

SetNoAIFlag's two forms are not one function with a different receiver. Both write bit 18 of [unit+0x194] — a second flag word, not the +0x2c SyncFlags every other object flag comes from, and one no map file can author. But the free form (0x00435470) applies three filters the member form (0x005de600) has not got: an unresolvable handle, an object without bit 22 of +0x2c (which map.md already records as the object is a mobile unit), and one whose vtbl + 0x50 answers non-zero — that slot is IsDead, since Obj::IsAlive (0x005aae00) is nothing but its negation, over 229 call sites. So the list form skips buildings and skips the dead and the member form flags either when named directly. Nothing here reads the flag yet, which is the honest state: its three readers in gbr.exe — 0x0043ad2c, 0x00446c8e, 0x0044745c — are all inside the AI-helper machinery.
Group spawning (done)SpawnGroup/1, SpawnGroupInHolder/2, AddToGroup/1, RemoveFromGroup/1, RemoveFromAllGroups/0, Obj::IsInGroup/1 and GetGroupSize/1 are implemented — coverage 82% → 84%, containers 81% → 85%. Three things it turned out to be, none of them "the group tables already exist":

The stored order was a correctness bug, as recorded. CVXGroup::Add (0x00572410) is a std::find and a push_back with no comparison of ids in it, and Remove (0x00572210) erases rather than swapping with the back. The table sorted; it keeps first-add order now, and a group query yields that order because 32 shipped Group(...).SetCommand sites and 25 .GetObjList sites apply an operation per member in evaluation order. The save loader had to move with it: it required ascending ids, so the first correct save of every real map was refused while the whole C++ suite stayed green.

The two deques are <scriptobj flags> bit 27, and it is 39% of the shipped data. Add routes on [obj+0x2c] & 0x08000000, and some forty collection sites skip an object carrying it. That is 10,610 of 27,070 objects — 77% of 5_Great_Battles_Britain — every one of which this engine had standing on the field, enrolled and targetable, from turn zero. docs/formats/map.md listed the bit as unexplained and had the shape of the answer: "nearly a subset of belongs-to-a-type-1-group, but 5,268 group members do not carry it". The 5,268 are the live halves. It was a property of memberships, not of objects.

And spawning rewrites a named-object binding, which that document also carried as open. Zama now goes 307 → 407 combatants as its own sequences bring reinforcements in; Numantia's load-time enrolment falls 1,203 → 710 and its idle scripts 1,542 → 1,049, a difference of exactly its 493 templates.
The rectangle family's tailrect::Set/4, IntersectRects/2, AddRects/2, rect ==/!= and the lvalue edge accessors are all read and none is registered, because none has a single call site in the installation. rect::Center is read too and deliberately unbound for a sharper reason: every shipped .Center is on a GAIKA, so binding the rect body to the name would answer all of them with “receiver is not a rect” where they trap honestly today. The bodies and their addresses are in sim/world_host.cpp for the day a caller appears.
The animation family (done)Ten entry points over 110 sites, all in data.pak: StartAnim/2 (35), TimeToAnimFinish/0 (29), TimeToActionMoment/0 (26), SetState/1 (16), PlayAnim/2 and /3 (13 each), and four ones and twos. data.pak 86% → 87%; the corpus sweep 71 distinct → 65, and its hit count 2,064 → 1,374.

The eighth instance of the signature failure. World::play_anim and World::enter_state were written, hashed, saved and rendered — the turn loop advances every object's timeline and the renderer reads the cursor — and world.hpp says in as many words that they are "the shape the script VM's PlayAnim host function will bind to". Nothing bound it. So 28 shipped classes (the blacksmith fires, the wells, the obelisks, the barrack horses, every flame) ran DATA\SUBAI\ANIM.VS against an entry point with no body, and that one file was the largest trap in the corpus at 522 hits. Found by asking what a hashed field was for, which is a third way in after "what would a declared entry point have called" and "why is this trap reachable at all".

Only PlayAnim suspends, and ANIM.VS is the proof it must. That file is while (1) This.PlayAnim(1, This.pos); with no Sleep — a returning PlayAnim spins the budget forever, which is the shape Unit::Attack was caught in. gbr.exe agrees: Obj::PlayAnim and Flying::PlayAnim are the only two of the ten through the suspending registrar. Their mechanisms differ and one does not cross — the Obj form starts nothing and waits on the object's order queue, which this engine has not got — so both are implemented as the Flying form, start now and suspend for what is left. A tick of latency at the front, recorded rather than left to be found.

Two names are off by one, and one of those was already written down as something else. The original's animation array is one lower than the entity document's idx and Obj::StartAnim decrements before it indexes, so the two cancel; Flying::PlayAnim and Unit::GetAnimDuration pass their argument raw. The corpus proves it twice: CROW_IDLE.VS calls PlayAnim(0, …) and PlayAnim(16, …) and no shipped entity declares 0 or 16 — add one and they are 1 and 17, which every crow declares — and EAGLE_MOVE.VS computes .GetAnimDuration(4) / .GetAnimDuration(0), which is 5 over 1, the attack animation over the walk. world.hpp had recorded the first of those as an ordinary miss.

SetState/1 is two entry points wearing one name: twelve of its sixteen sites are sq.SetState(SS_IDLE) on a squad and four are a grave stepping its decay through the entity's states, so one body dispatches on the handle type. Five things the original's per-object animation state carries and this engine's does not are named where they are missing — transitions, an animation origin, a duration override, an absolute start time, and Flying::PlayAnim's Z. Fifteen faults injected, thirteen caught, and both survivors are the same shape: a guard the original writes that another component here already enforces (MovementSystem::face's zero check, AnimTimeline::normalise's clamp). Each is annotated where it lives rather than left looking untested.
The small predicates (done)
and four wrong receivers		InShip/0 (9 sites), IsInAir/0 (7), IsBuilding/0 (2), SetNoselectFlag/1 (6), InHolder/1 (2), abs/1 (1), HealStamina/1 (11), plus Obj::Clear/0 and the Hero::SetAutocast/autocast pair. One field or one bit each, and the value is in what each turned out to be.

IsInAir is registered once, on Flying, bit 22 of the second flag word [obj+0x194]. There is no Settlement::IsInAir, which this page's census had. IsBuilding is on Ship and is a progressive verb — [ship+0x230] > 0, still being built — and has nothing to do with the 0x00800000 Building bit; SHIP_IDLE.VS's while (.IsBuilding()) Sleep(100) never terminates under a class test. InShip is a question about the holder: it asks the holder record for its ship slot, where GetHolderSett asks the slot beside it, which is how six shipped sites spell in a holder that is not a ship, therefore it has a settlement. InHolder/1 returns an int, and the arity is the only thing that says so — four *InHolder methods are registered and no two are the same function; both shipped sites compare the result against .count, which a bool would spin forever on. abs(INT32_MIN) is INT32_MIN. HealStamina fans out over a group, which eight of its eleven sites need, and it is SetStamina's guarded sibling — it clamps where SetStamina does not and refuses a negative outright, so it cannot drain.

Obj::Clear makes a handle invalid, and the retail body writes to the wrong place. It resolves the handle and then stores the sentinel at [object] and [object+4] — into the object, not into the stack slot whose address it overwrote two instructions earlier, and on a handle that does not resolve the pointer is null. This writes the slot, because that is what IsValid reads and what CROW_IDLE.VS needs; transcribing the fault would corrupt an object or crash. It had been refusing 207 times a pass, and the comment standing over it named the gap without closing it.

Four new object flag bits (in_air, noselect, building, autocast), so the flag word is thirteen bits and the world save is version 10. Only one of the four has a writer: nothing here flies, nothing builds a ship, and no AI casts. Both those absences are stated where the flags are declared rather than left as a silence. Fifteen faults injected, twelve caught; all three survivors were HealStamina, which had no test at all — the clamp, the negative and the fan-out, which is to say each of its three findings.
The idle scripts' own names (done)
and two types nobody owned		Nine entry points and a language feature, and between them they took the corpus sweep's hit count from 2,064 to 666 — a third of what it was two blocks ago.

IntArray and StrArray are not host entry points at all but two types the VM hands out and subscripts, registered beside ObjList in the executable's own type table as codes 0x0c and 0x0d. Forty declarations across 38 shipped files, and WorldHost::default_value listed them as "other domains'" where no domain owned them — so a script that declared one and wrote to it trapped, which is what four sequences did every pass. Assignment grows: the idiom that requires it opens those four sequences verbatim, an array declared empty and written past its end four times. A survey of every declared array name in all 885 scripts finds no .count, no member of any kind and no whole-array assignment, so the model is two operations and nothing more. Saved and not hashed, on ObjListPool's precedent and for its reason.

FindUnitToLearn/0 (480 hits, the largest single trap) is the barbarian druid's learn ability picking a target, and its predicate's three class exclusions — Sentry, RamUnit, Peaceful — had been read as unidentified. HasSpecial/1 (205) turned out to be one bit of a 36-entry table this project already had: the class property's tokens are the table's display names and the script constants are indices into the same table, so curse is 35 on one side and Curse is entry 35 on the other. FindNearBird/0 filters on nothing but the class and the distance — no aliveness, no visibility, no diplomacy, so a crow will fly toward a dead crow, and the test asserts it. Obj::Clear/0 was refusing 207 times a pass under a comment that named the gap without closing it, and the retail body writes its sentinel into the object rather than into the stack slot whose address it overwrote two instructions earlier.

The gate's five. GATE_IDLE.VS is eleven lines and every gate runs it: rescan a 350-unit neighbourhood twice a second, close on any enemy, open for friends alone, close for an empty street. LookAround is a cache refresh and not a query — it writes the two booleans the other two read, which is why the pair is meaningless without it. Neither OpenNow nor CloseNow suspends although both go through the suspending registrar, and that has its own test.

Seven more object flag bits across the block (in_air, noselect, building, autocast, on_minimap, enemies_near, friends_near, gate_open), so the flag word is seventeen bits and a u32; the world save went 8 → 13 in one session. Fifty-four faults injected across the block, fifty caught. All four survivors are the same shape and are annotated where they live: a guard the original writes that another component here already enforces.
The caster searches (done)
and the map payloads nobody handed over		Eleven more entry points, and the corpus sweep's hit count went 666 → 404 — a fifth of what it was three blocks ago.

The four searches. FindUnitToHeal/0 (136 hits), GetBestCurseTarget/0 (36), FindUnitBelowILevel/1 (23) and GetBestCrippleTarget/0 (10) share one skeleton — RPRIEST_IDLE.VS says so in a comment — and each alternates with FindUnitToLearn through action = 4 - action, which is why one script produces a hundred and thirty-six hits. Two of them score: heal minimises a health fraction, 300 · health / maxhealth + max(0, edgeDistance − range), with a candidate too lightly wounded to be worth a whole heal parked at a flat 1000 rather than rejected; curse maximises attack; cripple maximises stamina behind a real predicate. The heal threshold is self.level + GetConst("HealAmount") — exactly the amount the script is about to heal, so engine and script agree on the number from two directions.

CanSee/1 needs no fog, which is the good news of the block: the name promises it and 0x005ab9d0 is five instructions — if the observed object is not stealthed, true, from anywhere on the map.
