It leads the site census among unimplemented members at 57 sites because every caster script re-checks it twice per target.

Two more map payloads nobody was handing over. IsPointInWater/1 (127 hits) consults the map's terrain-type layer and nothing else — 64 units per cell, one byte per cell, water is type 13, and the point is biased by half a cell before it divides — and that layer was never loaded, so the answer was always "not water". The stonehenge pair reads the same kind of thing it already had. Each new payload has its own session-level test now, because a fault that reads one and never binds it survives every test written against the setter directly, and did.

The stonehenge count is not what its name says: mages of one alliance, provided nobody hostile is also here. A single hostile soldier zeroes it and aborts the scan, which is why Military non-mages are in the predicate at all — they cannot add and exist to spoil. Its twin IsStonehengeControlable is the same scan against the commanding player, so it answers differently on different peers, and that is correct: its five sites are the five spell verify scripts, which decide whether this player's right click does anything.

Sixty-one faults injected across the block, fifty-two caught. Every survivor named either a fixture that was not discriminating or a guard the original writes twice. The one worth repeating: a score on a health fraction and one on raw health order an identical field identically, so a fixture where every candidate shares a maximum health cannot tell the real rule from the obvious wrong one. It takes two maxima and a health between the smaller and twice it.
The heading family (done)
three approximations of π, and one wrong turn		Seven entry points over 66 sites, and between them they are how every bird in the game decides which way to point: GetVecByDir/2 (17), GetDirByAngle/1 (16), Unit::SetUser/1 (13), Unit::user/0 (8), OnLeft/2 (6), GetAngleByDir/1 (4) and Obj::IsInState/0 (2). Five scripts reach them — CROW_MOVE.VS, EAGLE_MOVE.VS, the two eagle hover scripts and HEN_IDLE.VS — and the four free functions are the integer trigonometry the flying pickers below were blocked on.

There are three approximations of π in gbr.exe and no two of these functions share one. point::Rot multiplies by 0.017453292519444445, which is 3.1415926535/180; GetDirByAngle by 0.017452777777777779, which is 3.1415/180; PickFlyingPoint's scan by 0.017444444444444446, which is 6.28/360; and GetAngleByDir finishes with 57.297469361769856, which is 180/3.1415. They are not interchangeable: a full turn under the second is 6.2830 radians, 0.0106° short, so a table built on one constant and used for another is wrong by about a unit in a hundred. The visible consequence is that GetDirByAngle(90) is (0, 99) rather than (0, 100), and a full turn is not the identity.

Two of GetAngleByDir's four quadrants come back rotated half a turn, and that is the original's own bug. 0x0051b789 computes acos(|y| / |v|) — the angle off north with the sign of y thrown away — and then repairs the quadrant from the two signs, applying 2π − a where it wanted π − a and π − a where it wanted 2π − a. So due north answers 0, due east answers 90, and due west answers 89; the function is discontinuous across both axes. Reproduced exactly, because its only consumer feeds the result straight back through GetDirByAngle and steers on it, and a bird that turned the other way is a divergence in a hashed position. The pair are not inverses either — one measures off north, the other builds off east — so CROW_MOVE.VS's angle = GetAngleByDir(dir); newDir = GetDirByAngle(angle + 30) is a reflection about the diagonal followed by a turn, not the thirty degrees its author wrote.

acos becomes a tangent search, and the near-axis case is what forces it. Every answer is floored to a whole degree, so only floor(t) and ceil(t) are ever needed and never t itself: t ≥ d is |x| · 230 ≥ |y| · tan(d/K) · 230, exact integers with no square root and no division. A cosine table cannot do it — cos(90/K)² is 2.1e−9, which rounds to 2 at Q30 and puts the boundary seven per cent out — and tan is large exactly where cos is small. Checked against a double-precision transcription of 0x0051b720 over 240,000 vectors, including every vector within eight units of the origin and 20,000 sitting one unit off an axis: zero mismatches. GetDirByAngle's table holds its answers rather than its cosines, because the length 100 is a constant in the body and every result is a pair of bytes.

GetVecByDir and point::SetLen disagree about the one input with no direction. 0x0051b6de writes the square root — zero — into both components where 0x00697d81 writes the requested length into y; HEN_IDLE.VS is the site that can reach it, with a hen standing exactly on the centre it is measuring from. And OnLeft wraps: it is a cross product in 32-bit registers, and two coordinates near the top of a large map multiply to about INT32_MAX/2 each, so the difference overflows and the sign — which is the whole answer — flips. Widening it to 64 bits gives the right answer and the wrong bird.

Unit::user is a field nothing in the engine reads, hashed and saved anyway (world save version 15). It is script scratch: CROW_IDLE.VS writes 1 to mean this crow has decided to land and the three Britain ship sequences use it as a per-transport loaded flag they then sum. A field two peers can disagree about without the hash noticing is a desync three turns later; a save that dropped it reloads a crow that has forgotten. IsInState is the absence of an animation, and what component 27 in the object's list really is has not been recovered — the reading comes from its two users, and see the corrections table for what this page had. Both point the same way: two shipped scripts open while (!.IsInState) Sleep(50) before their first PlayAnim, which is a bird waiting out what the idle script last started, and PickFlyingPoint wants a flock-mate that is not in a state and is in the air, which is a bird whose heading is live because it is mid-flight. This engine already had that bit hashed and saved as WorldObject::animating, and the reading has to make the wait loop terminate, which the opposite one does not.

Twenty faults injected, nineteen caught. The one survivor is annotated where it lives and is genuinely untestable: the overflow guard in the tangent comparison bites at about 397,000 units, and the original's own length is a 32-bit sum of two squares that has already wrapped by 46,341 — so there is no right answer up there to disagree about, only defined behaviour versus undefined. Getting the second-to-last one caught took finding the thirteen degrees of 360 where a whole turn of 3.1415/180 moves a component by one unit, which is what makes a reduction test discriminating rather than decorative.

And the corpus sweep did not move, which is worth saying rather than hiding. Coverage went 31,258 → 31,324 sites and data.pak 15,043 → 15,096, but the trap tally stayed at 56 distinct and 404 hits: every script that reaches these seven names reaches PickFlyingPoint first, on line 59 of CROW_IDLE.VS, and stops there. The payoff is the thread below.
The crow flies (done)
and a flag with no writer, finally written		Twelve entry points over 29 sites, and one line of CROW_IDLE.VS was 207 of the trap tally's 404 hits before them: GetTerrainHeight/1 (9), Flying::z/0 (5), MIN/2 (3), MAX/2 (2), RandomOffset/1 (2), Flying::PickLandingPoint/1 (2), Unit::dir/0 (2), Flying::PickFlyingPoint/1 (1), Flying::AdjustFlyDir/1 (1), Query::GetAverageDirection/0 (1), Unit::ForceIdle/0 (1) and Flying::IsLanding/0 (0 sites, and see below for why it is here anyway).

The height layer had never reached the simulation, and it is the fifth map payload with that story. Maps/<n>/Terrain.height.grid is 32 units per cell and one byte per cell; the application has read it for the renderer since Part 2 and SessionInputs had no field for it, so every point on every map was at sea level as far as a script could tell. GetTerrainHeight is 0x0053dc00, a **bilinear** sample — two horizontal lerps and one vertical, each (b − a) · f / 32 with a divide that truncates toward zero, so a rising slope and a falling one are not mirror images — wrapped in the clamp into the map rectangle that 0x0051b4b0 does first. The far cell on each axis is flat rather than wrapped, because the original clamps the neighbour rather than reading past it. And the scale question the map specification leaves open never has to be answered: Flying::PlayAnim range-checks its z to −1 … 1024 and every shipped site builds one out of GetTerrainHeight, so the whole family stays inside the layer's own 0..255 and never converts to world units at all.

A bird has no stored altitude. Flying::z (0x0051b040) computes one from three states: on the ground it is the terrain under the object; mid-animation it is z_from + (z_to − z_from) · elapsed / cycle; otherwise it is z_from. The pair are [obj+0x1cc] and [obj+0x1d0], written by Flying::PlayAnim and by nothing else — z_from from Flying::z itself, so each animation starts where the last one left off. The boundary is the mechanism: the original tests the clock against the animation's own range with jb/ja, so the instant an animation ends still interpolates and answers z_to exactly — and that is the instant the suspended script resumes and asks again. Answer z_from there instead and the chain never advances: every animation starts where the last one started and no crow ever climbs. Both are hashed and saved (world save version 16), because CROW_IDLE.VS reads z = .z and branches on it every iteration.

The ninth instance of the signature failure is closed, and the writer turned out to be one expression. ObjectFlags::in_air was declared, hashed, saved and false in every session this engine had ever run — read by Flying::IsInAir at seven shipped sites, written by nothing, so CROW_IDLE.VS's if (!.IsInAir()) was always true and no crow in the game had ever left the ground. 0x0051bc4e is in_air = !(z_from == GetTerrainHeight(pos) && z == −1), which reads as a bird is in the air unless it is descending and already at ground level. There is no take-off event and no touch-down event to have missed: PlayAnim(16, …, 80 + h) puts it up because z is not −1, PlayAnim(17, ptLand, −1) keeps it up because it is still high, and PlayAnim(18, .pos, −1) — run after the landing animation has already put z_from on the ground — is the one that clears it. The companion [obj+0x1d4] is z == −1 on every call, and Flying::IsLanding is that and in_air: a bird still up but on its way down. It has zero shipped call sites and is registered anyway, because it is the first time the two bits it reads have been decidable.

The pickers. PickFlyingPoint tries a flock-follow first — the objects of its own class within its sight less a hundred, and the first that is animating and airborne, which is a bird whose heading is live because it is mid-flight — and failing that a twelve-direction scan at 30° steps, each candidate scored as 50 · (airborne creatures within 100 of it) + turnAngle, best-so-far starting at 10,000 with an early accept below 60. PickLandingPoint shares the signature and the query and nothing else: for each mate that is on the ground or already landing it tries five spots at mate.pos + rand(±100), accepting one that can be landed on and that is between half and twice a hundred units away and within about 55° of the heading. Failing every mate, a one-in-six decides between (−1, −1) and a straight-ahead landing — and the roll only happens if there was a mate to try or a mate in front, so a bird entirely alone lands ahead without rolling at all. Every draw is Y before X, in both pickers and in RandomOffset; one generator serves the simulation and swapping two consecutive draws changes every number after them.

0x0051c210 is a three-part “can I land here” and every part of it was already in the tree: inside the map rectangle, tested twice, once biased by half a terrain cell on both axes; the terrain-type byte at the biased point being water if and only if the class's water_unit is set; and the 16-unit obstruction cell under the raw point being free. Water is 13, which is IsPointInWater's constant, and the +32 is the bias docs/formats/pass.md already records. AdjustFlyDir (0x0051be50) needed no trigonometry to be interesting: within 800 units of an edge it rotates the bird's heading and the caller's point by 90° until the near edge is the one at low y, leaves them alone if the rotated point already heads away, otherwise turns the heading ±15° — a Rot, so the first of the three pis, and the same table — keeps the turn if it improves, and unrotates. Query::GetAverageDirection sums the headings and divides by the count, with no null check: a stale handle faults in the original.

The scan is where the third pi lives. 0x7beb58 is 6.28/360 and nothing else in the executable uses it, so a “quarter turn” of the scan is 1.5700 radians rather than pi/2: scan_offset(120, 3) is (0, 119) and scan_offset(120, 6) is (−119, 0), while step 0 is exactly (120, 0). The Q30 table was checked against the double the original computes for every radius from 0 to 4,000: zero mismatches. And both pickers end in point::SetLen rather than GetVecByDir, which matters for exactly one input — the vector with no direction, which SetLen gives one, pointing north.

Two divergences, both deliberate and both written down where they live. The retail flock query is interned: 0x004fee10 walks a pool keyed on (object, class, radius, flag) and hands back the same query object every time, so a retail crow mints one handle in its whole life and that handle sits in the hashed slot table. This engine has no interning pool and minting one per call would grow the table without bound, so the flock is evaluated directly. And the retail animation carries the bird to its destination, committing the position at the next StopAnimation, so a retail crow's script-visible .pos runs one animation behind; here it arrives at the end of the call instead. Every quantity computed at a PlayAnim — the facing, the ground under the bird, the in_air comparison — still comes out at the previous call's destination, because that is where the object already is; only what .pos answers between two calls differs. Not moving at all was not an option: CROW_MOVE.VS is while (.DistTo(pt) > 100) around a PlayAnim, and a bird that never arrives never leaves that loop.

Fifty-four faults injected, fifty-four caught — but only after two sweeps that were mostly a verdict on the tests. The first sweep caught 32 of 52, and the twenty survivors were all in one place: everything asserted through a picker rather than at the thing itself. A landing test that accepted either answer asserted nothing, and every one of the six landing-rule faults walked past it; a scan test that checked the distance from the bird was invariant under worst-instead-of-best, no-early-accept, crowding and the fold alike; two flock decoys that happened to face the same way as the real mate could not tell four conditions apart. can_land and heading_ok are exported and tested where they live, the scan is pinned by which of the twelve candidates wins (which took a scratch program to find the headings that separate them — 45° off north tells “first good enough” from “best of twelve”, 100° tells folded from unfolded), and the pickers' draws are pinned against Rng::state() by a reference generator replaying the exact sequence, which catches the count, the order and the bounds together where a coordinate assertion catches none of them.

And the last survivor was worth all of it. “The landing bit is left out of the saved flag word” survived because the save fixture had quietly stopped exercising eight of its eighteen flag bits: it set them as (i % 4) == 2, (i % 5) == 1, one more modulus per bit, while the object loop stayed at six — so from building = (i % 9) == 6 onward the condition is never true for any i, and building, autocast, on_minimap, enemies_near, friends_near, gate_open, messenger and landing were never set in any saved world in the suite. The comment directly above them says “every bit the packer can write has to be set on somebody here”; the pattern that comment established is what broke it. Behind that sat a real defect in this thread’s own work: pack_object_flags gained bit 18 and kObjectFlagMask did not, so deserialize refused every save carrying it — a load failure, not a dropped field, and invisible because nothing ever set the bit. Two blind spots that bite only together. The flags are (i + k) % 3 == 0 now, so every bit is set on two of six and clear on four however the fixture grows, and a new test walks the sixteen writable bits and asserts each appears in both states — over the fixture rather than over the round trip, because a bit that is never set round-trips perfectly and proves nothing.

And this time the sweep moved. Coverage went 31,324 → 31,353 sites, and the trap tally fell from 56 distinct / 22 messages / 404 hits to 55 / 21 / 197 — one line of one idle script was half of everything the whole installation still traps on. What leads it now is IsEnemyInSquadSight/0 at 132, alone.
The squad readers (done)
two reads with no writer, in the game rather than in this tree		Twelve entry points over 305 sites, and they take the installation past 93%: Squad::State/0 (89, the most-read member there is), Squad::GAIKAIn/0 (61), Unit::SetLastAttackTime/0 (40), Squad::Leader/0 (37), GAIKA::ID/0 (33), Unit::IsEnemyInSquadSight/0 (20), Squad::ClrCmd/3 (13), and StateTime/0, DelOrder/0, Unit::Curse/0 and IsCursed/0 at three each. Almost all of it is stored state the squad table has carried since it was written; what was missing was the readers.

Two of them read fields nothing writes — and this time the gap is the game's. IsEnemyInSquadSight resolves the receiver's squad through [unit+0x174] and answers [squad+0x7c] != 0. A byte-pattern sweep of the whole .text for writes to [reg+0x7c] finds seven; exactly two are on a squad and both are constructors writing zero (0x0044440b, inside a run of edi = 0 stores, and 0x0045402f, which writes the literal 0 beside [esi+0x28] — the state field, which is what identifies the object). The only reader is the entry point. So it is false on every unit in every retail session too, and the four idle scripts that ask — rpriest_idle.vs alone was 88 of this engine's trap hits — take the same branch there as here. SetLastAttackTime is the same shape: it writes [obj+0x1a4], a second writer sets the same now wherever a unit actually attacks, and the only reader in the executable is Unit::Dump. No host entry point returns it, so **no script can observe it** — and it therefore stores nothing here, with a test asserting the world hash does not move. Unit::user is stored for the opposite reason (it has a getter), and the two cases are worth keeping apart rather than treating a field as state because the original has one.

The mask order was a choice nobody had made. SetCmd and ClrCmd both compose (flags | nSetFlags) & ~nClrFlags, and the alternative — clear first — differs on exactly one input: a bit named by both masks. No shipped site names a bit twice; 27 of the 30 SetCmd sites pass a set mask of literally 0. So the corpus cannot settle it, the order rests on the argument names in the signature table and on the two entry-point names reading the same way round, and it is now a stated choice with a test on it instead of whichever way the expression happened to be written. It surfaced as the one survivor of nineteen injected faults — against code that had been in the tree since the squad domain was written.

What is left of this thread is the SquadList cursor — Cur/0 (72), Next/0 (32), EOL/0 (27), Lock/0 and Unlock/0 (20 each), plus Rewind, Size, Eval, Select, Add and GAIKA::GetSquads/4 — about 171 sites. sim/objlist.hpp already says where it goes: “every one of the 131 Cur/Next/EOL receivers is a SquadList … the protocol belongs to sim/squad.hpp”. Expect it to move the site census and not the tally, for the reason the heading family did not move it: nothing in this engine mints a GAIKA, so gaika.GetSquads fills an empty list and every loop body under it is unreached. Saying so first is cheaper than discovering it after.
The GAIKA node
what the squad iterator is really waiting for		GAIKA::Count, Eval, GetStrat/1 (11), GetGaika/0 (27), settlement, Center, ApproachingSquads, LSA. sim/gaika.hpp models the handle and refuses to model the node, and that decision stands: CVXGlobalAI::Persist() names the fields (GAIKAs, GAIKASlots, slotresx, slotresy, MAIKAs, and per node settlement, squads, coverage, adjcount, shipcount, shipneed) which is enough to see a fixed-resolution slot grid over the map and a node per region, and not enough to say how the grid is cut or what coverage counts. GS_CAPTURE.VS shows what depends on it: gaika.Count(AI_STAYING, AIPlayer, ownC, allyC, enemyC, enemyCHidden) is a census of a region this engine cannot delimit. What would settle it: a save file with a CVXGlobalAI block decoded against those names. The lists are no longer empty, and the census is not what stops a capture. Since the node table was built as a stated approximation, GAIKA::Count and Eval total the squads filed under a node, and on Crossroads GS_CAPTURE.VS prints GAIKA Own: 11 … Enemy: 0 and reaches SS_Capture tens of thousands of times in 12,000 turns (the note above census_of in sim/squad.cpp, which still said every total was zero, is corrected). What the approximation does decide is exploration: a node is Explored once its centre is, and p0’s town, a walled corner, never is. The link pass already credits every 128-unit slot to a node, which is enough for a narrower reading — explored once any of its slots is — now adopted and labelled; see “Why no computer player takes a town”.

The evidence it waits for is not on a retail install, and that was checked rather than assumed. A shipped installation carries no saved game: Profiles/ holds only player.ini and two .usr settings blobs, and tempadv.sdw — the one file with a save-shaped name — is zero bytes. Nor do the nine desync dumps help: a case-insensitive sweep of the whole Logs/ tree finds no GAIKA, GlobalAI, MAIKA or slotres, which is consistent with aihash being zero in all nine. So this needs a save produced by playing the original, which is a different kind of work from everything else on this list.
The SquadList cursor (done)
and three names that already belonged to somebody		Eleven entry points over 224 sites, and the installation crosses 94%: Cur/0 (72), Next/0 (32), EOL/0 (27), Lock/0 and Unlock/0 (20 each), Size/0 (18), Rewind/0 (12), and GetSquads at all four arities gbr.exe registers. All of it is DATA\AI — the general-strategy layer, the scripts that decide what an AI player's armies do — and GS_GUARD.VS is the whole idiom in five lines: gaika.GetSquads(SL, AI_ALL, AIPlayer, AI_OWN); SL.Lock; while (SL.EOL == false) { squad = SL.Cur; SL.Next(); … } SL.Unlock;

A SquadList is a list and a position in it, and the position belongs to the list. The original keeps a deque at [list] and an iterator pair at [list+4], so two scripts walking one list would tread on each other — and nothing shares one, because GetSquads takes the caller's **by reference** (type 0x128, which is SquadList plus the by-reference bit, the same arrangement point::Rot uses). That is why no host function here ever mints one and why the pool needs no mark-and-sweep, where ObjListPool does. SquadListPool is ArrayPool's shape: keyed by declaration site, released with the script by the same one teardown hook, saved and not hashed — and the cursor is saved with it, because GS_GUARD.VS sleeps in the loop above its walk, so a save can be taken part way through one and a load that rewound would order every squad a second time. World save version 18.

Lock is not bookkeeping, and it explains a hole in the constant table. Squad::Lock (0x004217e0) is or byte ptr [squad+0x30], 8 and Unlock (0x00421820) is and … 0xf7, on the same word Squad::GetFlags reads with a movzwl; the SquadList forms are those two writes applied to every member. 8 is exactly the value missing from the SF_* run — 1, 2, 4, _, 16, 32 — and it is missing because it is not a script's to set. The bit has two readers in the whole executable, both in the squad-management code where squads are disbanded or merged, so it means a script is walking this list, leave these alone. Nothing here disbands squads yet; the bit is written because GetFlags and TestFlags can see it and because that is what will have to consult it.

Three names were not taken, and the overlap check is what said so. Member lookup is case-insensitive, so a second define replaces the first silently — and briefly did: IsValid/0 is world_host's over 257 sites, and claiming it broke right-click verb resolution, which is how it was found. Add/1 is objlist's (27 of its 29 receivers are ObjLists, two are SquadLists), so the branch went inside that body, where the comment used to say refusing a SquadList “names that gap”. And Select/1 was left alone entirely: 10 of its 11 sites are wagon.Select and obj.Select, so claiming the name would have answered for ten call sites this domain knows nothing about. What 0x0042bee0 does with its argument — it indexes the per-player squad table by n − 1, so the argument is a player — is written down here for whoever takes the wagon's.

The trap tally did not move, and this page said so before the work started. 62 hits before, 62 after. Nothing in this engine assigns a GAIKA to a squad, so GetSquads filters on fields that are all zero, every list comes back empty and every loop under it runs zero times and terminates. That is the correct behaviour for an AI with no territory graph; what would change it is the row below. Predicting it in advance cost a sentence and saved rediscovering it afterwards.

Twenty-four faults, twenty-three caught, and the first pass caught eighteen. The six survivors were all one mistake in different clothes: a test that reaches a branch by accident rather than on purpose. EOL on a stale handle and on a non-SquadList receiver are different branches and only the first was tested; Size at cursor zero cannot tell “length” from “what is left”; and the refill-rewinds assertion passed for the wrong reason — the list happened to be empty at that point, so set_cursor clamped to zero and the check held whether the rewind happened or not. The one survivor left is annotated where it lives: Next's bounds test is a second guard on a property set_cursor's clamp already holds, and only its return value is load-bearing.

And the sweep itself got four times faster. It was patch one file → rebuild → run 48,000 checks → restore, strictly serially, and a one-file change gives ninja a single compile job — so twenty-four faults was ten minutes of one core on a ten-core machine. scratchpad/faultrun.py gives each of six workers its own git worktree and build directory: 24 faults in 160 seconds at 616% CPU, with the worktrees persisting so later sweeps pay nothing to set up — and 33 seconds once the test suite itself was fixed, because a sweep runs the whole suite once per fault. Eighteen times faster than where it started, from two changes that compound. It also stopped the sweep touching the working tree at all, and let a fault target a header, which the serial driver could not — the first thing that caught was a fault against kSquadLocked that had been silently failing to apply.
The class script hooks (done)
ondie, onkill, onenter		All three, and 2,497 hook launches across ten of the seventeen shipped containers in 300 turns each: UNIT_ON_KILL.VS 1,129, UNIT_ON_ENTER.VS 755, MILITARY_ONDIE.VS 536, CMERCENARY_ONDIE.VS 73, and four more between the druid, the priest and the enchantress — seven of the ten distinct hook scripts the installation ships, which are the kill book, the Spoils of War conversion, the Warrior Tales payout and the mercenary purse. The seven containers that fire none are the seven where no headless run lands a blow or reaches an Erase. No host entry point is involved, so no coverage number moves; what moves is that a whole category of shipped content now runs. kWorldVersion 23 → 24 and kStateVectorVersion 13 → 14, for the latch below.

The seam is one interface and it is the shape the plan predicted. sim::ClassHookRunner is declared by the core, carried on World beside the class graph — configuration, not state — and implemented by GameSession::Impl, which is the one thing holding the class graph, the chunk cache and the scheduler at once. HostContext::ScriptLibrary is the same argument one layer up. Three firing sites share it: CombatSystem::enter_dying, CombatSystem::hit and perform_erase, plus Settlement::AddUnit on the interface side. CombatSystem::apply_damage grew a World& to reach it, which is what hit already had.

The routine runs at most once per object, ever, and that is a fact rather than a policy. 0x005141b0 refuses when [obj+0xbc] is non-zero and writes 1 to it on the way out at 0x0051446b. It matters here more than there: the original fires ondie as the object is going away, while this engine leaves a corpse standing for death_duration_ first, and a script can Erase one inside that window — without the latch a soldier killed and then erased pays Warrior Tales twice. It is ObjectFlags::ondie_fired, hashed and saved, because fifteen objects sit in the dying state across the nine dumps and a save can be taken with it set. Taken whether or not a script starts, because the original’s write is at the tail and is not conditional on the launch.

A hook is spawned detached, and that one decision is the difference between a mechanism and a subsystem nobody can reach. 0x006a0360 passes the object’s handle as the script’s first argument and binds no receiver, so a hook is a peer of the object rather than one of its coroutines. perform_erase kills every coroutine the object owns three lines after firing ondie; spawning it the way start_object_scripts spawns idle would have that loop kill the script the line above had just started, on the erase path only, and every test that checked the death path would still pass. This project’s signature failure, avoided by reading four instructions.

What a hook sees on the erase path is nothing, and the shipped scripts say so. The coroutine does not run at the fire; it runs when the scheduler next reaches it, by which time an erase has freed the object and the handle resolves to nothing — Obj::player answers -1 rather than trapping, so the script runs to the end and learns nothing. That is the original’s behaviour too, and it is why every one of the fourteen shipped ondie scripts opens on if (.IsValid) or if (.InHolder()). The payout is real on the death path, where the corpse is still standing.

onenter sits on the entry, not on the roster. 0x005d3e10 detaches the object from its previous holder, refuses when the new one has no room, and only then writes the holder handle, drops the position to (-1,-1) and fires — with the settlement resolved from [holder+0xc], which is Settlement::object from the other end. So the fire is on AddUnit/ForceAddUnit and deliberately not on EconomySystem::garrison_add: UNIT_ON_ENTER.VS converts the unit’s carried Spoils of War into settlement gold, and a load that replayed every garrison membership as an entry would pay every one of them again.

The census this page carried was wrong in both halves. Not eighteen classes: fifteen declare one, in seventeen elements — ondie on fourteen, onkill on Unit, onenter on Unit twice, which is one of the fourteen duplicate-sig cases and inert because both name the same file. Declaring is also not the number that matters, because methods are inherited: after resolution 203 of the 845 classes answer ondie and 271 answer each of the other two. And nothing calls them — a word-boundary sweep of all 885 shipped scripts finds zero mentions of any of the three, which is what makes them the engine’s to fire. docs/formats/sc-xml.md carries the table now.

The library has a fourth source, and it is the only one that cannot be primed from the objects. A hook outlives the object it was started for — ondie’s subject is by definition on its way out — so prime_library walks the class graph for the three sigs rather than the world, or a save taken while one is suspended would name a chunk no surviving object’s class declares and Scheduler::deserialize would refuse the whole load with not_found. Said plainly: constructible, and not observed on the shipped corpus. 1_Great_Losses_Rome fires 233 hooks in 120 turns and round-trips under either walk, because Military sits above every soldier and some soldier is always still alive; it takes a map whose last instance of a hook-binding class dies while its hook is suspended, which is what the fault sweep below made a test build. The class walk is correct by construction rather than by census, and is also 17 rows against 21,890.

Twenty-five faults injected, nineteen caught first pass. Three of the six survivors named cases the tests did not have and now do: the latch applied to the whole family rather than to ondie alone (a soldier that kills twice books one kill), onenter fired before the garrison had accepted the unit, and prime_library walking the world instead of the class graph, which is the paragraph above and the test that now catches it. Twenty-two of twenty-five on the second pass. The remaining three are unobservable by construction and are annotated rather than tested, each where it lives: the null-receiver guard is redundant as a refusal and load-bearing as the latch's precondition; a surplus argument to ondie is dropped by script::start, so only an under-count of a hook's arity can ever be seen; and handing onenter the holder instead of the settlement reads identically, because SettlementStore::for_object maps all four handles of a composite to one row.

Left out and named. The onfinish attribute — 10 elements, 6 files — is the fourth member of this family and how the engine fires it is not read. Holder eviction on erase is still open, as the erase thread left it.
SetFeeding after Place (done)
and what it was hiding		FeederSystem is world-bound and reconciles against World::objects() at the head of every turn, so a unit a script places was not in the chain until the next one — and OUTPOST_BEHAVIOR.VS calls u1.SetFood(20); u1.SetFeeding(false); two lines after its Place, with no turn boundary in between. enrol_if_eligible is the predicate sync_with_world already applied, lifted into one function and called from place_object and from SpawnGroup's per-template spawn. The original has no chain to be late for: a CVXUnit is a feeding unit from construction.

And it was the wrong diagnosis, which is the part worth keeping. Fixing it changed nothing on the real map, because the unit was never placed at all — map_size was zero and Place refused. Twelve traps that read as a feeder problem were a match-setup problem two layers up; see the corrections table. The timing gap was real and is fixed on its own evidence, but the thread that surfaced it was somebody else's.
The narration layer (done)Closed by four threads above rather than by itself, which is why it is worth striking rather than leaving: the camera, the chrome and the control groups took View, PlayMovie, ShowHint, BlockUserInput and SetShortcutSel; what a script asks the interface to show took CreateFeedback and the rollover family; the conversation layer and the mission’s notes took the other two. Nothing declared under this heading is unimplemented: host_coverage.py --leaders lists none of them.

And the corpus agrees. trap_sweep.py over the 25 runnable maps reports 10 distinct traps in 1,554 hits and not one of them is a narration entry point — the list is Breakpoint (1,532 of the hits, in one idle script), GetVec/3, BestGate/1, SetName/1, AIStop/1, and five one-hit oddities. It was eleven traps in 1,562 hits until Hero::IsArmyOutside got a body; its 8 hits were the second-largest entry on the list. Missions narrate: Numantia gives 4 of its 11 notes and Danube 2 of its 5, in a headless run with nobody watching.
Boarding (done)This row used to read “Ship::UnitsCount is refused on purpose: the ship holder carries no occupancy and nothing boards, so any number would be invented”. There is boarding now, so there is an occupancy to count, and the refusal has no subject left.

Nine entry points and one feature. A ship told to pick somebody up keeps an ordered list of the units it expects; they walk to it; each one that gets within 150 units steps into its holder and comes off the list. Ten shipped scripts — SHIP_BOARD, UNIT_BOARD, UNIT_BOARD_COMMON, HERO_BOARD, their four ..._ONFINISHs and SHIP_BOARD_ONFINISH.

The unit’s own field is not the list, and that is what the family turns on. [unit+0x18c] — Unit::GetShipToBoard — is stamped by NotifyBoardUnit and cleared by nothing: not the cancel, which erases from the deque and never touches the unit, not a successful board. So it names the ship that last asked and outlives the asking, which is exactly why both ..._ONFINISH scripts can reach a ship back through it. Deriving the list from that field would have been wrong twice: a cancelled unit would look like a member, and a unit two ships had asked for could only name one. The original lets it sit in both deques and so does this.

Only two of the three readers prune. NumUnitsToBoard is four instructions and touches nothing, where AreUnitsToBoard and BestCandidateToBoard drop the dead first — so a ship whose expected party has died still reports them, and UNIT_BOARD_COMMON.VS ends its loop on that number. The full-ship clear is an equality test and a ship can get past it: BoardUnit checks distance and not capacity, and the script tests UnitsCount == UnitsMax on the line after it boards.

Two things are not reproduced, each with the reason written where it would be missed: the [ship+0x1d8] re-entrancy latch, which guards a deque against the ..._ONFINISH scripts its own kills launch and cannot change any state observed afterwards; and one comparison in BestCandidateToBoard that tests a zero-or-one boolean against the ship’s holder handle, which has no image in an engine that does not share the original’s handle numbering.

35 injected faults, all caught — after three rounds. The first found a guard duplicated between the entry point and the table, so a fault in the table’s half was invisible behind the other; two more crashed the suite instead of failing it, both in this file’s own tests dereferencing what a fault had emptied; and the army test never gave the hero a place in the queue to keep, so the branch that keeps it was never exercised.
Two GameSession gaps (done)Both were closed and the document was not told. docs/formats/save.md still said CommandSystem::set_scheduler is never called, that nothing enrols a Combatant, and that neither CombatSystem::set_class_graph nor MovementSystem::set_grid has a caller. GameSession::create calls all four, and CombatSystem::reconcile enrols from the world at start and again at the top of every advance.

The consequence was a whole domain resting on synthetic tests, and that is over. The same document listed combat among the systems whose section is “nothing to lose: unchanged from turn zero” on every real map — an honest answer at the time, because an empty system has nothing to save. On 6_Great_Battles_Danube a 200-turn session now enrols hundreds of combatants, lands 6,002 blows and kills 420, and deleting the combat section is caught by load refused. campaign and areas are still on that line for their own reasons, which are the map’s and the format’s rather than the wiring’s.

The other half of the entry — that adding a system moves every map’s world hash, so a conformance run pinning hashes needs rebaselining after one lands — is not a gap and stays true. It is a note for whoever adds the next system.
Multiplayer (done, but for the rated drop path and polish)Deliberately last, and started from the end that is not networking: sim/netcmds.hpp is the command stream and the order every peer applies it in, and conformance::check_lockstep proves two peers driven by one stream agree on shipped maps. The netcmds fold is this engine's, because no dump prints a command payload.

The turn negotiator is the second slice, and it still opens no socket. sim/lockstep.hpp is the protocol a transport will carry: one packet per peer per turn, empty or not; orders given while turn n is next run on n + delay; a turn runs when every peer has spoken for it and never on a guess, because the one peer that guessed wrong is playing a different game. A repeated packet is a harmless duplicate and a different packet for the same (peer, turn) is refused, since believing either lets two honest peers be told different things. The wire omits issuer and sequence and the decoder restores them from the sender and the position, so a peer cannot speak for another player. Every count is checked against the bytes behind it before anything is allocated, and every prefix of a valid packet is refused.

The length is agreed, which is what tick.md said it had to be. The dumps renegotiate mid-session (400 → 460 → 800; 460, 291, 213, 200), so each packet carries its sender's proposal in real milliseconds, and every peer takes the largest, clamps it to the observed extremes and converts it at the game speed. At 999 that is the dump's own 800 → 799. Largest, the bounds and the default delay of two are this engine's and labelled as such: the logs record what the pump ran, never when a command was issued or why a length moved.

check_netplay is the proof, and it makes two comparisons. Each peer gets its own session and only its own player's orders, over a loopback that delivers every packet late, out of order and twice. Every peer is compared with every other, and the first with an unnetworked record of the stream and schedule they agreed on. The second comparison is not redundant: peers that all advance by the real milliseconds instead of the converted length agree with each other perfectly, and only the unnetworked run catches it (injected and caught by that comparison alone). Twenty-one faults were injected and nineteen caught. One survivor is the count guard: its only effect is an allocation that the reads after it would refuse anyway, and it stays on the hostile-input argument. The other is deleting the unnetworked comparison itself, which no test sees on its own. Injected together with the real-milliseconds fault, it lets that fault through, and that pairing is the evidence the comparison does something.

A false pass on the way, the same kind the last slice found. The first corpus run reported 0 stalls and passed on both maps: a packet is needed delay + 1 rounds after it is sent, and the network was never later than that, so the path that makes lockstep lockstep never ran. The default lateness is past the slack now, and imconform netplay fails when no peer waited. tests/test_corpus_lockstep.py asserts on stalls, duplicates, refusals, the spread of lengths and the orders agreed, on Zama and Numantia, at delays of two and zero.

The link layer is the third slice, and the core still has no socket. UDP loses packets and the negotiator stalls on a lost one, correctly and forever. sim/netlink.hpp handles loss the way lockstep games always have: every datagram carries every turn packet the far side has not acknowledged, oldest turn first, up to a byte budget under the IPv6 minimum MTU, so a lost datagram is repaired by the next one and there is no retransmission timer. Acknowledgement is per origin and contiguous: “I hold every packet from P below turn N”. A packet is forwarded on every link but the one it came from, so a peer linked to everyone is a relay and only the host needs a reachable address. Each datagram carries a stamp, the last stamp heard on that link, and how long it was held, so a round trip is now − echo − hold with no agreement between the two clocks. The proposal is the slowest link's smoothed round trip spread over the delay's turns with half again as margin; that rule is this engine's. check_netplay's relay mode proves it: four peers on a star losing a quarter of their datagrams play one game, measured proposals are agreed, and a link that loses everything is a deadlock rather than a pass.

Twenty faults, twenty caught, but only after seven survived the first pass, and each survivor named a topology the tests had not built. The hardest was not sending a packet back where it came from. In a star that check can never fire, because a client drops a forwarded packet at once and the hub learns every packet from its origin, which already acknowledges it. It matters only in a mesh of four, when a node learns a packet second-hand across a gap, and that is now a test. A check that it never offers a packet to its own origin could not fire in any topology, since a peer's own packets are contiguous from the moment it offers them. It is gone, and the header says why.

The socket is the fourth slice, and two processes now play one game. engine/net is new: a UDP socket and a steady clock, with no SDL, so the headless tools link it; the WebAssembly build has none, since a browser has no UDP. The lobby is three messages whose codecs are in the core (sim/netlobby.hpp). A joiner's hello carries the protocol version and a hash of its data.pak and map, so a joiner with a different game is refused before the match rather than discovered by a desync inside it. The host's start carries the match id, the joiner's slot, the seed and the lockstep configuration, and is resent until the joiner's first link datagram arrives. A refusal is readable across builds, because “you are a different build” is the one refusal a different build has to read. NetMatch is the pump: it feeds datagrams to the link layer, sends on a 15 ms cadence or at once when there is news, and proposes what the link layer measured. imconform udp-host and udp-join run it between processes, and tests/test_corpus_udp.py compares host with joiner on Zama and Numantia, through 30% deliberate loss, and with a joiner holding another map, which is refused. tools/check_core_boundary.py now forbids socket headers in the core.

A run is no longer reproducible, and that is correct. With proposals measured, the schedule depends on real round trips, so two matches on one map and seed agree on the orders and differ in the world. Nothing compares one match with another; everything compares the peers inside one. The first real run also caught a refusal on an honest network: the host's resent start arriving after the match had begun, read as a malformed link datagram. The pump now lets lobby traffic pass mid-match.

The app plays it, the fifth slice. --host PORT and --join HOST:PORT run the lobby before the window opens, and engine/app/netplay.hpp takes over three things. A right click, a command bar row and a surrender stop acting on the session and become orders for the agreed turn. The clock runs agreed turns at their agreed real length, and a menu no longer holds it. Pause, step, save, load and the editor are refused. tests/test_corpus_app_net.py runs two windows on different seats, each right-clicking with its own selection, and requires the same orders on the same turns and identical hashes, on Zama and on Numantia through 30% loss.

Four things had to change for that to be true, and three of them would have desynced silently.
  • Command-bar orders: NetOrder could only carry a right click, so the command bar and surrender became order kinds, on wire version 2 and protocol 2. CommandBarSink applies them over the actors the order names, filtered by is_commandable exactly as a right click is. A peer that names another player's army is not given it, and a test says so.
  • Which slot is human: MatchOptions::human was the local player, so each peer would have run the AI for the other's human. Every peer now builds the same options: networked, every seat human, and the match's human the lowest seat, because it is hashed. In a networked game one human's result no longer ends the match, and match_status(world, viewer) asks “did I win” of this seat.
  • Difficulty and map index: both reach the simulation, so the host's travel in the start message rather than each machine's options. The bonus scripts are skipped on every peer alike until the lobby carries each player's choice.
  • Looking: a peer's interface runs scripts no other peer runs, which was the fourth question. It was measured rather than assumed: imconform observe watches one of two sessions, running the command bar's and the info bar's verifiers and the cursor's order resolution every turn, and no hash moves on Zama, Numantia or Crossroads. An injected RNG draw in a verifier is caught at turn 1. The UDP peers now also play as their own seats, since local_player reaches scripts through the screen entry points, and 150 turns on each map agree.


The shipped screens host and join, the sixth slice. The main menu's Multiplayer is enabled and opens MPMENU.INI. Its game list is filled by a LAN broadcast, a typed address wins over the list, and GameSpy stays disabled with the rest of the online front. Host goes through the scenario list to MPGAMEMENU.INI as the host's lobby. A joiner sees the same screen from a roster the host streams, edits only its own row, and presses I'm ready; the host's Start waits for every ready mark. MULTI.INI carries “connecting”, every refusal, and a player leaving. The start now carries the whole players' screen, meaning every row's type, nation, team, bonus and AI strength plus the settings screen's rules, and every peer runs the skirmish setup from it. So a networked game has the nations, teams and bonuses its host chose, where the command line's form still plays the map's own.

What moved to make that possible. The map now travels as a path and a file hash, because a joiner from the list does not know it until told. The hello carries data.pak alone, and a joiner that lacks the map byte for byte leaves and says why rather than waiting to desync. The protocol is at 3. A peer leaving mid-match ends it for everyone with a message, because dropping one player needs every remaining peer to agree on the last turn it played, which is not done. The lobby's exchanges have their own tests over real loopback sockets (engine/net/tests, run by verify.py), twelve faults injected into them and all twelve caught. Discovery was listing one host twice, once per address it answered from; the screenshot found it.

Departures, speed and chat, the seventh slice. A player who leaves no longer ends the match. Every peer that learns of the departure freezes at what it holds of that player’s packets and reports the bound. The host, as coordinator, decides the largest report once every peer still playing has reported, and every peer applies exactly the departed player’s packets below it (sim/netdepart.hpp; the agreement is this engine’s, made explicit where the original’s split agrees only implicitly). On that turn the computer takes the seat, as the original’s drop routine does (0x00406840 into the body behind AIStart, 0x00434ca0), through a departed order the negotiator writes into the agreed history. The host leaving ends the match, visibly. SetSpeed is an order, as it is in the original (0x004c6ce0, 0x004e67c0): every peer writes the clock’s speed as it applies the order and converts every later turn at it, and a match can fix its speed. Chat (INGAMECHAT.INI in the match, MPCHAT.INI in the lobby) rides the link datagram and the lobby’s roster and choice, reaches every peer once and in order, and never reaches the command stream: a lossy match played with chat and in silence has the same hash on every turn. The protocol is at 6. Faults injected: 31 into departures, 14 into speed and 23 into chat. The survivors named five missing tests, now written, plus three mutants that could not change anything; the two that were dead checks are gone.

What the original does, read rather than guessed. docs/engine/netjoin.md reads the late join, the speed and the drop off gbr.exe. The original has no late join: who is in a match is fixed at its start (0x004071b0), and the connected mask is only ever cleared. So a join handshake would be this engine’s own, and the document proposes one: an admit order in the agreed stream, then a save streamed from the host. It also confirms the command order netcmds.hpp had labelled (the pump at 0x004f79d0 runs commands by issuer, then in issue order), and finds that the app reads GameSpeed as a turn interval when it is a speed position (13 → 999).

The single-player speed, and speed from a script. GameSpeed is a speed position, and it runs both ways. A match starts at NormalSpeed (0x0052683b) and writes the position back from that speed (0x006e71e0), so the shipped 13 is 1000 written back. OK on the options screen sends it forward as 999 (0x006e7ff0), which is tick.md’s 800 → 799 dump: a player pressed OK without moving the slider. Both directions live in sim::game_speed_from_option and option_from_game_speed (sim/tick.hpp). Single-player posts its speed as an order through sim::LocalOrders, converting a turn before applying its orders, the same rule the negotiator keeps. A match at 1000 now runs in real time rather than eight times faster. SetSpeed (0x004c6ce0) posts a set_speed order through HostContext::outbox, as the local player, unless the match fixed its speed; GetSpeed (0x004c6220) reads the clock. The app does not run SCDEBUG.XML’s bindings, so the keypad is not wired, and TogglePause has no call site, so it is recorded in vs-host-api.md rather than built. Faults injected: 18 into the conversion, 17 caught; 11 into the script side, 10 caught; each survivor is noted where it lives.

A late join, the eighth slice (this engine’s own: the original admits nobody once a match has started, netjoin.md question 1). A player can join a running match on a seat the computer took when its player left; an empty open seat is not joinable, because none exists in a running match. The host decides the first turn S of the joiner’s part, past every packet it has sent, and says so as a fact in every link datagram beside the drops. Every seat’s part is now a list of stints, and every departure fact names its stint, so a seat can leave, be joined and leave again. The negotiator writes a joined order into turn S; applying it is AIStop (0x00422720) for the seat on every peer, the inverse of the takeover. After turn S−1 the host streams GameSession::save() beside the link layer: a header with the start, S, the stream hash so far, the stints and the chat numbering, then acknowledged 1 KiB chunks. The joiner loads it and hashes like everyone else from its first turn, on the world, netcmds and every channel. The match plays on while the save travels and waits only from S+delay+1. It departs from netjoin.md’s proposal in five places, recorded there: admission is a datagram fact rather than an order in the stream, because a relayed first packet would otherwise reach peers that refuse it and never be resent. Proved in core_tests (check_netplay with joiners through loss, a speed change on the turn before, and a seat joined twice), with imconform netjoin on real sessions, over real sockets in net_tests, and between four processes in test_corpus_udp.py and four app windows in test_corpus_app_net.py, including through the shipped screens. The protocol is at 7. Fifty-two faults injected: the first sweep caught 42 of 51, the survivors named six missing tests and one real ordering bug (a stint’s end applied before its admission), and three are explained where they live. A flaky chat test went with it: a joiner a turn behind stopped short of turns it already held when the host left; it now runs them first. A takeover is shown as the original shows it: the seat’s name with the language pack’s “(AI)” appended, the translated drop line, and PlayerDropped.wav once, through the first sound-effect path (platform/audio.hpp). The marker is presentation, beside the match and in no hash or save (ComputerSeats, sim/netdepart.hpp), and a late join takes it off again, which the original never has to.

The computer’s war between peers. imconform lockstep, netplay and netjoin refused Crossroads, which gives nobody an order at turn 0, and behind that their session was not a skirmish at all: no terrain, templates or entities and no start_match, so no army could march. --skirmish builds the map as imrun does and starts it as the app starts a networked match, with the peers as humans who give no orders; the guard is a blow between two players, and every peer and late joiner now runs from its own seat (Scenario::start_as), because the local seat is the one input two peers are sure to disagree about. On Crossroads, lockstep on the map’s own setup agrees through the capture, and its final session hash equals imrun’s (11dc4c1e235fadfe); netplay agrees over 2,400 hostile turns (1,730 stalls, 1,991 duplicates); a seat handed to the computer and back twice agrees through 1,500 turns; over UDP with loss, two and four processes agree, including a late joiner seated mid-war; and watching from seat 0 through the war moves no hash. The late join found a real desync. GameSession::load restored the script watermark but not whether the per-turn idle pass runs, so a session loaded without being started never gave a newly trained unit its idle script, and the joiner parted from its peers one to four turns after it was seated — first in the session section’s watermark, then world, script, movement and AI. The load now takes that from the saved game (a watermark is saved only by a game that ran object scripts); the app and imsave were never affected, since they start a session before loading. 17 faults injected, 17 caught, one after the never-fought test was lengthened past the sentries’ first fight with the wildlife. Short runs are in the Python suite; the long ones are opt-in under verify.py --war. The short war runs no longer wait for the computer to start a war: its first blow on Crossroads moved with each faithful fix (turn 261, then 736, then 405, then out of reach). They play Alesia as a networked match between players 0 and 1 (--seats 0,1), whose armies stand 68 units apart and fight from turn 1, and add a strike (--strike 1): on the first turn a seat can, the seat nearest an enemy right-clicks it, chosen from each peer’s own world on that turn, since a skirmish’s unit ids follow the negotiated turn lengths and no list fixed in advance can name them. 120 turns each, seconds rather than minutes. The never-fought guard runs 20 turns of Crossroads; the long opt-in runs keep Crossroads’ own setup. Making them so found a real bug: a peer catching up ran many turns on one stale clock reading, so the turns it heard were stamped old and its partner was dropped as silent after 15 s; imconform and the app’s catch-up now read the clock per turn.

What is left in Part 7: the rated drop path (0x00524090, inferred to declare defeat), and TogglePause and the SCDEBUG.XML keys if a debug keymap is ever wanted. A joiner cannot pick its seat and only one joins at a time; both are this engine’s limits, not the original’s.
Three names off the top of the block list (done)RevealHiddenEnemyUnits/3 (5 sites), Hero::IsArmyOutside/0 (3) and GAIKA::GetDistToPlayers/4 (3) — 11 sites and 9 more scripts. Names with no body 193 → 190; sites 346 → 335; blocked scripts 145 → 136. None of the three needed a new field, a new system or a new hash channel, and one of them had been recorded as needing all three — see the corrections table.

The first two are one word per object. RevealHiddenEnemyUnits is kSyncHidden, kSyncUnit and is_enemy, then the hidden bit cleared; IsArmyOutside is [member+0x154] != 0xffff over the hero's army, which is ObjectState::holder. What made them worth doing first is not their size: they were the sole blockers of three shipped scripts each, which is what --block ranks and what neither of the other two rankings can see.

19 faults injected, 17 caught. The two survivors are annotated rather than tested and both are the same shape — a guard whose behaviour something else already guarantees. Dropping the hidden test clears a bit that is already clear, so no state moves; dropping the out-of-range player guard reaches PlayerTable::is_enemy, which refuses an invalid viewer three lines later. Each is kept for what it says and what it costs, not for what it changes, and the sweep is the thing that established the difference.

The waiting loops are the point of the second one. All three of its sites are while (!.IsArmyOutside()) .FormKeepMoving(500); immediately after a FormSetupAndMoveTo, so an unimplemented answer was not a missing feature but a hero that never marched. The dead-member rule matters for the same reason: a handle that no longer resolves is skipped rather than counted indoors, which is what lets the loop terminate over an army that was killed inside a building.

And the third was not blocked on the GAIKA node after all. It is a GAIKA member, which made it look like thread #53's problem — and this page said so, in as many words, one revision before it was written. It is not: 0x0042c1c0 walks the world's settlements, keeps the ones whose central building is a heir of BaseTownhall (which is what SettlementKind::stronghold already means here), measures a straight line, doubles it when the settlement's node is in a different LSA from the receiver's, and files the least by side. Everything it needs — the node table, the areas, the settlement store, the relations matrix — landed with the partition. What it needed from the node was center and lsa, and both have been there since.

Its sentinel is −1 and that is load-bearing. The three out-parameters are initialised to −1 and stay there for a side with no home, which is why ESH_FOODTRADE.VS and ESH_GOLDTRADE.VS both follow the call with if (nEDist < 0) nEDist = 32000;. A zero would have made no enemy anywhere the most attractive answer their nEval = nEDist − nDist ranking can produce rather than the least. 12 faults, 12 caught — but only after the first sweep found two survivors and both were the fixture rather than the code: own and ally were equidistant, and each side's nearest home was also its last. The layout says so now.

And it is reached on a shipped map, at a length the trap sweep does not run to. This name never appears in trap_sweep.py's 60-turn tally — unlike IsArmyOutside, whose 8 hits it removed, the AI helpers that call this one have not got that far by turn 60, and the tally is unmoved at 10 traps in 1,554 hits. At 200 turns on Danube they have: the save is 368,995 bytes against 368,931 at the parent commit, same map, same seed, same object count, and it still loads, still matches at every one of the ten turns after the load, and still re-saves byte-identically with every section fault caught. A sweep that finds nothing is evidence about the sweep's horizon as much as about the code.
One command taken off one building (done)Obj::CmdDisable/1 — 2 sites, both in 3_Great_Losses_Egypt's seq2.vs, which opens by taking “BuySlaves” off its market and “Chariot of Osiris” off its temple. Names with no body 24 → 23; sites 29 → 27. Blocked scripts stay at 10: seq2.vs also calls Unit::AddBonus/5, and a file finishes when its last blocker does.

It is the by-name half of a flag this engine already had. ObjectFlags::commands_disabled is [obj+0x9c], which SetCmdEnable writes and is_commandable reads: an object that takes no orders at all. The other half is a linked list at [obj+0x78], and vtbl+0xd8 is the predicate that asks both — false when the word is zero, then false again when the command is in the list. CmdDisable is vtbl+0xdc and appends to it; CmdEnable is vtbl+0xe0 and removes from it. Two virtuals apart, sharing the predicate one slot below them, which is how the pair was found at all: the entry point itself is a name lookup and a virtual call.

The append is gated on the predicate, and the gate is observable. A CmdDisable issued while SetCmdEnable(false) stands writes nothing, so lifting the flag brings the command back — the script that thought it had taken it away had not. Nothing in the shipped corpus does both to one object, but the ordering is the original's and it is the fault the sweep found first.

The reader is the original's own. 0x00560461, inside ObjList::GetCanExecCmd, calls that same vtbl+0xd8 — so this engine's GetCanExecCmd now asks the same third question after the row and the bound method. Its other readers in gbr.exe are four sites in the interface's own button availability, which this engine's command bar does not gate yet; that is named rather than guessed at.

A dead receiver is not refused, and a stale handle is not a trap. Neither the entry point nor the virtual has an IsAlive test, where the neighbouring SetCmdEnable prints for one and returns; and a handle the object table cannot resolve jumps past the whole body to the same cleanup the unknown-name path uses. Both are the opposite of what the surrounding family does, so both are asserted.

The state is a name, not an index, and it is saved. The original stores the command's index in its global registry; CommandTable here is sorted by folded name, so an index would move the moment a DATA/COMMANDS/*.XML merged in a different order and the save would then mean something else. Section version 11 → 12. Not hashed — CommandSystem has no hash override at all, which is the standing asymmetry this page already records, and the section bytes are what catches a dropped set.

16 faults, 15 caught — after three rounds. The first left six survivors and four of them became tests rather than annotations: the fold in the membership test is reachable, because CommandTable::set keeps the later spelling of a row it overwrites and the stored name then disagrees in case; an emptied set leaving its row behind shows only in the bytes; and the loader's three validity checks needed a section built by hand, since nothing in this tree can write a bad one. The second round found three faults that crashed the suite instead of failing it, all in this work's own tests indexing a span a fault had emptied — the same defect this page records against the boarding sweep, and the reason those indices are behind REQUIRE now. The one survivor is an equivalence: CmdEnable drops every copy of the name, which is the original's remove, and nothing can put two in.

CmdEnable is implemented and deliberately not bound. No shipped script calls it, so it is absent from declare_shipped_surface and binding it would widen the declared surface, which Part 5 does not do. It exists on the system because it is what makes the state a set rather than a one-way latch, and because the original's pair is the evidence for what the list holds.
Two caster searches, a supply destination, and a score that is not ready (done, and one refusal)Druid::FindUnitToRevitalize/0 (7 sites, the sole blocker of two scripts), Druid::FindUnitToHide/0 (4) and Settlement::BestToSupply/0 (2, the sole blocker of both). Names with no body 190 → 187; sites 335 → 322; blocked scripts 136 → 131.

It is FindUnitToHeal's twin, and the differences are the finding. Same skeleton — a predicate built from the caster, a circle of the caster's own sight, the least score kept, an AI-roster fallback this family declines — but three terms apart. Heal minimises a health fraction plus reach; revitalize minimises reach and nothing else, so a far unit at a tenth of its health loses to a near one at two thirds. Heal parks the too-lightly-wounded at a flat 1000; revitalize parks the ones the spell would take past their own maxhealth at the same 1000 — the same device pointing the other way. And a hero halves its own score, floor included, which nothing in heal does: a hero has to be more than twice as far before a plain unit is preferred.

One exclusion in it is not a graph name. The predicate rejects a candidate whose RTTI descriptor is CVXDruid — the type object at 0x00821174 — where the rest of this family tests class names. That is NativeClass::druid here, and the test fixture grew a native-class parameter to be able to spawn one, because a unit carrying a cpp_class="CVXDruid" graph class does not exercise it.

12 faults, 12 caught — on the second sweep. The first had four survivors and every one was the same fixture defect: the reach term clamps at zero, so every candidate inside the caster's range scores identically, and four assertions that looked like they were testing an exclusion were being carried by a tie the earlier object already won. The blockers are heroes at point-blank range now, which gives each of them a score that would strictly win, and the clamp itself has an assertion saying the tie goes to the lower object id.

FindUnitToHide cannot return a target on any data, and that is the finding rather than a gap. Its scan admits only owners in the caster's friendly mask — [record + 0x10] ^ 0xffff — and its predicate then calls Obj::IsEnemy on the same pair and requires it true. Those are complements of one bit, so the conjunction is empty for every caster on every map. Three things fix the polarity independently: sim/player.cpp already records that SetRelation maintains enemyflags as the exact complement of that bit; GetBestCrippleTarget, which targets enemies, passes the unxored word to the same constructor and calls the same IsEnemy; and FindUnitToRevitalize, which targets allies, passes the xored one and tests no relation at all. It reads as cripple's predicate with heal's mask.

It is bound anyway, because the original does not trap. It returns an invalid handle and DRUID_HIDE_GROUND.VS spends its RetryCount retries and walks on; a declared name with no body would stop the script at the line, which is less faithful than answering nothing. The two contradictory clauses are written out side by side rather than collapsed into a return, so the emptiness is derived from the reading and a revised reading is one line. This is the second dead thing in the same feature — the corrections table already carries Sacrifice::IsInvisibility, which compares a class name against "Hide" when no class named Hide ships — and the druid's hide spell is what both of them are for. 4 faults, 4 caught.

BestToSupply compares areas, and that is the only reason it works here. It is the nearest town hall of the same owner in the same LSA: 0x0042c800 takes the u16 at [node + 0x10] for the receiver's own node — the same word GetDistToPlayers compares to decide whether to charge a crossing — refuses outright if it is zero, and then walks the settlement vector keeping the least straight-line distance between centres. ES_VILLAGE.VS writes the answer down beside the call: "the return value is always stronghold".

Had it compared node identity instead, it would answer nothing on every map this engine loads. The node partition here is the stated approximation — one node per settlement, so no two settlements ever share one — while the LSA partition is measured and exact. The entry point reaches only the half that is measured, which is a piece of luck worth recording: the approximation's blast radius is smaller than sim/gaika.hpp could promise. And the receiver is not excluded from its own answer, which is the original's; neither shipped caller can reach that branch, because one is a village and the other an outpost and both fail the town-hall test. 8 faults, 8 caught — two on the second pass, after the first found that the only candidate surviving the three exclusions was also the only candidate at all, so minimising and maximising were the same walk.

And GetTeamOverallScore/1 is decoded and deliberately not bound. It leads the block ranking and it stays there. The arithmetic is settled — gold_used / 1000 + (power_score × military_rating) / 100, averaged over the team, with military_rating the expression GetTeamMilitaryScore already computes — but two of its three inputs are missing in different ways: gold_used is a counter nothing here maintains (three known writers, ordinary work), and power_score is a whole-map census that is half recovered — the settlement walk is legible (20 points for a town hall, 3 for anything else, plus warehouse gold and food and a per-unit value over the garrisons) and the second pass over the object grid runs through a local functor whose per-object body is not identified. Binding it now would mean inventing a census, which is what sim/gaika.hpp refused to do for the node table. sim/match.hpp carries the whole reading, what would settle it, and what would then still be needed.
One stat taken off one hero, and an argument the game never reads (done)Unit::AddBonus/5 — 1 site, and it is the last name standing between 3_Great_Losses_Egypt's seq2.vs and its run. Blocked scripts 10 → 9; names with no body 23 → 22; sites 27 → 26; the trap sweep 20 hits → 19. And the campaign containers now have no declared-only name left at all — 16,834 of 16,834, the first half of the corpus to close completely.

It is five addends over a unit's class numbers, and the game caches what this engine derives. The site is GoodHero.obj.AsUnit().AddBonus(0, -10, 0, 0, 0): ten points of slash armour taken off the hero the mission is about, which is the mission's handicap and not a reward. The original keeps the record in a side table of its own — a map from object id to five ints, held by the singleton at 0x9c0914 — because the numbers it modifies are cached on the object: [obj+0xc8] maxhealth, [obj+0xcc] maxstamina, [obj+0xdc] attack, [obj+0xe4] armour against slash, [obj+0xe8] against pierce. RecalcBonuses's virtual (vtbl+0xc4, 0x005d9e53) is the rebuild that copies all five off the class and then adds this record back on top, before it goes on to items and skills — so the record is precisely what survives a rebuild that a written-through field would not.

Here it is a field on the combatant, and that is an equivalence rather than a shortcut. Nothing in this engine caches a derived stat, which is why RecalcBonuses next door has always been a no-op and stays one: attack/0 reads profile.damage plus the addends on the spot, so the rebuild has already happened by the time anybody reads one. What the side table buys the original is that the entry dies with the object — the teardown at 0x005db50f erases it by id — and a field on Combatant gets that from reconcile, which drops the row of anything that has left the world. A Mutate drops it too, for the reason the four modifier fields beside it are dropped: the original mutates by minting a fresh object, and the key goes with the old one.

The entry point reads four of its five arguments, and this engine does too. 0x005e0ce0 pops the frame from the top down; the five ints occupy four bytes each above the receiver's eight, so they sit at top-20 through top-4. It reads top-20, top-16, top-12 and top-8, stores top-8 into the record's fourth and fifth slot, and never touches top-4. The stack pointer still moves by the full 28, so nothing downstream notices. Unit::RemoveBonus (0x005e07f0), whose signature is identical, pops all five — the two bodies differ by exactly one argument, which is what makes this a slip in one of them rather than a convention in both. It is faithful here, unobservable on shipped data (the one site passes 0 for both), and asserted: a test passes a fourth and a fifth that differ and pins which one lands.

It accumulates, and only RemoveBonus floors. 0x005e0c7b is the arm taken when the table already holds a record: it adds each of the five into the stored one, so two calls of +5 leave +10 and no caller can ask what it alone contributed. RemoveBonus subtracts and then clamps each of the five at zero (0x005e076c onwards, setle/dec/and, five times over) and drops the record when all five reach it — which is not symmetric, and means RemoveBonus(0,0,0,0,0) against a record holding -10 leaves 0. Any removal at all wipes every negative addend on the object. It is implemented and deliberately not bound: no shipped script calls it, so binding it would widen the declared surface, and it is on the system because it is what makes the record a quantity that can come back down.

Five readers had to be taught the record, and one of them is the census. The damage step on both sides, attack/0, maxstamina/0, both the health and the stamina cap, the two target filters that test a candidate's own attack — and maxhealth/0, which belongs to the object model rather than to combat and now asks the combat system when the object is a registered combatant. The census is the one that would have been missed: 0x004439e6 reads [obj+0xc8], [obj+0xc0], [obj+0xdc] and [obj+0xe4], all four the object's cached numbers rather than its class's, so Squad::Eval, GAIKA::Eval and the power score all move with a bonus. MatchSystem::record_kill is the boundary and stays where it was: it reads [victim+0x3c]+0x294, the class property, so a kill is worth the same whatever a script had put on the victim. Section version 12 → 13, state vector 15 → 16; not hashed, because no modifier on a combatant is and the original recomputes the fields they feed.

30 faults, 30 caught — after one round of four survivors, every one of them a reader no case had reached with a bonus on it rather than a claim in doubt: the projectile's armour at impact, the attacker's own half of the damage step, HealStamina's ceiling, and the maxstamina addend, which nothing that goes through the entry point can tell apart from the maxhealth one.
One class counted in one node, and a line of the corpus that was lying about the argument order (done)GAIKA::Count/3 — 2 sites, both in GS_CAPTURE.VS, and the entry point sim/squad.cpp had refused twice. Names with no body 22 → 21; sites 26 → 24; GS_CAPTURE.VS’s blockers 3 → 2, which leaves the 173-site strategy needing only Squadize/3 and allenemiesinholder/1.

The refusal was honest and the evidence for lifting it was in the executable all along. The stated reason was that the first argument’s meaning was unsettled, because the corpus contradicts itself: the two live sites write gaika.Count(AI_ENEMY, AIPlayer, "Peasant") and a commented-out line three above them writes gaika.Count(AIPlayer, AI_OWN, "GDruid"), and the values cannot separate the two orders because AI_ENEMY and AI_STAYING are both 4. 0x004330b0 separates them in one instruction: it decs the second argument before anything reads it. No side mask survives a decrement, and every 1-based player argument in this corpus wants one — it is the same dec GAIKA::GetSquads does at 0x004395a5. So the positions are (sides, player, class) and the dead line is simply stale. The body reads its player argument raw rather than through the file’s shared player_arg, and that is deliberate: player_arg folds “0 or less” and “past the sixteenth player” into one kNoPlayer, and 0x004330b0 treats them differently — the first forces AI_OWN over every squad, the second names nobody and selects none.

It is the census asked about one class, with two differences and a tail. Presence is not a parameter: where Count/6 filters on the caller’s mask, this one requires AI_STAYING outright, so a squad marching in or out is never counted however the caller asks. And SF_PEACEFUL squads are counted here: both walks share the classifier at 0x0044e1f0 and its third argument is the flag gate — the census passes 0 and gets the skip, this passes 1 and does not.
