Wildlife standing in a node is a body to “how many Peasants are here” and is not a body to “how strong is the army here”, which is a real asymmetry rather than a slip. The sides mask is tested against the relation, the way Eval/3’s trailing argument is; a caller naming no player at all gets AI_OWN for every squad rather than an answer from the alliance table.

The tail is the half that answers a real number today. After the squads, 0x004330b0 asks the node’s settlement (0x00420c00) for its sentries and adds the ready-sentry count — [settlement+0xf4], what GetSentry hands out and PutSentry gives back — when the asked-for class is Sentry or something under it. The direction is the surprising one and is asserted as such: 0x0059c020 walks the asked-for class’s ancestors looking for Sentry, so RSentry collects the roster and Unit, which every sentry is, does not. The squad half of this entry point is zero for the reason the whole GAIKA family is — nothing here assigns a node to a squad — but sentries_ready is live world state that the economy writes, saves and hashes, so GS_CAPTURE.VS asking how many sentries stand in a settlement it means to take gets an answer.

One gate is deliberately half-built, and the missing half is the next name. The original guards the tail on two numbers, [+0xf4] > 0 and [+0xf8] > 0; only the first is reproduced. [+0xf8] is the sentries’ strength, which 0x005c4930 recomputes as the per-sentry eval times the roster on every write, so the second gate carries nothing the first does not unless the per-sentry eval is zero — and that number is Settlement::EvalSentries (0x005c4a60, 1 site, still on the leader list). It appends Sentry to the settlement’s own building class name and scales that class’s maximum health by a per-player factor, so it is zero only for a settlement whose sentry class does not exist. Stated rather than guessed at.

And it corrected the census next door twice — see the corrections table. The neutral-player skip became the flag the executable actually tests, and the note that named 0x00420c00 as a fog-memory pass named the wrong function. The settlement’s contribution to Eval and Count is a real gap and is now written down as one; it is not filled here because for Eval it needs that same per-sentry strength, and filling it for Count alone would make the two disagree about a node in a way the original never does — GETGAIKASTRAT.VS reads them back to back.

26 faults, 23 caught, and the three survivors are each a proved equivalence rather than a gap: two null-class early-outs that the walk below would have answered the same way, and a kNoPlayer test on a relation lookup that PlayerTable already refuses. All three now say so where they live. The first round left three real survivors and each bought a case: a second settlement node, so that “the node’s own settlement” is a claim a test can fail; a negative roster, because the original’s gate is a signed jle; and an unowned settlement, which answers to no mask rather than folding onto player 0’s row.
Whether the garrison is all that is left (done)GAIKA::AllEnemiesInHolder/1 — 2 sites, both in GS_CAPTURE.VS. Names with no body 21 → 20; sites 24 → 22; and Squadize/3 is now the only thing standing between that 173-site strategy and its run, which puts it at the head of all three rankings at once for the first time.

It is the question a capture strategy asks before it commits. Both sites read it after the strength census has already said the defence outweighs the attack, and both take the same two branches: everything hostile indoors means SS_Siege, anything still standing outside means break off.

Two early refusals, and both answer false. 0x00430dc0 never looks at a squad when the node has no settlement — a region node has nothing to be inside, so it cannot make the claim — or when the settlement’s owner is not the asking player’s enemy. That second test is [owner_record + our_id*4 + 0x24] & 1, bit 0 of the settlement owner’s row for us and not of ours for it, which is the same asymmetric direction PlayerTable::is_enemy is written in and for the same evidence. The shipped maps carry one-sided truces, so the orders differ, and a case here pins which one.

The walk is Count/3’s with one different mask. Zero strength skipped, relation must carry AI_ENEMY, SF_PEACEFUL skipped — the classifier’s third argument is 0 here where Count/3 passes 1, so wildlife in the street does not stop a siege — and the presence mask is 6 rather than 4: staying or leaving. A squad on its way out is still standing here; one merely on its way in is not here yet. The family really does use both masks, and BestTargetInGAIKA above uses 6 too.

And a squad is judged by its first member alone (0x00443df0, the front of the member deque, which is the leader when there is one). Not every member: one unit decides for its whole squad, so a squad whose leader walked inside while the rest stand in the street reads as indoors. That is the original’s reading, it is not obviously intended, and it is reproduced rather than improved on — a squad is meant to be somewhere as a body, and the other way round would be inventing a rule.

One call in it is dead and is named rather than reproduced. After the loop, 0x00431025 calls the settlement query 0x00420c00 with four out-parameters and reads none of them; the value returned is the flag the loop set. The query has no side effects, so writing it here would be a statement whose only effect is to look faithful.

20 faults, 18 caught, and both survivors are proved equivalences that now say so where they live: a null-settlement early-out that SettlementStore::for_object refuses on its own first line, and a kNoPlayer refusal that the enemy test three lines down already makes. The first round cost a debugging session to a different kind of fault entirely — the test held Squad* across a create, and SquadTable keeps its squads in a vector, so the pointer dangled and answered plausibly. Both GAIKA tests now look their squads up by key, and both say why.
The squad former, and the last reachable script the surface blocked (done)Squadize/3 — 2 sites, and it was the sole blocker of a 173-site strategy. Names with no body 20 → 19; sites 22 → 20; blocked scripts 9 → 8, and GS_CAPTURE.VS runs. Of the eight that remain, five can never run on the original either and two are one name each; there is no longer a script this project is holding up on its own.

This is the first code in the tree that decides which army a body belongs to. Everything else in the squad and GAIKA family reads a membership somebody else built. The previous revision of this row said the rule was read end to end and deliberately not written, and named three things standing in the way; the decision to build it was taken, and all three turned out to be answerable rather than blocking.

The entry point is small and reuses what was already here. 0x00447330 — empty the caller's list, then per body a fresh single-member squad inheriting flags, both GAIKAs and StateSetTime from whatever squad it was in — is regroup_into_fresh_squads, written for SquadList::Train and reused by the siege planner, so there is one body for it rather than two that can drift. What is new is the regrouper it feeds, 0x00446a70.

The rule, in the original's order. A hero leads its own squad and takes its army with it — which is the identity sim/hero.hpp measured over 24 heroes in the dumps long before anything here could form a squad, and nothing ever merges into a hero's. A unit inside a holder belongs wherever its holder belongs, and to nothing at all when the holder belongs to nothing. A unit already within 256 units of its own leader, in a squad already pointed where the caller wants it, stays put. A ship never merges in either direction. Otherwise the first squad of the player's that will take it, on nine clauses — same destination GAIKA, same state, same flags word, fewer than ten members, same Squad::AIDest, a first member that exists and is not a hero and is not the object, not a ship, druid-ness equal, and within 256 units — and a leader that moves has everything it leaves behind re-homed after it. Failing all of that, a leader keeps its squad and a follower starts one.

The three obstacles, answered. [obj+0x194] & 0x800000 is a UnitFlags bit the loader discards, so the refusal it gates can never fire and is named rather than dropped. Bit 30 of [obj+0x198] is a per-object word this engine does not model — the merge requires two bodies to agree on it, so with the word absent both sides read false and the clause is satisfied, an equivalence given the absence rather than a guess; and it is no longer a mystery either, because its four readers all use it as an exclusion beside IsHeirOf("RamUnit") or beside the hero test, and 0x004385e5 wants IsHeirOf("Military") and the bit clear. It marks a body that does not count as a soldier. [squad+0xbc] has no reader in any of the 885 scripts, so nothing observable depends on it and it is not carried. The 256-unit radius and the ten-member cap are read out of the executable and are not inferences.

One thing this engine refuses to reproduce, and it is a crash. When a held body's holder is in no squad, 0x00446be9 returns null and 0x0044756c dereferences it at [eax+0x50]. Here that answers “no squad”, the list gets no entry, and the body stays in the fresh squad the walk gave it — which is where the original leaves it too, since it never takes that squad away either.

28 faults, 27 caught, and the sweep earned its keep twice over. The survivor is the locked-squad clause, which is unreachable through the only caller this engine has — every body arrives in a squad minted one line earlier, and a fresh squad is never locked — and now says so where it lives. More importantly the first round found a real divergence rather than a weak test: the walk was passing the caller's nState into step 1 instead of the original's 0xffff placeholder, which looks equivalent because every squad the list names ends up carrying it either way. It is not, because the state is one of the nine clauses. With the placeholder in it a body can never join a squad that existed before the call; with nState in it, a body joins any pre-existing squad already in the state being asked for — and GS_CAPTURE.VS asks for SS_KillAll over a map full of squads already in SS_KillAll. Squadize forms squads out of the bodies it was handed and joins them to nothing else, and the placeholder is what says so.
What a wall full of sentries is worth, and one class that had lost its nation (done)Settlement::EvalSentries/0 — 1 site, and the second half of a gate GAIKA::Count/3 had been carrying with only its first. Names with no body 19 → 18; sites 20 → 19; GS_SIEGE.VS’s blockers 3 → 2.

It answers a strength, not a count. The site is one line — nInHolder += gaika.settlement.EvalSentries; — added to UnitsInHolderEval() and then weighed against the enemy strength the census reports. 0x005c4a60 is perSentry × roster, where the roster is [+0xfc], what GetNumSentries answers, and not [+0xf4], the number actually standing in the wall — the cached figure at [+0xf8] uses that one instead, so the settlement carries two strengths built from the same per-sentry number and different tallies.

The per-sentry figure, every term read: quarter = (maxhealth × 5) / 4, power = damage + armor_slash, level from the environment integer SentriesLevel in the settlement’s own scope, and perSentry = (power × (level + 13) × quarter) / 1000 + 1. Both divisions truncate toward zero and the + 1 is outside the second, so a sentry that evaluates to nothing is still worth a point. The three class fields are [class+0x294], [class+0x2a0] and [class+0x2ac], and their names were read out of the class loader’s own strcmp chain rather than assumed — maxhealth, damage, armor_slash, with the first agreeing with what MatchSystem::record_kill already had for that offset. Nothing in the 885 shipped scripts writes SentriesLevel, so 13 is the only factor the retail content ever sees.

And the sentry class is computed, not read. 0x005c4830 takes the settlement’s central building, asks its class for a race index, turns that into a letter and looks up the concatenation: RSentry, TSentry, and six more. It is not Building::GetSentryClassName, which reads a class property declared only on walls and gates — a town hall has none, so the property route cannot answer here and the computed one can. The two agree where both apply. The eight letters came out of a jump table at 0x005a7c00 as G R C I M B E T, which is sim/player_host.cpp’s kRacePrefix exactly: two tables derived from unrelated code paths, with no difference between them.

It also found a live bug in code that had been here for months. The class loader (0x005a2400–0x005a3390) accepts, for each race, a capitalised and a lowercase spelling of the place and of the demonym — Britain, britain, British, british, Briton, briton all read as 5 — and race_from_name carried only the eight place names. Across the installation’s 821 classes the spellings used are Gaul 58, RepublicanRome 52, Carthage 45, Britain 44, Egypt 44, Germany 44, Iberia 44, ImperialRome 29, Mutable 2, None 1 — and German 1. TOutpost, the Germanic outpost, was reading as raceless: its raceStrPref was empty where every other Germanic class answers T, and Settlement::UpgradeBestBarrack formats that letter into a command name. One class, and exactly the sort of thing that surfaces only as a barrack that will not upgrade in one nation.

20 faults, 20 caught. The first round left two survivors and both were the same shape as the last commit’s: an empty-roster gate that only shows on a negative roster, because the original’s is a signed jle; and a raceless-building refusal that needed the raceless Sentry class present in the test graph before picking it up by mistake could show. The first round also cost a debugging pass to a dangling Settlement* held across a create — the same vector-reallocation trap the squad tests hit one commit earlier, in a different store.
Inside the walls, and through the hole — the last two names in GS_SIEGE.VS (done)Gate::Inside/1 and Squad::InvadeThroughGate/2 — 1 site each, and between them the last of a 111-site strategy’s blockers. Names with no body 18 → 16; sites 19 → 17; blocked scripts 8 → 7, and five of those seven can never run on the original either — the reachable remainder is two AI files and nine names.

Gate::Inside(squad) is a walk with a certain shape and an inferred mechanism. 0x00429310 asks one predicate of every member and all of them must say yes, so an empty squad is inside and one man outside decides for the rest; a member in a holder says no before anything geometric is asked. Gate::Outside (0x00429420) is the same walk inverted, sharing the predicate, which is what pins its polarity — it is registered by gbr.exe, called by no script, and deliberately not bound. The predicate itself (0x005295d0) mints a pathfinder query, sets bit 0x100 in its options, asks for a route from the unit to the gate’s settlement’s central building, and answers yes when the route comes back empty. What that bit selects is the one thing not read; with the two entry-point names and the call site it can only mean “needs no route, because it is in there already”. This engine asks the same question of the structure it keeps for it: the member stands in the same LsaPartition area as the central building — the decomposition of walkable ground into regions crossable without an obstruction, which is what “needs no route” measures, and a lookup rather than a search per member per tick.

InvadeThroughGate is five guards and then two orders. The gate must resolve, be a building (kSyncBuilding), and be very broken; the squad must exist and must not carry SF_NOAI. Then the squad takes the caller’s state and the clock, and every member has its queue replaced with advance on the gate’s own position and then capture on the settlement’s central building — the two verbs a human player would give. Two details are the original’s and are reproduced: the walk stops at a hero, so whoever stands behind one in the deque gets nothing, and the lock is temporary — kSquadLocked goes up for the duration and the pre-walk flags word is written back on every exit, including the early one, so it is a re-entrancy guard and not a lasting mark. One write is named and not reproduced: each ordered member gets bit 19 of the map’s UnitFlags word, which the loader does not keep.

And a second live bug, in an entry point with ten call sites. Building::IsVeryBroken was health < maxhealth / 10 here and it is / 20. The tell is the shift: after mov eax, 0x66666667; imul, sar 1 is a divide by five, sar 2 by ten and sar 3 by twenty — and the executable writes sar 3. Ten had been read off the magic constant without counting the shift. InvadeThroughGate settled it from a second direction, because it guards on the identical five instructions before it will order anybody through a hole; the two now share is_very_broken rather than each carrying the arithmetic, which is the point of finding it this way. The corrected threshold moves a real number: a wall at 8% of full health used to read as very broken and does not.

22 faults, 22 caught, after a first round of six survivors — and every one of the six was a case the tests could not reach rather than a claim in doubt. Two needed the gate and the town in different areas before “it measures against the central building” could fail; one needed a settlement planted on the water, because kNoLsa on both sides of the comparison is the only way to see that no-area is not an area; one needed a handle naming no squad rather than an empty one; one needed a unit standing in for a gate, since a real gate passes the building test; and one needed a member that already had an order, because replacing an empty queue and appending to it look identical.
Where a squad is actually sent (done)GAIKA::GetDestPoint/1 — 1 site, one of the three in AIOSENDSQUAD.VS. Names with no body 16 → 15; sites 17 → 16.

It is not the node's centre, and that is the whole reason it exists. An army ordered at a walled town wants the ground outside its nearest gate, not the square it cannot walk into, and the unit argument is what decides which gate is nearest. 0x00427b80 answers, in its own order: (0, 0) for a GAIKA naming no node; the node's centre for a node with no settlement; and from there the settlement's central building position as a running answer that every remaining branch either improves on or leaves. When the central building is a heir of BaseTownhall it takes the nearest gate to the unit and steps 450 world units beyond it, along the ray from the town centre through that gate — and the bounds test that follows refuses rather than clamps, falling back to the central building when the offset leaves the map rectangle. No gate within the original's own initial best of 200,000 units leaves it there too.

One branch is not read to the end and says so. A heir of Outpost takes a second path whose guards are read — the settlement's owner is not player 15, the settlement is hostile to the unit's owner on the same one-directional rule AllEnemiesInHolder uses, and an object the settlement holds at [+0x5e] resolves — but whose 1,300-unit offset is built from two points this project has not identified. This engine answers the central building position there, which is where the original's own running answer sits whenever one of those guards refuses, and is a place a squad can walk to.

The order of the two class tests is the executable's and deliberately not this engine's SettlementKind: kind_of asks Outpost first because an outpost is the more specific thing, and 0x00427c19 asks BaseTownhall first. They differ only for a class descending from both, which the retail install has none of — agreeing by accident is not agreeing.

One departure, and it is about tests rather than about the game: a world with no match rectangle at all skips the bounds test instead of failing it. Refusing everything there would make the entry point answer the town centre in every synthetic world, which is a different function.

18 faults, 16 caught, and both survivors are proved equivalences that now say so where they live: a node's centre and its settlement's central building are the same point by construction, because GaikaTable::build seeds one from the other; and the len <= 0 arm of the vector step has a zero numerator as well as a zero divisor, so nothing can tell it from its absence except the division by zero it prevents. The first round left seven, and closing five of them took a non-gate building nearer the unit than any gate, a gate standing on the town centre, a gate 900,000 units away, a settlement whose central building descends from nothing, and a handle whose object is gone.
The shortcut, and the only three-order itinerary in the family (done)Squad::UseTeleport/4 — 1 site. Names with no body 15 → 14; sites 16 → 15; and AIOSENDSQUAD.VS is down to one blocker, which puts PrepareAiTransportShip/5 at the head of the sole-blocker ranking.

It is the shortcut arm of a pair. AIOSENDSQUAD.VS writes if (teleport.IsValid) l.Cur.UseTeleport(teleport, state, cmd, pt); else l.Cur.SetCmd(state, 0, SF_ADVCHOOSER, cmd, pt); — the same journey with and without a shortcut, which is why the two arms share state, cmd and pt. Three guards, each answering false and each releasing the cmd string on the way out: the receiver must resolve and be a CVXTeleport (a class test, where GetEnterPoint next door takes any object at all), the squad handle must name a live squad, and that squad must have a first member.

Then up to three orders per member, the queue replaced rather than appended to: cmd at the teleport's own door, teleport with the teleport as its argument, and cmd again at the caller's pt. The first of those is issued only when cmd is one of move, advance or sneak — 0x004298ac is three fixed-length comparisons and nothing else — so any other verb teleports the squad without walking it to the pad first. That is the original's behaviour rather than an oversight; the one call site only ever passes one of the three. The walk stops at a hero and the lock is temporary, both exactly as InvadeThroughGate's are.

And the door is GetEnterPoint's door. 0x005cda00 plus 0x004db650 is the same pair 0x004dce60 runs — the object's own origin plus the nearest element of its type-1 point list — so the two now share enter_point_near rather than each deriving the list. One difference is named: the original passes a literal 0 for the water/land selector here where GetEnterPoint passes “is the unit a ship”, so a teleport's door comes off the land list however the leader swims.

18 faults, 16 caught, and both survivors are the same hole rather than two: measuring which unit the door is taken nearest to needs a teleport with an authored door list, which needs a height layer for sim/entrance.hpp's round trip to keep a point at all — and the GAIKA bench has no height layer, while the bench that does has no teleport. The picker and the list selector are pinned where the layers exist, in a new case over the shared enter_point_near; the choice of unit is read and written and says so where it lives.
The crossing, and a debt this page said would come due (done; the debt it narrowed is paid two rows down)PrepareAiTransportShip/5 plus the four Ship::*AiTransport entry points — and AIOSENDSQUAD.VS runs. Names with no body 14 → 13; sites 15 → 14; blocked scripts 7 → 6, five of which can never run on the original either. SQUADMONITOR.VS is now the only reachable script this project is holding up.

This is a debt coming due rather than a new name. sim/world_host.cpp answered three constants for Ship::HasAiTransport, ClearAiTransport and GetTransPt, and the note beside them said exactly why that was allowed and exactly when it would stop being: the values were derived — no reachable path could change the field, so its value was the one it was born with — and the note ended “whoever implements PrepareAiTransportShip must add the state and these three readers in the same change, or they become three silent lies”. A derivation resting on “nothing reaches this” is exactly the kind that expires. This is what paying it looks like.

The entry point is short and the work is in two places. 0x0042e180 runs the same search CheckLsaPath runs, refuses anything but a length of three, and takes element 1 of the route — the water in the middle. That is why lsa_route now exists: the original's search fills a list of area ids and this reads index 1 out of it, so the search that answers the length has to be the search that names the sea, or the two can disagree about which one a ship is wanted in.

The acceptance rule was already in the tree, written from the other side. ship_available_in is 0x00441730's predicate and CheckLsaPath has been asking it of every water area since the pathfinder bootstrap: living, the player's, standing in that sea, and its running command is idle, boardunit or advance. Two of those three carried documented approximations, and one of them is now real: a boardunit ship is available only when its pending order equals the caller's, verb and point both, which was always true while no writer existed and every ship carried "" and (-1, -1). A ship already ferrying somebody else's crossing is now correctly refused. The advance clause is still the family's standing approximation and says so.

New state, saved and not hashed. The order is a verb and a point per ship. The original keeps it on the CVXShip; here it is AiSystem::ShipTransport, because the dumps print no such field and aihash is zero in all nine of them — folding it into World::state_hash would pull a subsystem into the determinism contract the shipped build left out of it, which is the argument sim/squad.hpp already makes for a squad's AI fields. Section version 13 → 14.

One debt is narrowed rather than paid. Ship::ApplyAiTransport was a no-op over values nothing could change; it now does its tail — the clear — which is a real effect the moment a real order can exist, and is what SHIP_IDLE.VS reads on its next pass. What is still missing is the middle: the squads the original issues toward the transport point. That matters less than it looks, because AIOSENDSQUAD.VS gives each squad boardship itself two statements after it asks for the ship, so this is a second route to a dispatch that already happened. The first thing to pull on is that 0x00442880 takes a deque and not a string, which the old note had as a string parse.

The scan order is this engine's and not the original's, and it is named: there the area keeps a list of the objects standing in it; here the sweep is ascending by object id. The two agree on which ships qualify and can disagree on which qualifying ship comes first, which matters only when more than one is free in the same sea.

20 faults, 17 caught, and all three survivors are proved equivalences now annotated: an unnamed player that the route search refuses one line later anyway; a command-count test that command_name's own empty answer already covers; and the route's reverse, which cannot matter while the length guard admits only three-element paths — element 1 is the middle whichever end it was built from. Closing the length guard itself needed a picture this file did not have: two seas, land and water alternating five areas across, so that a route of five exists to be refused.
The detour, and a script that gets a vote (done)Squad::CalcGoAround/0 — 2 sites, both in SQUADMONITOR.VS, and the head of the leaders list. Names with no body 13 → 12; sites 14 → 12; SQUADMONITOR.VS 5 blockers → 4. Coverage 33,951 → 33,957.

It is geometry, then a script, then a walk. 0x0042e300 takes the front member’s position P, the current node’s centre C and the destination’s D — the destination being AIDest with DestGAIKA behind it, the reading squad_ai_dest already is — and scores every neighbour of the current node two ways. A side test first: -cross(D-P, N-P), and a neighbour is kept only when its sign matches the current centre’s, so the detour is on the side of the line of march the squad is already on; zero counts as not-negative on both sides, and the test is off entirely when the centre itself is on the line. Then the score, |(N-P)·(C-P)|, smallest wins — the neighbour most nearly sideways from where the squad stands — with a tie going to the later neighbour, because the skip is on strictly-greater. Then CheckMAIKA.vs gets a vote. A candidate that survives both tests is put to the shipped script through the same trampoline GetEconomyScript and MinNeed use (0x0043d670 beside 0x0043d740), on the owner’s search path and with the owner 1-based, and a false drops it. The script runs after the score test and only for candidates still in the running, which is load-bearing because it has side effects — it raises the neighbour’s priority — and they land in this order. The first neighbour is always asked, because nothing has been kept yet.

Two refusals after the search, and one write before the walk. A survivor that is the squad’s SrcGAIKA is going back, not around, and one that is the destination is the way there; both answer false. Otherwise SrcGAIKA is overwritten with the detour node — which is how the monitor’s SetMAIKA(sq.SrcGAIKA, sq.AIDest, sq.GAIKAIn) on its next pass records the detour as the way-point — and the walk is the family’s: lock up, every member in deque order, lock restored. Per member the running command is rebuilt from its kind (a plain verb, a point-verb at its point, an object-verb at its object — the three type tests at 0x0042e638, 0x0042e683 and 0x0042e6e2, which are the three values CommandArgKind has), the tail is dropped, move to the detour’s centre is appended and the rebuilt runner behind it, and the runner is ended: [run, rest...] becomes [move, run′], step aside, then carry on. A class binding no move is passed over without ending the walk, because the hero test is only reached by a member that was ordered.

The trampoline’s failure answer is the opposite of its sibling’s. 0x0043d740, the int one, answers 0 when the runner reports a failure; 0x0043d670, the bool one, answers 1 (0x0043d70c). A missing file is therefore no objection to a detour, and that is reproduced. A file that is there and cannot finish is reported as this call’s failure, which is run_script_now’s rule everywhere — and run_script_now is exported from sim/ai.cpp now rather than local to it, with ai_script_chunk beside it, because one runner is one set of refusals.

The arithmetic is the original’s width. Products and sums are 32-bit and wrap; nothing a shipped map can hold makes them, and the wrap is kept so the sign is the original’s sign in every case rather than in every case that has come up.

36 faults, 36 caught — after three rounds. The first sweep left three standing and each became a test rather than an annotation: AIDest winning over a differing DestGAIKA; the script being told SrcGAIKA and not GAIKAIn, which needed the stand-in script to record what it was told; and the owner’s search path, which took two profiles carrying two copies of CheckMAIKA.vs that disagree about the south. A fourth, added in the second round — a dead front member — survived once because the dead slot’s (-1, -1) happened to pick the way back, and was made observable by changing where the squad came from. The bench is five towns in a cross on open ground, so that every claim about sides and scores has a node on each side to be wrong about.
Where to flee to: four gates and a weighted distance (done, two equivalences labelled)NearestHospital/1 — 1 site, in SQUADMONITOR.VS, the free function a fleeing squad asks for its refuge. Names with no body 12 → 11; SQUADMONITOR.VS 4 blockers → 3. Coverage 33,953 → 33,957.

A hospital is an own settlement with room and food. 0x004316b0 walks every node in id order and keeps the settlement that passes four gates: it exists, it is the squad’s player’s, its holder is not full (the count-and-cap pair Settlement::IsFull reads, Holder::full here), and its store holds 300 food or more — [[set+0x5c]+0x1c], which took reading Settlement::food beside Settlement::health to name: the word at +0x5c is the warehouse and the one at +0x5e the holder, and the two readers differ by exactly that byte.

Then it weighs the enemies standing there. Every squad filed under the node whose strength is non-zero, whose owner the squad’s player counts an enemy, and whose presence carries AI_STAYING — staying alone, not the census’s “in this node” — adds its strength. One exclusion differs from the census’s and is written down as such: a squad carrying SF_PEACEFUL is left out only when its owner is player 14 or 15 (0x004319c6, 0x004319cb). A peaceful squad of a real player counts here where GAIKA::Eval skips it on the flag alone; the test plants one of each. The node’s settlement query is asked too and its answer added when it reads as an enemy staying, which for a settlement the second gate has just made the asking player’s own is identically zero, and is not reproduced. More than three quarters of the squad’s own strength standing there and the settlement is passed over.

The score is a distance with three multipliers. The plain distance from the front member to the central building, isqrt through the table at 0x0067c3d0; times three halves when the central building is a heir of BaseVillage (0x00440010, the same test RRepair reads); doubled when the enemies there are worth more than half the squad’s strength — reachable, but not restful; and times the area-path length, 0x00441fe0 with the empty transport string, which is the worker CheckLsaPath is and lsa_route already was, now declared in the header. No path drops the hospital, and the worker’s side effect — bumping the ship need on an unreachable sea — happens here as it happens there. Lowest wins, and a tie keeps the earlier node.

The other two are equivalences and are annotated where they live: a strengthless squad adds nothing to a sum, and a settlement node’s centre is its central building’s position.
The wagon that follows the army, and the ceiling (done, three equivalences labelled)Squad::EvalAttach/2, Squad::FoodComing/0, Squad::SendFoodWagon/2 — one site each, the last three names in SQUADMONITOR.VS, and the monitor runs. EvalAttach is a score in two factors. 0x00424550 refuses four ways — not an object, not a hero, leading more than fifty, or bound somewhere other than the receiver (AIDest against AIDest, squad_ai_dest on both sides) — and otherwise multiplies the hero’s standing against the caller’s floor (n - min + 1 at or above it, n - min + 100 below: the under-strength squads are worth far more) by level / 10 + 1, the hero’s effective level through the same vtbl+0x114 AttackSetForTraining reads, now effective_level_of in the economy header rather than local to it. 14 faults, 14 caught.

The other two needed a thing this engine did not have, and the honest version of it was already half there. In the original a food wagon is a unit — a Wagon-class peasant with a cargo and a kind, in a peaceful squad, running follow on the soldier it was sent after — and FoodComing (0x0042a5b0) sums the cargo of every such wagon whose follow is aimed at a unit in the asking squad, while SendFoodWagon (0x0042a750) looks first for one to redirect, then for the nearest own settlement with 100 food within range, loads a mule there through the body CreateMuleFood uses, and orders it follow. Here a wagon in flight has been a row with a build timer and no object since the economy landed — the journey is abstracted away for every wagon the economy moves, and the cargo lands when the timer runs out. So the mule after an army is the same abstraction one field further: Wagon::follow, the unit it was sent after, saved and hashed; a wagon with one has no destination and is never delivered — when built it stays in the table as the larder of whatever squad that unit is then in, and the feeder’s food search draws it down as its second path, after the holder’s store and before starving. That is the original’s mechanism — a hungry unit finds the wagon following its army the way it finds any food within reach — with “within reach” read as “in my squad”, which is the one thing about it that is this engine’s and is labelled. FoodComing is then the sum over that table by membership at the time of asking, built or not, and SendFoodWagon’s second phase is written as read: own, 100 food or more, within range, nearest strictly, the load clamped to the store.

What is written down rather than written. SendFoodWagon’s first phase — redirecting an existing wagon whose running command is not aimed at a unit and, when aimed at a building, only at one in a BaseTownhall settlement — cannot select anything here, because a wagon has no object and no command; and the decision between the two phases (the wagon unless the settlement is 1,200 further) is therefore the settlement every time. Both are described beside the body and neither is code. max_load is not applied, which is CreateMuleFood’s standing and is named there.

All three are annotated where they live.

It snapshots the passengers (the holder’s list, before anything moves), lands them through UnboardAllUnits’s own body — 0x005c84b0, now unboard_all in sim/boarding.hpp and shared — finds their squads (0x00442880 takes the list, not a string, which was the old note’s first thread; each unit’s squad handle appended to a deque once, in order of first appearance, a non-unit left out), and orders each squad on through 0x0043ec00, the core all three Squad::SetCmd overloads share and now squad_set_cmd: the squad’s own state restamped, no flags set, SF_ADVCHOOSER cleared, and the crossing’s verb at its point — the cmd and pt PrepareAiTransportShip was handed. Every shipped hero started at level 1 with no skills and no items (done)The map carries per-object attributes the simulation never read: Level on all 16,171 units, hs<Skill> on the heroes, slot<n> on 205 inventories, amount on 26 traders, stamina on everything and display_name on 93. So Aeneas took the field at level 1 with nothing on his belt, every veteran legionary at level 1, every loaded caravan empty. The writer's verbatim attribute list made them readable, and the loaders were read for what each one does. Level is 0-based. 0x005dd510 parses it and calls the unit's SetExperience (vtbl+0x118, 0x005dbf70) with exp_table[Level + 1], the running-total threshold table at 0x009bf87c, and level_for_experience (0x005d28c0) of that is Level + 1 — the 5,125 at Level="0" are level 1, the 3,797 at Level="9" level 10. Here that is the earned level with the progress counter at zero. Skills are written, not spent. The hero loader (0x00530810) matches attributes against the hs* table (0x00824b68, the enum's order) and calls SetSkill (0x0052def0), which clamps to [-1, 10] and writes the byte with no look at a balance, because there is no stored balance: AvailableSkillPoints (0x0052da80) is derived, min(10 per offered skill, level) - spent — one point per level. load_skill is the first; the second is the balance itself, derived at every read rather than stored — so a level-up is a point without anyone granting it, a skill sold back is a point again, and nothing can drift from the skills and the level that are saved. The stored balance and its grant_skill_points went with it (section version 21). Items go in by AddItem (0x005af5d0: every slot* attribute in document order, the item minted by id, a full holder refusing), amount lands in [obj+0x1cc] which Wagon::amount reads, stamina in [obj+0xc4] unclamped — which is why the dumps show 20 on buildings whose class says 0, a disagreement this tree had left standing rather than read — and display_name in the string at [unit+0xa0] that the info bar shows. inventorysize is parsed and dropped. A script does not outlive its object. UNIT_AI_KILLALL.VS (Egypt, Balcans) resumed from an Attack on a unit that had died and been despawned meanwhile, and its .AddCommand found the handle naming nothing. A string against an integer compares as strings — the 76 hits, all ESH_BUILDARMY.VS:381's if (EnvReadString(set, "NeedTechSucceed") != 0) reading a slot EnvWriteInt fills with "0" or "1", the only two such sites in the corpus. The retail compiler is statically typed and refuses an operator it cannot resolve (Infix operator %s can not be applied.), and the file ships and the AI researches, so it resolves this one through a conversion; which one is inferred — int→str is the conversion + already has, str→int exists only as Str2Int — and the two readings differ only on the first read of an unset key. Labelled in vs-language.md with the emitter's address for whoever reads the opcode.

Squads at placement, and the manager that gates them (done)With the AI running headless, no computer player ever raised an army, and IMRUN_AILOG=1 — pr to stdout with every AIV_Log* switch on — said why in one line, 1,680 times: Skipping [no mil units]. The strongholds that were one building, and the six things behind them (done)IMRUN_SETTLEMENTS=1 showed every settlement on Balcans with gold 0 and food 0 and every Mutable stronghold as a single placeholder. Two gaps and four more behind them.

The map’s <settlement> numbers — gold, food, population, maxpopulation, maxgold, maxfood, extrasentries — now reach the settlement (748 of 748 shipped elements declare all six), laid over the class’s settlement_gold/settlement_food/settlement_max*/population/max_population the way the settlement constructor (0x005c3e60) reads an argument of -1 as “the class’s”. fill_settlement_class_defaults in sim/settlement.hpp is that rule, and every building inside the element goes on the roll.

Mutable settlements become a race’s at start_match — sim/mutable_settlement.hpp, from 0x00552bf0 / 0x00551bc0 / 0x0058e3c0: two passes over the placeholders (players 1..8 first, then the rest, whose race is the owner’s if it has one and otherwise the nearest first-pass conversion’s, Gaul when there is none); a template drawn among Packs/RandomMapSettlements.bfhp’s 64 by name prefix (“Carthaginian Town”, the eight prefixes of 0x00590bd0/0x00590ec0 in Race order) with the world RNG; the placeholder’s buildings destroyed, the template’s stamped around its position on the 128-unit grid with the template’s own sub-cell offset and clamped to the map; the record rebuilt in place with the old name, population pair, gold and food clamped to the new ceilings, loyalty over; the placeholder’s alias (NO_MyTown on six conquest maps) rebound to the new central building (0x00551e6d). Installation::settlement_templates() reads the pack; SessionInputs::settlement_templates carries it. Labelled: the template’s centre is the box over member positions, not footprints (entity+0x288 never reaches the core); no footprint is stamped into the obstruction grid, as for every runtime building.

With towns standing, IMRUN_TRACE=<file> — every host call a script makes, with arguments, outcome and line, through a CallTrace tap on the VM that synchronous runs inherit — walked TS_EgyptTactic.vs to four more gaps, all in the corrections table: BestBarrack over an anchor-only roll, CanResearch/TSResearch refusing every hire and purchase (TSResearch now runs the shipped TSH_Research.vs), Research appending behind idle, onfinish never running, and no command ever charged. Balcans at ten minutes: heroes hired, research completing, 199 training orders, gold spent, the first blows landed. 50 faults, 47 caught, 3 labelled equivalences. Section version 17 → 18 (the squad watermark, the finishing commands). GameSession::load rebuilds the LSA and GAIKA tables over the settlements as restored.

This row left one regression open — the save sweep at 23 of 25, Balcans and Crossroads diverging after the load with a freshly trained unit walking in — and two newly reached trap messages, AIDest/0 and TakeNearbyItems/1 on the handle GetSquad answers for a squadless hero. All three are closed in the row below.
The two things a load put back wrong, and neither of them was hashed (done)The save sweep’s last two failures, and both were the same shape: state a load did not restore, in a place no hash channel looks, found only because imsave compares every turn after the load and IMSAVE_DIFF=1 names the object. Neither was the materialisation, the tables, the watermark on the squads or the finishing list — the previous row had ruled those out, correctly.

The first was a watermark the save did not carry. GameSession keeps the first object id start_object_scripts has not considered, so that the per-turn sweep gives a SpawnGroup copy its idle without a bit per object. It was process state: the restored session had been created and started over the map’s objects, so its watermark sat at the map’s next_id(), and the first turn after the load offered idle to every object spawned since the match began. Five units on Balcans — the ones freshly trained and walking into their towns under an enter command, whose own idle had been retired — each got a second UNIT_IDLE.VS, and that script’s Stop(1000) dropped the route the original was still walking. Turn 41, one turn after a 40-turn save. The save now has a session section — the first that belongs to neither the world nor a system — carrying that one number; a save without it is refused rather than defaulted, because the default is the wrong answer above. State vector 16 → 17. docs/formats/save.md has the layout.

The second was a seam a move-assignment took away. At the 100-turn save, four turns after the load, a unit already inside its town walked back in: UNIT_ENTER.VS’s .settlement.Units().Contains(this) answered false on the restored side over a garrison that held it. Settlement::Units is an alias — a pool entry that resolves to the holder’s roster through a function pointer EconomySystem::start installs on the live pool — and World::deserialize decoded the pool into a fresh object and move-assigned it over the live one, resolver and all. Every alias handed out after a load read as empty. The pool now adopts the decoded entries and keeps its own resolver (ObjListPool::adopt_entries), which is the one way a decoded pool becomes the live one.

The tool grew two things on the way. IMSAVE_TRACE=<text> is imrun’s call trace on both sessions at once, tagged A and B, so a divergence is read as the first host call the two sides answered differently — which is how both defects were found, in a diff of two traces. And the reference session, a second run of the same seed that check 3 compares against, now has to save byte-for-byte what the first run saved: asked of Balcans while its divergence was open, it held, which is what sent the search to the load side.

And the two trap messages were one rule, missing. pack_squad(kNoSquad) is the invalid handle (kNoType, 0) on purpose, so that .IsValid reads it; it is therefore what GetSquad answers for a hero in no squad, and the squad domain’s receiver check called it a wrong type. HERO_AI_KILLALL.VS asks .GetSquad.AIDest == .GetSquad.GAIKAIn of exactly such a hero. The invalid handle now names no squad, which every squad entry point already had an answer for — AIDest and GAIKAIn zero, TakeNearbyItems false — and a value of some other type is still refused. The whole installation traps zero times, on every runnable map, with the AI on and every stronghold standing.

Three faults injected — the watermark left where the fresh session put it, the pool move-assigned, the invalid handle refused again — three caught, each by the test written for it: test_group.cpp round-trips a session with a spawned copy whose idle was retired, test_economy.cpp round-trips a world across an alias, test_squad.cpp asks AIDest of kNoSquad. 25 of 25 saves round-trip; 351 Python tests pass — the corpus-globals ladder was still pinning selu as the residue two commits after sim/orders.cpp bound it, and the residue is empty now, which the test says instead.
Where the numbers come from

python3 tools/host_coverage.py joins imcheck surface against a fresh parse of all 885 shipped scripts. Its last column — call sites resolving to no table entry — is a correctness figure, not a coverage one, and should stay at zero. imcheck globals prints the 109 compiled-in constants for the same reason, so that a corpus census reads the table the engine was built with rather than a list somebody transcribed into a Python file. imrun $GAME <map.bfhp> [turns] [ms] [map-number] reports enrolment, areas, terrain, sequences, campaign state and the trap tally, most-hit first, and python3 tools/trap_sweep.py runs it over every map in the installation and totals them. It discovers containers by content — which is how it found Packs/RandomMapSettlements.bfhp, a real runnable map inside an LZIS wrapper that corpus.container_paths cannot see — and asks each one which maps it holds rather than hardcoding that the conquest numbers its seven 3, 4, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10.

Two things the tally is not, and both have cost time. It is not comparable across runs unless the turn count and turn length match, and no figure previously quoted on this page said what its were — at 3 turns the same install gives 96 distinct messages against 60 turns' 35, so the tool prints its settings beside its number now. And it ranks what a run reached, not what is broken: a site is only counted if every statement before it already works. The receiver defect below showed about sixteen hits against 554 unreachable call sites, understating itself by roughly 35×. Rank by what has to be understood; let the tally say only how much stops trapping once it is.

The tools this loop needs, and what they cost before they existed. python3 tools/verify.py is every check in one command: the build first, as the stale-binary guard requires, the net tests alone (they open real sockets on timers, and once failed under load), then every other check at once. The quick tier is about 9 seconds wall on a RelWithDebInfo build-core; --full adds the app build, the save sweep and the Python suite, run across every core with pytest-xdist under --dist loadgroup, for about three minutes; --war adds the war pass and is in no tier. Both sweeps run their maps in parallel and print exactly what a serial run prints, which was checked. The fixtures check keeps each file’s verdict until the file, the installation or the checker changes, so it is under a second warm. Most of what had made verification slow was IMPERIVM_SPATIAL_CHECK riding on every Debug build and answering each spatial query twice; it is now an opt-in CMake option. What still costs most: the save-path fault test, one run of about two minutes on a single worker. Running them by hand costs more than running them, and getting the order wrong costs a round trip that says rebuild first and nothing else, which this page’s author has now paid twice.

tools/re/ is the reverse-engineering toolkit, and it is in the repository because it was being written from scratch every session. gbr.py reads the player’s own gbr.exe — disassemble by address, find strings, find cross-references. hostregs.py prints every host entry point the executable registers, with its body address and argument type codes, which settles a receiver and an arity in one line; docs/formats/vs-host-api.md is inferred from call sites and says at the top that the executable wins where the two disagree, and it already has, repeatedly. faultrun.py runs a fault sweep across six git worktrees in parallel. They need capstone, the only third-party dependency anywhere in this project, and it is confined to that directory on purpose: nothing that builds, tests or runs the engine imports it. docs/legal.md rule 2 governs their use — they read, and what they print stays on the contributor’s machine; naming an address so a reader can check a claim is what the rest of this tree does, reproducing the instructions at it is not. Two lines of pasted listing had got into sim/world_host.cpp and are prose now.

Two measurements worth keeping, because both were three times cheaper than they looked. The trap sweep over all 29 maps is 29 seconds, not minutes — it had felt slow only because it was being run in the background behind monitors with a minute’s granularity, so half a minute of work cost several. And core_tests was 26 seconds and is now 0.6: HostRegistry::declare asked find whether an entry already existed, find re-sorted the table whenever it was dirty, and declare had dirtied it on the previous call — so building the two-thousand-entry shipped surface was two thousand full sorts of an ever-growing table, once per test fixture, about a thousand times a run. An exact-signature map on the declaration path alone fixes it; find is left on the sorted order, because it is the hot path and the lazy reindex now happens once after the declarations rather than once per declaration. That number multiplies: a fault sweep runs the whole suite once per fault, so the same twenty-four faults went from 160 seconds to 33 — and from ten minutes before the worktrees. Two changes that compound to eighteen times, neither of which was the one that looked slow.

And a note on how the regression tests here get written. Twice now the sequence has been: inject every plausible single-point fault into the mechanism, run the whole suite against each, and let the survivors name the tests. It is slower than writing tests from the code, and it produces a different set — the six cases combat gained are not the six anybody would have written, and one of them only works because the obvious version of it passes with the fault injected. A test that has never been seen to fail is not a check.

Standing decisions

Rules that govern the work

No test opens a window. The app has a headless mode — --headless or IMPERIVM_HEADLESS=1 — that creates the GPU device with no window, draws into a texture of --width × --height, reads shot: and --screenshot back from it, sends sound to SDL’s dummy driver, never takes focus (on macOS it runs as a background process with no Dock icon, activation policy “prohibited”), and paces frames at 60 Hz as a display would. tests/conftest.py sets it for every test, so anything pytest launches is headless; 14 app tests pass that way, and a window probe sampling every 0.3 s through a whole run counted none. A window is for a person: IMPERIVM_HEADLESS=0. Checked against a windowed run of Alesia: pixel-identical over the overlap, every pixel equal. The only difference was size — macOS shrank the 1100×850 window to 1100×828 to fit the screen, and the centred camera moved the picture 11 px — which headless mode cannot do, since its texture is exactly the size asked for. On Linux, headless still needs an X11 or Wayland display for SDL to choose Vulkan.

A test is about what it asserts, not about when the AI acts. A check that needs combat orders it or gets it from placement, and a scripted app test waits on turn:N or a paused frame and asserts nothing that depends on which turn a key landed on. The network war tests broke three times in a day while they waited for the computer to go to war. Likewise a network test waits on the fact it asserts — a seat recorded, an acknowledgement heard — rather than on a number of turns that happened to take long enough on a quiet machine: the late-join net test counted a joiner seated once everyone had run thirty turns, but the host learns it only from one acknowledgement datagram, lost like any other and resent 40 ms later, while the joiner is thirty turns in about 15 ms after loading. The fixed drop sequence kept that ack off a lost slot on a quiet machine; under load, extra keepalives shifted it onto one, about one run in twelve. The test now waits for the host to hear it; the product, which resends until acknowledged, was never wrong.

No test writes into the installation. Everything the app writes — the F5 quick save and F9’s read of it, the save dialog’s slots, settings.ini, Profiles/, <conquest>.campaign.ini, the editor’s maps — and every read of its own files goes to one user directory: --user-dir DIR, else IMPERIVM_USER_DIR, else, in a window, the installation’s Saves/ (the editor’s maps: Scenarios/), as a player expects. Headless with neither, it is a fresh temporary directory removed at exit, and the start-up line user data: says so — a temporary directory rather than refused writes, because a refused save takes a different path from a made one. tests/conftest.py sets IMPERIVM_USER_DIR to one directory per session. tests/test_corpus_app_userdir.py presses F5 and changes an option against a shadow installation (the real one’s data linked in, a Saves/ of its own) and finds that Saves/ byte-for-byte unchanged; against the app before the rule, all four of its tests failed. Found because a scripted headless key:F5 quick-saved over the owner’s Saves/quicksave.bfhp. The headless tools (imsave, imrun, imconform, imcheck, immap) write only where --out, --campaign or --golden tell them.

The core stays freestanding. engine/core may not touch SDL, the filesystem, the clock, threads, ambient randomness, or floating point; tools/check_core_boundary.py checks it. Required by the conformance harness, and portability to WebAssembly, iOS and Android is the same property seen from another angle.

Determinism, without exception: no floating point in simulation; one RNG, explicitly seeded and serialised as world state; iteration order is state. Systems run in registration order, and that order is part of the simulation's definition.

What the original does not hash: pathfinder, exploration, scriptstate and aihash are zero in all nine dumps. Pathfinding and fog were deliberately excluded from the determinism contract; respect that rather than improving on it.

Ship no assets, ever. The executable reads the player's own installation in place. No original code, no decompiler output, no bundled installer. See docs/legal.md.

Label inference. Where shipped data underdetermines a rule, implement one reading, mark it, and record the evidence and the alternatives. Combat carries three counter-unit modes because the evidence genuinely supports two orientations and asserting one would have been dishonest.

Every format spec carries a “what is still unknown” section. A specification that quietly omits what nobody understood is worse than one that names it.

Vertical slice before breadth. The failure mode is eighteen months of infrastructure with nothing playable. Force the milestone even if most parts stay stubbed.

Imperivm remains the property of Haemimont Games and FX Interactive. This project contains only independently written code and file-format documentation, and requires you to own the original game.

Sizing is indicative. Three to five engineer-years to full parity; the playable vertical slice is reached: a skirmish plays to its end by itself, in the app as headless.
